Wondering in the Wilderness

A Modern Philosophical Dialogue

by A. M. & N. V. Pope

An imaginary philosophical discussion on the spontaneous lines
of the Greek dialogues, set in the wilderness
of modern British Columbia, Canada

Published by Philosophical Enterprises
10 West End, Penclawdd
Swansea, SA4 3YX

© N. V. and A. M. Pope

ISBN 978-0-9503790-7-4

This book is dedicated to the memory of
Donald Edward Pope (1904–1954),
father of Neville Vivian Pope, who provided for
his children a bit of Athens from which to
sally forth into Babylon.

Acknowledgements

The idea for this book was conceived on a long holiday in British Columbia, in 1988, staying at the real Helmcken Falls Lodge, then owned and run by our daughter Beverley and her husband, Peter Louwerse, to whom we are indebted for introducing us to the delights of the wilderness and the people who live there. These include Jean and Ian Cummings and other members of staff at the Lodge; John and Edwina Kurta of Kamloops and their son, Jim. Some of the adventures narrated were shared with these folk. We are also indebted to the very helpful assistant at the Tourist Information Centre in Clearwater who recommended the use of Roland Neave's excellent and informative book, Wells Grey Park.

However, we should like to point out that although the locations used in the book actually exist and that this narrative is based on the incidents described, the only genuinely named characters are the horse, Steel, and the dogs, Smokey and Blue. But although the human characters in the book are fictional, the characteristics are real enough.

We would also like to acknowledge the help and encouragement given

by Penelope Gower, of Briton Ferry, UK, at whose urging this book is published.

Mary and Viv Pope

Foreword

Despite all the miracles of modern scientific and technical achievement - or perhaps even because of them - the vision of an integrated philosophy of nature which was sought by the ancient pioneers of Western culture, as far back as the ancient Greeks, still eludes us. All that the common man may contemplate, nowadays, as a result of all those centuries of intellectual effort is a veritable wilderness of logically conflicting ideas and bizarre theories about the world and man's relation to it.

But perhaps we have given up 'within an inch of the treasure'. Removed from the sort of environment in which to be 'informed' is to be entirely cynical about any hope of a true understanding, the characters in this book venture into this wilderness of ideas in the relaxed, academically unfettered manner of those early philosophical pioneers. This is to continue, in a natural and unselfconscious way, the dialogue those thinkers began, more than three thousand years ago, but which has yet to be completed.

1]

Synopsis

This work is a modern continuation of the philosophical theme raised in Plato's dialogue Theaetetus and in George Berkeley's Dialogues Between Hylas & Philonous, namely, the question of what the world is, as compared with how we perceive it. This present dialogue is, of course, as different in style from those earlier dialogues as they were from each other, given that these are written in different eras. Moreover, this present dialogue takes account, as those earlier ones quite obviously could not, of the advances in our knowledge of the world that have since accrued from modern science.

The participants in the dialogue are fictional characters from various walks of life who happen to be holidaying in a wilderness park in British Columbia. They spend their days hiking, canoeing, riding and fishing, during which time there are one or two adventurous incidents. In the evenings they return to their hotel, a former hunting lodge, where their after-dinner conversations, centred on those incidents, gradually turn into a sustained philosophical discussion on the theme of man's relation, as an interactive observer, to the wilderness and to the universe in general.

Out of these conversations, a thesis gradually emerges, based on some ideas supplied by Dave Turner, a semi-retired communications engineer, whose radical ideas the group find interesting. The antithesis comes, initially, from Turner's old friend, civil engineer Tony Carter. However, his most implacable opponent is a conservatively minded professor of physics who appears at dinner one evening. Discussion begins on the subject of man's smallness in comparison with the hugeness of the wilderness and then turns gradually to issues about relativity and the intermediary role of light in perception. As the dialogue moves towards its logical conclusion, naive but relevant questions about some crucial issues of modern physical science and cosmology, are introduced by members of the group and explained in commonsense language to those like Rick Jackson, an intelli-

gent but uneducated salesman, who is quite fascinated with ideas entirely new to him.

The 'story', such as it is, is fictional but is based on some real-life incidents. Though hopefully of sufficient interest in itself, it is not meant to compete with literary novels and adventure stories. Its purpose is to carry the philosophical argument and to provide light relief, or "breathingspace' where necessary. The story-line, however, is not entirely incidental, for not only does it provide the conversational thread on which the 'beads' of the philosophical argument are strung, it also serves to illustrate the philosophical points made, avoiding undue abstraction and keeping the discussion as I'down-to-earth' as possible. In this way, the progression of both the story-line and the philosophical argument converge naturally towards a logical conclusion, with the aim of stimulating new thinking along the lines begun by the early philosophers but which, in our hectic modern industrial society, is now lost amidst the mundane concerns of politics and commerce.

September 2009

Chapter 1: The Lodge

Man the Microbe

The man from Oregon looked around him at the forests climbing the steep river gorge towards the snow-capped peaks beyond.

"My, this is big country!' he declared to the two men approaching him. "You're the first guys I've seen in days!'

The two men agreed. Having carried their large Indian canoe to the top of the river-bank, they had stopped for a moment's rest at the place where he had happened upon them as he pulled up in his camper to photograph the river at that point. These men were in their late fifties, he guessed, and obviously well-adapted to the outdoor life.

'Say,' continued the man from Oregon, "Where can I get some food and a beer around here? I'm sure tired of my own cooking.'

'I know what you mean,' sympathised the shorter, stockier man. "There's a good place a few miles on down the road, the Lodge, where we're staying. It's a log building with cedar cladding. You can't miss it.

'Especially as it's the only building for miles,' added his companion, jocularly.

'Thanks,' replied the American. 'I guess you guys are English. That right?'

'Right,' answered the taller, sparer one, 'though Tony here's turned native and lives in Vancouver. I'm still based in the old country. But I come to British Columbia whenever I can. Out here a man's got room to breathe.'

'Sure is,' agreed the American. 'By the way, I'm Rick Jackson, from Portland, Oregon.'

'Dave Turner,' said the taller man, proffering a handshake.

And I'm Tony Carter,' said his companion.

The three stood watching the flow of the water, as the river sped on its way towards the nearby falls. Then Carter spoke.

"What brings you to BC?' he asked Jackson. 'Td have thought there was plenty of space in your own country.'

'I aimed to go to Yellowstone, but the forest fires this year drove me north. You guys come here a lot?

'Every year for the last five years since the lodge was opened in 1983.' Turner replied, 'ever since we discovered this area. And as long as we can use the Lodge as a base, we'll keep on coming.'

The two Englishmen had been friends since their childhood, but Tony Carter had left England to work as a civil engineer in Canada, where he had founded his own engineering company based in Vancouver, the running of which he now left more or less entirely in the capable hands of his sons. His companion, David Turner, was also an engineer but in the field of communications. He had moved into the rapidly developing information-processing industry and now, having sold his patents on a new real-time computing system to a company which continued to employ him in an advisory capacity, his job was little more than a sinecure. Both men had therefore won their freedom to spend as much time as they wished at their leisure pursuits in their advancing age much the same as when they were boys.

Since those early days they had always enjoyed the outdoors, so now that they were semi-retired they were keen to make full use of what the wilderness regions of British Columbia had to offer, so they were spending a few weeks at the hunting-lodge, now turned motel, in one of the province's wilderness parks.

Carter began hauling the big canoe up to where they had parked their truck. "Well, come on, Dave, or the moon'll be out next!' he called. "Let's get this thing aboard or we'll be late for dinner.'

"What time's dinner?' asked the American.

'Seven o'clock,' Turner told him, 'but you'll have to book in first. If you hurry on ahead, you'll be in time. When we get there, join us for a drink.'

'Be glad to. Need a hand with that thing?'

'Thanks, we'te okay - got it down to a fine art,' replied Carter. "You pop along and book yourself in.'

With a cheery wave, Rick Jackson walked back to his camper and eased his large frame into the driving-seat. By the time the two Englishmen had loaded their canoe and their gear onto their truck, he had driven off in search of the lodge.

Travellers to the Lodge were always impressed by its setting in a forested valley of the Canadian wilderness. To those who gathered there, after a day's hunting, fishing, riding or canoeing, it was always a welcome sight, and its warm friendly atmosphere, good food and beer provided a relaxing end to an arduous day.

After a golden autumn evening, dusk was now descending across the valley. The high mountain peaks could still be seen bright with snow, pink with sunset against the darkening blue of the eastern sky. All around stretched a distance deepened by its silence.

Rick Jackson was pleasantly surprised at what he found when he arrived. The Lodge was as the men had described, built of rough-hewn cedar logs, with cedar cladding on the outside, seasoned relics of a disastrous forest fire. It was three storeys high and surrounded by a wooden deck or verandah. Inside, it was warm and inviting, with the scent of pine-smoke and cedar, and he felt immediately at ease. This feeling was enhanced by the welcoming smile of the receptionist who, he later discovered, was the owner's wife.

He introduced himself, as the Englishmen had recommended, and after explaining about his camper, she called a member of staff to take him to the camp site, where his vehicle was soon hooked up to the services.

After a shower and change of clothes, Rick strolled back to the Lodge, passing, on his way, the large corral and the log chalets where non-camping guests stayed. He paused outside the Lodge for a while, drinking in the scene. The forest came to the edge of the road on one side, but the Lodge itself was set in meadows, with a little creek running past the corral.

'Hi there!' came a voice from above. Looking up, he could see the two Englishmen sitting on the balcony which ran around the Lodge's second storey.

'Come and join us,' Carter called.

"What'll you have to drink?' asked Turner.

'A beer will be just swell,' Rick answered.

He went inside. From the reception area, he climbed the wide pinewood staircase to the first floor, which contained the spacious dining room and bar. As he joined the two, at their table on the balcony, his drink arrived.

'Just what I needed? he said, watching a shaft of sunlight illuminate the cool amber liquid. He downed a half of it with relish. 'Nectar? he pronounced. Then, as he slowly sipped the rest, he went on, conversationally, 'In my line of work I travel all over, and I thought the U.S. of A. was big. But this place is something else! It sure cuts a man down to size"

Yes,' replied Carter. "Before we met you at the falls, Dave and I had been trekking and canoeing since dawn and we didn't see a soul - not even so much as a footprint or, thank God, even a piece of human litter. In the city we fancy we're something. Out here, with nothing around but mountains, forests and silence, you can feel like a microbe.'

"That's right,' responded the American. After almost a week on his own in the depths of the Great Outdoors, the joys of complete solitude had begun to pall.

'But that's what I like about it,' Carter continued. 'I find it helps to restore a natural sense of proportion. It's a great antidote to the sort of enlarged sense of self-importance that society instils in us!'

'Which, I suppose,' commented Turner, 'is why some people prefer never to venture far from what's familiar to them.'

Carter agreed. 'Yes, but for some the thought of the wilderness excites them. They romanticise about it and like to see it in pictures, through a window and so on, and prod at it like poking a lion behind bars. But not everyone likes to be right out here amongst it, dealing with it on its own terms, as we do. Right, Dave?'

At that moment, the owner of the Lodge joined them.

Carter made the introductions. 'Rick, this is our host, Don Frost. Don, this is Rick Jackson, from Portland, Oregon.'

'Ah, yes,' said Don, shaking Rick's hand. 'Megan told me about you. I see you got your camper hooked-up all right. Had a nice day, everyone?" he asked.

'Fine,' responded Turner, 'canoeing the Clearwater really is something! And portaging the canoe in that terrain takes some doing, eh, Tony?'

'You could say that,' his friend replied. 'By the way, Don, thanks for bringing the truck back from the lake. As you can imagine, Dave and I were very relieved to find it waiting for us at the falls.'

'No problem,' replied their host, another Englishman. 'Glad you enjoyed the trip. My wife and I did that run when we first came out here. We aimed to explore all the lakes and rivers but in seven years we've still done only a fraction of them.'

'Hi, Walter,' Don called, turning to a young man who was quietly sitting in a corner of the balcony, sipping his beer. 'Come over and meet these friends of mine.'

'Be glad to,' the young man responded. He moved across to where the little group was sitting.

''m Walter Collins, from over Blue River way,' he said, shaking hands. 'Pleased to meet you, guys.'

Don made the introductions. 'Tony Carter, Dave Turner and Rick Jackson,' he said, indicating the three in turn.

'Say,' the newcomer said. 'I couldn't help overhearing what you were saying, about how vast this country is and how small it makes you feel. That's just how it gets me, especially when I horseback over the mountain trails.'

'You a rider?' asked Rick. "Wish I was! Never had time for it, though.'

'If youve got time tomorrow,' suggested Don, 'my wrangler's taking out a group to ride some trails in the morning. It would be no problem to add you to his list.'

Rick looked rather doubtful.

'Well, I dunno,' he said, 'I ain't ever been on a horse.'

'Go on,' Carter encouraged. "You'd enjoy it. Good chance to see some wildlife. Tell you what, how about us going along as well, Dave. We can postpone our fishing-trip till the day after,'

'Yes, why not, eh?' Turner grinned. "You want to watch Tony, Rick. He's an organiser. He'll organise your holiday if you let him.'

'No problem,' the American assured him. 'Probably do me some good

to get my butt off that driving-seat and onto a horse. I drive plenty in my job.'

"That's settled, then,' said Don. 'Three more riders for Charlie tomor-

>

Tow.

Feeling the chill of the evening, the men moved off the balcony into the softly lit log-walled dining room, which was cheerfully warmed by an enormous wood-burning stove. They helped themselves to food from an inviting array set out on a large board under a warming-lamp and sat together at a corner table to eat it. The sun had long since set, its last rays turning the pinky whiteness of the mountain peaks to a delicate rose-red.

The warmth, the beer and the satisfaction of enjoying a good meal began to work on them, and between long and relaxed silences, their conversation flowed easily.

'D'ya know,' said Rick, 'on the road from Jasper, when I headed out across the mountains, I began to wonder if everyone in the world had disappeared except me. Then I came upon a guy who'd broken down. I stopped to help and he told me he'd been waiting two days for someone to come by! Can you believe that!'

Recollections of a similar kind eventually brought them back to the topic which had engaged them earlier, of how small and insignificant a man could feel when confronted by the vastness of nature.

"What brought that home to me most,' Turner said, 'was on a flight back to England. After it got dark I sat in the cockpit behind the captain. I stayed there, chatting with the crew until the sun started to come up over the top of the cloud-layer - a sight if ever I saw one! At that height you couldnt look at the sun, of course, except through the green visor that came up across the window - automatically, as it seemed to me.'

'Some guys sure get all the luck,' commented Rick. 'How come I never get invited into the cockpit?'

Tt was Carter who answered him. "You've got to have the nerve to ask someone, Rick. That's how he does it.'

'It's not nerve, Tony,' countered Turner, 'it's my insatiable curiosity. I remember another time when I sat in the jump-seat, behind the crew.

When the night was at its blackest - over Baffin Island, or somewhere - you could see the atmosphere curving away below, picked out in a kind of faint luminosity. Then I looked up into space and saw, high above us, the Aurora Borealis. I'd seen it from the ground, of course, but never from above the clouds, so I hadn't realised how high-up it was. It was like the folds of a lace curtain seen by a bug underneath. It was waving as if a window had been left open.'

"That must have been real spooky,' marvelled Rick. 'Sure, I've seen it from the ground, but from there it always seems the same height as the clouds.'

Turner continued. "It reminded me of my first look through an astronomical telescope. I was about eighteen at the time. It was a large reflecting telescope, you understand, with the mirror silvered on the face, not the back, to minimise the amount of glass you were looking through. Of course, I'd seen photos of the planets and viewed them through binoculars and ordinary telescopes, but this was different. Looking at Jupiter, for instance, made me feel that I could reach out and catch it like a beachball, and I could clearly see its moons. The longer I looked, the more it seemed I was seeing all that hugeness directly, as though I was right out there in amongst it. And the space seemed bigger because the stars, although much more intense, looked even smaller and further away than they do to the naked eye.'

"That must have been really something!' marvelled Rick.

'It was,' answered Turner. 'It led me to studying astronomy, which convinced me more than anything of the immensity of it all.'

'I guess it doesn't hurt,' said Walter Collins, 'to have that kind of lesson in humility.'

'Perhaps you're right,' replied Turner. "But it makes me wonder, is that necessarily a good thing? I've met some people who don't like astronomy. Tt makes them feel very insecure. They might conclude that whatever they think and feel is of no consequence in this vast scheme of things. What, they might wonder, can any of us matter in that great ocean of stars, galaxies and space? What, they may say, will it all amount to when this very, very minor star we call the sun eventually shrinks to a white dwarf and

then expands to consume the solar system, erasing all traces of mankind's existence, as sooner or later, astronomers tell us, it must.'

Now that the first rush in the dining room had eased, Don had come over to join them.

'But why,' he asked, responding to Turner's last-heard remark, 'should any of that worry us when none of us will be around to see it?'

"That's true, Don,' Turner replied, 'but perhaps not everyone would find that reassuring. I didn't, for instance. I remember, most vividly, the sense of utter despair which, at one time, those thoughts instilled into me.'

This caught Walter's interest. "Why was that?' he asked.

"Well, it seemed to me that on that astronomical scale, all our human values and concerns are so very small and silly.'

'Do you still feel like that, Dave?'

'No, I don't, thank God!'

Walter was now intrigued at this somewhat surprising revelation of Dave 'Turner's. He didn't strike him as a man who would be concerned about such philosophical issues. 'If you'll pardon my curiosity, Dave, what happened to make you think differently?'

'Tr's a long story, Walter, and I wouldn't want it to strike the jarring note.'

"Why not? laughed Carter, his curiosity roused by this sudden new insight into his old friend's mind. "You got something to hide, Dave?'

"Thanks a bunch, Tony,' smiled Turner. "Nothing scandalous, I assure you. I simply don't feel that there's time to go into the full details of it.'

'Speaking for myself, Walter put in, 'Td surely like to hear about it. How about the rest of you guys?"

'Suits me,' said Rick, 'I ain't going nowhere!'

Carter agreed. "That's right! We're on vacation, here and, anyway, you cant say something like that, Dave and then just drop it. Come on, tell us about it.'

'Well, okay. On your own heads be it! As I say, that feeling of insignificance and insecurity began to get to me. This was during my early twenties, whilst working as a telephone lineman. I tried confiding it to others - including you, Tony - but it was like trying to converse under water. I was simply not wised-up and articulate enough for it.'

'Not many of us are at that age,' said Carter sympathetically.

'T realise that now,' replied Turner, 'but at that time I didn't recognise it. When I attempted to communicate it I was advised, by a psychiatrist, no less, not to think such deep thoughts, to cheer up and just get on with life, to busy myself with passing exams aimed at promotion in my job, making money, enjoying social success and all the pleasures that go with the mundane life ...

"The things that most of us do,' put Carter.

'Just so, Tony, but something kept nagging at me, and I couldn't understand why others didn't have these same feelings. I got the distinct impression that the people who should have been giving me answers were just trying to fob me off. In other words, they were trying to make me drop this disturbing awareness of mine, suggesting that, in effect, ] should blind myself to things I saw as clearly wrong, or false. In trying to engage people in logical discussions about it I was soon made to feel like a social pariah, something green at the bottom of a milk bottle.'

Rick was sympathetic. "You must have been pretty loused-up, feeling like that, Dave,' he remarked.

Walter had been listening intently. 'Yes,' he said, "It sounds like you had a very hard time, psychologically speaking.'

'Maybe,' Turner replied. "But spare me your sympathy! He smiled ruefully. 'I was searching for something, something deeper than what I perceived around me, a something-I-know-not-what - some kind of truth, I suppose, behind all the noise and confusion. But, of course, a young man's natural drive doesn't just stop when he becomes confused. Eventually, I turned that confusion on its head and threw it right back on its source. I wont say I became a drop-out or a desperado, but I did become extremely arrogant and wilful, something of a sociopath, you might say, scornful of all the idols of authority I had looked to for guidance but which turned out to have feet of clay. As far as I was concerned, there was no real authority anywhere, only the "jumped up", presumptuous sort, which seemed so utterly vain and stupid on the cosmical scale of things.'

Carter interposed. 'I remember that time well, old friend, That was when you developed your obsession for reading and writing sombre poetry.

Remember that thing by Omar Khayyám? You recited it so often that I can quote some of it myself, even now. How does it go? . . . "Into this universe and why not knowing, Nor whence, like Water, willy-nilly flowing. And out of it, as Wind along the Waste . . ." I can't remember the rest.'

""T know not Whither, willy-nilly blowing",' Turner finished for him."

"That's it,' said Carter, 'and there was that other bit, about the "veil" . . . how does that go?'

Turner again obliged, quoting with facility:

"When you and I behind the Veil are past, Oh but the long long while the World shall last, Which of our Coming and Departure heeds, As much as Ocean of a pebble cast.""!

"That's the one, Dave. Heavy stuff! What a pain-in-the-butt you were in those days! It's a wonder you had any friends left, since none of us could really understand what was bugging you. But there was another side to him,' Carter told the others, 'which gained him quite a following. When he wasn't being so morose and sarcastic we had some very good times. Looking back on it, I suppose our crowd found his arrogance and contempt for authority somewhat attractive. None of us, in those days, felt much sense of responsibility, but Dave least of all! He treated obedience of any sort as though it were a mortal sin. At that age, of course, most youngsters are rebellious. But the majority are also, to some extent, naturally cooperative - naturally social. Not Dave, though!' He paused, looking quizzically at his friend. 'In some ways, Dave, you seemed - strange to say - almost conscientiously antisocial - an interesting nutcase, as I recall.'

'Don't remind me, Tony,' said Turner, with a shudder.

'And what made you change, Dave?' enquired Walter. The young Canadian found it difficult to picture this distinguished-looking Englishman as the sort of person these two were discussing.

1. The Rubdiyat of Omar Khayyám, Transl. E. Fitzgerald, verse 48 in 2nd ed., 1868, Collins, London (Ist ed. 1859).

"What makes you think he did?' joked Carter, and dodged a playful punch from his friend.

But Walter was not to be put off. 'Seriously, though, Dave,' he persisted. "What eventually brought you out of that negative state of mind? Was it that like so many of us, in the end you got tired of the friction and decided to conform?'

"Grew up, you mean?' Turner grinned, wryly. "No. Because the cause of my depression had. been rational, the solution, for me, also had to be rational. I couldn't take to drugs or any kind of panacea like religion or any other belief-system - except, sometimes, the thought of suicide.'

'So, what happened?' asked Walter.

"Well, I eventually found, to my surprise, that there was something I'd missed, a logical "flip-side" to the way P'd been thinking up till then. To give the full details would be tedious. Let's just say that when I thought it through I became aware that what I had assumed was a depressing realisation of the immensity and indifference of it all, was in fact, a fallacy.'

He rose from the table and helped himself to dessert. Walter wondered if this was a signal that the subject was closed. However, he was reluctant to let the matter drop, even at the risk of offending someone who, after all, he had only just met, so when Turner came back to the table, Walter found himself returning to the subject.

'You said, Dave, that you found a fallacy in that earlier, disturbing way of thinking. I'm sure we'd all like to know what you mean by that.'

Turner's mind seemed to be wrestling with something long past. 'Yes, I suppose it is rather curious, come to think of it. The new awareness came upon me in the same way that the old one had, as a result of my interest in astronomy - in the philosophy of nature, you might say. I realised that it wasn't so much my own mind but our whole cu/ture and the science it's based on that had made me think in that peculiar way. . .'

"What peculiar way?' Carter interrupted.

"The way we've been discussing, Tony, which sees the universe as a sort of wilderness; a great void sprinkled with mindless mechanical systems of atoms and so on which, in the end, are the only realities - according to our physical science and cosmology, that is.'

"What's so peculiar about that?' asked Don. "That's the standard way of seeing things, I would have thought!'

'My point exactly, Don. But as I would have argued at the time, if we think in that way, then how else can we see ourselves other than as mere accidents of nature, less than the tiniest bugs in some infinitesimal corner of an infinite and uncaring universe? Okay, so we rise out of oblivion, like bubbles in a swamp, and enjoy some moments of consciousness, pleasurable and painful, until oblivion finally closes back over us. But what point can we say there is in all that? What purpose does it give us in life other than to keep eating, sleeping and breathing in and out - to "survive" for as long as possible and get our "kicks", or at least to steer ourselves away from too much unpleasantness and pain which are bound to catch up with us in the end? Is it any wonder that in a society such as ours, so many prefer to take refuge in "wine, women and song" - or in drugs, as is more fashionable nowadays?'

'I see what you mean,' mused Walter. "But doesn't a faith in God or something help to offset that?'

'For some, as I've said,' Turner replied. "But, to reiterate what I said earlier, 1 had to find a completely rational, that is, a thoroughly objective and unbiased, /ogical solution, one capable of coming properly to terms with my scientific knowledge - such as it then was. I didn't find any religious answers convincing. At the time, I would have declared that taking to religion was as big a cop-out as taking to drink or drugs.'

'Tm with you on that, Dave,' Carter said, 'and it explains the relevance of Omar Khayyám, with his despairing resort to "wine, women and song". But, unlike you, those problems never bothered me. I was never as deep into that cosmological kind of perspective as you were. My concern was more with the "here and now", the world as it appears to me. I simply couldn't understand your overriding need for a "rational solution". How could I help you seek a "solution" when I didn't even understand the

"problem"?'

'And,' responded Turner, 'what J couldn't understand was why you and the others couldn't understand!'

Their companions, listening to this exchange, became aware of some deep anguish beneath. the controlled exteriors of the two old friends. Walter,

in particular, caught echoes of battles long past, and perhaps still not entirely resolved. Carter's next remark seemed to confirm this.

"Yes, Dave, I well remember how frustrated and ironical you used to get with the whole gang of us. But while I shared, to some extent, your awe of the infinity of things - as, indeed, I still do - it never sent me into your sort of fizz. The things that bothered you in that way were far too deep for me. I knew I wasn't sufficiently qualified to worry about such things - and neither, I felt, were you or, indeed, any of us, really. So, what you didn't understand, Dave, was that I couldn't see why you weren't as prepared as we were to leave it to the experts. I was happy enough with the hand that nature had dealt me. I suppose I'm too naturally optimistic and fun-loving - or stoical, perhaps - to have shared your continual "rage against fate".'

"Then yourre one of the lucky ones, Tony,' retorted Turner, with a just a hint of irony. Then, as if regretting this, he added, 'At that time, people with such optimistic and trusting natures as yours did, I confess, irritate me. But now I truly admire it. It's a kind of courage which laughs, you might say, in the face of adversity. Society, I now see, could never function without it! But, for whatever reason, I could never share that blind optimism. In me, that ability to tolerate ambiguity was somehow missing. So for the likes of myself - and I know I can't be the only one with that kind of affliction - the full realisation of those ambiguities in our society or, as I now see it, the lack of any rational compass, was mind-blowing. All systems of authority, political, educational, religious or whatever were purely arbitrary, with no common logical point or purpose.'

This gave the group pause for thought.

Then Don said, "That's got to be why so many youngsters, these days, have to take refuge in drink and drugs. The reality they are presented with isnt to their liking, so they have to look for something better.'

'I guess,' added Rick, 'that it's maybe why there's so much crime.'

'Undoubtedly,' Turner agreed, 'fuelled by the predatory trade in drugs. In a society which provides some real assurance of a man's place in it and of that society's purpose in the wider scheme of things, morality is in good season and social conformity with it is more or less automatic - as it once

was in the Golden Age of Athens. But when that assurance is seen to be bogus, any criticism of its entirely brittle norms and standards is a danger, and has to be treated as a crime. As you well know, the problem of dealing with that sort of crime used to be met, in past societies, either by liquidating the "heretics" or subjecting them to methods of "correction".'

"But you can't do that in a modern democracy, said Carter, 'so in a democratic society like ours, what do you do with your "deviants", your "heretics"?"

"That's just the problem, Tony, with modern heretics. If you can't answer their questions, they won't just simply go away. You can't just dump them, and they won't let you ignore them. So what's the answer? That's the problem our kind of society faces. If it could provide some genuine solutions to genuine questions, then it might command some natural conformity, the sort of conformity which is consistent with freedom. But since it doesn't do that, then, as I see it, unless we revert to purely totalitarian solutions, we are stuck with those malcontents, in one way or another, as excruciating reminders of our society's philosophical failings.'

'I see what you're saying, said Walter. "You feel that a democracy that is without true philosophical direction is unworkable?'

"Yes, Walter, I would go so far as to say that without any consensus philosophy on which to base a society, there can be no democracy, only a continuous oscillation between anarchy and tyranny.'

'Now that intrigues me, Dave,' responded Walter. "You reckon it's, somehow, our philosophical knowledge - or, rather, the lack of it - that's at the bottom of all these personal and social problems?'

'Hey, come off it, Dave,' Carter interposed, 'you're surely not suggesting that muggers and car-thieves, drug-dealers, murderers and the rest of the criminal riffraff are all driven by the same philosophical and scientific awareness that drove you!"

"Well, of course not, Tony. Social confusion takes all sorts of different forms. What I'm saying is that a "free society" without philosophical consensus can't possibly hang together. It's like a ship whose parts want to float off in all sorts of different directions. What chance does it have of staying afloat, let alone know where it is, or where it's headed? And, yes, Tony, I would say that it is our peculiar 'scientific approach to nature which has

made it all come apart at the seams. That "science" of ours has somewhere gone all wrong...

'But we cantt just radically unthink it just for the sake of social harmony,' interrupted Carter. "We are stuck with that science. . .'

'And all the uncertainty it generates,' responded his friend. "The logical consequences of its peculiar cosmology - or "chaosology", you might say - are undoubtedly felt more keenly by some than by others. Some, like those muggers and so on you mention, may not be aware of it at all. But however that may be, no-one really escapes the problem.'

"You think we're all affected, then,' said Don.

"Yes, I do,' replied Turner. 'It jumps up and hits us, one way or another, directly or indirectly. And that, in my opinion, is basically why our modern world culture is so confused and violent. How else can it be when there is all that vigour in human society with nowhere sensible for it to go on this small planet. Like steam molecules in a heated boiler, with no real answers to deep-probing questions about human purpose and rational coordination, what sort of alternative is it for enquiring young minds to be fed a fudge of conflicting dogmas, with all search for truth at the philosophical level more or less officially abandoned?

As he spoke these words, Turner was amazed at himself for being so open with these personal confessions. But somehow it felt alright in this peculiar mix of people. Strangely, also, it was the first time in ages that he had attempted to engage in this way with his friend Carter. As if in answer to these thoughts, Carter spoke up.

'So, what's your alternative, then, Dave?' he asked, with just a touch of defensive irony. 'You reject any purely expedient solution. You don't advocate escape in drugs or religion - or "pie-eyed optimism". And my policy of naive stoicism never appealed to you. So what other alternative do you propose? Some kind of mystical solution? That's all that's left, surely, if you reject Science and Politics.'

"You know me better than that, Tony,' responded Turner. He looked up as he was handed another beer. 'Cheers, Rick!'

"You told us, earlier, Dave,' Walter reminded him, 'that the alternative you were looking for had to be a rational one. I guess you must have found

it, or you wouldn't be discussing it. But you still haven't told us what it was!

Turner reflected for a moment. "Well yes, Walter, I suppose I did find it. Tony mentions mysticism. My rational solution, my philosophical "flipover", you might call it, was to declare war on all mysticism in general. And 1 soon came to realise that the biggest source of all of the mysticism is the one that pervades and inhibits our society in the name of what we traditionally call 'Science'.

'Eh? said a startled Carter. "With your well-known scientific interests, how the hell can you say that?'

"Well, for instance,' Turner replied imperturbably, 'take this "Universe" we started discussing, which science regards as so vast and so real and of which we occupy only a tiny corner. Have you ever laid eyes on that "Universe"? Has anyone?'

Carter was amazed. "You're kidding, Dave! Surely, you can see it wherever you look. What else was it you saw when you looked through your telescope?"

'Not ail of it, that's for sure,' replied Turner.

'Hell, I realise chat, Dave! But if we don't ever see the whole Universe, at least we do see a fair-sized chunk of it, with our telescopes, microscopes and all'

'But I'm talking about the universe, Tony, not tiny chunks of what seems to us like one. How can we ever know that there is such a thing as a universe, a cosmic unity, if we can never see it? Where, other than in some imagined God's-eye-view of things, does what I see, what you see and what anyone and everyone else sees ever add up to that kind of unified Whole?'

'Well, alright, so we never see the Universe as such, Carter admitted, 'but it has to be there, hasn't it? Logically, I mean.'

'Why logically, Tony? The fact that we talk about a "Universe" which no-one ever sees or comprehends can in no way guarantee that such an entity exists, other than in human imagination.'

'You're losing me, Dave!' said Carter. Turner could see, from the look on the faces of the others around the table that he was losing them, too.

'Let me put it another way,' he offered. "The fact that we can think of an angel doesn't make that "angel" real, does it? I mean, what logical neces-

sity is there for us to think of what we conceive as an angel, with wings, feathers and so on, as existing anywhere other than in our imaginations?'

"True,' admitted Carter, 'but where does that get us?"

"Well, it's the same, surely,' answered Turner, 'with the concept of a Universe. The idea of a "Universe" existing in its own right, apart from our finite perceptions of it, can never be anything more than a belief, like a belief in angels or fairies. That institutionalised pure belief in a "Universe", with a space of its own and a time of its own, and of which we are never more than a tiny insignificant part, is the mysticism which, as I discovered, lies at the root of our Western Science. That, basically, is what I'm saying.'

"When you put it that way, Dave,' said Walter, 'I get a glimmer of what you mean. But if you take that belief in a self-sufficient Universe away, where does that leave us, if not in that dreadful state of mind you described to us?"

"Well, Walter, the "bottom line" in truly scientific realism is, surely, to start by shelving a// pure beliefs and, instead, to look carefully and critically at what is presented directly to our senses. My realisation that our classical idea of a "Universe" underlying all our sensations and perceptions, that we can never see or comprehend as it is "in itself" is just such a belief, openedup for me a whole new way of thinking, a whole new intellectual ballgame.'

'And what difference, exactly, did you find?' enquired Walter.

"Well, I gradually came to realise just how much the character of what we call the "universe" is dependent on the way we perceive things, the way we examine and explore them and describe our discoveries to one another in the language of the culture in which we are born and raised. So much depends, therefore, on the way we are educationally conditioned, the sorts of instruments we devise for studying the world, the grants that are awarded or withheld for making and employing those instruments, the opportunities allocated - or barred - for publishing our results . . . need I go on? In short, I came to realise how absolutely essential we are, as observers, to the scheme of things as we seek to describe it. In that way, we can't be as incidental and insignificant as our classical cosmology leads us to believe. We are, each one of us in his own way, essential to it as a member

of a community of observers. As a famous philosopher once put it, in this sense, "the world is language". This was a revelation to me.'

Turner paused for a moment to sip his beer before he continued.

'So you see,' he said, 'the fallacy I had committed was in failing to realise just how much of a purely human construct the "Universe" of science implicitly is. Failing to realise this, I had unwittingly accepted Science's word for it as though it were an irrefutable fact. In other words, what our typically Western "science" had taught us to think of as "physical reality" was, I had assumed, unquestionably real and that all else was illusion.'

Rick was getting confused, as were the others. "But surely science proves what's real with all the experiments and things?'

"That's not necessarily so, Rick,' Turner replied. "Let me explain. My salvation from my depressing mind-set came when it dawned on me that this so-called "realism" on the part of science had never been more than a peculiar dogma. I suddenly saw, from a purely personal, rational point of view, that this peculiar "scientific" tradition of thinking was just a social product, based on one of two different logical ways in which the "cat" of our historical development could have jumped. But it seemed to have jumped the wrong way. So I decided to set aside that historical alternative and explore the other logical track.'

'But what other track is there?' asked Don, 'Do you mean a new kind of science?"

'In a way, yes, Don. This meant seeing it all completely afresh, starting with what our sensations are telling us, directly, in the plainest possible language and reserving judgement on all traditional precepts. I then felt that I was no longer a tiny and insignificant creature, cowering in some corner of the "Universe", or a mere microbial dot in the wilderness. I felt that as an observer - especially as an individual free-minded observer - I had some real priority and therefore responsibility for perceiving and thinking in a critical way. In a sense, I was suddenly as big and significant as all that I beheld. In other words, f became what, if only I'd realised it, I had been all along; an integral part of nature, not some remote and impotent spectator as my society had led me to believe.'

Chapter 2: The Restaurant

Man the Measure

Looking around, Turner could see that the other diners had left and that the waiters were discreetly clearing the tables. Suddenly feeling rather selfconscious about what he now felt had been a somewhat long speech, he said, 'I think we should be leaving, gentlemen.'

'No hurry,' Don assured him, 'but I reckon the staff would appreciate it if we moved away from the table. Fetch your drinks and we'll sit by the stove.'

When they had settled themselves, Walter took up the discussion where they had left off.

'Say, Dave, that idea of the individual observer being right at the centre of things sure is interesting. I like the way it makes each of us important in his own right. It re-introduces a sense of self-respect and responsibility. Thar's a belief that's surely good for society, and it happens to be central to most religions.'

Carter pounced on this. "But I thought Dave said that this "flip-side", observer-centred. view was based on logical reason, not religious belief.'

'That's right, Tony,' Turner replied. 'My conceptual "flipover" was no religious conversion. It was, as you say, based on logical reason - scientific reason, dare I say. But I grant, Walter, that what you say about religion may be true, though it wasn't what I had in mind in changing my views.'

Carter was still doubtful. "That's as may be, Dave,' he said, 'but this idea of yours, of reality being man-centred is alien, surely, to any truly scientific way of thinking!'

'Alien to our classical scientific way of thinking, yes, Tony, but it shouldn't be alien to our modern science.'

'Ah, now I think I see where you're headed,' said Carter. after a thoughtful pause, "You're thinking of relativity theory, I reckon, with all its talk

about physical measures being "relative to the observer". But surely you're not suggesting that relativity literally makes the physical world as it is and as we observe it all one and the same!'

Unperturbed by his friend's somewhat vehement reaction, Turner replied, 'It all depends, Tony, on what you mean by "physical world". According to classical physics, it would be absurd to identify the world of matter with the world of observation -- or mind - in that way; but not according to modern physics, especially in the context of relativity, as you say. So you're right about that. As you seem to have guessed, it was my introduction to relativity which began to change my whole way of thinking - which completely inverted it, you might say.'

'As you know, Dave,' Carter responded, 'I studied relativity, too, because it interested me. And on one or two occasions, if you recall, we talked about it. But that was not in this personal connection, which I now find surprising. So, okay, but where does relativity say that the physical world and our perceptions of it are one and the same? I never saw it quite that way.'

"Well, doesn't relativity tell us that there are no measures of space, time, mass or motion existing in their own right, that these dimensions of matter, and therefore matter itself, are all integrated with our observations of it? And doesn't that include, by implication, all the vastness you observe, this wilderness, this planet, the system of stars, galaxies and so on? If you accept what relativity says, then you can't separate yourself from what you observe. So in that sense you would have to say that the world - the truly real world - is all part and parcel with your observation.'

Carter was appalled. 'Hell, Dave!' he exclaimed. "You know that's not what relativity means. Anyone with even a smattering of science knows that relativity, as you say, makes mass, length and time relative to the observer. But the way you're reading it, you're implying that nothing exists unless it's observed!'

Turner made to reply, but Walter Collins was quicker. "That's not a new idea,' he said. 'People throughout history, well before Einstein, have made that same claim, that "Man is the measure". One of these was George Berkeley, 2 famous Irish Bishop, who said "To be is to be per-

ceived."' To this he added, apologetically. 'I'm a clergyman, you see - nondenominational, I might add - and reading such things was part of my studies.'

'Ah,' said Turner, 'that explains the point you made about religion! But I agree about Berkeley. His philosophy came very near, in some ways, to anticipating the relativism of modern science.'

Turning to Carter, he continued: "To deal with your point, though, Tony, tell me, what else can it mean to say that something is relative to the observer if not just that, that it consists in being observed?'

"Well, yes, space and time are relative in that way,' Carter agreed, 'everyone knows that!' He paused, suddenly uncertain, and looked around at his companions for confirmation. 'Do we all know that?'

Like Don, Rick had remained quietly attentive throughout this exchange between Carter and Turner. Now he answered Carter's question.

'T've heard about it,' he said, 'but that's about all'

"Same here,' said Don, 'I've heard about "Einstein" and all that, but like Rick, I'm afraid that's as far as it goes. Like most people, I've always thought it was something highly intellectual, way above my head and of no real concern to me in my daily life.'

"Well, then,' continued Carter, 'let's leave out the technicalities. As I understand it, Dave, those observations which Einstein says are relative to each of us, are like different perspectives in space and time. Our ordinary three-dimensional views of the world are relative in the same way. But there's always a real world behind them which is what it is in itself, absolutely, independently of those perspectives. Don't-you agree, Dave?"

'You're right, Tony,' replied Turner, 'in Einstein's relativity there is such a permanence, underlying the observational perspectives. But that theory, as you must know, is contradicted by its arch-rival, quantum theory . . ." He broke off, remembering Rick and Don. Turning to them he said. 'Sorry, guys, perhaps we're getting a bit "airborne" here. You say you know next to nothing about these things, so perhaps I'd better explain. We needn't go deeply into it. Let's just say that according to standard relativity there is an underlying permanence, of the sort Tony mentions. Einstein called it his "four-dimensional space-time continuum".

'T've heard that phrase before,' put in Don, 'but I've never really understood what it meant. It's always seemed like science fiction to me.'

"Me, too,' added Rick.

'Well,' responded Turner, 'perhaps that's because it really és just science fiction, all that stuff about time-warps and so on. That's not the sort of relativity we're concerned with here, because it's so highly mathematical and abstruse. For our present purposes we can forget about that. It's not about the real world as we know it, so don't let that faze us. Let me explain. We all know what is meant by three dimensions. But adding a fourth dimension, mystifying though it seems, is not so, really. Just think about it. It only means adding to those three spatial dimensions, the further dimension of time, without which three-dimensional objects would have no duration or permanence - no existence, really.'

'That,' Carter remarked, 'reminds me of what H. G. Wells wrote in his novel The Time Machine, that it is impossible to imagine an "instantaneous cube".'

"Yes, I always thought that was a good explanation,' said Turner. "There's certainly no way in which a real cube can be without duration. So you see, Don, that's all relativity means by a "space-time continuum". There's nothing spooky about it. It means the permanence in the fourth dimension, time, of ordinary three-dimensional objects.

'T see that,' said Rick. 'If that's all it is, then P've got it"

'Just one small thing, though, Dave,' interposed Carter. 'As you said, those dimensions are all dimensions of perspective, not of some absolute world of classical imagining. But those dimensions of perspective radiate from a point. In other words, they are polar. How do those point-centred or polar dimensions square with ordinary Euclidean space, the space we are familiar with, in engineering, surveying, architecture, and so on - that is, the world we walk about in?'

'No problem there,' Turner replied. 'It's okay, because in public communication and interaction, all these different point-centred perspectives interlink to form what we normally regard as ordinary objective rectangular or cubic space. Also, may I remind you, old sport, that mathematically speaking, these polar dimensions and the rectangular frame-dimensions are inter-convertible.'

'Ah, yes, from polar to Cartesian and vice versa. Thanks for reminding me, Dave.'

Turner resumed his train of thought. 'So let's get back to what we were saying, about there being, in the theory of relativity, a permanence, or continuity underlying the observational perspectives. This is in complete contrast to relativity's arch-enemy, quantum theory, according to which there is no such underlying continuity or permanence. At the most basic microphysical level, zothing is permanent. Everything reduces, ultimately, to disconnected, or discrete (the word used in physics) events. These are called guanta. A quantum is not an atom. It is a unit of interaction between atoms. All atoms swap energy in whole packets of these units, and there are no interactions between bodies other than in those ultimate units of energy-times-time, or action. Unlike the classical 'atom', the time, or duration of a quantum interaction is very short indeed. This makes what seems to us the permanence of things in ordinary perception an illusion. At the quantum level, everything is seething, or jiggling, Even the atoms of a diamond, which we think of as the most permanent object there is, are all jiggling in and out of existence in the same way as in any other object.'

Turner paused, wondering whether he was going into things too deeply for his companions. 'Are you still with me, guys? he asked.

'Ye...e...8, I think so,' replied Don, somewhat uncertainly.

'It's surely a lot to take in,' added Rick. Carter nodded sympathetically.

'How about you, Walter?' Turner asked.

'It's tough to take in,' Walter replied, 'but to think of the very existence of things as taking place in bits is fascinating.'

'But isn't that the same as saying it's all atoms?' queried Carter.

'Not so, Tony,' Turner replied. 'It is in complete opposition to the atom, as Democritus conceived it. Democritus, by the way,' he said, addressing Rick and Don, 'was an ancient Greek thinker who came up with the idea that everything is made up of tiny bits of hard matter which he called "atoms". On the other hand, the quantum has no permanent position or continuous motion. Far from being a "space-time continuum', the physical substratum of quantum physics is a discontinuum. This means that everything, at root, exists and moves in completely unpredictable quantum

jumps. So any impression we have of permanence is no more than appearance. This is well known to experimental physics. So any idea of an underlying material permanence, as in Relativity and atomic theory, is an illusion.'

For a long moment, Turner scanned the faces of his listeners. Then, Carter said, "Carry on, Dave, I'm finding this most interesting. P''ve never thought of quantum theory in quite that way, as atoms flicking in and out of existence. But when you think of it, that's what quantum indeterminacy amounts to.'

Encouraged, Turner continued. 'As you are aware, then, Tony, this conflict between two equally well-proven theories has remained a major scandal of modern science for nigh on a century. When I learned about it I was disconcerted. Science had seemed to me so utterly confident, so absolutely God-given reliable. But here it was, caught out in complete contradiction, and this signalled to me, quite clearly, that the science I had learned and respected was, and never had been, more than a human construct.'

'I guess that's true when you think of it,' said Walter wonderingly. 'I've never looked at it like that before.'

"When J realised that I felt free - indeed, obliged - to review the situation for myself. I was convinced at least of the experimental truth of relativity and quantum theory in their separate spheres. But this brought home to me the sheer unreliability of my previous trust in this highly flawed, not to say schizophrenic science, with its so-confident theories of underlying realities. So, for the time being, I decided to put all that conventional theorising aside and work it out for myself. So, for the time being, I disregarded all theoretical talk of a "real world" behind the appearances and took those appearances themselves, the actual phenomena, as my fundamental starting-point in a new search for true physical reality.'

'But surely, Dave,' expostulated Carter, 'to turn your back, in that way, on the outside world and see reality only in the appearances is to invite madness! I mean, what about those great valleys, rocks and gorges you and I saw today? Are they no more than "appearances" flicking in and out of existence? They're still out there, aren't they, right now, in the dark, with

no-one observing them. And what about the ages during which they were formed, when the lava piled layer upon layer, to be cut through by glaciers, and so on? We weren't there to observe all that! Are you really saying that without us or anyone else like us being there to observe it, it never existed?"

'Well, Tony,' Turner replied. 'it all depends what you mean by "observe". Philosophers like Berkeley - as you well know' he said, in an aside to Walter - 'thought of "observing" as essentially a human, "mental" process, entirely distinct from the way we think of matter interacting with matter. But in modern physics that distinction is no longer tenable. As we now know, observing and physically interacting both involve exchanges of energy, so where, exactly, do we draw our line? To think of an absolute distinction between mental intercommunication and physical interaction is like trying to distinguish absolutely between pebbles and boulders. The

distinction is purely arbitrary.'

He moved aside to allow Don to stir up the ashes and put another huge log in the stove.

Carter was puzzled. 'So what are you saying, Dave? That it's all mental or all physical? Which?'

'Neither,' his friend replied. "What I'm saying is that the old categorydistinction between mental and physical, between "mind" and "matter", was not found written in the sky! It was an invention of man, of René Descartes in particular; and like all man-made inventions, that distinction stands to be revised in the light of new knowledge. That new knowledge comes, in this case, from relativity, in which the word "observers" makes none of the old distinctions between people and their observational instruments, which may be bits of pure matter. In that sense, everything is either actually or potentially an instrument of perception. So, for the purposes of relativistic physics, all those particles of earth, air, fire and water, the so-called "natural elements" which make up the world, are as much rudimentary "observers" as human observers are glorified "machines". Admittedly, the ways in which atoms and molecules interact, compared to the ways in which people communicate, are so limited and statistically

predictable as to be practically automatic. But that doesn't mean there's any truly radical difference in kind.'

Carter was finding this difficult to grasp. 'Let me get this straight, Dave. You're saying there's no longer any clear distinction to be made between observations - the sorts of informational interactions with nature which creatures with minds, like us, have - and physical interactions between things like atoms and molecules. But how can you justify that? Those two kinds of interactions are completely different.'

"In what way?"

Carter shifted impatiently. "Well, I'd have thought that was obvious. A physical interaction consists of things pushing and pulling one another, either by direct contact, or indirectly by means of forces like electricity and magnetism, all in strict obedience to known physical laws. The other consists of - well, mental interactions, like observing things and thinking about them, deciding what to do about them, and so on, all in a way which is entirely personal and capricious.'

'But isn't "observing,"' queried Turner, 'initially a physical interaction in which your nerve-endings are physically affected by the environment? And isn't what the observer does about it another physical interaction with the environment - a muscular push or pull?

'I suppose so,' agreed Carter, somewhat grudgingly.

Turner continued his questioning. 'And what about the processes in between, where the nerves convey their impulses to the brain, where those impulses are kept going round and round in various channels or stored in some other way until they are sent out along other nerves to trigger the muscles which move things? Aren't those processes physical, Tony?'

"Well, of course they are!' Carter replied.

'In fact, is there any part of that process which is definitely nonphysical?'

Carter pondered a moment and then admitted, "No, I don't see how there can be.'

'So, okay, Tony, where is there any hard and fast distinction between observing and interacting? As I say, where do we draw our line?'

Carter looked deep into his beer-glass, as though it were a crystal ball. 'Okay, Dave, I see what you mean,' he conceded. 'You're saying that any hard-and-fast distinction between "observing" and "physically interacting" is purely fanciful, that, basically, it's all interaction, on a continuous scale of simplicity or complexity.'

'Basically, yes,' Turner replied. "You could say that physical laws like those of mechanics, chemistry, electricity, and so on, are what they are only because the interacting entities are relatively simple, with simple and statistically predictable responses to which it is easy to apply "laws", and that as those entities get more and more complex they become less and less automatic and more "lawless", as it were.'

'Okay,' said Carter, 'you mean that their interactions then conform less and less to simple "mechanical" laws and become more and more like the laws that govern a society, such as moral, political and forensic laws.'

"That's it, Tony,' agreed Turner, 'except that in this case, those interrelations we call "physical" are more suitably called "communications", which is more apt, I would say, than "observations". But there's no absolute difference between these in the nature of things. It's all no more than a question of semantics - of /anguage, in point of fact.'

Here, Walter interposed. "But this does seem to imply, Dave, that it's all basically physical.'

'Ah, no, Walter, I see I've misled you. The fact that you can give a physical account for every stage in the process of communicating information isn't the same as saying that the communications themselves are entirely physical. In what you might call a "physical" interaction, some atom in a distant object, a distant star, say, interacts with another atom in the eye of the observer or in some observing instrument. And all processes involved in observing an object are of that same inter-atomic sort. But to assume from this that all observation is "physical" in that very narrow sense would be like assuming that because every piece of a pointillist painting is a dot, the picture itself is a dot. It's the patterns or forms that the various physical interactions make that constitutes an observation, in the same way that it is the patterns of the bits of paint, not the bits of paint themselves, that make, say, a Georges Seurat masterpiece.'

'And those patterns, submitted Walter, 'are not physical?'

'Not in the old materialist sense of "physical", the one that stems from Democritan atomism. In that earliest kind of "physics" it was thought that nothing existed but material "atoms" or, rather, "atoms" and "void". Anything else was what Democritus called "convention" . . .'

"Hold it there, Dave,' interrupted Don, "You've just said that everything reduces to quanta. Is this any different from atoms and void?'

"Yes, Don, the difference is that although Democritus split the solidity of matter into "atoms", he never thought of doing the same with its duration. If he had, then his "permanent and irreducible material atoms" would have been seen to be events, not bits of matter. In Democritan physics, those atoms had only the two defining measures; mass and motion in space. In modern physics, the terminal irreducibles, or rather, the quanta, have the three defining measures mass, motion and time. In other words, they are not bits of matter swanning around passively and haphazardly in space, waiting to bump into one another to create those objects that are familiar to us. These guanta don't exist and move about 'in space', they are space - and time and motion. They are discrete, intrinsically unconnected packets, or quantities of action into which all physical objects and processes ultimately analyse. All space and ail time analyse down to these terminals of microscopic analysis, and at that level there is no space, no time nor matter other than within these tiny packets of action, and there is no space or time outside, surrounding or separating them. Hence the name quanta applied to these ultimate physical quantities.'

This obviously gave Don something to think about. Meanwhile, Walter took up where Don had left off.

'But you were saying, Dave, that in the old kind of "physics" there was thought to be nothing, basically, but "atoms" and "void". And now, from - what you said to Don, it seems that the same applies to modern physics as well, except that Democritus' "atoms" are now replaced by quanta. What's the difference in that respect?'

'Okay, Walter. As you say, the same applies to those particulars, whether we regard them in the old way as "atoms" or in the new way as "quanta". The point I was making is that what those classical physicists failed to

realise - and perhaps even modern physicists fail to realise - is that it takes more than just those discrete particulars themselves to connect them into a plurality, or form - in the same way that it takes a reader to turn a string of letters into a word, or a sequence of sounds into articulate speech or music.'

After pausing for a moment, Turner continued: 'So the essential difference, Walter, is this. In the old physics, the only thing that could interrelate those blind and randomly moving mechanical "atoms" so as to form physical objects, as wholes, was, implicitly, either pure chance or Classical Science's tacitly presumed all-over cosmic deity or demon. In the new physics there is no such ubiquitous observer. There are, as we discussed, only point-centred perspectives which are - well, what else but ourselves, as individuals, in communication with one another by the use of what we generally call language? That's why, in my flip-side way of thinking, I came to see that it has to be our personal observations, shared with others in an observational community, which are prior in any scientific study or description of the world. To presume that we can somehow just step behind those observations and see something even more fundamental than them, like those "atoms" floating about in some way that "God" may be presumed to see them, and all coming together to create our perceptions of them .. . Well, that was all part of what I came to see as the classical fallacy. We're never, ever, in a position to see behind those observations of ours. Observations - and I'm including instrumental as well as direct observations of course - may analyse out to observational elements, that is, to data or quanta, as I indicated just now. But any analysis of observation always begins and ends, inevitably, in observation itself Even the physical data are terminal irreducibles of that same analysis.'

As the companions around the stove mulled this over, the only sounds that could be heard were the muffled voices and the occasional clash of dishes and cutlery from the kitchen behind the dining-room. Evenwually, Carter spoke.

'Let's get back, then, Dave, to my question, earlier, about the physical formation of those mountains and gorges. I still don't quite get it. Forgive me if I am repeating myself, but how can we, right now, as human obser-

vers, connect with all that geological stuff. You're not saying, surely, that all those processes of rock-formation and so on, most of which no-one ever observed, never happened?'

"Of course not, Tony.'

"Okay, then since we weren't there to observe it, how are we supposed to know it was ever there?'

"Well, let's think of it this way,' offered Turner. "How does any human observer know exactly what another human observer observes?'

Carter thought about it. "You tell me,' he said.

"Well, they can't, can they! No two observers can occupy the same spot in the same instance and see what they both see at the same time. They can only discuss it, that is, communicate their perceptions, which, of course, stresses dependence on the correct use of language.

"Yes, of course,' Carter agreed.

'And those "observers", I think we've agreed, are not necessarily people.'

'Just so, Dave. As you said, all physical objects, even atoms can be regarded, to some extent, as "rudimentary observers".

'Right, Tony. And, following from what we've been saying, the more rudimentary those observers are, the more nearly their "communications" become physical interactions. and vice versa.'

"With no radical distinction, as you said, between the two,' added Walter.

'Precisely! said Turner.

"Okay, then,' Carter conceded. 'So now let me rephrase my question. How can we know what those "rudimentary observers" observed when there were no human observers around? You're saying that we communicate with them - or they with us - but that it's more usual to call these most elementary communications "physical interactions". Right?

'Right,' Turner replied.

'So I suppose you would say, Carter went on, 'that we can work out from whole processes of those physical interactions, or at least from the physical records of those interactions in rocks and things, what happened in the world when there were no observations going on - of the human kind, I mean.'

44, Turner nodded. "Yes. Since the processes involving those "rudimentary observers" are of the sort which conform to simple physical laws, we can reconstruct the history of all we see around us, in retrospect, in the ordinary way, by projecting backwards from what we presently observe in terms of those simple laws. These tell, in a very obvious way, how the mountains were formed, how the rivers cut through the rock, and so on, without us having to assume that human perception was ever actually involved in the process until now, in retrospect, of course.'

'So,' reflected Tony, 'our knowledge of things that went on in prehistory and of things going on even now in places like the next room where neither we nor anyone else may happen to be present is, as you see it, all part of an extended observation.'

Walter agreed. "But in a much wider sense of the word as used by Berkeley.'

"Yes, it's all basically information, added Turner. "What we see in the rocks and other more or less permanent features of the environment is the observational end-result of relayed informational processes, of communication, just like the history which comes to us in written or spoken form but in a much more elementary language and on a much vaster scale, which you might call the "language of nature".'

Their companions had, for the most part, listened quietly as the two old friends batted their ideas to and fro. Now Walter joined in again.

'Let's see, Dave, if I can sum up what you're saying. You say that in the final analysis we should think of atoms, molecules, et cetera, as low-level observers and communicators - "observicles", let's call them - and that it is these chains of simple communications-cum-interactions, relayed on and on, in space and in time, which give us our so-called "physical" connections with things in general, both historically and geographically, things that we, personally, may not actually see, touch, smell or whatever.'

"That's right, Walter,' Turner assured him.

'I guess,' said Rick, 'you mean like fossils, bones and pieces of pottery, bits of information like that?" The American, whilst enjoying the novelty of the ideas being expressed by his new-found friends, had been diffident

about speaking. Turner's reply, however, now reassured him that he had not misunderstood.

'Exactly, Rick, plus all the other bits of information we receive from what surrounds us at every waking moment. All that information is, if you like, a kind of ocean of data in which we swim like fish.'

Walter had a point to make. "But surely those rudimentary "observers" can't comprehend their world in the way we do. It seems to me that that makes prehistory, as we read it, entirely subjective.'

'Not "subjective", Walter,' Turner replied. "The proper word is relative - there's a difference. The fact, for instance, that throughout prehistory there may have been no observers capable of seeing that landscape as we would see it, with all its colours, scents, sounds and so on - observers, that is, of complexity comparable to ourselves - doesn't mean it never happened, or that it never happened until now. Quite obviously it did happen. The evidence is there, all around us. But - and this is the crux of the matter, Walter - that history of things is all part and parcel of what we presently perceive. It all exists relatively to our own observations, right now.'

A look of perplexity was etched on Carter's weather-beaten face. 'So if, as a life-form we all disappeared off this earth there would be no-one around to make sense of that information. Our history - the history of the world and even of our universe, perhaps - would disappear along with us! Yet those automatic knock-on effects you call "physical" would still carry on in their own elementary way, completely uncomprehended. What the hell are we supposed to make of that?"

This point was taken up by Walter. 'I see what you're saying, Tony,' he said. 'If no observers like us had ever evolved, then in what sense could our planet exist, or the universe even, when there is none of us there to comprehend it as such? Despite what you say, Dave, you seem to imply that if we disappeared, if ever there ceased to be any complex observers like us, then the world as we know it would not only cease to exist, it could never have existed. Like a book that has never been read, all that "information" would go begging. Without our being there it could not even be information, if you get my drift.'

'I do see what you mean,' responded Turner. 'But is it any different even when there are complex observers like us to read the information? We can

never read more than the tiniest fraction of all the information that is around us, whether it be about prehistory or what surrounds us right now, like the insides of rocks, kernels of nuts, the depths of the oceans or of astronomical space, the insides of empty rooms or whatever. That infinitely vaster proportion of what is there to be known may never be known to us or to any other similar organism. But, to echo Tony's argument, who, in his right mind, would deny that those vast amounts of information exist in the systematic and connected way that they do, just because we tiny beings don't witness them?'

Walter was quick to pick up this apparent contradiction in Turner's argument.

'So, Dave, what about what you said earlier, about rejecting religion? From what you now say, it seems to me that, deep down you must believe in a Whole, or Universe, a totality of things which is comprehended or "observed" in some entirely superhuman way. And what is that belief if it is not, implicitly, a religious belief?'

'Turner smiled. 'I have to say both yes and no to that. No, because the only observer capable of perceiving or comprehending such a totality would be what we call God, and I wouldn't presume to comprehend what God comprehends; and yes, because whoever believes that there are systems of things existing beyond human ken - and any sane person has to believe that - implicitly believes in the existence of some kind of superhuman, overall "observer", otherwise nothing hangs together. Without such a co-ordinator of microphysical events into forms, everything becomes so completely dissociated that it makes no sense to even speak about it. So, whether or not one admits to a belief in God, one must believe in some kind of cosmic order - well, either in that or in complete and utter unspeakable Chaos, which no-one of sound mind could ever seriously contemplate.'

"That's an interesting statement, Dave,' observed Walter, 'especially from my professional point of view, that you think every sane person implicitly believes in the existence of some kind of superhuman Presence. I guess that makes every sane person a closet deist or theist - as you yourself must have been in those days of despair you told us about.'

"Yes, perhaps, Walter. But admitting that there must be some such Presence is not the same as presuming an intimate knowledge of that Presence and being able to second-guess its perceptions. As I say, this is, implicitly, what Classical Science has sought to do, with all its confident talk about a "universe", "universal laws" and so on, as though things beyond human perception were somehow altogether familiar to that selfordained "scientific" priesthood.'

'And that "priesthood" persists,' continued Turner, 'in modern physics and cosmology. Take, for example, all this talk about the "Big Bang". It started as a theory to explain what were thought to be galaxies receding faster and faster from us, in proportion to their distance. But although this is no more than a theory, it is now written into the textbooks as if it were absolute incontestable fact. But does anyone ever actually see the galaxies receding? What the astronomers, Hubble and Slipher, actually observed was a shift towards the red in the spectra of those far-off galaxies. But this is not the same as seeing those galaxies receding, Besides, the fact that all receding objects show a redshift doesn't necessarily mean that all objects showing a redshift - such as those distant galaxies - are receding. That is like saying that because all murderers are human beings, all human beings are murderers. It is what logicians call a "howler" of a fallacy.'

Carter nodded his agreement. 'Can't quarrel with that!' he said.
