'In any case,' Turner continued, 'it ignores all other non-recessional explanations of the redshift, which makes it a dogma, by definition. And not only that, Tony,' Turner was in full flow, 'but the whole mess of logical implications following from that galactic recession interpretation makes no sense at all. For instance, one implication is that reeling it back, as it were, to the past, some thirteen or so billion years, all those galaxies were somehow crammed together into the smallest space imaginable. That should have signalled the absurdity of that particular "recessional" interpretation of the Hubble redshift, leaving it open to explore other, more logical interpretations. It should have been seen as what, in better days, would have been called a reductio ad absurdum of the "cosmic expansion" interpretation.'

Rick was about to say something, but Turner anticipated him. 'Sorry, Rick,' he said. 'What that means is simply a reduction to absurdity. When

our science was in better logical shape, proving that some proposition or theory ended in nonsense was a way of sorting out the good from the bad assumptions, as the best way forward in developing scientific understanding. And to me, Rick, if that absurdity had been recognised at the start, we would have been spared having to contemplate what is surely the silliest idea of cosmic creation that was ever conceived, that of the "Big Bang" origin of the universe.'

'But, Dave,' said Carter, 'if that idea is so absurd, then why is the Catholic Church, as I am told, funding research in it?'

Walter interposed. 'I guess that's obvious. It's because it is seen as scientific proof of God's Creation.'

Turner nodded. "Yet that is not surprising. As far as I'm concerned, it shows just how silly the Church has been in the past - like, for instance, having the gall, three hundred years after threatening him with torture, to "forgive" Galileo for being right about the earth orbiting the sun, and not the other way around - for being right, in effect, when they were proved. wrong.'

'Is that so?" Don asked. "That's incredible, when it should, obviously, have been the followers of Galileo - which includes all of us, nowadays, I guess - who should be forgiving the Church for its persecution of Galileo!'

'Quite so, Don,' continued Turner, 'In any case, I find it very strange that this "expanding universe" idea should have caught on so, especially when the whole idea, literally analysed, is pure nonsense.'

"Tell us why, Dave,' invited Walter.

'Well, take the word "universe",' Turner went on. "The literal meaning of this, surely, is everything there is. This means a complete cosmical whole, omitting nothing, with everything included within it, all space, all time, all matter, erc., and nothing left out; that is, with nothing outside it, not even empty space. Do you agree?'

They all nodded, and Carter voiced their agreement 'Of course!' he said, 'How else should it be?'

'And that,' resumed Turner, 'is also how the Big Bang cosmologists understand the term. So, think! If the wiverse is expanding, then what can it be expanding into? Literally speaking, to say that it is expanding into

nothing is the same as saying that it is not expanding. So on that score, the suggestion of an "expanding universe' is flatly self-contradictory, hence just pure nonsense!"

It was Don who responded this time. 'It's absolute baloney when you think of it like that! he exclaimed.

'I agree, Don,' Turner replied, 'and by the same token, if there is nothing outside this allegedly "expanding universe", then obviously there is nothing with reference to which that rate of "expansion" can be gauged.'

He could see that he had the undivided attention of his listeners, so he continued. 'And there's another thing. Take the so-called "primeval atom". With the whole universe inside it and nothing outside by which its size can be compared, how can it possibly be described as tiny or large? Examined in this way, the whole "expanding universe", together with its "Big Bang" implication is complete and utter nonsense - a real lulu of a reductio ad absurdum of the recessional interpretation of the Hubble red shift!'

'So you would claim, then,' said Walter, 'that no matter how we might improve our astronomical telescopes, we will never see back to what I read somewhere the NASA people anticipate with their planned launch of the Hubble telescope. From what you say, Dave, no matter how far we manage to see, there will be just more and more of the same - galaxies upon galaxies going on for ever.' '

"Yes, Walter,' replied Turner, 'a normally distributed Steady-State Universe, in effect. It is definitely what I would predict.'

The clergyman sat silent for a few seconds, staring at the bubbles rising in his glass. 'I guess I misunderstood you, then, Dave,' he said, 'when you said you wanted to dump that Western idea of a "universe". I now see that you don't reject that idea entirely. You just want to make sure that it remains what it has to be, an open-ended concept.'

'Youre absolutely right, Walter. I felt that classical scientists had been treating the Infinite. with far too much familiarity, just as their predecessors, the Medieval priests, had done with their Canon laws, and so on. For me, it was just more of the same sort of priestly presumption. So in order to avoid dogma I felt - and still feel - that in the pursuit of natural

philosophy it's necessary to maintain a healthy scepticism regarding all scientific theories on all counts.'

'So,' put in Walter, 'you wouldn't go along with the classical idea that there are "universal laws", of physics, chemistry or mechanics, basically governing everything?"

'No, Walter, that's not quite true. I do believe that there are laws of universal application. But to say that certain laws have "universal application" is, for me, not quite the same as saying that they are the "laws of the universe".'

'What's the difference?' Carter asked.

'The difference, Tony, is that the one refers to the universe of discourse and the other to the "God's-eye-view universe" of classical physics.'

'Ah, I see,' said Walter, 'or at least I think I do. You're saying that scientists must stop "playing God" and refrain from presenting their own "graven images" of the universe as though they had divine approval for presenting them in that way; that they should always be mindful that those ideas they have of a "universe" are never more than man-sized, or finite.'

'Right again, Walter. I was simply saying - with due deference to your calling - that as I see it, to adopt such a "God's eye-view" of things is not only presumptuous but pointless, since we are as ignorant with such an assumption as without it. All it does is to mask our ignorance by allowing us to think that although we ourselves can't comprehend the Whole, we nevertheless know something superhuman that does. It kids us into thinking that we have some kind of knowledge which in fact we don't, which is the basis of all dogmatism. And that, as I came to see it, is the dogmatism of classical science.'

'Strangely enough, Dave,' said Walter, thoughtfully, 'that conclusion of yours is not so unfriendly to true religion as it might seem.'

Turner stood and took the empty glasses to the bar. There was no-one there, so Don got up and refilled them. When they were seated again, Carter wanted to digest the answer Turner had given to his earlier question.

'Alright then, Dave,' he said, 'so everything is relative to perception. Everything that exists is observed, if not by us then by some superhuman

intelligence whose nature and perceptions we mustn't speculate too dogmatically about. That, you seem to be saying, is a minimal concession which any person in his right mind must make to a belief in God - or, at any rate, in some comprehending principle, superior to himself.'

'And to human society,' broke in Walter.

'Some kind of cosmical being, in fact,' added Don. 'I've always considered myself an atheist, but this makes me wonder, now, if that's a rational position.'

'Hooray to that!' said the clergyman.

This pregnant remark was followed by a long silence, during which they all seemed lost in contemplation. Then Walter again voiced his thoughts.

'I'm beginning to appreciate, Dave,' he said, 'how different your "flipside" view is from that of the science orthodoxy. From what you say, I guess you feel that we've returned to the way things were in the Middle Ages, when the power of the Church had pretty much stopped scientific progress in its tracks, except that this time it's Science itself being the blockage that needs to be dealt with.'

'And in those days,' added Carter, 'bucking the authority of the Church was a heresy, punishable by death.'

'So I guess you're saying,' said a rather pensive Don, 'that the modern version of heresy is to buck the authority of modern Science.'

'Modern Physics and Cosmology, yes,' replied Turner.

'So, basically, Dave,' Carter said, 'you're a modern heretic.' Jokingly he made as if to back away from Turner. 'Nice knowing you, old friend,' he declared.

'Youre in good company, then, Dave,' Walter remarked, 'because as I understand it, just about every significant advance in the history of science was a heresy in its day.'

'Bully for heretics, then! Don exclaimed.

'The only difference nowadays, though,' added Turner, wryly, 'is that heretics don't get tortured, they get smothered!'

A thoughtful Walter said, "What we've been talking about this evening

raises, for me, all sorts of interesting questions, some of which I'll need

time to think through.' Draining his glass, he got to his feet. 'Anyway,' he said, 'that was a great discussion, guys, and thanks for including me in it. I surely would like to continue it if we get the chance. Till then, I'll say goodnight.'

Rick Jackson, too, stood up, a little uncertain of himself.

'Yeah, it sure was interesting, as far as I could follow.' He drained his glass. 'It's not my usual kind of chat, I guess. You guys know so much! But it's time I hit the sack. G'night, see you in the morning!'

'Nine o'clock sharp, then, Rick,' Carter reminded him, 'down at the corral.'

Chapter 3: The Cabin

Physical Interaction versus Language and Communication

The following morning held promise of another warm day. It was real Indian-summer weather, with the air clear, scented and still. The gold leaves and silver trunks of the end-of-season birches were brightly contrasted against the deepening green of the conifers.

After breakfast, the riders gathered outside the lodge to await Charlie, the wrangler, who was down in the corral supervising the grooming and saddling of their mounts. While they were waiting, Rick Jackson surveyed those who were to be his companions for the next twenty-four hours. He had been rather taken aback to learn over breakfast that this morning's ride was through the bush to a distant cabin where the group were to spend the night. Once again, Carter had quashed his misgivings and persuaded him to come along.

Despite his "hail-fellow-well-met' manner, Rick was basically a shy man, and although he had enjoyed the company of the Englishmen the previous evening he had been very aware of his ignorance of the sorts of things they were discussing. Now in his mid-thirties, with a failed marriage behind him and little time in his working life to make friends, the fact that Carter and Turner seemed to like his company was very flattering. Nevertheless, the prospect of a full day and night with them filled him with some apprehension.

As soon as they find out how dumb I am, he thought to himself, they'll give me the bum's rush. Hope to hell there's someone in this bunch I can talk to on my level.

There were eight altogether in the group, six men and two women. Much to his surprise, Rick noticed that Walter Collins was among them, looking most un-clerical in his seasoned check shirt, riding boots, jeans and cowboy hat.

'Hi there, Rev!' Rick called out to him. 'Glad you're coming along. A few prayers might help to keep my butt on the horse.'

'Don't fret, Rick! Walter laughed. 'Charlie's a great wrangler. I know him well. He'll make sure you get a horse that suits you.'

Soon, Charlie Graham joined them. He was a cheerful-looking man, aged somewhere between thirty and forty, who seemed to have stepped out of a Western movie. Like many of those who lived in this particular part of the wilderness, he was a drop-out from the rat race. His doctorate in psychology was therefore a well-kept secret and guests at the lodge assumed he had spent all his life riding horses and communing with nature - an illusion his employers were happy to foster. His drawl, accordingly, was what might have been expected.

'Howdy, folks,' he said, 'I guess you know my name - Charlie, in case you don't. Okay, so let's get to know each other before we ride out. I already know Tony and Dave, those two funny English guys over there.' He grinned, confident of causing no offence. And then, to the clergyman, 'Hi, Walt! . . . An old riding buddy of mine,' he explained. What he did not explain was that he and the pastor had been friends since college days. 'Now how about the rest of you? Come on, ladies first.'

One of the women spoke up. 'I'm Helen Taylor and this is my sister, Marian.' Helen was a good-looking woman in her early thirties, one would guess. Her sister, a softer, prettier version, looked several years younger.

That left Rick and the other two men. One was tall with a haughty and confident bearing which suggested to Rick that he and this person might not get on, though he guessed they were about the same age. The man's riding outfit was immaculate and his accent was one which Rick recognised as coming from the privileged echelons of Boston society.

'lm Noel Findlay,' said the Bostonian. His arrogant manner also irritated Charlie. But remembering that these people were employing him, he smiled in response and turned to the remaining two members of the group. One was a man who had no particular qualities to mark, being of average height and appearance but who seemed an 'okay guy'. The wrangler was reassured of this when the man said, in a quiet, laid-back Canadian voice, "Hi! Pm Andy MacInnes. My friends call me Mac.'

Then it was Rick's turn to introduce himself, but he was forestalled by Tony Carter. "This big, worried-looking chap here, folks, is Rick Jackson, a new American friend of ours. Hasn't been on a horse before. That's why he's looking so apprehensive. But you'll see he doesn't fall off, won't you, Charlie?'

Rick laughed somewhat ruefully and then Marian Taylor said, 'I'm glad someone else feels like that. I've always been a mite nervous of horses. Helen's the horsewoman. I'm only here to keep her company.'

The wrangler smiled. "There's just nothing for any of you to worry about. We've got horses to suit every rider, good, bad, or indifferent. Come on, the horses are ready, down at the corral, so let's get there and mounted up. Then we can get on our way.'

The ride through the bush was long and interesting, the trees giving way here and there to alpine meadows, dense with flowers, and breathtaking views of the river, mountains and gorges. Engaged as they were, each with his or her own thoughts - and, in some cases, concerns to remain seated - talk was at a minimum. Occasionally, they stopped to examine the tracks of animals and discuss their habits. When Rick had read about people escaping from justice into the forest he had wondered how they managed to get caught in a wilderness like this, with all that huge area in which to lose themselves. But having now witnessed the impenetrability of the undergrowth, in places, with its tangles of fallen trees, he could see why it was necessary to stick to the trails. Trapping a fugitive in these parts, he mused, would have to be like trapping a bear or a moose, a job only for those knowing the terrain, the avenues through it and the lore of the wild.

Several hours later, the party arrived at an old log cabin in a forest clearing. Some were distinctly saddle-sore, but all were glad to dismount and walk the last few yards to the small corral. After unsaddling their horses, which were obviously happy to be rid of their human burdens, and leaving them to munch their oats, each rider then stowed his gear under a large

tarpaulin. There was a call from Walter, who was kneeling, half way between the cabin and a small outhouse. 'Hey, Charlie, come and see this! . . . Bear

prints,' he said, when Charlie reached him.

'Fresh, too,' agreed the wrangler. 'Must have scared him off when we were approaching. He must have been trying get into the cabin.'

By this time the others had joined them. Charlie stood up. 'Don't want to worry you, folks, but there's a bear not too far away. He's not likely to come any closer - we've been making too much noise - but just be careful.'

The others studied the prints. Marian Taylor was obviously worried.

"What do we do if he does get closer?'

Charlie's voice was reassuring. 'He won't. Bears, like most wild animals, are shy of people and as long as you don't mess with them, they won't mess with you.'

'Unless, of course, it's a female with cubs,' put in Noel Findlay. 'On a trail, north of here, I came across some trail-builders who had retreated from a large bear which absolutely refused to move. They saw I had my gun, so I went back down the trail with them to shoot it; but by the time we reached the spot the bear was gone.'

"You're obviously a crack shot, then,' said Charlie, with just a touch of disdain in his voice, 'because if you don't kill a bear with your first shot, you're in deep trouble. There's nothing meaner than a wounded bear.'

'That's right,' the quiet Canadian, MacInnes, affirmed. 'It'll surely turn on you then; and what's more, a bear can run at nearly thirty miles an hour! Pve clocked them with my truck. You'd never outrun one, even if you were Ben Johnson* - except, perhaps, going downhill. A bear doesn't like sprinting downhill because its claws don't grip.'

'Say, fellas!' Rick Jackson interposed. 'You're sure doing a great job of stopping us worrying"

Thus admonished, Charlie, Mac and Noel looked rather sheepish. 'Trying to amend matters, Noel put in hastily,

'The case I was talking about was unusual. The bear was obviously protecting her cub which was probably up a tree, nearby.'

'And you were going to shoot it?' The disgust in Helen Taylor's voice was unmistakable.

"Well," said Charlie, with seasoned diplomacy, 'we're not going to shoot bears, this trip. As I said at the start, so long as you're all aware of the habits

2. Canadian Olympic sprinter.

of these animals, and that we're deep in the bush here, where the animals have room to avoid us, we should be okay.' He didn't want any of his charges getting excited or collapsing in hysterics. His job was to provide a wilderness experience which was genuine enough without being too genuine. He didn't wish too much discomfort on anyone, least of all himself. 'Come on, folks,' he said, 'let's get the gear sorted and get supper going.'

On the deck of the cabin were their sleeping bags and rucksacks. There was also a large cool-box, a crate of beer and several bottles of wine. How these things had mysteriously appeared there, ahead of them, was something the wrangler hoped no-one would ask. Perhaps they thought the bear had delivered it, he laughed to himself.

Remote though it seemed, the cabin was, in fact, no more than five miles from the lodge along a more direct route. As soon as they had left on the trail that morning, Don had Joaded up his truck with their gear and driven along the dirt track with their supplies. Yes, it was a true wilderness experience, but the lodge made good business in seeing to it that it was never too wild.

Some time later, full of barbecued steaks, chicken, wieners? and baked potatoes washed. down with the beer and wine, the riders sat around the campfire for a drink before settling down in the cabin for the night. The evening air was still and warm and a feeling of bonhomie pervaded the group. They felt they had known each other for years.

"Well, Rick, you survived,' smiled Turner.

Rick was standing with his back to the fire, ecstatically soaking up its heat. 'Yeah, it was great! I'm real glad I came along.' His eye caught Marian's and they smiled at each other. He picked up a log and placed it near where Walter and Turner were sitting chatting.

'['m glad you enjoyed it, Rick,' said Walter, as Rick sat down. 'T did too. I'm used to visiting my outlying parishioners on horseback but seldom in company with others.' He paused, then continued, 'I was just telling Dave that I've been thinking over the discussion we had last night.' He looked

3. Sausages.

around apologetically, as he became aware that the others were listening. 'I met with Dave, Tony and Rick, at the Lodge with Don last evening,' he explained, 'and we got talking about some ideas of Dave's which I found quite fascinating. I hoped we'd have the chance to go into the matter further but I guess this is hardly the time and place.'

"Why not?' asked Helen. "We've got nothing else to do. Let's hear it.'

"You sure about that?' Walter queried doubtfully. "What about the rest of you guys?' They murmured encouragingly, so he went on. "Well, I was mulling over what Dave was telling us about how different Modern Science's view of the world is from what we ordinary folks are used to - now that it includes Relativity, Quantum Theory and all that. Reading about these subjects usually sends me into a coma but when discussed objectively and carefully - with Dave's very interesting take on it, I must add - I can see now that they are as much a part of nature as those rocks and forests around us. I was thinking about this on our ride from the Lodge.'

"That sounds intriguing, Walter,' Helen remarked. 'Maybe Dave would like to explain it to us too. In what way should modern science make us think differently, Dave, and why?'

Turner felt embarrassed. "Your interest is gratifying, Helen, but I'm sure you would be bored with that sort of thing. Most people are.'

Noel Findlay took this as a challenge. "How do you know we should be, if you don't tell us?' he asked.

'Fair enough, Noel,' said Turner. But Walter sensed the older man's reluctance to expound his radical ideas to such a disparate group, and having been the one to raise the subject, he now felt obliged to help Turner out.

"Well, to put you in the picture,' Walter began, 'it started with us all saying how insignificant and small this wilderness makes us feel, but Dave put a different slant on it by reminding us what modern science tells us, that everything out there is what it is relative to ourselves, as observers, which in a sense makes us as big and significant as the whole of what we observe. On our ride, it occurred to me that this puts what we normally think of as physics into its proper perspective. It made me realise that science is a finite /uman construct, with all the failings and frailties that the

human mind is heir to, instead of what many scientists sometimes presume it to be, a kind of direct insight into the "Mind of God"?

'Just as the Church did before it, then,' Helen ventured.

'T told Dave that such an inversion of thought was absurd,' put in Carter, 'because I thought he was saying that this took all the reality we see around us and put it inside our heads. But then he showed us that an "observer", in the way modern relativity means, doesn't have to be a conscious human being but could be anything that responds to or communicates with anything else.'

'All in little quantum jumps, or packets of energy,' interposed Rick.

'And that left me thinking,' added Walter, 'that there is some similarity between your views, Dave, and Karl Marx's, in the sense that you say there's no such thing as "dead matter" nor "disembodied mind" but that mind and matter are somehow intimately intertwined.'

"Yes, I suppose there is a similarity when you come to think of it,' Turner agreed. 'My argument that there is no absolute distinction between things "mental" and things "material" does sound like Marx's "dialectical materialism".'

'That's what I felt,' Walter went on, "because matter, for Marx, was not entirely mechanical. It also had those forward-looking and planning capabilities we normally associate with mind.'

Rick broke in. 'I'm a mite surprised at you, Walter, a man of the cloth, talking about Karl Marx. Marx was an atheist, wasn't he? I''d have thought you guys would be the last to push Commie ideas, especially now it's been shown they don't work.'

Walter was obviously amused. 'You're right, Rick,' he said. 'I doubt if Marx himself would admit to being a Marxist if he could see what his political ideas have come to. It reminds me what a politics professor once told me at theological college, that "Marxism is a slogan, not a theory." But I'm not commenting on the political aspect of Marx which, as you point out, Rick, has now had its day. I'm concerned, here, only with what he thought about matter. And yes, he has been dubbed an atheist, but his views were not unlike those of Christianity - like his belief, for instance, thar life is a continual conflict between opposing principles. For us Christians, as you know, those opposed principles are Good and Evil.'

Carter was spurred by this. Like many of his contemporaries, he had flirted with Communism in his student days.

'But,' he pointed out, "Marx didn't see the conflict.as being polarised in that way. He saw it, rather, as being between all sorts of logically opposing motives and principles. This was his "dialectical process", which is what he called the opposition of thesis and antithesis.'

"Those weren't Marx's terms,' corrected Noel. "He borrowed them from Hegel and then claimed that he'd turned Hegel's theory upside down.'

Rick was beginning to feel that his fears about his companions were being realised. He could see that, unlike the previous evening, he was now way out of his depth in what was threatening to become a display of erudition. But what the heli! he told himself, the sooner they find out I'm not educated, the better. Thus decided, he spoke out.

'Hey, hold it there, guys. Who's Hegel?'

Walter suddenly understood how the big American felt. "Sorry, Rick,' he said, 'let me explain. Hegel,' he continued, 'was a German philosopher living at the beginning of the 19th century. He believed that the history of the development of man was a series of conflicts - not what we call war but a dialectic, or an argument, between all sorts of contradictory thoughts or ideas. So, what shapes human history, according to Hegel, is mind or spirit arguing with itself, as it were, striving to resolve all those different factions, or "theses", into some sort of synthesis, as he called it.'

"That's right,' Carter put in. 'Marx accepted this idea of Hegel's, the dialectic, but applied it to material processes because matter, he felt, was more real and substantial than "mind".'

'Marx,' Walter added, for Rick's benefit, 'claimed he had found Hegel "standing on his head", and that he inverted him, philosophically speaking, to set him "on his feet".'

'So for Marx,' ventured Rick, 'matter wasn't just the sort of stuff that machines are made of. It's what everything is made of, including thinking beings like us.'

"That's a good way of putting it, Rick,' said Turner. 'So you see why Walter saw a similarity between Marx's "dialectical materialism" and my argument last night.'

'Yeah, I figured that,' replied Rick, 'about there being no real difference between mental and physical.'

'But,' cautioned Turner, addressing the group in general, "Don't get me wrong. I wasn't pushing either Hegel or Marxism, here. That's strictly politics, not science. Anyway, there's one big difference between Marx's ideas and those we discussed last night,'.

"What's that?" Carter asked.

"Well, as you know, Tony, for Marx, matter is the self-willed and forwardlooking driving-force behind everything, and its rules are not so much those of physics and mechanics as those of economics and statistics.'

'But,' countered Carter, 'those rules, for Marx, were still of a kind-of mechanical nature, in which it is those statistical-stroke-economic ends which determine the means, leaving virtually no scope for individual morality and free will. The end justifies the means, as it is said...'

'And that,' concluded Walter, 'is why, in its political implementation, Marxism became so "materialistic". That's certainly different, Dave, from what you are proposing, with its emphasis on individual consciousness - or the human spirit, or "soul" you might say - as a shaper of history. That would be anathema to the old Marxist doctrine.'

"Wouldn't it just!' chuckled Carter, 'especially since it smacks so much of individualism!'

However, something was worrying Rick. "But there's still gotta be a difference between mind and matter in your way of thinking. They can't just be blended together like Marx did. Right, Dave?'

"Why's that, Rick?'

"Well, how else can you explain what goes on at those atom-smashing laboratories I read about, like Brookhaven and that one in Europe somewhere . . . What was it called?'

'CERN, in Geneva?' offered Noel.

"Yeah, that's it. In places like that they ain't looking for bits of "mind", are they, or bits of matter that think. They're looking for bits of pure brainless clockwork. I was down in Brookhaven last Fall, on a sales assignment, and some guys were telling me all about it. They're spending billions of dollars looking for the "elementary particle". But that don't fit in with

what you said last night, Dave. If history is made by complicated lumps of matter like us, which can think and plan ahead, then what are these guys hoping to find by smashing matter to bits?'

'Your guess is as good as mine,' shrugged Turner. 'As it's said, "there's more than one way of skinning a cat". So, since no-one's clairvoyant about the absolutely best way to understand nature, that "atom-smashing" approach is as good or bad, I suppose, as any - as long as those who take that approach will admit that it isn't necessarily the only or the best one there is!'

'But surely,' argued Noel, 'the aim of those researchers at places like Brookhaven and CERN is to find not only the ultimate material particles - nature's cogs and spindles, as you might say - but also the basic forces of nature which put them together and make them work."4

'Yes,' agreed Turner, 'I don't deny that that is their declared intention, to describe all the different bits and pieces of matter, the forces of interaction between them and so on. And by discovering these things they believe they will eventually discover the "key to the universe", the ultimate explanation of everything, including the way animals think, plan and so on - even the secret of life itself! Who can declare, with complete confidence, that theirs is not the approach that's eventually going to succeed?'

'But, gee, that's wild! breathed Rick. "Do these guys really think that by looking for the smallest particles there are and the way those bits of material push and pull one another they're gonna find the gizmo that makes it all tick?"

'It seems they do,' replied Turner.

'So how do you feel about that, Dave?' Rick persisted.

"Well, they may be right, of course, but from my own point of view it's like smashing up chessmen to discover the rules of chess!'

'And for that,' mused Rick, 'they get those billions of dollars!'

"That fits in with something I read somewhere,' Charlie interposed. Having seen to the horses, he had just rejoined the group and was pleased

It gets rid of that dreary "scientistic" preoccupation with reducing everything to bits of dead "clockwork", as you put it, Rick, and gives everything back to observation as we know it. In this way of thinking, nature is no more nor less than what it appears to be, with all its colours, sounds, scents and so on.'

By now the stars were out and the air was practically still, with only the cool drifting scent of the forest wafting over them, causing the logs to flame and crackle.

Turner broke the silence. "Yes, Marian,' he said. 'Walter's got it right. Look at those stars up there! With our telescopes and other instruments we know how far off they are. We learn that they are all part of our immense Milky Way galaxy - that great misty band up there - and that there are galaxies upon galaxies stretching away as far as our telescopes can reach. What I was saying is that this is the primary reality, that is, the actual

appearances of things. We should continually remind ourselves that this is where all science starts, with physical phenomena, that is, with things as we actually perceive them, not with theoretical entities underlying them. This is nature, not the stuff that exists primarily only in theoreticians' minds. This is the view we were discussing at the Lodge, and it was this realisation, Helen, which emancipated me from the way of thinking about science which you, Marian, described as "deadly dull and dreary". My studies of that sort of scientific so-called "Realism" depressed me unutterably. It made me feel less than a microbe in the scheme of things. So, what Walter calls my "new slant" - my "conversion", if you like, Helen - was in switching from that dreary, supposedly 'realistic' view of atomistic science into what I later learned is called phenomenalism. In this new context I realised that it makes more sense to think of everything, from atoms to humans, as simple or complex "odservers" than as mindless, mechanical bits of matter.'

Helen broke in. "Like Hegel and Marx, as we were discussing just now.'

'This gave us a lot to think about, Helen,' added Carter. "But now, listening to all this talk about misuse of language makes me wonder, Dave, about that word "observer". Don't you think it could be just as confusing as the way Marx misemployed the word "matter" to describe something forward-looking or mind-like?'

'T thoroughly agree, Tony,' answered Turner, 'Tt's just that the word "observer", I suppose, was the only one that came to hand in Einstein's vocabulary to describe the focus of relativistic physical dimensions. But however it came about, it's the word that's gained currency in the context of relativity theory and in that context we seem to be stuck with it.'

'I wonder, then,' said Helen, 'how much of all this confusion in understanding may be due simply to problems of language. For instance, in my job - by the way, I teach English to foreign students - I often find that ideas expressed perfectly sensibly in English seem like nonsense in another language, and vice-versa.'

This reminded Marian of something. 'Do you remember, Helen, that time when { had a letter from my French pen-friend?'

'Oh, yes,' replied her sister, 'when you were in grade school and you'd started to learn French.'

'That's right. Well, on the back of the envelope he had written "Fast, fast, small manufacturer". This surely puzzled me, as you can guess, until I realised that what he'd intended to write was "Fast, fast, dear postman".'

'Turner joined in the laughter. 'Ah, yes,' he said, '"Vite, vite, petit facteur". I bet it puzzled your postman too! Anyway, I agree with what you say, Helen, about problems of language. But this problem Tony is thinking about, Marian, isn't just a case of plain mistranslation of words. The problem goes much deeper. It's rather that none of those languages which take their cue from Western science seems to have any words at all, nor any thought-patterns even, which are capable of expressing this neutral idea of what I've called - however ineptly, Tony - "observer".'

'Because, of course,' added Helen, 'the language and the thought-patterns go together, so where problems of language arise there are problems of . thought, also.'

Walter asked, "Didn't Leibniz try to solve a similar problem by using the Greek word "monad"? As I read it, he applied this term to a whole series of entities, from the most rudimentary to the most complex - from atomic particles, you might say, to human observers and even beyond. So it could be that "monad" is the word you're looking for, Dave. What do you think?'

Rick Jackson broke in. "Hey come on, you guys, you're losing me again. Could someone tell me in a nutshell something about this Leib-whatever his name is? Another Heini, right?

'Sure to be,' drawled Charlie. "This wilderness is full of them - second only to the Japs?

It was Noel who obliged. 'Leibniz, as you rightly guess, Rick, was German - a philosopher, earlier than Hegel. He did most of his work in the late seventeenth century, devoting himself to finding an answer to the philosophy of Descartes...'

Rick groaned. "Gee, another one? Who's he?'

To his surprise, Helen answered him. 'He was a seventeenth century French philosopher, Rick. He's the thinker responsible for dividing the world into "mind" and "matter" in the way we've been discussing. The

» >

division Descartes introduced is known as "Dualism".

"Thanks, Helen,' Rick smiled. 'I get it. It was Descartes who split the world into mind and matter and Leibniz, like Dave, was looking for a way to stitch it back together again. Right?'

'Just so, Rick,' smiled Helen.

"To get back to your question, Walter,' said Turner, tossing some more wood on the fire, 'I think you're right about Leibniz. I seem to recall that Lenin regarded his monadology as very similar to Marx's dialectical materialism. The trouble with Leibniz's "monads", though,' he went on, 'is that unlike atoms or material things in general, they don't interact or communicate in any way. In Leibniz's theory, all appearance of interaction is due to a "pre-established harmony" between the monads - as if various bits of a conversation were pre-recorded on different tape-recorders and all set to run in a synchronised way to sound like real conversation.'

Walter considered this carefully. "Yes, Dave,' he said, 'I see, I guess, that that's not what you want. It's essential that your "observers" do really interact and-or communicate.'

Marian said sympathetically, "Isn't there some combination of words, then, or phrase, to describe what you want, Dave?'

'Perhaps there is, Marian,' Turner replied, 'but if so, then I can't find it. That's why I'm stuck with "observer", because the pitch is queered by the language instituted by Descartes. Our normal modes of speech, in most European languages, at least - and that includes American and Canadian, of course,' he put in hastily - 'divide the world into the two distinct categories "mind and matter", leaving nothing to signify anything neutral between the two.'

'And all because it was a quirk j in the ideas of a seventeenth century French philosopher! commented Carter, as he held up a newly-uncorked bottle of wine. Helen held out her beaker for a refill as she corrected him.

'Well, not quite, Tony, because that language-distinction, in the European languages, predated Descartes, so he was as much a victim of that dualism as we are. But in any case, what we want is an entirely neutral concept which does away with that distinction.'

'I must admit that until now,' remarked Marian, 'I hadn't really considered the extent to which the way we see the world depends on the language we use. How very relative it all is!'

'Yes,' replied Walter. 'It's our language which makes either sense or nonsense of what we observe. This was the point made by Wittgenstein.'

"This one's just gotta be German,' said Rick Jackson.

This made them laugh again, and Walter continued. 'Sure, Rick. This one, though, was a modern German, a pioneer of modern linguistic philosophy. His views are extremely difficult to explain in detail. It was the hardest part of my philosophy course - but we needn't concern ourselves with that. All I want to say, here, is that in Wittgenstein's view, practically all the problems we ever encounter are problems of correct or incorrect uses of language.'

"How can that be?' Carter enquired. 'If I fail to push a heavy wheelbarrow uphill, is that a "problem of language"?

'Perhaps it's a problem of finding the right swearword,' suggested Rick.

'Perhaps indeed, Rick,' laughed Walter.

Carter's question was answered by Turner. "But, seriously, Tony, in Wittgenstein's sense, that zs a problem of language - of communication. Your identification of the problem and its solution both involve communication; that is, verbally or by your behaviour (which is also a kind of communication). This is in deciding what's wrong in a way which can be communicated to yourself and to others - and, of course, in communicating this decision to the wheelbarrow. Wittgenstein argued, as did other linguistic philosophers (English this time, Rick), like Gilbert Ryle and J. L. Austin, that it is all a function of the language of the culture to which you belong. Every part of the process of identifying and solving problems, or of doing or thinking anything which makes sense, involves language - "proper usage" the Wittgensteinians would say. And by the same token, any inability to identify a problem correctly or define a solution is basically a problem of language.'

'But as Walter says,' Helen put in, "Wittgenstein's work is hard to understand. When I studied him, in my course in Linguistic Philosophy, I found it difficult to accept, as I think anyone does, that so much depends on our use and misuse of language.'

'T certainly found it difficult,' Turner agreed, 'but that was mainly because it seemed to me that these "language-philosophers" had a misplaced,

entirely naive faith in the sufficiency of "proper usage" as an answer to all problems - which is why it was sometimes called Naive Realism. They never seemed to consider the sorts of problems arising from an insufficiency of human language as a whole, no matter how "properly used", in expressing developments on an expanding scientific front.'

'That,' said Helen, 'was undoubtedly what created the educational rift between Arts and Science.'

'Turner agreed. "Yes, and the misunderstandings across that gulf become larger by the hour. I've known scientists ridicule linguistic philosophy as claiming - as they see it - that the whole world, the stars, space, the instruments we use to study them, and so on, are all "nothing but language" - as if anyone in his right mind would claim that!'

"Yet, when you think of it,' commented Walter, 'there is a lot in what these linguistic philosophers say. 1 mean, how can you decide what an entity is or does, or what its disposition may be, without language?'

'I entirely agree, Walter,' Turner said. "Unfortunately, those linguistic philosophers took the word "language" as meaning /uman language - specifically, the Oxbridge-type language, in other words, the language of the UK universities of Oxford and Cambridge and the typical Arts scholar, ruling-out the concerns of science altogether. This was the other side of the educational coin. Some of these "linguistic" philosophers would say that extending the meaning of "language" to communication between nonhuman organisms, instrument-systems and so on, is a prime example of "misuse of language", and in their language conservatism they would have fought tooth and nail to oppose it.'

"But surely the word "language",' argued Charlie, 'is also used, and in a perfectly commonsense way, to describe communication not only between human beings but also between inanimate objects, like computers for example. How would those linguistic philosophers cope with that development?" ,

'No doubt the hard-liners among them wouldn't,' said Turner. "They would undoubtedly have scorned, along with those computers, that extended use of "language" as a symptom of their dreaded "Scientism" - a dirty word among these "Ordinary-Language" purists. The scientific use

has become established, however, thanks to a communications-engineer called Shannon - one of your own countrymen, Rick. Claude Shannon worked for Bell Telephones. He was the originator of what we now call information-theory, which deals with information-processes, or "language", in a much more generalised way than was ever contemplated by the linguistic philosophers."

'And,' said Carter, for the benefit of the others, 'as a telecommunications engineer, Dave, you should know all about communication in that technical sense.'

"Yes, but even for communications-engineers, Tony, this work of Shannon's is still relatively new on the scene. We mustn't assume that everyone who uses Shannon's discoveries, even in his work, is aware of what they imply for developing science and philosophy. So this brings me to my point,' Turner continued, 'which is that in this extended use of "language", which is now quite common, even outside science and technology, the word applies to any information-transaction or signal whatsoever, not only between human observers but between the whole hierarchy of informational communicators we talked about, complex or rudimentary, animal, vegetable or mineral.'

'I see!' said Walter. 'Now we're getting somewhere. This fits perfectly, Dave, with what you were saying last evening. If there is no categorical division between mind and matter, then there is no categorical division between observing and physically interacting. And that means there is no categorical division between energy-processes and communication, nor between human communication (or "language") and communication generally. It's all very logical and consistent. I like it! It makes sense!'

'Perhaps, then,' Marian suggested, 'the word "communicant" is the one you want, Dave, as a replacement for your "observer".'

5. 'The term information, in its everyday meaning, describes an abstract quantity which is almost impossible to measure.' (information and Communication Theory, A. M. Rosie, Van Nostrand, London, 1973). Since then it has become recognised as I'a fundamental concept in information theory . . . that the amount of information in a message, called information content, is a well-defined, measurable

'I think you've got something there, Marian,' said the clergyman. 'For me the word "communicant" has a nice ring to it.' The others shared his amusement.
