Before Walter could reply, Rick broke in.

"Okay," he said, 'so you say that the world is all language? That sounds weird!'

'It does, Rick,' Turner answered. "But that only shows, as I've said, how set in its ways our Western thinking has become. There simply aren't any natural-sounding ways of expressing that neutral state of affairs. To express what needs to be expressed, you'd have to revise practically the whole of conventional language, with all its knock-on linguistic consequences. All our present modes of speech which rest on the categorical distinction between "mental" and "material", between "interacting" and "communicating", between "physical" and "psychological", "mundane" and "spiritual"

. . and so on, would have to be modified, right across the board, and during the transition that would leave us practically tongue-tied.'

'Meanwhile,' offered Noel, 'the predicament we're in, you are saying, Dave, is like that in the joke about the Irish deciding to swap from driving on the left to driving on the right, with the cautionary decision to make the changeover gradually!

"That's so right!' answered Turner, after the laughter had subsided. Both Helen and Charlie reflected that Noel might not, after all, be such a 'stuffed shirt' as he had first seemed.

Carter was thoughtful. 'Seriously, though, it certainly shows,' he said, 'how difficult it is for our common language to accommodate advancing scientific knowledge, without either having to put the brakes on it or else getting left behind -- as the Church was after the changes that took place after Copernicus and Galileo?

By this time, the sun had long set and they were becoming aware of the chilly night air at their backs. Marian Taylor shivered.

'Gee, I'm cold. Come on, Helen, J know you don't want to break up this discussion, but it's late and if I stay here I'm going to freeze to death!

But her sister was enjoying the cut and thrust of the argument. A woman of considerable intellect, Helen Taylor had been delighted to find such congenial company in the wilderness, and was enjoying it all the more because it was completely unexpected. So now she was reluctant to leave. But Rick felt sympathetic towards Marian.

"Marian's right.' he said, 'It sure is damn cold, now I notice it. And we gotta be up early tomorrow. Ain't that right, Charlie?'

'Sure. We must be on the trail by seven o'clock sharp. After I get you guys back to the lodge, I'm leaving you to take another group out to White Horse Bluff - Gee, I sure hope they're not as brainy as you all,' he grinned, reverting to his Westerner's drawl. 'So if you ladies want to get yourselves tucked into your bunks, you'd better go in before the rest of us.'

'Okay, then,' Helen said. "You go in first, Marian, I'll be right along.'

When Marian had retired indoors, the discussion began to wind down.

'It certainly shows,' said Walter, 'how trapped we are within the language of our own culture. We rarely consider that the way we usually think and talk is not the be-all and end-all of all language but is something that needs continually to be measured against language on a much larger scale, the scale of the language of nature, I would say.'

Turner stood up and stretched his back. "Before we hit the sack,' he said, 'I feel I should apologise for letting my ideas hijack the conversation again this evening.'

'I should jolly well think so? scolded Carter, with mock severity. "His mother was Welsh, you know,' he stage-whispered behind his hand to the others, 'and the Welsh are famous for their preachers and teachers.'

'Really?' said Helen seriously. 'I didn't know that.'

'They're singers and rugby players, too, so I've heard,' added Noel.

'Is that so? Do you sing, Dave?' she asked.

'Only rugby-songs, I'm afraid,' answered Turner.

'And I guess you sing them beautifully,' she said, naively. 'I've enjoyed this evening so much,' she added. 'In the circles I usually move in, I seldom find anyone who wants to discuss anything serious, except jobs, babies, houses and automobiles, or politics or finance. I find that all so boring.'

"Then it was fortunate that we all happened to want a wilderness experience at the same time,' stated Noel with approval.

"Yes, I guess it was,' murmured Helen. For some reason she couldn't define, she was still wary of Noel. "Well, good night, everyone,' she said.

After she had gone into the cabin, Walter turned to MacInnes. "You haven't had much to say for yourself, Mac. Did we bore you out of your skull?'

'Not completely,' was the reply. 'I was out of my depth a lot of the time - you don't get to study much philosophy when you're a geologist - but what you were saying about the state of our language makes me think about the sheer messiness of the culture we've built around it. I've not seen it like that before and I confess it spooks me more than a bit to think how ignorant we might all be, while prevented by our language from thinking freely"

"Well, maybe that's no bad thing to consider,' Turner said. 'After all, "ignorance is bliss". What worries me, though, is how long this bliss - if that is what it can truly be called - can continue. To me it's like the tension that builds into an earthquake, the longer it builds, the more catastrophic the result. But I would say that the more people are alerted to the dodginess of our institutionalised language and to the developing crisis in the rift between it and a proper understanding of nature, the more chance there may be of easing the present stress on it and allowing some progress in common understanding to take place, rationally and smoothly.'

'Hey, pack it in, now, Dave, there's a good chap,' Carter broke in. "We really ought to turn in.' He was tired and just a little exasperated at the prospect of the discussion restarting.

'Don't worry, Tony, I've had enough for one day. I'm no spring chicken either, not like the rest of these fellows, and I need my kip.'

The men chatted and joked for some minutes more. Then they, too, went into the cabin to settle down for the night.

Chapter 4: The Lake

Substance and Appearance

The ride back to the lodge next morning was every bit as enjoyable as on the way out but, apart from a few animal sightings, it was uneventful. Charlie took them by a shorter route than the one they had ridden the previous day and they were unsaddling their horses in the Lodge's large corral, well before ten o'clock.

"We've got plenty of time to get the boar up to the lake to do some fishing, Dave,' said Carter. "Let's see if we're in time to order a packed lunch.' He disappeared in the direction of the lodge.

Turner stayed where he was. "What are the rest of you doing today?' he asked.

'It's back to work for me,' replied MacInnes. 'I've got to meet up with my students. They've been over at Mahood Lake for a couple of days, helping with a survey. See you sometime soon, I hope!' Then, with a cheery wave, he went on his way.

'Helen and I are going to Helmcken Falls,' answered Marian. 'She wants to take some photographs around there and I'm going to do some sketching.'

"You're an artist, then, Marian?' Noel remarked. 'Any good?'

'Marian's good enough to earn a comfortable living as an illustrator,' Helen responded rather sharply. She could be very defensive of her younger sister.

'Please accept my apologies, my dear Marian. I meant no offence.'

Noel's expression of sorrow made Marian laugh. 'None taken,' she replied. 'Come on, Helen. Let's get changed. I feel mussy after sleeping all night in these clothes. So long, everyone, see you tonight at dinner.'

'She's right,' said Noel, surveying his companions. 'We all look like bums. I shall also take a shower and change.'

'Before you do,' Turner called as Noel began to walk away, 'tell us what your plans are.'

'Nothing very definite. I'd thought I might go into the bush over Green Mountain way to stalk moose, if there's a guide available.'

'I doubt it,' said Walter, as he came to join them after helping Charlie with the horses. 'I heard Don telling someone he was short of staff in that department.'

'I guess you're right,' Noel replied. 'I should have booked a guide when I arrived. So I shall probably spend the day doing the usual sight-seeing route, Ray's farm and all that.'

'There's no need to spend the day on your own,' Turner found himself saying. 'Our boat will take four or five passengers. What sort of a fisherman are you?

'Okay when it's off-shore, or deep-sea fishing but I must confess to being an inexperienced fresh-water angler. Swordfish and sharks are my usual scene, but still, I'd like to come along.'

'Fine,' said Turner. 'See you at the gate, then, in about half an hour.' He turned to the remaining two. 'And what about you fellows?'

Walter answered first. 'I'm going along with Charlie to White Horse Bluff. The wolves are in that area earlier than usual, I'm told. He's got a lot of inexperienced riders today, so he needs some help.'

"That leaves you, Rick. Any particular plans?' Turner was genuinely concerned about the American, sensing the man's loneliness and shyness. He liked his honesty, willingness to admit ignorance and his quick grasp of new knowledge and ideas - rare qualities, in Turner's experience.

'We-e-ll, I guess PIl be moving along, Dave,' Rick replied. 'T've got to get the camper back to Calgary, where I hired it, so I'll head out for Jasper. Ir'll give me time to see the sights in the Rockies.'

Turner sensed the younger man's hesitation. "When have you got to get the camper back?' he asked.

'In ten days,' said Rick.

'Good heavens, man, you've plenty of time to do all that. Come fishing with us. I know I can speak for Tony. We'll be glad to have you youngsters along. You can do all the lugging about for us,' he joked.

'Gee, that's big of you, Dave.' Rick's reply was made lightly. He was wary of appearing too eager. 'If you old guys are in such a bad way, I guess I'll be doing you a favour if I come along, hey?'

'But of course,' replied Turner. 'See you in a few minutes.'

A short time later the men met at the lodge gateway where Carter's powerboat was parked on its trailer. Carter had been a bit taken aback to learn that he and Turner were to have company. He liked to do his fishing in the companionable silence which only two old friends can share. But he was a kindly man, despite his sometimes abrupt manner, so he greeted Jackson and Findlay with good grace.

"Then I'd better ask Megan to prepare two more packed lunches,' he said.

'That's been seen to,' Turner informed him, genially. "They should be ready by now.'

Well, would you believe it! breathed Carter, to himself. He's clairvoyant!'

About an hour later, the men were unhitching the trailer at the launching ramp on Clearwater Lake. The twenty-mile drive along the roughlysurfaced logging-road had taken them past waterfalls, over creeks, up and down deep valleys and, in places, uncomfortably close to the steep banks of the turbulent and tumbling Clearwater River.

Gazing into the water from the end of the floating pier, Rick remarked, "This lake sure lives up to its name, don't it? You can see way down, clear as crystal.'

Carter agreed. "Yes, you can, here, near the shore, but out in the middle it's a few hundred feet deep.'

Getting the boat into the water was not difficult, because Carter and Turner were old hands at it. Noel noticed that there were two engines at the stern. The one, which was larger and obviously more powerful, was in the centre of the transom and the smaller one was set to one side.

"What's that smaller engine for, Tony?' he asked.

"Two things,' Carter replied. "The forty-eight Evinrude is too much for fishing, so for that purpose we use the smaller one, the eight horse-power,

which we call the "Putt-purt". It also serves as a spare, for emergencies.' "Makes sense,' said Noel.

Soon they were all on board and roaring away northwards, cleaving the still surface of the lake like a skinner's knife. The forest came down to the water's edge, halted by narrow strips of sand and what Rick at first thought was fencing. But soon he realised it was the reflection of beached logs, all along the shores of the lake. The effect was weird but beautiful as, somehow, the reflections made an almost regular pattern all along the shoreline.

Noel watched the white slash of the wake, with the spray shooting into the air and spreading-out behind them on the smooth blue surface. "We aren't going to do much fishing at this speed,' he remarked dryly.

'We're going on up to Azure Lake first,' answered Turner. "That'll take about two hours. We can picnic up there, then come back into the Clearwater this afternoon to fish. Thought you might like to see the Azure. Boat's the only way to get there unless you want to spend about a week hiking through the bush.'

'Sounds good to me,' said Noel.

'Me too,' added Rick. He was thrilled by the speed of the boat and entranced by the scenery all around him. His home state was similar, he knew, but in his job he hadn't had much time to appreciate it. Now he felt that this must be one of the most beautiful places on earth. He voiced his thoughts.

His companions agreed. 'And I thank God that British Columbia is still relatively undiscovered,' added Carter. 'Its not jammed with tourists - at least, not yet.'

It certainly wasn't. On the lake, which was more than twenty miles long, they saw only one other boat - or, rather, a floating hill-billy log cabin built on a large log raft. With a pitched, thatched-type roof, it was incongruously buzzing around in circles with fishing-lines out but with apparently no-one attending them. Approaching the end of the lake, where the river entered the Clearwater, Carter powered-down the engine to a steady chug-chug across the placid surface. Rick sat in the stern contentedly musing about what he could see. In the relative quietness of their present motion he mentioned his mistaken impression of a fence to the others. Then after a contemplative pause, he added 'Reflections sure are weird!'

'Yes,' said Turner. "We suppose that they're somehow i or on the lake's surface, and it's strange, isn't it, to realise that they aren't. I mean, just look

at that path of sparkles made by the sunlight. As I view it, it leads directly from me towards the sun. But neither that path nor those sparkles are the same for you or any of us. The path is different and the sparkles are different for anyone viewing it.'

'That's right,' marvelled Rick. 'I never thought much about it before. And that light-path's stayed with us all along, even when we were going lickety-split. It points towards us whether we're still or moving no matter what speed, and, as you say, it's different for each of us.'

Turner agreed. 'And it's the same, of course, with the reflections of the logs and the mountains and so on. None of them is actually there, either in or on the lake. Like the path of the sun-sparkles, their positions are different, as you say, for each one of us. And what is this whole scene if it's not reflections off surfaces of one kind or another, reflections which are different for everyone observing it?'

'Hey, I get it,' responded Rick. "Don't that go back to what we've been saying about "observers" and relativity? I see what I see and you see what you see, but neither of us sees exactly what the other sees. The whole world we see is different for each one of us. Right?'

'Right,' said Turner. 'So now you can see what, basically, relativity means. It reminds me, Rick, of an argument I once got involved in during a philosophy discussion - I used to attend classes in Philosophy for the hell of it, you understand,' he added.

Carter turned round from the steering wheel and announced, 'Now you know why I think he's crazy!'

'I didn't know you were listening,' countered his friend, unperturbed. 'Anyway,' he continued, "The lecturer had given us, for homework, the question as to what is reflected in a mirror when no-one is looking at it. It caused consternation, because no-one could answer it. Just think! To imagine what that mirror looks like you have to imagine it reflecting something. But, as you must know, any set of reflections you may imagine depends on some viewpoint in relation to the mirror. A point to the left of the mirror reflects things to the right and a point to the right reflects things on the left. A point below reflects things above and a point above reflects things below . . . and so on. But with no-one there, which of those

viewpoints applies? To imagine it, you keep finding yourself choosing some particular viewpoint in your imagination, which defeats the object of the exercise. As it seemed at the time, there's no way you can imagine what that unviewed mirror looks like. Is it filled with all possible reflections or with none at all? What does the mirror look like, in itself, with no-one looking at it? It was like asking how this scenery appears in itself, to no-one at all, or from all viewpoints whatsoever at the same time. The whole thing is a conundrum. The next homework question was, "Suppose you are in an airplane confronted by a terrorist who demands that you prove to him that you exist as a real person, not just a figment of his own imagination, and he promises that if you fail he will indulge himself by blowing your head off." After that, some people left the class, never to return. Believe it or not, it was later discovered that some of these had been traumatised to the extent that they had to seek help from their doctors. One of them even had to have psychiatric help for clinical depression.'

'Gee, that's awful,' said Rick.

'So is that what happened to you, Dave?' his old friend joked.

'No, of course not, as you well know, Tony, but it just shows how dangerous ideas can be.'

'That's right,' Noel added. 'History is full of accounts of such ideas, like

those of Christ or Socrates, for instance.'

While they had been talking, the boat had slowed to less than walking pace.

'Sorry to bring you back to the mundane, gentlemen,' interrupted Carter with some urgency in his voice, "but we're getting into dodgy water. I need lookouts on both sides.'

Rick and Noel could see why when they peered down into the water. Hanging, some of them vertically, no more than a foot or two below the surface were dozens of water-logged tree trunks brought down by the river which joined the Clearwater to Azure Lake, their jagged ends threatening to tear holes in unwary small boats.

For the next ten minutes the only sounds were calls of 'Keep left!' or 'Look out to your right!" and other similar instructions, punctuated from

time to time by curses and the occasional recrimination. In the urgency of this situation, they were unconscious of the extent to which the politenessbarrier was being broken down to cement their friendship.

Then they were safely through and into the river channel.

'Right, hold onto your hats,' Carter called, 'I need plenty of poke to beat this current.' He opened up the throttle and the bow lifted high as it accelerated up the now swiftly flowing river. "The river's low, Dave,' he shouted, to make himself heard above the roar of the engine.

Turner shouted back, 'We're later than usual this year!' For the benefit of the others he explained, through cupped hands, "There's not been the usual snowfall, the last few years, and most of what snow there is will now be gone from above the Alpine.' As they went sweeping round the first bend in grand style, Rick, with his head above the tonneau, released his tension with a wild cowboy whoop. For a short while, they continued in this fashion, up the now clearly perceptible river gradient. It was heady stuff!

'This is great! Rick shouted. Then, suddenly, as Carter's head was momentarily turned to say something, Turner seemed to go crazy.

'Throttle back!' he shouted to Carter. 'Shoal ahead!'

Carter slammed the throttle shut, but too late. The boat hit the obstruction and leapt over it. For a moment or so the boat continued on under its own momentum. Then it slowed, turning lazily, broadside-on to the current. All became quiet. Soon, the only sign of motion was with reference to the river-bank, passing swiftly by. Carter and Turner moved around like characters in a speeded-up film, while the others, in the silence that ensued, peered anxiously over the side.

'No damage done that I can see,' Noel called.

'This side seems okay, too,' said Rick.

'Hoist the prop, for Crissake!' yelled Carter.

Turner was already there, expertly heaving the engine onto its spigot. Then he called to the others, 'Quick! Each of you, grab a paddle!'

It was only then that Rick's ears became adjusted to the subdued roar of the river, and his eyes adjusted to the rate at which they were being carried downstream, back towards the gushing, jagged vee the boat had made in

the shelf of submerged wood and detritus. Realising with alarm the danger of entering that funnel broadside, and not fancying being tumbled and perhaps ground between the boat and the logs in that torrent of glaciercold water, he took a paddle and helped Carter swing the bow towards the breach.

'Over here, Noel!' Turner called.

Noel had also found a paddle and, with it, helped Turner bring the stern of the boat around. Eventually, with much concentration and a comparative minimum of shouting, the risky passage through the breach was safely completed. But they needed the engine in order to steer the boat through the turbulence, so as soon as they were through, Turner swung the engine off its catch and lowered the propeller into the water. Carter then restarted the motor, and gingerly put it into gear. His three perspiring companions watched with bated breath.

'It's okay,' he called, as the propeller began to spin, but as the motor was accelerated they could all feel a strong vibration in the hull.

"Well, the sheer-pin's okay, but the blades are bent,' Carter announced. "We'll have to get back to the lake and fix the prop.'

The return trip downriver was made as sedately as the current would allow. In such turbulence, with the engine on very low revs, the bow had to be kept in front by constant use of the paddles. Only by looking at the bank could they tell how swiftly they were going and the dangers that would follow from being swept, broadside, over some underwater obstruction. Well aware of this, they were all on the alert for further hazards. Even so, Rick felt exhilarated and said as much.

'That's fine for you,' Turner responded dryly, 'but at my age, I can do without that kind of excitement.'

'Come off it, Dave!' said Carter, not at all concerned about the incident. "You're as much an adrenaline junkie now as ever you were!"

When they safely reached the lake, they beached the boat on a spit of sand just beyond the river mouth and canted it over so that the propeller was well clear of the water. Fortunately, the shaft was not bent, only one of the blades, so Carter donned his waders, found a pair of smooth stones, and using the one as a hammer and the other as an anvil he proceeded to straighten the damaged blade. Turner collected some wood for a fire and

in a short while had a kettle boiling. By the time lunch was organised, Carter had finished his hammering and was ready to join them.

'It should be okay now,' he said, "but just in case it packs up, folks, we've got a spare prop in the locker, and if the worse comes to the worst, we've still got the putt-putt. But if that's all we've got,' he added, 'we'll be going home by moonlight - if there is a moon, that is! But at least we will get home - perhaps,' he added, jokingly.

The Englishmen knew that despite its placid appearance, the lake was prone to swift and violent storms and that in a sudden fog it could carry a boat without an engine quietly to destruction at the lake's outlet at Osprey Falls. Other boats - and lives - had been lost in such ways on the Clearwater.

'Look!' said Noel suddenly.

Following his pointing finger, they could make out a large bird of prey, perched in one of the tallest trees, haughtily surveying the mouth of the river.

'An American bald eagle, would you believe!' breathed Noel. "Isn't that something! Although it's the American emblem, you now hardly ever see them in the States.'

'Watch it carefully,' said Carter. 'It's waiting for the fish to rise. When we see it leave that branch we'll know it's time to get out the fishing tackle.'

The conversation, over lunch, was mostly about the previous drama, the propeller and the eagle - which had flown off when Noel got too near in trying to photograph it. When this conversation was exhausted, a thoughtful silence ensued.

Suddenly, out of the blue, Carter said, 'I was thinking, Dave, when we were powering up the river, just before we hit the shoal, that in our talk about reflections there was something we hadn' considered.'

'What's that, Tony?' his friend asked.

'Well, when you were talking about the positions of those reflections relative to observers, you didn't mention that colours also differ for differently moving observers.'

'Hey? How come?' Rick asked.

Carter explained. "You remember when that house-boat passed us on the lake, or when we passed it, rather - it doesn't matter which, since it's all

relative - did you notice how the note of its engine dropped from a higher pitch to a lower one as it passed?'

"Yeah, like the "neeee-aaaaah" noise kids make when they try to imitate automobiles passing at speed.'

'That's right,' said Carter. 'Well, it's the same with light as with sound. Stars approaching us show the same kind of rise in pitch as cars do when they are approaching, and the same kind of drop in pitch when they're moving away. Technically, it's called the Doppler effect, after the man who first wrote about it, Johann Doppler.'

Just a minute, Tony,' interposed Turner, "You're talking about "pitch". I suppose you know, Rick, that "pitch", in light-terms, is what we sense as colour.'

'Now you mention it, yes,' Rick replied. "But I reckon you're learning that you must always check me out.'

Turner grinned. He found Rick Jackson's interest and honesty quite engaging.

Noel said, "Yes, it's easy to forget, I guess, that an object's colour depends on how you are moving relatively to it. So for us, when we were speeding up that river, the colours of the scenery in front of us were shifted towards the high-pitch end of the spectrum - the blue end - and those behind us were shifted towards the red. In either case they were different from the colours of that scenery as we see them now, just sitting here.'

'Not noticeably, of course,' added Turner, 'because we weren't travelling fast enough. But it would be detectable, even at this slow speed if we had the right instruments. And having no speed relatively to an object is nothing special, just one state among many - an indefinite number of possible states, in fact.'

"We must remember, too,' Carter pointed out, 'that there are many more colours in the spectrum than the human eye can detect. The full spectral range goes from deepest infra-red to utmost ultraviolet and gamma, most of which are no more than darkness to us.'

'Just so,' Turner agreed.

Rick broke the ensuing silence. 'Okay,' he said. 'So now we don't just have all kinds of places to choose from in seeing the world. We've got all kinds of speeds to choose from as well! So, amongst all these positions, speeds and so on, when do we ever know what's the real situation? What about that happening, back there, when we jumped that hoop-de-do on the river? Was that real or did I just dream it? Was it the same for you guys as it was for me?' He paused. 'But, gee, what's the point in asking that? How do I know I'm not dreaming all this, right now? How do I know you guys are really there outside my own imagination - like your "gunman" in the exercise, Dave - and that I'm not dreaming all these discussions we're having? I reckon I can see why those classmates of yours quit. It's kind of spooky, eh?' Then, after a contemplative pause: . . . "Sheesh!"

Rick's woeful expression made them laugh. Then Carter said, with a wry glance at Turner, 'Now you know, Rick, what it's like to be a philosopher! Keep thinking like that and you'll go as barmy as some people I know.'

But Turner could understand the younger man's worried thoughts. Hadn't he "been there" himself? Unlike those disturbed students, however, he had decided to worry the thing through, His own philosophical mindset could not accept any compromise on issues of truth. Truth is truth, he had decided, and there's no decent way of "bottling-out" of accepting it. He would have to follow it through to whatever might be the logical conclusion, good or bad. From what he had learned of Rick, he judged that this man was a decent-minded person who would not be lacking in that kind of philosophical "bottle", so he continued.

'Sorry if it bothers you, Rick, but the inescapable fact is that even "good old solid matter", or mass, as physicists call it, is as dependent on perspective as colour is. It's relative in the same way. The same goes for size, length, duration, all the various properties and qualities of objects. It all depends, not only on the position of your viewpoint but also on your motion with regard to the object - or, of that object relative to yourself, of course, which is the same thing.'

There was a pause as Rick pondered what Turner had said. Then, "Gee, my brain hurts!' he exclaimed, clutching his head, theatrically. 'I need time to take this in.'

Lunch over, they re-floated the boat. The small engine was started, and with Carter once again at the helm they puttered quietly round in circles, just beyond the mouth of the river, clear of the water-saturated hidden logs. The rods were hoisted out and the baited hooks were trailed expectantly behind.

For a while, they sat quietly, occasionally jerking their lines in the hope of attracting the fish. Watching the water, Noel found his thoughts returning to their discussion. His annoying air of superiority was, in fact, not without justification. Possessed of a keen intellect, and with the added advantage of having wealthy and adoring parents, he had benefited from the best and widest education money could buy. Well-travelled and wellread, his was a formidable mind. To find himself in the company of a man like Turner, whose ideas both intrigued and challenged him, was good fortune indeed. He turned towards Rick.

"To take up what you were saying, Rick,' he began, 'no, it can't be all a dream. There has to be a real world, behind all those relative perspectives. I mean, those perspectives have to be perspectives of something - not matter, or mass, I agree, Dave, because mass is just one of those measures that are dependent on perspective, as you've just explained. As the velocity of a body increases relatively to us, so the energy of its motion turns into mass. But as I understand it, there is something objective, something real and substantial independent of all our different views of it.'

Before anyone could respond, Noel went on. 'I mean, take those two mountain peaks up there on the skyline. During our journey up the lake, my perspective of those peaks has continually changed. At every point, their distances in depth and breadth of vision were different. Yet by means of trigonometry we can work out the true distance between those peaks, the distance that remains the same, no matter who views them and from where.'

'How do we know that?' queried Rick.

'It was proved by one of Einstein's associates, an ace mathematician called Minkowski - yet another "Heini", I'm afraid, Rick, but of Polish descent.'

This prompted Carter to intervene. 'What this guy Minkowski did, Rick, was simply to apply, in four dimensions, the three dimensional

geometry everyone knows. When you see a tower on a hill in the distance, it may look to you as though the tower is round, while from my different point of view the tower may look square But behind all these different viewpoints the tower is just what it is, no matter what, and we can tell this, as you say, Noel, by trigonometry. But to work out the true shape of the tower, Rick, you don't have to solve equations. You simply look at it from different positions and estimate it accordingly, which we all do, more or less instinctively, without any need of maths. So what this fella Minkowski did was simply to apply the same geometry that we instinctively apply to objects in space to events in time, so that it measures four dimensions instead of just the usual three, But the result is the same in that it tells you the veal distance-time, or interval as it is called, between events viewed at a distance such as, say, a pair of exploding stars.'

'So there's at least that much reality, then, behind our different viewpoints,' said Rick, showing some sign of relief.

Turner took up Carter's point. "Yes, that, Rick, in relativistic terms is the substance behind the appearance. But this is not the same substance that there is supposed to be hidden behind, beyond, or underlying the appearances, or phenomena, as in classical science. This new kind of objective reality is integrated with the appearances, not apart from them as in classical "realism". So perhaps you can see how vital the difference is between these two approaches to physical science.'

'You mean between what you called, if I remember rightly, the "realist" approach to science and the approach of relativity? responded Rick appreciatively. 'So there is still substance to things. I'm surely glad it ain't all a dream after all.'

'Quite right, Rick,' replied Turner, 'you're learning fast.'

Carter said, turning to his old friend, 'I see now, Dave, that this was your inversion - conversion or whatever you like to call it - from your original depressing "man the microbe" view of the universe to your subsequent, more positive "man the measure" interactive view.'

'Yes, Tony, that depressing view, as you call it, is the sort of view that develops all too often among intelligent and enquiring young people in our Western society, built as it is on the philosophy of atomic materialism. It's a recipe for despair, the feeling of pointlessness which fosters the "Poor

Me" attitude of the social dropout. As I came to see it, it breeds the sort of cynicism, nihilism, self-indulgence and the manic pursuit of pleasure - which may well involve "kicks" in various forms of violence towards other persons and society generally. And if that fails to satisfy, then seeking escape by drugs or even suicide becomes inevitable. This is the curse of this society of ours, as I see it, a feeling of complete helplessness in the face of things.'

After a long contemplative pause, Turner struck a more positive note. "This might remind you, Tony, of the conversation we had with Walter and Don, the other evening at the Lodge. I didn't mention it at the time, but I see a logical connection between this disastrous Western tradition and our customary idea of light as a travelling intermediary.'

'I don't get you,' replied a puzzled Carter. "What logical connection?'

'It puts a barrier between physical reality and the appearances of them. That makes us think that we only see external things as they were, not as they really are, and that makes us feel powerless to change things because they have already happened.'

"That's an interesting idea,' said Noel. 'I sure wish I'd been involved in that discussion. Maybe you could give me a quick run-down of what it was about.'

Carter obliged. "Well, Noel, the discussion was about the importance of the observer in modern physics and the conflict there is between relativity and quantum theory on this issue.'

'I see,' said Noel, 'the dreaded "EPR" paradox, I suppose. Is that what you talked about?"

6. This is the notorious 'EPR' paradox, so named from a paper published in 1935 by A. Einstein, B. Podolski and N. Rosen. In that paper the authors presumed to have demonstrated that quantum theory is incomplete, that there are 'hidden' connections between quantum events like the emission of a photon at A and its arriving at B. Thirty years larer, J. S. Bell, of CERN developed a principle according to which this EPR assumption could be tested. On this basis, experimental results were obtained in 1978 by J. Clauser and his team in Berkeley, USA and in 1982 by A. Aspect's group in Paris, demonstrating that there can be no such 'hidden connections', that the photon 'leaving A' and 'arriving at B' are not two separate events but a single event, an instantaneous at-a-distance quantum correlation between the two places, with nothing between. This was taken as a refutation of Einstein's claim that there could be no 'faster-than-light' physical influence.

Yes,' replied Turner, 'but we didn't go into much detail on that. For your information, Rick, the "EPR" or, to give its full title, the "Einstein Podolski Rosen" paradox, is that relativity, on the one hand, tells us that that nothing can travel faster than light while, on the other hand, quantum theory tells us that a quantum interaction between distant objects is instantaneous. The "paradox" is that there is experimental proof of both these seemingly opposite and contradictory assertions. We also discussed the silliness of some modern theories, like the "Big Bang" dogma, for instance, which is based on the fallacy of the "expanding universe". This alerted me to a fundamental duplicity on the part of modern science. It disabused me about what I previously thought of as modern science's logical integrity and made me embark on my own logical odyssey, as we discussed at the cabin, last evening.'

"Very interesting!" Noel remarked. 'Very interesting indeed!'

It was Rick who next spoke. 'So,' he said, 'tell us, Dave, how does this answer the question you told us about of the mirror in the unoccupied room?"

"The simple answer, Rick, is that there is no such thing as a completely unoccupied room. It may be empty of Auman observers but not of what Walter nicely called "observicles" in our discussion at the cabin. Each of these rudimentary quantum receptors is as much a centre of observation as any of us humans would:be. The only difference is that the amount of information conveyed in an interaction involving just one or two quanta is much smaller than one involving statistical numbers of quanta, as normally occurs in interactions involving the eye or some other optical device, such as a camera, say, or close-circuit TV.'

'Yup,' replied Rick, 'I guess I can see that. It follows from all we've discussed. But what about that other thing, the terrorist who holds a gun to your head? How is that little sonofabitch dealt with?

'T'm afraid, Rick,' Turner replied, 'that the answer to that problem is not so simple. It would involve a whole change of philosophical mindset on the part of the terrorist, which would undoubtedly be impracticable in that

se situation.

'Lucky, then, eh, Dave,' put in Carter, 'that it was just an academic exercise to get people thinking philosophically.'

"That's right,' replied his friend. 'It was to sort out "the men from the boys", so to speak, and get things rolling. Those of us who stayed the course found what that course was about, just as those others would have done if they hadn't panicked and run. They would have seen how that question is answered. It is along the lines of what we have been discussing here about what reality really, is. As things stood, it reminds me of what Francis Bacon, I think it was, said about truth. "What is truth?" said jesting Pilate and would not stay for an answer".'

Noel reeled in his line, re-baited his hook and re-cast it. Then he returned to the main thread of the argument.

'As for that question of truth and reality,' he began, 'let's get back to our discussion of those mountain peaks What we were talking about were true distances between those peaks behind the differences in perspective, But now, instead. of the distance separating those peaks, we are talking about them being separated in distance-time. In other words, the distance separating those peaks, as calculated by all the differently moving observers in the way we agreed is not the true distance, because it doesn't state the time at which that distance was measured. Over time - geological time - that distance may change. So, to get it dead right we should say that they are at that distance at the same instant. In other words, to get it right, we have to say where objects were and when. So as events, rather than just positions, the usual three dimensions aren't enough, so they have to be calculated in four. That's surely the reality we've been looking for, the "space-time continuum" that Einstein describes as a substratum that underlies all the various relative perspectives and appearances.'

"That, Noel,' said Carter, 'is what we were discussing at the Lodge, but Dave didn't accept it.'

'Really? Why not?'

To Noel's surprise, it was Rick who answered him. "Dave said there's another theory, quantum theory, that shows there's nothing like that continuity in nature, So this is another way in which relativity and quantum theory don't gel. Ain't that right, Dave?'

'Indeed so, Rick,' Turner replied, pleasantly surprised that Rick had remembered and understood. He turned to Noel. "What we're talking about here, of course, Noel, is Einstein's general theory of relativity.'

"Yeah, I'm with you. General relativity supposes that all physical events are geometrically linked-up, behind the scenes, in a continuous, underlying four-dimensional way. According to that theory, yesterday's happenings, today's happenings and tomorrow's happenings are all, in a sense, already there, all geometrically connected-up and waiting to be "played" like the wiggles in the groove of a gramophone-record.'

"Well put, Noel,' Turner responded, pleased to find the young Bostonian so knowledgeable about a subject most people avoided like the plague. "That's what philosophers call determinacy. It's like what Omar Khayyam said: "Yea, the first dawn of creation wrote what the last day of reckoning shall read."'

'Khayyám's poetry,' Turner continued, 'was romantic but notoriously depressing. His idea was that since everything is determined by the mindless mechanical motions of atoms, there's nothing we can do about anything in this uncaring material universe, so there's no point in anything but to escape from it all by steeping ourselves in wine - or drugs as is more likely nowadays.'

Carter intervened. 'So this was the sort of stuff that depressed you, Dave, as you were telling us?'

'Not quite, Tony. This Omar Khayyám stuff was something I just happened across which seemed to express very nicely the way I was thinking at the time.'

Carter again took up the thread.

'As you may know, guys, in complete opposition to that determinacy assumption of Einstein's, a fella called Werner Heisenberg pointed out that all events at the fundamental quantum level are indeterminate.'

'Known as Heisenberg's indeterminacy, or uncertainty principle,' Noel explained rather unnecessarily.

'It opposes what Einstein said,' continued Turner, 'because it says that on the quantum level of physical existence, fundamental physical events - quantum events - are completely unconnected. Their individual occurrences, as physicists say, are discrete and indeterminate.'

'But surely,' said Rick, 'something makes them all hang together.'

'And in the way Einstein and Minkowski described,' added Noel.

"Well, whatever that something is,' Turner replied, 'on the evidence of quantum physics it isn't geometry - at least, not as Einstein and Minkowski envisaged it.'

Carter was remembering some of the things he had read about Einstein and relativity. "But, Dave,' he said, 'hasn't that "geometrical" approach to physics been proved, in its predictions of the orbits of planets like Mercury, the bending of light-rays in gravitational fields and so on?'

"Yes, Tony, but that doesn't logically exclude the possibility of other theories making the same relativistic predictions while avoiding the conflict with quantum theory that we discussed.'

"That's true,' Carter conceded, thoughtfully.

From the fishing point of view, the exercise had been a failure. "This lake must be full of distilled water! Carter complained, inspecting his bait untouched on the hook. Throughout this and other such remarks, Noel sat on the thwart of the boat, like a slim-line, sartorial version of Rodin's 'Thinker'.

Eventually, Turner remarked, "You look worried, Noel.'

'Sorry,' Noel apologised, 'my mind was working along the lines of what Rick said, earlier. Are you really suggesting, Dave, that in the end there's nothing there at all, that what we call the world is just something projected by myriads and myriads of observers, or "observicles" in myriads and myriads of different and unconnected ways?"

'Ah, I see what's worrying you,' replied Turner. "You're wondering, again, where, in all this relativity and indeterminacy of things, there is any true objectivity, or reality.'

"That's it exactly!' was Noel's frustrated response, "We seem to solve it only to lose it again. So how do you view all that relativity - that subjectivity of things? How do you answer the problem of solipsism, for instance?"

'Gee, guys,' came Rick's plaintive tones, 'there you go again! How many times have I gotta tell you that if you want me to follow all this, you gotta explain it to me in words of one syllable.'

'You do yourself an injustice, Rick, said Turner, kindly, but amused. 'I think you're a much sharper cookie than you recognise. Anyway,' he continued, 'solipsism is believing that you, yourself, is all that exists and that everything else, other people like us included, are figments of your own imagination.'

"What that "terrorist" thought in that exercise you described to us. So he was a... what would you call him?'

'A solipsist - exactly that!' Turner replied.

'But,' said Rick, 'like Noel says, it can't really be like that, can it? I mean, it can't be all just a kinda dream. So, what's the answer? I sure hope there is one.

Once again, Noel reeled in his line and recast it. 'One answer to that, Rick," he said, 'was proposed by the philosopher George Berkeley . . .'

Rick interrupted him. "Hey, Dave, that's the guy you and Walter talked about at the restaurant, the one who was a bishop or something.'

"That's the fella,' replied Turner.

'Sorry, Noel, J didn't mean to interrupt you,' Rick apologised. "You were saying?"

'No problem, Rick. Berkeley's answer to that solipsistic, or catastrophic self-centredness problem was what you might expect from a churchman. He said that the reality underlying all those different perceptions - what holds it all together behind the scenes - wasn't geometry or anything like that but the mind of God.'

'Yes,' Turner agreed. 'For Berkeley, God was the spiritual underpinning of our existence, the unifying principle, or substance, behind all the relative perspectives and subjective appearances.'

Rick was thoughtful as he fiddled perfunctorily with his reel and line. 'But I thought you said, Dave, in that discussion at the Lodge, that nobody can even guess the way God sees things.'

Carter turned from where he was one-handedly steering, whilst lazily trailing a bare foot in the water. 'Rick's right, Dave,' he said. "That's what you said in that discussion the other night, that we can't presume even to guess how God sees things. It was where that discussion ended, as I recall.'

'Yes,' Turner agreed, 'we seem to be re-tracing that same discussion now, don't we, but on slightly different lines. You see, Noel, what Rick and Tony are referring to, here, was my conclusion that there's no point in appealing to God, in the way Berkeley does. Whatever we think God thinks is pure presumption on our part, since there's no way in which the finite can comprehend the infinite. And this same conclusion seems to follow from our present discussion. Ask yourself, for instance, how God, assuming he exists in anything like the form we customarily imagine, might view things. Would he see what all the myriads of different observers see, from all their various viewpoints and speed-frames all at once? What could that overall God's-Eye view of things possibly mean to us - or, indeed, to any finite observer? It's like trying to imagine what that mirror we spoke of looks like from all possible angles at the same time. Since we have no way of comprehending what God might perceive, what possible gain in understanding can we get from Berkeley's answer?"

'In other words,' remarked Carter, dryly, 'what the reality is behind the appearances is, you might say "God knows!" But then,' he continued, 'didn't we counter that by admitting that there has to be some belief, of the kind Berkeley was talking about - a "minimal concession', I think you called it - to a belief in God, or some such being, the only way of thinking, you said, which is consistent with sanity?'

"Yes,' Turner replied, 'undoubtedly, in order to believe that all these relative impressions of ours aren't in any way connected, other than in our own imaginations, you'd have to go beyond solipsism and become virtually insane. It's a danger which threatens anyone thinking in that philosophical way - as you've joked about, Tony. But it's no joke. I've no doubt that this is why so many people avoid philosophy like the plague. To them it might seem like jumping off into an abyss, as it must have seemed to those people I talked about who left the Philosophy class. But as you say, Tony, our present discussion is developing on somewhat different lines. Now we seem to be looking for another kind of coherence principle. It isn't matter, we've decided, and it can't be Minkowski's four-dimensional geometry, or Einstein's space-time substratum, and if we think of it as God, then all we're saying, in effect, is that what it is, as you so nicely put it, Tony, "God knows" - an expression of complete and utter ignorance.'

'So that, I guess, is the science-crisis you talked about, Dave,' ventured Carter, 'that Western science gives us no clue as to what physical reality is, which you regard as the curse of our materialistic atamism?'

"Yes, Tony. Deep down, you might say, that's the problem, that in view of all the conflicting bits of scientific knowledge we now possess, we still have no way of deciding on some kind of integrating principle to save us from complete philosophical disintegration, or social insanity.'

'As if we weren't already well on the way! remarked Carter, sardonically.

There was a thoughtful silence while the boat chugged quietly around, the only events connected with fishing being the unravelling of the occasional tangle of lines.

'T've never known the fish so quiet!' Carter remarked. 'I think that when you frightened off that eagle, Noel, you must have frightened the fish off as well!'

"Or it ate all the fish before we got here!' offered Rick, helpfully.

'Perhaps it's all this philosophy that's frightening them away,' responded Noel. Then with a note of scorn in his voice, he added, 'I know it always does with my friends at home. Mention anything the least bit philosophical and people push off, just like these fish?

Turner concurred. 'I used to feel like that about people, Noel. But, really, can you blame them? My experience in that area tells me that it's some kind of survival-mechanism, making people choose the kind of healthy, blissful ignorance you despise rather than risk this "insanity" we've talked about in search of truth.'

Silence fell, once again, while they concentrated on their fishing, except for Noel, who, again remained statue-like, deep in thought. Eventually he spoke.

'But there's just got to be an answer.'

'Like what, Noel?' asked Carter.

"Well, how's this? Getting back to what you said, Tony, about colour. If we look at a fast-approaching star we see its colour Doppler-shifted towards the blue. Right? But isn't that shift the same for all the colours of

the star's spectrum? And that spectrum, as we know, is the "fingerprint" of the star's chemical composition - what you might call its "bar-code" of colour bands. So no matter how far motion shifts those spectral lines away from the normal, the relationship between the lines - the compositionfingerprint, or bar-code - remains the same. Sodium is still identifiable as sodium, for example, in its relationship to the other lines, and carbon as carbon, and so on, no matter how large the spectral shift relatively to the observer.'

Rick wasn't sure he understood and said so. It was Carter who explained.

"What I think Noel is saying is this. Think of a tune. No matter how you alter the pitch of the instruments or change the key, it's still recognisable as the same tune. That tune's identifying characteristics remain the same, whatever key it is played in. The chemical composition of a substance, shown in the distributions of its spectral lines is like that, a tune, and the overall shift in the spectrum of that substance, due to relative speed, is like that tune played at a different pitch or in a different key.'

"Got it! said Rick.

"Yes," Noel enthused, 'that's a neat way of putting it, Tony. I mean, can you imagine, Dave, a Doppler shift so large that it would change the whole character of a substance, say turning hydrogen into helium - or on Tony's analogy, like turning the "Stars and Stripes" into "God Save the Queen"?

As Turner was about to answer, his rod flicked and they all watched expectantly while he carefully reeled in his line, but there was still nothing. After renewing the bait, he cast his line once more. "Great idea, wasn't it,' he said, apologetically, 'inviting you fellas to come fishing!'

'No problem,' remarked Noel, 'Pm enjoying myself - with this challenging discussion, the quietness, the scenery and all. It's as if we were back at the dawn of rational philosophy in the Golden age of ancient Greece before modernity took over.'

"Me too,' agreed Rick, 'even if it is brain-bending and spooky, as it must have been to those Greek guys you talk about.'

The flicking of Turner's line had created some expectancy that they might not, after all, return to the lodge empty-handed. However, after

Gathering the horses

some time had elapsed with no further action, Turner once again took up the thread of the discussion.

'You've argued very convincingly, I think, Noel,' he said, 'that the existence of a substance as what might be described as a character-complex - an information-complex, if you like, which Tony likens to a piece of music - is the same for all observers regardless of their relative positions, speeds, and whatnot. So I agree that in spite of the relativity and subjectivity of all the various aspects of things, there is, as you say, the reality, or substantiality, of the thing in itself, as an identifiable whole.'

'T'm real glad, Dave, that you agree that there is reality,' Rick broke in. 'Solipsism must be a pretty scary state to be in.'

'agree, Rick. So what does the reality of anything consist of if not a full observational inventory of its identifying qualities, properties-and characteristics; those informational wholes by which we identify things? This makes the substance of an object what it is in itself, as we see it plainly before us - something we may actually inspect, not something "underlying" and remote from all possible inspection, like hidden mechanisms or invisible parts, or the geometrical "space-time substratum" of General Relativity - or "God", if you like.'

Noel was so intent on listening to Turner that he had completely forgotten he was supposed to be catching fish. Now he gradually became aware of a strong and steady force dragging his rod downwards till its tip was almost immersed in the lake. The strain was threatening either to snap the line or pull the rod out of his hand. A shout from Carter brought him to full awareness.

'Hey, Noel, I reckon you've hooked some "hidden mechanism" - a submarine, by the look of it!'

The others reeled in their lines while a contrite Noel spent the next few minutes trying to unfoul his fishing line from a submerged log, as Carter reversed their course over the obstruction. When the line was cleared, Noel reeled it in and put the rod down.

'I warned you I was an inexperienced fresh-water fisherman. But I'd rather concentrate on what you were saying, anyway, Dave.'

"Too bad you can't do the two things at once, like me,' joked Rick.

'What, thinking and drowning worms!' said Noel good-humouredly. 'Some skill, that!' He decided that he quite liked his brash fellow countryman.

'So,' he said, addressing Turner, 'you agree, then, Dave, that the character of a thing, as it is in itself, behind all our different views of it, is the common substantial factor in relativistic observation?'

'That's right, Noel," replied Turner. 'It fits with what I believe, that there's no necessity to think of observation as a mere facade behind which there's some hidden "other reality" - or "substance", in the archaic meaning of that word. In my opinion, searching for that "substance", or "underlying reality" behind light as we see it and which we assume to be travelling invisibly in space, has been the undoing of Western philosophy right from the start. If we're to take proper account of the findings of relativity and quantum physics, other than as bits of scientific wizardry, then we have to accept that this "facade" - the phenomenon, in philosophical language - is no mere facade but is a real part of the real thing. This character-complex of views, perspectives and so on, I would say, is the only reality there is, so far as it can ever concern us. It is infinitely more real, certainly, than those fleeting and indeterminate quantum-events to which it may be reduced.'

Whilst fishing, they had progressed several miles along the lake, aided by the river current flowing southward. The sun was now disappearing behind the mountains and a chill wind feathered the surface of the water, creating dark patches of ripples like fathomless holes in the silvered surface. The lengthening shadows gave an ominous appearance to the scene which, earlier in the day, had so entranced Rick. Half-forgotten tales of monsters lurking in the depths of lakes stirred in the back of his mind and he gave an involuntary shiver.

Noticing this, Turner said, 'I suggest we call it a day, Tony. Don't think we'll be catching anything now.'

'You're darned right, Dave,' Carter replied, "You've definitely frightened them off. They've heard, anyway, that brainy people eat nothing but fish, so philosophy's bad news on this lake. But yes,' he said to the others, 'wed better start back. It'll rake at least an hour to get to the end of the lake, even if the engine's okay.'

They looked on rather anxiously while he hoisted the putt-putt and lowered the big engine into the water. To their relief, it started straight away and soon they were scything the smooth surface of the darkening lake as they sped, flat out, in the direction of the landing stage.

Chapter 5: Back to the Lodge
