The Paradox of Finite and Infinite Photon-speed.

The light was fading fast by the time they reached the landing stage. Turner left the other three waiting there while he fetched the truck and trailer. He backed the trailer down the ramp and into the water so that the boat could be manoeuvred onto it. It was a tricky operation against the current, which was now flowing quite swiftly towards the outlet of the lake, but eventually the boat was securely fixed and towed up the ramp. All four men were tired and hungry and looking forward to getting back to the lodge. Slowly, Carter drove the truck and trailer up the steep hill to the road while the others walked behind.

"Hold it? shouted Noel. "There's a wobble in one of the wheels!'

The truck came to a halt and they all gathered round the trailer to look at the offending wheel. It was definitely loose and when Turner removed the hubcap, they could see that the bearing had almost disintegrated. The engineer in Carter was outraged.

'Hell, 1 had this bloody thing serviced before we came up from Vancouver. Fine job they made of it! Now what?'

"We leave the boat here, that's what,' said Turner. "We can't take that trailer down the track with its wheel in that state.'

Carter was obviously annoyed but practicality took the upper hand.

'Let's remove the engines and put them in the back of the truck.'

'Is that really necessary?' Rick asked, surprised. "There ain't anyone around up here.'

"Well, 'm.taking no chances,' Carter replied, 'wilderness or no wilderness.'

By the time they were ready to begin their journey back to the lodge, it was completely dark. Again Rick felt the shivers up his spine and he was

thankful for the lights of the truck and the companionable warmth of the other men. Turner drove as fast as he dared, the headlights now and again revealing deer in the middle of the dirt road.

"We're already an hour later than the time we told Don we'd be back,' he said. "They'll be out searching for us.'

Sure enough, when they were within about seven or eight miles of the lodge, they saw headlights approaching. Turner signalled and came to a halt. A few minutes later they were joined by Don Frost.

"You all okay? We gave you the usual hour's leeway, but when you still didn't show, Megan became anxious.'

'Sorry, Don,' Turner was really apologetic. "We had trouble with the trailer. Had to leave it back at the lake.'

'Glad to see you all in one piece, anyway,' replied their host. "You know our rules, so we thought we'd better come and look for you. Let's get going or Megan will worry about me, as well.'

On returning to the lodge, Carter made it his business to apologise to their hostess for causing such anxiety. In this country it was essential to let someone know where you were going and what time to expect you back. It could be, quite literally, a matter of life and death. Megan scolded him a little, but kindly, then treated him to one of her lovely smiles.

She's undoubtedly one of the reasons Don has built up such a good business here, thought Carter, then aloud he said, 'You're a sweet girl, Megan.'

They were, of course, late for dinner and by the time the four men were ready to sit down to eat, the other diners had gone and they had the restaurant to themselves. They were more than ready for their food and while they were enjoying it, had little time for conversation. Then Rick, just finishing his dessert, voiced their thoughts.

"Don's chef sure gets my vote.'

The others murmured their agreement. Then, the meal over, and reluctant to go out into the chill night air, they moved nearer to the inviting warmth of the stove. Their host emerged from the room behind the restaurant.

'Can I get you anything, gentlemen? The staff have finished for the night, but I'll keep the bar open for you.'

"That's very decent of you, Don, but we've already put you to a lot of trouble,' said 'Turner.

'Not to worry,' their host replied, smiling. 'Pve had a tough day and it gives me an excuse to sit down and have a drink and some company.'

'In that case, the first round is on me,' said Turner. "What'll it be, gentlemen?'

After a few drinks and some hilarity, caused by their recalling of the day's events, they were in an expansive mood. They also recounted to Don some of the discussion they had had at the lake. Eventually, 2 propos of that, Carter said,

"Dave, something's been bothering me about these discussions we've been having.'

Turner was pleased that his friend had, at long last, begun to consider things he had steadfastly refused to think about in the past. Though the best of friends, it had become plain over the years that, intelligent, knowledgeable and physically fearless as Carter was, he was frightened, as so many others were, of delving too deeply into anything even remotely philosophical. When they were younger men, Turner had sometimes challenged Carter about this, but it had threatened to strain their friendship. Feeling instinctively that to persist might damage their relationship, which, in other respects, he much enjoyed, he had learned to avoid discourse of that more deeply contemplative kind. But what, he wondered, had brought about this recent change in Carter's attitude? It must be the company, he thought. It really was a most unusual mix, suggestive of the sorts of groups which, as Noel said, must have gathered in places like Athens, during that Golden Age of Greece, when such discourse was natural and enjoyable, and in which the ideas which led to modern civilisation were forged. The ambience of this group was doing something to Carter - to them all, in fact, himself included. It was as though the group was possessed of some strange dynamic of its own. He would have to be careful not to say or do anything to spoil it. So, as though there were nothing unusual in this new interest of Carter's, he said, 'Okay, Tony, tell us what's bothering you.'

'Well, as I see it, what you're saying, Dave - and I hadnt' really thought of it before, being an engineer, not a philosopher - is that not all the prob-

lems in understanding physics are just problems of poking and prodding things to find out what they are, like stripping-down bits of machinery into their ultimate parts to find out what makes them tick. . .'

Turner nodded. "Smashing atoms like smashing chessmen to find the rules of chess, I think I said, Tony.'

"Yeah, something like that,' Carter agreed. 'Anyway, that's what I supposed research in physics was about. But I've been thinking about that discussion we had at the cabin, about that fellow Wittgenstein and so on.'

"We more or less agreed," broke in Noel, 'that in the last analysis the real problems are problems of the correct or incorrect interpretation of direct information, problems of language, in effect.'

'Now that I find that quite fascinating,' Carter said, 'and so obvious when you think about it. Like Marian, I had never realised just how dependent on conventional language our perceptions - and conceptions - of the world must be.'

'But more than that,' he continued, 'I see now that it's not just human language, because underlying it, you might say - is that other language we spoke about, what Walter called "the universal language of nature", which is communicated between your "observers" or "communicants" on all levels, simple and complex. Am I right?'

"Yes, that's a fair summary, Tony,' Turner replied.

'So I suppose in that universal language,' Carter went on, 'objects, in themselves, are what they really are naturally, and how they appear is how we describe them or interpret them in human language. In that case, it seems to me, the trick in understanding what things are and how they interrelate with one another, not just scientifically but philosophically, is to interpret that natural language correctly into human language. Now that's a different kind of ball-game altogether from the one I'd thought of as science.

Carters mind, Turner thought, had obviously been busy. If only his friend had not avoided using these mental capacities in their younger days. How much easier, he mused sadly, this search for understanding would have been if, from the start, they had been able to co-ordinate their adventures in the philosophical sector. However, there was no hint of rancour in his voice as he replied.

"Yes, Tony, and the nearer we get to interpreting those real characters of objects correctly, as character-complexes, or phenomena, in the way we discussed, the more nearly an object as it really is in itself and as it appears in observation become one and the same. In that limit, the old problem of reality and appearance we talked about disappears.""

'I see that now, Dave,' said Carter. 'It's so easy when you shift from thinking of the substance of a thing as some sort of "matter" to thinking of it, instead, as a character-complex, like a tune - a phenomenon, as you say. It's obvious, isn't it? For instance, when I saw you, the other day, from the lodge balcony, riding up the road with Charlie, there was no difference between the you I recognised at that distance and the you I see right here.'

Turner nodded. "No more than there is any difference in a tune which you recognise when you hear it played loud or faint, nearby or at a distance, or even in your head when it's only a memory.'

'Yes, of course. So even when you aren't here but somewhere else, you're still the person - the character-complex - that I know. I mean, I don't have to know every molecule in your body in order to identify you, do I? What I know of you is, essentially, your character and personality. Even if there were doctors or scientists who knew every one of those molecules of yours individually, they might not know you as well as I do even when you're out of my sight. So in that sense, for anyone who knows you sufficiently well - like Pat, for instance - Dave's wife,' he explained to the others - 'the Dave Turner you are to them and the Dave Turner you are to yourself may be one and the same, as near as makes no difference.'

During this dialogue, the others sipped their drinks and listened, intrigued.

'In fact,' agreed Turner, 'there's good justification for saying that in some ways that other person, your Margaret, say, might know you even better than you know yourself, as Pat would undoubtedly say about me. But you started by saying there's a problem, Tony.'

'Well, what's bothering me, Dave, is this. Although it makes sense to say that an object as it is in itself, as what we've called a character-complex, and

7. This issue of the so-called 'epistemological problem', or 'problem of knowledge' in philosophy is dealt with in depth in J. L. Austin, Sense and Sensibilia, OUP 1962.

our knowledge of that object - the way it appears to us - can be one and the same, what about the space which separates us from objects like the stars and galaxies? Those objects are a long way off.'

'Ah, yes, I see,' replied Turner. "You're wondering how do we answer those who say that the light from them - bringing the information which forms those character-complexes in observation - takes a long time, years and thousands of years in some cases, to reach us.'

'Exactly! So how, they will ask, at those distances, can those charactercomplexes of things as they really are be the same as we observe, with all that distance in between. In the time which the light takes to get here, the objects may have changed so much that the way we see them is no longer the way they really are but the way they were, long ago - maybe even ceasing to exist in the meantime.'

'Yes, that occurred to me, too,' interposed Noel, 'but in a slightly different way. In my case, I was wondering how it could be said, in the context of relativity theory, that those distant bodies exist relatively to the observer - that is, in terms of their mass, length, duration, et cetera - when they are so far away, so dissociated by distance that, as you say, Tony, the information which gives us our perceptions of them, by the time it gets here, is perhaps centuries out of date. In those cases, surely, the way those objects - those character-complexes - are in themselves and the way we see them can't possibly be one and the same - which is, I think, what you're saying, Tony. So isn't there always, Dave, that old distinction between the object and its appearance? Because if that's how it is at large distances, then it's got to be the same at any distance, no matter how large or small.'

'Ah, I see what's worrying you both,' Turner said. 'You can't see how it can be claimed that what things are and the way they are in themselves are simultaneous with our perceptions of them, when it's a proven fact that the light by which we see them takes time to travel.'

"That's it, in a nutshell, Dave!' Carter replied. 'For them to be the same object, the speed of light would have to be instantaneous, and as you said to Rick, on the boat, the speed of light is not instantaneous. That's where we seem to come unstuck, with the fact that photons, which carry the information from the object to the observer, have a finite speed and can get lost en route in the space between, and so on.'

"What exactly are photons?" enquired Rick.

'A "photon", Rick, is a quantum of light-energy,' Noel informed him, 'a unit of energy, or action.'

Don Frost broke in. 'I've heard the term "photon", but I must admit that my understanding of what it means is rather vague. I thought it was a travelling light-particle, but now you say, Noel, that it's a unit of energy...'

'Let me explain, Don,' offered Noel. "The atoms of bodies interact by exchanging bits of energy, the smallest bit of which is called A, a fixed amount of energy-multiplied-by-time, or action. It's rather like a system of coinage with the bits of action being exchanged in fixed amounts.'

'""Aitch"? That's a weird name for a piece of interaction,' remarked Rick.

'I suppose it is,' replied Noel, "but it had to be called something, and that's what it was called by Max Planck - yes, another German, Rick. He was the first to discover this coinage-like character of action. It makes energy-transactions between objects impossible in amounts smaller than the value of that smallest "coin". In light interactions from the stars and objects in space,' he continued, 'those "coins" are the light-quanta called photons.'

"Thanks. I begin to see the light,' said Don, wryly.

'No, Don,' smiled Turner, 'as a matter of fact, you don't. When we look into space we see stars and whatnot, and we assume that this is because of light travelling from those things to us. But you never see that light, do you?'

Turner's companions looked at him with such varying degrees of puzzlement on their faces that he laughed. "No, I've not "flipped",' he said, 'As a matter of fact, P've studied that question in some depth. Do you want to hear it?'

'Yes, I'm intrigued, go ahead,' said Don as the others nodded agreement.

'Well then, if you'll bear with me, let me first fill in a few background details.' His audience settled back to listen as Turner continued. 'Anyone who's in any way involved with science at present can scarcely avoid noticing that there's a crisis going on in physics, though you arentt likely to read about it in the tabloids. This crisis, as I think we've discussed, is due

to a dispute between the two main areas of theoretical physics; relativity theory, on the one hand, and quantum theory on the other. This rift is caused by differing ideas about the nature of the "photon".'

"Why should they think there's a difference?' asked Rick. 'How come something so small can mean different things?'

"Well, you see, Rick,' Turner continued, "The sources of those ultimate bits of optical information, on which our knowledge of the world depends, are those particles of matter we call "atoms". In our traditional way of thinking, atoms give off those bits, or packages, of energy to travel in space at a finite speed c, supposedly the fastest speed of all.'

"That speed,' Noel informed them, 'is a hundred and eighty-six thousand miles per second - or three hundred million metres per second, as near as dammit. It's a constant which can't be exceeded,' he added, 'because at that speed a body's mass becomes infinite and so can't be accelerated any further. Some refer to it as the "light-barrier".'

"Why can't that speed of light be exceeded?' asked Rick. "Barriers are made to be busted, like the four-minute mile and the speed of sound.'

'Ah, but you see, Rick,' Noel continued, 'as you probably know, for a body to be accelerated, some energy has to be imparted to it. Think of an automobile. The faster it goes, the more gas it uses.'

"That's just right, Noel,' said Turner, resuming his argument. "But mass and energy are inter-convertible - that's to say, every kilogram of mass can be converted, if I remember rightly, into something like ninety thousand billion-billion joules of energy which, of course, is why the atomic bomb is so powerful. Now by that same token, every ninety thousand billionbillion joules of energy imparted to a body in making it travel faster is equal to a kilogram of mass. So as Noel has said, the more you try to accelerate a body the more massive it gets and the harder it becomes to accelerate it. This means that the energy spent in accelerating a body is effective only in diminishing proportions. So eventually a limit is reached at which the body cannot be accelerated any further because its mass is infinite. Do you see that, Rick?'

"Yes, I do, I guess,' Rick replied, 'in principle, that is. The numbers don't mean much to me but I can see that they're absolutely huge.'

'So you see, it follows that there has to be a speed-limit,' added Noel, 'which is in the nature of things, not something imposed by us humans.'

For a few moments, no-one said anything. Then Turner went on.

'That speed-limit, Rick, is the so-called "constant speed of light". But that isn't, strictly, really a speed.'

'Hey, come on! objected Carter. "The speed of light, c, is one of the most well-established constants in physics! So what are you saying here?'

'I'm saying, Tony, that c isn't a speed. It's an unreachable speed-limit, for those reasons Noel has explained. What kind of "speed" is it that nothing can possibly travel at? And what kind of "speed" can it be that is constant for all relatively moving observers?"

"What is it, then, Dave?' Carter asked, somewhat belligerently. Turner could see that his friend was struggling with this.

'Patience, Tony. I was coming to that. The fact, as quantum physics shows, is that those ultimate constituents of light, the "photons", can't possibly travel in the way light is supposed to travel. They are immediate, spaceless and timeless one-to-one energy transactions between object and observer, with no question of anything travelling or happening in any way between. In other words, leaving its source and arriving at its destination are, for a light-quantum (or "photon"), not two events but a single unanalysable event, regardless of the distance involved.'

'But that's ridiculous!' expostulated Carter. 'If that's so, then the evidence that light travels in space and the idea that it consists of instantaneously transferred action-quanta are fundamentally opposed.'

'Yes, it is ridiculous, Tony,' said Turner, 'and that's precisely what constitutes the crisis I was talking about. In physics, those two assumptions, that light travels in space and that it consists of instantaneous quantum-jumps are both established beyond question. That's the basic dilemma facing science, that the particles of light, the photons, simply do not and cannot travel in the way light is supposed to travel. From what Noel has explained, those photons cannot travel at the speed c, because to do so, their mass would have to be infinite, To sunbathe would be to be flattened out of existence.'

"Wow, this is some crisis,' said Don.

Turner continued. "Yes, Don. This is what we discussed on the lake earlier. As Noel pointed out, it's a well-known paradox in physics. And you can see that since this dilemma occurs right at the root of modern theoretical physics, it's not surprising, is it, that all kinds of other, seemingly insoluble problems arise at other levels. How, for instance, are gravitational influences transacted between bodies? Do they travel instantly or at the speed of light? Are they quantised in the same way that light is? Those are the sorts of questions science is asking. But how can we hope to answer these questions when our ideas of light and physical separation are in such a mess?"

Don got up and went to fill their glasses, while what Turner had explained sank in. When their host returned, Carter spoke.

'So, what's your answer, then, Dave,' he said, "because I can see by your smug look that you've got one. You needn't hold out just for dramatic effect.'

"Well, as a matter of fact, I do have some ideas,' Turner replied, with a grin.

'I knew it,' said Carter, with mock resignation. 'Okay then, Dave, let's hear it.'

"Very kind of you, Tony,' Turner said, smiling at the banter. 'My suggestion, then, for what it's worth, is this, that the speed of light is both finite and infinite - that it is both instantaneous and travels at speed c.'

'Hey, that's a great solution, Dave!' said Carter, ironically.

'But how can that be?' enquired Noel. 'How can the speed of light possibly be doth finite and infinite? That's a contradiction, surely!'

Well,' Turner went on, 'we've talked about evidence for the speed of light. And we've also spoken about science being the interpretation of such evidence. What that alleged evidence of light-speed actually shows, in its absolutely minimum interpretation, is not that "light travels in space" but that it is the space - and the time - that are in the light.'

'Eh? said Don, speaking for them all, 'I think you'd better explain that,

Dave.'

"Let me ask you this, then,' Turner responded. 'Suppose I set up a circuit with an electric lamp and a time-switch which makes the lamp flash on and off in quick succession. Could that sequence of flashes at that same spot properly be called a motion?'

'No way,' replied Carter, 'obviously not!'

'And could it any more properly be called a motion if that same sequence of flashes were spread out in a line?'

'No, although it may create an impression of motion,' Carter admitted.

'So,' Turner summarised, 'we can agree that a sequence of separate observational events doesn't necessarily signify motion but that it can be made to give an impression of motion, as you say, Tony. As we all know, this phenomenon has been exploited by cinematography, advertising and television. Movies and video cartoons are a good example.'

'Yes, I'm sure no-one would quarrel with that,' said Carter, "but where's all this leading?'

'What it's leading to is this,' resumed Turner. 'In what way does real motion, as traditional physics presents it, differ from that "impression" of motion we've just described? The answer, I suggest - and this is borne out by all the evidence of quantum physics - is that ultimately there's no difference whatsoever, that all motion is-fundamentally of the same sequential or cinematographic kind. The quanta, or 'photons', as they are usually called - or, rather, miscalled - which are the basis of this phenomenon, don't move. They are not the sorts of things that can move. They're the ultimate "quantum-stills", as it were, out of which all distance and motion are "cinematographically" produced in observation, where the observer is the "film-projector" or "information-processor".'

'But,' queried Noel, 'how does that explain those time-intervals which photons take in crossing space? Experiment shows that the time between a photon leaving its source and arriving at its destination is proportional to the distance divided by the speed c. So how can the photon-contact be instantaneous?"

'Easily!' Turner replied. "You know your relativity, don't you?'

'Reasonably well,' Noel answered.

'Tell me, then. According to relativity, what happens to the time-standard of anything which travels relatively to an observer at the speed of light?"

'It becomes infinitely dilated - infinitely stretched - relatively to the observer.'

'And how fast, according to relativity, does a photon travel?"

'At the speed of light, speed c.'

'So how long is the lifetime of a photon relative to an observer?'

'infinitely long.'

"How long, then, does it take a photon, in its own infinitely long lifetime, to travel any finite distance marked out by the observer?'

'No time at all,' Noel promptly replied, 'instantly.' Then, after a pause, as the realisation began to dawn on him, "Great Scott! I see what you mean, Dave. The photon, relatively to itself - in its own time, as it were - and no matter what the distance, is nothing but an instantaneous and immediate quantum-jump. So the "speed c", in physical experiments, is not the speed of the "photon" - which is just an observational quantum-blip - but the projected speed, the rate of quantum-sequences. Now I think I understand!"

"You see my point, then,' said Turner, 'that ac the quantum level, the level of the so-called "photons", there can be no motion, as we normally understand it, any more than there is motion in the pixels on a video screen. The motion is projected by the viewer out of the patterns made by those pixels. It's only at higher levels, in these patterns and sequences of the instantaneous quantum blips that there can be motion. That is to say, motion, basically, is never continuous but is always "cinematographic" in the way I described, made up of little point-like quantum "stills". It only causes confusion to think of these stills - these "photons" - as moving in the way bodies move; and that, I would say, is the sort of confusion which modern science generates. It is the underlying reason, I claim, why modern science is in its present state of crisis.'

After a short, expectant silence, Turner continued.

'So you see how right you were, Tony, to drop the idea that "matter and space" are fundamental to scientific research. You can see that this research should be trying to find the correct interpretation of information - in effect, a study of Walter's "language of nature". At that fundamental level there's no physics as such, no space nor time nor materiality, no "speed of

light", nothing.'

'So what I understand you to be saying,' put in Noel, 'is that at the lowest level there's only the bare quantum-information, which has to be put together by the observer - that is, interpreted in some way or other, before physics can even begin.'

'Yes, that's it," replied Turner.

'You could say, I suppose, Dave,' Carter said thoughtfully, 'that when we talk about "the speed of light in space" and the "time that light takes" to get from where it sets out to where it ends-up, is like reading about motion in a story, where the words and letters used in describing that motion are not, themselves, involved in the motion they describe.'

'You've hit it right on the head, Tony,' Turner approved. 'So you see that there doesn't have to be any division or difference, at the quantum level, between what a thing really is and how it appears in true and correct observation. That observation must correctly interpret the information about that object's essential character. And, of course, there has to be some observer (complex or rudimentary, as we've said) to read or process it and then project the information, using some or all of our five senses. And this, to my mind, makes "observation", in its widest sense, implicit in every account of physical phenomena and restores the only sensible meaning that can be given to "relativity". '

'Ah, I get it,' said Noel. 'So that's why you think relativity and the quantum are by no means antagonistic to each other.'

Turner nodded. 'Yes, they are as logically and intimately related, to use Tony's analogy, as the reading and writing of a script. Without that interrelation the script obviously can't convey any message. In the same way, without an observer - of some sort or another - "the universe" is a text unread, and a text unread is no text at all.'

This was followed by a deep contemplative pause. Turner's four listeners felt their minds being turned upside-down in their efforts to follow the implications of all he had said.

Finally Rick spoke. 'Gee, Dave, if you're right, then physics, as we usually think of it, is all up the creek!'

'No, Rick, I'm not saying that. Physics is fine - practical physics, that is, on the level where it belongs - the level of the scenario, in our terms, rather

than of the "screen" and the quantum pixels. Our present Arts/Science system of educational apartheid has created an artificial division in our studies of nature that doesn't exist in nature itself.'

'I guess that's the trouble,' remarked Noel, 'because it makes our institutions of Physics and Philosophy more or less complete strangers to each other.'

'Too true, Noel,' said Turner ruefully. 'Physicists therefore seldom realise that their supposed "stand alone" subject is really a form of philosophy, and is as prone to review as any other philosophy. Once, these disciplines worked together, under the heading of Natural Philosophy, but not any more. In many universities that title has now been officially withdrawn, as an archaism, no less.'

'Which,' mused Noel, 'is why you find atomic physicists, as you say, Dave, still dismantling chessmen to find the rules of chess. And think,' he added, 'of all the money and status that's invested in that vain pursuit. Socrates had to drink the hemlock for upsetting the establishment. I wonder what they'd do to modern heretics like you, Dave, if they got wind of what you're saying?"

'Treat them as harmless lunatics and ignore them, no doubt,' answered Carter.

Don Frost stood up, and stretched. 'Well, Dave,' he said, 'maybe just sowing a seed here and there is the best way to do it. I'm sure we'll all give it some thought. Tackling the scientific establishment head-on is no small thing and I think you're wise not to rack yourself trying. I wouldn't want you to get tortured by the establishment. But if you do,' he added with a grin, 'don't let on that you know me! It would be bad for business.'

'Tm touched,' smiled Turner.

'That's what I've always maintained!' retorted Carter, genially, as they all made ready to leave.

Chapter 6: Clearwater Valley

Indeterminacy, the Font of Communication

Indeterminacy: the Font of Communication

'Hi,' called Marian Taylor, as Carter and Turner entered the restaurant next morning. 'I thought we were all meeting for dinner last night. What happened?"

Carter explained briefly and the women made sympathetic noises.

'So you'll have to fix the trailer wheel today,' said Helen. 'What a shame! It's such a beautiful day, too.'

'It could be worse, it could have been raining! said Turner, cheerfully. "We'll have to go into Clearwater this morning to pick up the part, and then drive up to the lake again. Shouldn't be a long job, though.'

'Ah, here comes Noel,' said Carter. 'He's looking bright-eyed and bushytailed this morning.'

Noel brought his breakfast across and joined them. 'Good morning, ladies, gentlemen. What a marvellous day. Any special plans, anyone?'

'Fixing the trailer,' answered Carter.

'Of course, I should have remembered that,' Noel said. 'Can I be of any help?'

'Thanks,' replied Carter, 'but it's not a big job. Dave and I will manage it easily enough.'

Noel turned to the Taylor sisters. 'And what do you two plan for today, if you don't mind my asking?'

'Same as yesterday for Helen and me,' said Marian, 'only today we're going up to Bailey's Chute. We heard that there are still salmon jumping up there. It would make a wonderful picture.'

'May I join you, then?' asked Noel. The sisters looked a little surprised at his boldness. Then Marian answered quickly, before Helen could say

anything.

'But of course you may. We'll be glad of your company, won't we, Helen?'

Helen Taylor did not reply immediately, but then she asked, rather abruptly, 'Do you fish, Noel?'

Remembering yesterday's fishing fiasco, Noel answered, somewhat ruefully, "Yes, Helen. Why do you ask?'

'I hope you don't intend trying to catch any salmon while you're with us, that's all.'

'My dear Helen,' Noel spoke with exaggerated patience, 'when the salmon get as far upstream as Bailey's Chute, they are inedible through exhaustion and depletion. They're scarcely fit prey even for bears and osprey. I wouldn't dream of fishing there.'

'Good,' said Helen, shortly. 'Are you coming in our automobile or will you take your own?'

'If you're taking all the gear you had when you went off yesterday, I think Tl take my own,' replied Noel. 'I wonder what Rick plans to do. He might like to come along with us.'

'Good idea,' said Marian, as she rose from the table. "We'll meet you there. We're taking picnic pails. I'll tell Mrs. Frost that we might need two more.'

'That'll be fine, Marian. I'll confirm it with her when I've seen Rick.'

With that, the sisters left the men at the table and went out.

Carter cleared his throat. 'Are you sure you want to join them, Noel? Helen didn't seem too keen on the idea.'

'That wasn't lost on me, Tony,' said Noel, 'but, as you know, Bailey's Chute is deeper into the bush than Helmcken Falls and I don't think it's very wise for two women to go so far on their own. If Td said so, though, Helen would have gotten on her high horse.'

'Thar's a very admirable concern, Noel,' said Turner, studiously ignoring the incipient grin on Carter's face. "Tony and I could take some lessons from you on how to handle the modern woman. Hope you have a good day!'

Noel laughed. "Thanks, I hope so too!'

Breakfast over, the older men left to collect the truck and Noel called at Rick's camper.

'Sure, I think it's a swell idea,' he said, when Noel outlined his plans. 'I think Marian's cute - not so sure about Helen. She's a looker, for sure, but just a mite intimidating.'

'Helen is a very interesting woman,' Noel protested. 'She's not only good-looking but also intelligent and well-educated, an exciting combination, for me.'

'Sure,' replied Rick, a little dubiously. 'Like I say, she's a bit hi-falutir' for me. But, everyone to his own taste, I guess . . . Okay, I'm ready. Let's go.'

As the two men drove off, they saw Carter's truck, with Turner at the wheel, going in the opposite direction towards Clearwater. To their amusement, the Lodge's two large husky dogs were sitting in the back.

'D'ya think Tony and Dave know they've got stowaways?' laughed Rick.

They certainly did. As the truck pulled up near the main door of the Lodge to let Don know where they were going, Smokey and Blue, sunning themselves on the deck outside, had stood up to greet their old friends and, not least, the truck in which they had so often cadged rides. While the humans were talking, the dogs had, as usual, surreptitiously jumped into the back of the truck and refused to budge.

'Alright, dogs, you've made your point,' Turner had laughed. "You can come. But only so far as Clearwater and back, not to the lake.'

'You spoil them rotten,' said Don, smiling. And off they had driven.

Looking behind, Carter could see the dogs in the back of the truck, snouts uplifted savouring the flow of the air as they went along.

'T wonder,' he mused, 'what those dogs make of the world. You can tell, from the way they brace themselves and lean into the corners, that they obviously see the road ahead as well as we do. It's almost as though they were driving, because they so expertly anticipate every bend and bump.'

'And they obviously communicate,' said Turner. "The fact that they're here with us shows that.'

'I wonder how they do see things, though.' Carter went on. "The range of scents and sounds they respond to is so much greater than ours, but on the other hand, the range of colours they see is nothing like the range we

see, so what does the world seem like to them? How real or unreal is it compared with the way we see it..."

'... and how real or unreal would they judge our world to be if they could view it as we do?' Turner finished for him. 'And what about insects? Take bees, for instance. They can see whole ranges of colours to which we are blind - or at least, which we can see only when we have instruments that can translate them into our colour range.'

'I saw a T'V programme, once,' said Carter, 'which demonstrated flowers that had been photographed in ultra-violet light. It showed how some flowers which, to us, are very plain and ordinary, are highly patterned to a bee's eyes. Those patterns, it seems, are a kind of colour-language by which the plant provides landing-strips for the bees, with instructions for them to get to the nectar - and which best sets them up to transfer the pollen. This, surely, could be called communication, in the wide sense we've been discussing.'

'That's right,' Turner replied, adding, 'And then there's the bee dance, by which the bees communicate to each other when they get back to the hive, to show the other bees where to go to get the nectar. It's amazing, when you think about it. It just goes to show, doesn't it, how small and restricted a portion of the whole range of natural communication our human language takes. And in the very last analysis, as we've discussed, in its fullest possible range, everything communicates with everything else, right down to atoms - or "observicles", as Walter named them.'

'So it és all communication,' Carter mused.

'In the very broadest sense of the word,' Turner agreed.

For a while, they drove along in silence. Then Carter voiced his thoughts.

'The problem remains, then,' he said, 'of where, in all this sheer relativity and subjectivity of things - of the different communication-systems, or languages - is the real world, the world which is what it is in itself, independently of the way we, or other animals, plants and things, view it - that is, if it's accepted that there zs such a reality, which you have to do for sanity's sake, as we agreed.'

'Yes,' said Turner, 'as I said in our talk on the lake, that's a very old, very basic philosophical problem, the search for true objectivity, for the reality

underlying the appearances. It's been the preoccupation of philosophers since time immemorial.'

'I mean,' Carter went on, 'I accept what you said last night about there being no space-separation nor time-separation at the quantum level of observation, between the sources and receivers of the bits of information into which what we called a character-complex ultimately reduces . . .'

'Yes,' interposed Turner, 'the bits of information which make up the thing itself and the quantum bits which make up our knowledge of that thing are one and the same - although there are fewer of these bits per observer when shared out among a number of observers than there are provided by the source. This, incidentally, is what I would say gives that typical diminishing of the size and intensity of objects which we interpret as distance. It is simply the sharing-out of the energy in an expanding sphere of distribution.'

'I can understand that,' said Carter, 'and, as you say, it's like a tune, which is the same tune whether heard loud and near or faint and far away. But, granted that the object as it is in itself and as it is observed are, in this sense, the same object, even when there is only a minimum amount of information, and only when that information is correctly interpreted, it is still difficult to think of these objects as consisting of no more than just those appearances.'

'But why?'

'Well,' Carter replied, 'from what you seem to be saying, there is, in effect, no underlying or substantial reality to be considered other than in those appearances themselves. Do you really mean to say, Dave, that when all those relative qualities like the colours of things, all their definitive properties like mass, solidity and whatnot are accounted for - as bits of pure observation, that is - nothing whatever remains? You can't, surely, be saying this, that apart from the way objects "appear" to one another, or interrelate as character-complexes, they have no substantial existence, that their existence reduces to nothing but appearances or phenomena, as you call them?'

Both men held on hard as the truck lurched over a particularly jarring bit of the dirt road and skittered across the loose gravel. The dogs flattened themselves down on the floor of the truck as they slid to one side.

'Hey, Dave, where do you think you are, Brands Hatch?' yelled Carter. But-Turner, a one-time amateur racing driver, only laughed as, in order to correct the skid he spun the wheel expertly onto opposite lock to maintain control

'Sorry, fellas,' he called to the dogs through the open window, but their

faces were as inscrutable as Sphinxes.

As the road gave way to some heavier, muddy gravel, the truck slowed almost to a halt.

'Keep her out of overdrive,' shouted Carter. 'It's sticking, and if it stays on we wont hear ourselves think!

As the vehicle crabbed sideways, revving, Turner said something which Carter, holding on hard to the door-handle, did not catch.

'What did you say?" Carter asked, when the road became relatively smooth and their course steadied.

't SAID "YES, THAT'S WHAT PM SAYING",' shouted Turner, and then more quietly, 'I was answering your question.'

"What question?' said Carter. ;

'That things have no substantiality other than in their appearances to one another.'

'Oh, that? ... Well, if that's what you claim, then doesn't it reduce everything, once again, to that dream world, as Rick described it? This problem keeps appearing.'

'No, Tony, I've not been saying that appearances are without substance. What I'm saying is different. It is that the substantial properties and qualities of a thing, like its mass, solidity and other definitive physical attributes do not underlie the appearances but are all part and parcel of the appearances.'

'And you say that those properties cannot exist other than in an observational framework?'

"Yes, Tony, but when you use the word "exist" here, remember what we discussed about that word. We must bear in mind that "existence", according to that analysis, is not some sub-observational, sub-lingual, metaphysical state underlying the phenomena but is no more nor less than a function of

language. We must also bear in mind what we agreed, that there is not just one, single observational framework which we may identify as oneself. Any observation involves untold billions of "observers", or communicants - "observicles" as we are now calling them. Damn! he said, 'I wish there was some more suitable word. In fact, there as many "communicants" as there are distinguishable objects, animal, vegetable, mineral or whatever. So the "observational framework" you are talking about is that whole communicational network, or world, consisting of all those myriads of observers and "observicles". Without them, there can be no "self", nor anything else, no "world" or "universe" to even think of - though, undoubtedly, each point of focus is a "self" of some kind, complex or rudimentary, as we've been saying.'

After a pause to digest what his friend had said, Carter replied, 'I think I'm beginning to see what you meant when you said that nothing exists absolutely and isolatedly in itself, but that things can exist only in their interrelations with one another. And there's something else,' he continued, 'that we must also bear in mind.'

"What's that, Tony?'

"Well, Dave, we did say, didn't we, that these quantum bits, or "pixels" which are traded between the atoms which make up bodies, are as physical as they are informational.'

"That's right, They are as much concrete as abstract according to the traditional category distinction system.'

'As I now understand it,' Carter went on, 'for objects to be seen or detected their atoms have to transact energy with atoms in other bodies, like the eye or a camera, say, or even in a rudimentary way, with the atoms of walls and fence-posts. And since energy is mass. . .'

'In the ratio of units, c-squared,' put in Turner.

'Yes, I know that,' replied Carter, slightly impatiently, 'so in being seen or detected, a body must lose bits of itself, bits of mass-energy. This goes along with what you said about the informational bits from which the world is projected being as physical as informational.'

'Precisely!' responded Turner, changing gear as they approached a steep downgrade. A few years previously, a truck full of cattle had run away on

this hill. Most of the cattle were killed, while the remainder escaped into the bush - or so it was said.

'It seems strange, though,' Carter continued, thoughtfully, 'that this makes it impossible to think of seeing or detecting anything without any exchange of energy. Yet it all seems logically consistent and certainly gives point to the meaning of relativity.'

"Yes.' Turner was pleased that he was getting through to his old friend, who had always been a hard man to convince. 'Objectivity,' he continued, 'lies in a whole community of perspectives, an informational consensus, you might say. This does away with the old idea of an absolute "material substance" underlying the appearances, ticking over metaphysically, as it were, behind the scenes, and brings everything within the compass of observation. It makes observation - in this broadest, intercommunicational, sense, however inept that word might be - an implicit and ever-present factor of physical reality.'

Carter had always felt that Turner was an "idealist", with his feet planted firmly in mid air, but he was now beginning to revise his opinion and he had to admit that he was finding his old friend's ideas intriguing - although, at the same time, in some ways disturbing. Perhaps it was because they were getting older and wiser, he thought, and finished with their years of youthful "antler-clashing".

'Yes, Dave,' he said. 'I think I can see that your kind of relativism is very radical. It gets rid of the old absolute division between substance and appearance and puts them together - makes them coextensive, you might say.'

'Not quite coextensive, Tony, because there is still the fact of illusion to consider.'

Carter found this puzzling, and said so.

"Why's that?' responded Turner. 'In view of what we've just been saying, you can surely see that illusion and delusion - and hallucination - are the result of either insufficiency or misinterpretation of information - or both, of course . . .' Suddenly, Turner's voice dropped. "Hey, look at that fella!'

There, ahead of them, on the edge of the forest, not far from the road, was a bear ambling along unconcernedly, so black that it looked like a hole in the scenery.

"He's lucky,' said Carter, 'they're fair game at the moment, I hear. If Noel was here with his gun, that bear would be a goner! Slow down, Dave and let me get a photo of him for Margaret.' He reached for his camera, then exclaimed, 'Oh - oh.'

With the slowing of the truck, the dogs, sighting the bear, started 'yodelling' at the tops of their voices.

'Damn? said Turner, 'I'd forgotten about them.' So instead. of slowing down as he had intended, he had to speed up. 'I hope those idiot dogs have the sense not to jump out at this speed!' he breathed, clutching hard at the wheel.

But Smokey, the male, was now too old and arthritic to contemplate jumping from the truck, and as they lurched by, the 'hole in the scenery' closed up.

"Where the hell did he go?' said Carter, looking back. "There's no sign of

him now! All Pll have to show Margaret is a photo of a tree!'

They were now approaching Clearwater. Turning right onto Highway Five, the Yellowhead, as it was called, they followed it for a couple of miles, then again turned right towards what was sign-posted as the Business Route. Anyone expecting this to be the centre of a grand metropolis would have been disappointed. There was a laundromat, a liquor store, a mini "supermarket", a couple of banks, a drugstore, a cafe, an electrical goods shop, a clothes shop and a gas station, In other words, it was a typical small country town, except that its boundaries enclosed an area about the size of an English city.

The garage they were making for was on the side of the road opposite to the shops. Don had telephoned earlier to order the part they needed which, fortunately, was in stock. (In that country, one did not 'just pop out' to buy something without first checking that it was available.) Turner went in to pick up the wheel bearing. A few minutes later, he returned to the truck with a big grin on his face.

"What's tickling you?' Carter asked,

'I was chatting to one of the fellows in there and when he realised from my accent that I wasn't a local, he asked me where I came from. I said "Great Britain". "Where's that?" he asked, "Somewhere near England?"'

'Serves you right, Dave. Welsh blood or no, you and the other ancient Britons will have to accept that to most non-Brits, there is no longer the Britain you and I call Great. It's "England", or the "UK". You should have said you were from Wales. That would really have confused him! It would be like telling someone in China that you come from Clearwater!' Then he added wickedly, "Clearwater and Wales are about the same size, anyway.'

'Drop dead, mochyn Sais!'® retorted Turner, genially, remembering an explosive Welsh phrase of his grandmother's.

They stopped for coffee at the café nearby, during which time they discussed the state of the timber industry and the rapidly increasing price of land in the area. They had noticed the number of 'For Sale' signs burgeoning on the land bordering the road through the Upper Clearwater Valley and they shared a pessimistic prognosis for the future purity of the wilderness.

"You know, Dave,' said Carter rather gloomily, 'I imagine that Saxon England must have been something like this a thousand or so years ago, shacks in forest clearings, townships far apart, bears, wolves, clear skies and all. I wonder how long it will be before all this area gets just as overcrowded as England is now.'

'Not long, perhaps. But cheer up, Tony, it's not likely to happen overnight. As for myself, a place like this is great for a short while but I'm always quite happy to go back home where, in places like Wales and Scotland, there's still a lot of open country to move around in, despite what you regard as our poor old overcrowded UK. There, you can at least travel more or less in all directions instead of having your movements blocked by huge areas of impenetrable forest - unless you're a backpacker with a chainsaw, of course. I mean to say, what's the point about talking about how big Canada is, compared to the UK, when so much of it is virtually inaccessible? Our trip here from the Lodge, for instance, was, for the most part, just a narrow corridor through the brush, with great areas closed to us in between.'

8. Mochyn Sais means 'English pig'.

On the way back to the Wilderness Park, it was Carter who drove - for the safety of the animals and pedestrians, as he had remarked. For a while, the two men rode along in companionable silence. Then Carter spoke, returning to their earlier discussion.

'Let's get back to that problem of reality and illusion, Dave. You wanted to know what's still puzzling me. Its still the question of how do we know what's real and what's illusory among all the myriads of different perspectives and interpretations.'

'Well, what I'm saying, Tony, is that what is real is that which has substance to it, what else?' his friend replied. "But, as I keep saying, this is not the old metaphysical "substance" which we conventionally think of as lying behind the scenes. I know it's perhaps difficult to absorb.'

"Youre darn right,' responded Carter, 'it's a// so difficult to take in and keep it there. So, remind me again, Dave, what sort of "substance" do you mean?'

'I mean substance in the plain, ordinary-language meaning of what can be sensed by the coordination of our five senses - that character-complex, as we've called it, which is visible and tangible, solid as opposed to being an illusion, a mirage or hallucination and so on.'

To this, Carter replied,' 'Ah, yes, the problem of "illusion" you mentioned back there. But, surely, there are as many "substances", in that sense, as there are observers! Which, among all these projected "substances", illusory, delusory or whatever, is the real, the true substance of a thing. Forgive me, Dave, if I'm a bit dense, but if you want me to understand this -as, indeed, I want to myself- you might have to repeat' things, more than just once.'

'Okay, Tony,' replied Turner sympathetically. It was too easy to forget how long it had taken himself for these things to sink in. "Well, at least we know what a substance is not,' he said. 'It's not something ticking over metaphysically in the dark, in some cosmical "void", behind and underlying all the appearances of things, as in classical science.'

'Right, I've gathered that much,' said Carter, cursing as he pushed Blue's wet nose away from the gear lever. At the garage, the dogs had infiltrated

9. See J. L. Austin, op. cit.

the cab and had now commandeered the bucket-seats where, uncomprehendingly, they enjoyed just the sound of the men's voices. 'So tell me again,' Carter insisted, 'what is the real substance of a thing?'

'My answer,' said Turner, 'is to remind you of what you agreed at the restaurant, last night, that the substance of a thing is not what you get by stripping it down to its ultimate material parts - molecules, atoms and so on which, in our ordinary language sense, are not really substances, but transient events - flicking in and out of existence in quantum jumps. It is, as I think we agreed, a product of the correct, objective interpretation of information, of the universal language of nature. A "different scientific ball-game", I think we called it.' ,

'Yes, of course, I'd forgotten. As I say, it's so difficult to keep all this in your noddle.' Carter paused for a while as he drove, getting his thoughts together. Then he continued: 'So the true substance of a thing is now nothing more nor less than what we say the thing is by the correct and proper use of language. But now I'm wondering. Doesn't that mean that what a thing is, substantially and in itself, is nothing but what I say it is or you say it is - a product of language?'

'Provided we agree, and provided everyone and everything else agrees - that is, provided all this agreement is supported by the fullest possible evidence - yes. Because, after all, what else can we mean when we say that something is, or exists, but that we are correctly applying to it the present tense of the verb "to be", the word "is"?'

'Eh? expostulated Carter, and swerved to avoid something small that zipped across the road.

'I get it, though,' he said. "You're talking Linguistic Philosophy, here, Dave, Wittgenstein and all that, as we discussed at the cabin - with Helen and Walter, as I recall. That kind of stuff is tough, though, for a hairy engineer like me.'

'Don't knock it, Tony,' his friend replied. 'If only physicists and engineers knew just something about this there'd be some hope for a resurgence of natural philosophy.'

'Okay, so what's your point?'

"Well, suppose, for instance, I ask you what "cryogenics" is. What would you say?"

'I would say that it is the study of physical processes at low temperatures, approaching zero degrees-Kelvin,' Carter replied.

"Well, there you are, then. Note the recurrence of the word "is" in that statement. What you said, in effect, is that X is Y, where X is the subject of the statement, such as "cryogenics" and Y is the set of predicates, the words used to describe X, such as "the study of physical processes at low temperatures" and so on. The "is", here, is the copula, or the joining bit between the subject X (e.g., "cryogenics") and the predicate-system Y ("the study of low temperatures" and so on).'

Carter's interest in this discussion was flagging. 'This,' he grumbled, 'begins to sound less and less like science and more and more like a lesson in grammar, which I hated at school. As you may remember, this was why we became so skilled in truancy.'

"Yes, I well remember,' Turner responded, 'how we hated that at school and why, after we'd answered the register, as soon as the teacher's back was turned, we left through those mullioned windows and "legged it" into the countryside - until our parents found out! But bear with me, Tony. Perhaps it will help if 1 put it this way. What we are talking about, here, is not English grammar, which is very peculiar and involved, but /ogical grammar, the grammar of Nature, you might say, in which what a thing zs is the subject and whatever we say about it, by description or definition, is the predicate. Now I ask you, can you think of anything which is, or has existence, or "being", without saying or thinking what it is - that is, without saying (or predicating) something about it?'

"Well, yes, of course, Dave. J can think of something purely imaginary or fictional, which has no real existence whatsoever.'

'But those phrases, "purely fictional", "purely imaginary" and so on, are predications, arent they?"

Carter thought for a moment, the only sound being that of crunching gravel under the wheels and the only view being that of the dense forest passing by them, monotonously, on both sides.

'Ah yes,' he said, eventually 'I think I see your point . . . It's the same kind of conundrum as the problem of what's reflected in the mirror when no-one's looking at it, except that you're now extending that same argu-

ment to language. You're saying that in the same way that you can't think of what's reflected in a mirror in an empty room without, in your imagination, putting yourself in it, somewhere, you can't think of, nor even talk about something existing without using those words that are used to describe or define it.'

'In effect, yes, Tony. What I'm saying is that it's impossible to think, or even speculate, about anything existing, without implicitly employing language, without defining some "X" which, in your mind, is the subject of certain predications, X, Y, Z, W ... or whatever, and then linking these to the subject, X, via the copula "zs". In Linguistic terminology, that "X" is the substance you are looking for - so long as it remains an objective and substantial, unanimously agreed, item of reference.'

'Okay, Dave, now I do see what you mean. Quite obviously, it's pure nonsense to speak or even think of things existing which nothing can ever be said about. It reminds me of that daft comic song about the "Nonexistent Spaceman", whose only claim to existence was that he didn't exist.'

'Turner laughed. "Yes, I remember it well,' he said. 'And that makes my point, that even in saying that something "doesn't exist", or that it "isn't real", that it is "fictitious", "comic" or whatever, we are employing predications - in short, language. And that's the highly misunderstood sense in which Wittgenstein claimed that "everything is language", that it makes no sense at all to describe, or even think about, something "existing" without using language, This is so obvious that we aren't normally aware of it, any more than fish are aware of water.'

Carter's long-trained driving skills were unaffected by this contemplative discussion. 'Okay,' he said, 'so a thing is - has being or existence - you say, only when all the different observers, or whatever we might call them, concur on how to describe it, and in a commonly understandable language!'

'Yes, in some common language - or at least where, among all the different languages, including the language of science - there is some common nucleus of agreement about the evidence for it.'

'I think I see,' said Carter. 'So there's no escaping the fact that it is language which, in one way or another, confers existence, or substance.'

"Yes. Tony. This, it seems, is basically what Wittgenstein was saying. I mean, what else but language has that kind of capacity -- to relate one thing or item of data to another, to substantiate the existence of something and say what it is to the satisfaction of anyone who is az fait with the language used?"

Carter's credulity was going up and down like a monkey on a stick. 'But I still don't understand," he said. 'Does the mere act, then, of defining or describing something make it exist, creating it, as it were?"

'In a definite sense, yes,' his friend replied. "Lewis Carroll spoke about "slithy toves" which "did gyre and gimble in the wabe." By describing them in that way, he made those creatures exist - in the special category of fiction, that is, not of reality. And that's the qualifying predicate whose presence is implicitly understood by Carroll's readers in order that the words can have the kind of impact they normally have; that it is in the special category of fiction.'

Carter was still sceptical. "But what about in the actual world? What about those things which exist only in terms of partial knowledge and which when known more fully turn out to be different entirely from what we supposed them to be? - And I don't mean illusions or hallucinations, which can always be shown to be false on careful inspection.'

'Of course,' Turner agreed. 'A notorious case of what I think you're talking about is that of the whale, which Jonah, in the Bible, described as a "fish", and which, for centuries, was thought to be a fish, but which turned out in the end to be a mammal. But that only proves that we can't just arbitrarily decide what a thing is - except in pure artifice, of course. Otherwise, what something is, in the nature of things beyond our traditional language, is something we have to find out, logically and scientifically. And not even in our best uses of logic and science can we ever decide for ourselves what a thing is, other than provisionally, of course, and by continual revision in the light of advancing experience.'

'So you're really saying,' mused Carter, 'that what a thing is, objectively, in and of itself, whether known or unknown to us, is no more than what

Charlies farm The Chalet.

The River. our language says it is. He cursed Smokey, as the huge husky, unused to the heated cab, dribbled down the back of his neck.

'All P''m saying, when it comes right down to it,' said Turner, 'is that nothing exists absolutely in itself, but only relatively to other things, and that the interrelations involved are those, essentially, of a language - not human language, necessarily, but the much wider "language of nature".'

'But that still seems to me a hell of a long way off from what goes under the name of Science,' ventured Carter. And then, after wrestling with a particularly difficult bit of the steepening track, 'I doubt whether any cardcarrying scientist would accept that his work was about language!'

"Well, think about it, Tony. Okay, so it sounds like pure Arts - as you say, like a lesson in grammar. But isn't that what science is really doing, studying the grammar and lexicography, not of human language, of course, but of nature, the language whose ultimate elements are not the sounds or letters of our traditional alphabet but bits of quantum-information transacted between all things, in the way we discussed at the lake?'

Again, there was a short spell of silence, Then, after circumnavigating a fallen tree, Carter sighed and said:

'Lord, this is heavy stuff, Dave! I know it's what we've agreed in our previous discussions - like at the lake, as you say - and I somehow feel, at the back of my mind, that you're right. But it's awfully difficult to keep it all together in one's head and get it to gel. It's alright for you, you've obviously thought this thing through in depth. When did you do that, Dave?"

'It's been happening over a very long time,' his friend replied, 'but you never wanted to know, Tony. Not that I blame you, because J must have been difficult to talk to while I was going through the most tortuous phases. And then, of course, you emigrated.'

"Yes, you were an awkward devil at times,' Carter said ruefully, 'and we lost touch for a while, didn't we, until you made that business trip to Vancouver and we met up again. But how did these ideas of yours get started? What triggered it all off?'

"Well, you know how I once became caught-up in relativity theory?"

"Yes, you explained that to us the other evening at the restaurant.'

'It was from there that I was led to studying perception-theory,' said Turner, 'and from there to philosophy.'

'So that's why you got so involved in philosophy. I never could understand why.'

'Perhaps now you do, Tony. Anyway, my first brush with relativity

theory was in a lecture on astronomy. A lot of it went over my head, but at least it made me realise that something I had unquestioningly accepted was wrong. I had somehow assumed, without thinking, that time is the same everywhere. So if it was twelve o'clock GMT here, it was twelve o'clock GMT throughout the whole universe of stars and galaxies, as well as in space itself. That lecture made me think about it for the first time. So I asked myself, whatever justification had there been for that assumption. What is time, anyway? The answer I came up with is that time is simply what is registered by clocks. And everything, I decided, is a clock of some kind. And the ultimate clock, for me, was the spectrum whose frequency is so characteristic of a body, be it a star or a bit of metal held in the flame of a Bunsen burner.' 'This,' he went on, 'gave me my insight in to what Relativity is all about. Then someone said that my ideas in that direction were reminiscent of George Berkeley. I didn't know who this bloke played for but I went to the library and looked him up. I was intrigued by his merging of physics with perception, like Einstein's merging of physics with observation. To cut a long story short, this led me on to those critical followers of Berkeley, the Linguistic philosophers, like Austin and Ryle - and Wittgenstein, the guy Helen and Walter were talking about at the cabin. I didn't take to that kind of "linguistic" stuff at first, but some of it eventually rubbed off on me. Those fellows used to argue that "the world is language and language is the world". As an engineer, like you, I thought that absurd until I learned about Claude Shannon.'

"You mean the communications-theory bloke,' interposed Carter, 'the one who, you told us, extended the meaning of "language" in the way we talked about?"

'Yes. If only those Linguistic philosophers had taken their anthropomorphic straightjacket off the meaning of "language", their claim, if they'd

only known it, makes a great deal more sense. I doubt if they saw it that way, though. They seemed to me, at the time, a most dreary and effete, toffee-nosed bunch, forever concerned with "the proper uses of Ordinary Language", as though that were something written in the sky. They were always at loggerheads, if not with each other, then with the "ruffianly scientists" who regarded them, in turn, as self-appointed "Language Police".'

'I soon came to see what the issue was,' Turner continued. 'It was a tussle between what you might call a Linguistic Right and a Linguistic Left. The one faction was composed of the literary scholars, the Right-Wing conservatives who wanted to keep language as it was in the way they had mastered it at places like Oxford and Cambridge and didn't want it sullied with scientistic "barbarisms". They regarded "Ordinary Language" as more or less oracular. The other group, the Left-Wing Linguistic liberals, felt that this was too constraining. They wanted to soften-up traditional language to take account of the new scientific ways of thinking which the "Right" saw as causing nothing but confusion. As I saw it, it was no more than a glorified "political" struggle between Left and Right, carried on under the name of Philosophy.'

"How come you got so involved? I don't remember that happening.'

'No, you wouldn't, Tony, because it was when I was seconded for a few months to the South Wales division of my firm. It suited me as I had family in the area. One of my relatives was a student at the local university and it was through him that I began attending the meetings of its philosophy society. It was a hot-bed of Linguistic Philosophy, under Wittgenstein's best friend and "wing man", the philosopher, Rush Rhees.'

'Sounds like you had a great time! What did they make of you, a hairy engineer, getting in amongst them?' asked Carter.

'I used to make them very cross. Those experts in "Ordinary Language" couldn't cope with ordinary science questions, which they outlawed under the dismissive title of "scientism". In that group, at the mention of anything even remotely scientific or technical, someone or other was sure to shout "Scientism!" as though it were something unclean.'

"Which you reacted against, I'm sure, Dave, knowing you as I do.'

"Yes, old pal, I certainly did! It was during one of Rhees' lectures, in which he delivered one of his most manic diatribes against "scientism", that

I got up and walked out. I left the Society never to return. It's ironic, though, that he could never have realised the latent influence he had on me.'

'Stranger things have happened, I guess,' Carter replied. "But let's see if I've understood you so far. You've got rid of the old hard-and-fast distinction between "mind" and "matter" by turning everything into "information". Then you say that the information which your "communicants" or "observicles" - for want of any better word - transact with one another, forms a kind of "universal language" in which everything communicates with everything else. Each communicant, according to its abilities, builds up its picture of the world from these basic information-units or quanta. So, to my mind, that "information", in quantum bits like those "photons" we talked about last evening, has to be the objective element in it all, the basic "stuff" of existence.'

Turner considered this for a moment, then he said, 'Yes, Tony, as long as we remember that these quanta don't convey the information, by travelling in space in the manner of the so-called "photons". They ave the information. That is, they are, themselves, the unanalysable elements of information out of which we project space, time, motion and all other phenomena - even the phenomenon we think of as "light travelling in space". As you yourself suggested, these elements are to phenomena what the letters are to a text, or, you might say, the dots and dashes to a message in Morse code.'

'But what is this "information"?' persisted Carter. 'I find it easy to think of a "photon" as a particle of matter or a wave-pulse winging its way through space and striking the eye of an observer or some other instrument...'

'Yes, of course,' Turner interrupted rather impatiently, 'and along with hosts of other photons, it has a knock-on effect along nerve-fibres connected to the brain, resulting in an informational picture of the sourceevent.'

"Yeah, that's all very well and good, Dave, but you say it raises all sorts of philosophical problems about how can we ever know thar, and so on; but as a scientific picture it's easy enough to understand. On the other hand, having to think of that information as just patterns and sequences of

quantum events, like the words and letters of a text or the magnetic "blips" on a recording tape, is nothing like so easy.'

Carter was now in full flow. "Let me put it this way. The letters-on-apage analogy, in itself, is not difficult to understand, because the letters are connected by the page, and the blips on the tape are connected by the tape, and so on. But where's the "page" that your observer "reads", or the "tape" carrying those informational "blips" which the observer processes? | mean, what sort of picture am I supposed to have of this "information"? Is it physical, mental, concrete, abstract or what?'

Turner could sympathise with his friend's impassioned outburst, so he measured his reply carefully.

"Well, it all depends what you mean by "physical". Is what you pay for, in your electricity bill, physical? Is it "concrete" or "abstract"? What is heat, come to that. What is conveyed when heat travels from one body to another? When you come right down to it, questions as to whether heat energy or any other kind of energy is "concrete or abstract", aren't always helpful. One thing modern science shows us is that whatever reality is, it's nothing like what was classically conceived as "concrete".'

"Okay, so information isn't concrete, it isn't something you can lay your hands on. So what is information? This question keeps on popping up.'

'Information, Tony, like mass, time or energy, volts, kilowatt-hours. weight or whatever, is a measure. It was realising this, from Claude Shannon's work, that gave me a new insight. I learned that, technically speaking, that measure is the negative reciprocal of entropy. As an engineer, you understand what entropy is, surely!'

'OF course I do! replied Carter. "But as I understand it, entropy is a measure in mechanical thermodynamics, having to do with heat and nothing to do with "information" in your sense. As I learned it,' he continued, obviously remembering his physics text books, 'it was defined by Clausius in relation to the efficiency of steam engines. This was then refined by Boltzmann in his thermodynamical laws which he applied to a perfect gas...

'But, of course, that's the way I learned about entropy, too,' said Turner, 'as the good old "Second Law of Thermodynamics". It was only later that

I learned, as I say, about its connection with information-technology, and it dawned on me that it's the same law, whether applied to heat systems such as engines or to communication-systems, such as computers and even human speech.'

'Eh! How's that?' his friend asked.

"Well, think of it, Tony, the Second Law states, in effect, that as time goes on, distributions of things or events that begin ordered tend naturally to become disordered, and that left to themselves, those processes never happen the other way around. Right?'

'In effect, yes.'

'So in the nature of things, the sort of highly pressurised concentration of water molecules that you find in the boiler, say, of a steam engine is not likely to occur spontaneously in an open atmosphere.'

'No,' agreed Carter, 'entropy rules that in the nature of things the opposite to that is what will always take place. The distribution of the steam in the cylinder is basically improbable, so, in accordance with the Law of Entropy, it has to settle into a more probable, more random distribution. And since the random distribution is the ordinary atmosphere, the steam expands in the boiler, escaping into the atmosphere via the cylinder, and so powering the piston and driving the engine. That's elementary thermodynamics. So what's your point?'

Smokey's great head suddenly appeared between them, as if the dog was awaiting Turner's answer. Blue was happy, with her head stuck out through the window, enjoying the breeze on her face.

'Sit, Dog,' said Turner, pushing Smokey back into the jump-seat. Then he continued. "Well, look at it this way. Tony. Those collisions of the molecules are events, right?'

'Right,' nodded Carter.

'Does it make any difference, then, whether we think of those events to which that law applies as mechanical collisions or simply as events without any mechanical connection? J] mean, the striking of a steam molecule on the face of the piston is an event, and an improbable concentration of these events is always followed by a more probable distribution of these events.

In these terms, the law says that an improbable concentration of events Is always followed by a more probable distribution, regardless of what those events are. Do you agree?"

"Yes, I suppose so, if you put it that way.'

'So my point is just that, Tony, that you can forget about mechanical collisions and think of the law of entropy as applying purely to events. Let's think, then, of some other events to which the law of entropy applies. Suppose you are an astronomer at somewhere like Arecibo, listening to a constant hiss from space..."

'... White noise, you mean,' put in Carter.

'Yes, that's right. Then, after many boring hours of that you hear a change in that hiss. Now the hiss that you've been listening to for so long is, statistically, the most probable distribution of sound elements signifying a state of maximum entropy, or disorder. So when you hear that change in the sound, you are hearing something that is statistically improbable, right?'

"That follows,' Carter remarked.

'Now that improbability in the sound makes the astronomer prick up his ears. To him it signifies something, that is to say, it is a signal, a communication of some sort - not necessarily from some human or other intelligent source, but from something that is significant. And so far as it is statistically improbable in that situation, it's a measure of negative entropy, or order, which is called negentropy, or information.

Carter was quiet for a contemplative moment as he drove steadily along. Then he said, 'I think I see, Dave, where you're going with this. You are saying that although entropy started as a physical law it is found to extend to information, which is usually regarded as non-physical.'

'Precisely, Tony, and to take it just that little bit further, it turns every-

thing that is in any way observationally identifiable into information of one kind or another.'

At that moment, the dull and uninteresting blur of the passing forest was broken by the presence of an elk. "Look at that beauty!' Carter remarked, as the dogs started barking, lustily.

'Now there's an object lesson,' Turner said, 'of the very thing we've been talking about. That sudden break in the monotony of the passing greenery was negentropic in that situation, which gained our attention straight away as something significant, or informational.'

"Well, wasn't that opportune, then,' Carter responded.

There were no more elk to be seen, so they continued quietly on their way. Their the silence was broken by Turner.

'As you know, Tony, Boltzmann's mechanistic interpretation of the law of entropy was of particles travelling invisibly in determinate trajectories and bumping into one another in obedience to the laws of mechanics. On the other hand, in Shannon's information-statistical version, the mechanical collisions between determinately moving particles were replaced by pure events. Now in this case, there are no determinate mechanical connections whatsoever as there would be if those events were magnetic blips on a tape or letters on a page. Indeed, that lack of determinate connections is an essential basis for information. I mean, if all the characters in a printer's font were glued together, they wouldn't be available for communicating anything like news, would they?'

Carter thought about it for a while and then he said, "Okay, Dave, I accept that.'

After a short while, Turner continued. 'So you see, then, Tony, how that law of entropy fits what we said about quanta, which, as we agreed, are completely discrete and indeterminate events, not connected by anything. According to that law, anything but complete randomness among those quantum events has to be significant of something, something informative.
