And since those quanta, despite being what we've called informational "blips", or "pixels", convey no information in themselves, individually, any information they contain has to be statistical, that's to say, informationstatistical, involving large numbers of those quantum events - in patterns and sequences, as we already discussed.'

Carter remembered. "Yes, back at the Lodge, last night. So it all hangs logically together.'

'Tm glad you see that, Tony.'

'So remind me, Dave, what are those quantum blips, or pixels, composed of?"

'Blips of mass-energy,' Turner replied. 'In observing an object, you are pinching from it some tiny measure of the object's mass-energy. The body is therefore minutely less massive relatively to you as a result of your observing it than it was before. The difference in mass is the energy of the instantaneously transferred quantum. And it's the same, of course, when you are observed in your turn. You lose some quanta of your mass-energy relatively to that other observer. The process goes round and round.'

Carter was thoughtful as he drove along. "That also makes sense,' he said. 'After all, observing is an energy process, and that energy has to come from somewhere, and where else than from the mass-energy of the object observed - or from its atoms, at any rate! But where's the connection between that energy and information?'

'Let me remind you again, Tony. The "connection" is in no way mechanical or material. It lies entirely in the statistical probability or improbability of those discrete energy-occurrences, which decides whether those occurrences are informational or just noise. The part they play in communication is like that of the words and syllables into which a message can be analysed. The measure of information conveyed by sequences of those energy-blips is precisely the same as with sequences of words and letters in a sentence. So in informational terms, in dealing with sequences of these events we are dealing, in the widest sense, with language.'

They held their breath as they approached on their right, a precariouslyangled single-track steel-arched bridge over a torrential tributary of the Clearwater River as it sped towards the North Thompson.

The huskies behind them whimpered in sympathy with the tension and, like mechanical twins, they braced themselves in anticipation of this obviously familiar chicane in the route. Carter cursed as the tyres squealed against the edges of the polished iron tracks of the bridge. He knew Turner had never regarded him as the best driver in the world.

"That thing's going to collapse into the gorge one of these days, you bet,' he prophesised dryly.

After Carter had navigated safely through the hazard he said, "Yes, I see the connection, Dave - at least, I think I do.' Then, after pausing for thought, 'Just to make it stick, if you don't mind, Dave, run it by me just once again. Where does entropy come into these information-sequences?"

Turner answered his question. "Entropy comes into it this way. For a message to signify, there has to be some "base-line" or datum of nonsignificance, or randomness, against which the significance of the message stands out. That reference-datum of non-significance - or noise, as information-theorists call it - is the state of maximum entropy, or disorder. In such datum conditions, all you will ever observe are sequences of events which are, statistically, the most probable or predictable. In such sequences, there's nothing of note, nothing of significance; ergo no message, just noise. As we discussed just now, this is the way astronomers, with their computers, pick out significant information from the "white noise" of space.'

'Right, T've got that,' said Carter.

'And by the same token, of course,' Turner continued, 'any significance in the sequence has to be a measure of statistical zmprobability, or nonrandomness.

'Got that also,' Carter interjected. 'Just as well to go through it again, though.'

"Well, okay, so significance cannot come about by chance - which is forbidden by the entropy law. That's to say, randomness cannot produce nor in itself evolve into non-randomness. So significance has to be something put in by something non-random, by design, you might say. In informational terms, this measure of order or design - in other words, the measure of improbability, as in the sequences of sounds we utter in speech - is what creates messages, or communication. By putting systematic improbabilities into what would otherwise be purely random sequences of events, intelligence signals its existence. We may not know what the messages mean, since that requires some known language-system. But regardless of whether or not we understand the meaning conveyed, we can nevertheless determine that something of significance is going on by the measure of improbability in the sequences. And this measure of improbability, as I say, is just as objective a measure as kilowatt-hours of electricity or gallons of diesel.'

'So, Carter concluded, 'in short, you are saying, Dave, that in any situation where there's only an exchange of purely random "blips" or "dots and dashes" between communicants, no information is conveyed. But when the "blips" are patterned, or non-random, information is conveyed about something out there that fixes one's attention.'

'Basically, yes.'

Carter was thoughtful as he drove along in silence for a space. Then he said, 'As you say, Dave, to recognise that a sequence of events is random, or significant, we don't necessarily have to know its meaning.'

"That's true,' replied Turner. "There are strings of bits of data which are signals in information-theoretical terms but which are gibberish in ordinary human language and thought. As we were discussing earlier, there are bits of data which both we and Smokey and Blue can hear, like the sound of our voices, but which only you and I can interpret. If this engine developed a clatter it wouldn't signify anything to them. And it's the same for them, when they sense things that we can neither sense nor decipher. Nevertheless, the fact that those improbabilities are there in the sounds we make, and which can be measured, tells anyone whether they understand us or not, that they couldn't have come about by sheer chance. In messages, the odds have been systematically tinkered with, and that signifies something regardless of whether or not those messages are understood.'

There was a long pause while Carter mulled over what Turner had said.

"This takes some grasping, Dave,' he said, eventually. 'I can't honestly say I fully understand what you're saying, but it's interesting and, obviously, profound. I appreciate that it isn't something that can be got over in a few sentences. But it will come, I'm sure. We needn't rush it.'

Taking the gentle hint, Turner offered some lighter remarks and gradually lapsed into silence as he realised that Carter needed some time to

think,

It was past midday when they reached the lake. Smokey and Blue were no longer with them, having been dropped off at the Lodge on the way

back. By their drooping tails and skulking walk, the dogs had shown their displeasure at having their outing curtailed, but Megan Frost had given each of them a huge meaty bone to chew on to keep them happy. She knew that they might have caused problems up at the lake with their noses in everything while the men's attention was on their task.

The boat and trailer were just as they had left them the previous evening. However, as they approached, they noticed fresh bear-tracks in the dirt.

'H'm,' said Carter. 'I wonder where he is?'

'Can't be too far off,' replied Turner, 'must have been poking around in the boat. Reminds me of that time up at Rogers Pass when we were touring the Rockies with Margaret and Pat, the first time we came out to visit you. Remember?'

'Do I? chuckled Carter. "We were all walking along the old railroad track and we stopped to look at that crushed snow shelter. PII never forget how Pat shrieked when I told her those purply droppings were from a bear.'

'Turner also chuckled at the memory of his wife's panic. 'And she sang all the rest of the way, because someone had told her that the safest way to go through bear country is to keep making a noise.'

"Making sure we didn't see any other wild-life either!' responded Carter.

He recalled that Patricia Turner, like his own wife Margaret, never really enjoyed roughing it in the Great Out-doors. Years before, both women had tried hard to share their husbands' interests in skiing, sailing, and all those out-door pursuits that places like Canada had to offer. They were happier surrounded by family, city lights and home comforts. It worked out quite well, though, as Pat was enjoying her stay with Margaret in Vancouver, doing all the civilised things, like shopping, theatre visits and so on, while their husbands, as these women would have put it, 'played with their boats and things and acted like backwoodsmen.'

For a short while, the two men worked in silence, each knowing, automatically, or from the occasional grunt and curse, what was required of him. Then, the bearing firmly fixed and the trailer hitched up and ready to go, Turner said, 'Megan gave us a couple of packed lunches when we dropped the dogs off.'

'Let's have lunch, then, by the lake,' suggested Carter. 'It's a nice day. We don't have to rush anywhere.'

Sitting on the floating pier, dangling their toes in the clear, cool water, the two men companionably munched their sandwiches. The autumn sun was still hot enough for them to remove their sweaters and enjoy its warmth. The silence of the lake was accentuated by the quiet lapping of ripples under the pier and by the occasional distant cries of birds and other animals. (In this country, the bird-calls were less frequent than in Britain, because creatures here were not quite so keen to divulge their presence.) In the surrounding forest, in this clear air, the contrast between the firs and the autumnal yellow of the birches was breathtaking.

It seemed to Turner that Carter was unusually pensive. His friend was normally much more a man of action. Either he's thinking about what we discussed, Turner thought to himself, or perhaps his ageing bones, like mine, are hampering him more than he'll admit.

Turner's thoughts along these lines were eventually interrupted when Carter returned to their earlier conversation.

'Let's get back to what we were discussing, Dave. You say, and I accept, that complete randomness or indeterminacy, at the most fundamental level of nature, is an essential requirement for order to be superimposed upon. It's the objective and measurable datum of non-significance, or meaninglessness and without that randomness at its root, communication would be impossible.'

'Absolutely.'

"Well, I've been thinking about that. It seems a bit of a paradox, doesn't it, that there has to be disorder for there to be order.'

'It might look that way, Tony. But think. As I say, how would a newspaper be able to convey its messages to its readers if all the characters in the font were programmed to appear in pre-ordered alphabetic sequence? What sorts of "news" could those predetermined print-sequences possibly convey?'

"Yes, I got that,' said Carter.

"The same applies,' added Turner, 'if those pre-ordered connections between the characters were replaced by the tape connections you mentioned - or by any sort of matrix connection whatsoever.'

'T can see that, too,' Carter replied, 'so it follows that the basic elements of communication must remain loose - that is, unconnected or indeterminate - so that they can be arranged and rearranged in various meaningful ways for constructing messages. Looking at it that way, I grant, the appearance of disorder on the fundamental levels of physics isn't really a paradox. It's no more than what's to be expected on grounds of sheer commonsense, if intelligence and communication are to be regarded as real.'

'Precisely? Turner agreed.

After a moment or two's thoughtful silence, Carter continued. "But speaking of real, what does all this have to do with the question of reality which we began with? That question, I feel, has probably been answered but if so then J haven't got it clear in my head.'

Turner was gathering up their belongings as he replied.

"Well, we've agreed, at least, Tony, that whatever reality is, it's not some kind of underlying and determinate "hidden mechanism", or connection between atoms, of the "hard-wired" kind which classically-minded physicists sought, since that would preclude any kind of free and real communi-

an cation.

"Yes, that's now fairly plain. But what #s real, then, if it isn't these hidden mechanisms? I'm still struggling with that.'

Both men were now on their feet and the discussion continued as they strolled back to the truck.

Answering Carter's question, Turner said, 'For us it's what by a commonsense consensus, is real, or non-illusory in terms of our very best and correct use of our most-evolved language, based on the best information available. Isn't that what we decided?"

"Yes, but how,' Carter persisted, 'can we be sure that what commonsense decides really is real - if you get my meaning. I'm sorry, Dave, but this question of reality and unreality keeps on cropping-up. My mind keeps snapping back to it.'

'That's perfectly understandable,' his friend replied, "because we're so steeped in the old traditions of thought that these new ideas are difficult to assimilate. So, okay, let's look at it this way. Suppose I asked you what red

is. You could show me something that's red - or a whole heap of red things whose only common characteristic, let's say, is that they are red. Having done that, what would you say to me if I then asked you, "But is that really red?" What could you say but something like, "Well, I call it red and everyone else with normal vision calls it red, so what else is required?"

'I get it,' said Carter, 'so if you call something red and get everyone else to agree that it's red, then you can use that word "red", quite unambiguously, as a word that everyone understands.'

'That's right, Tony. This common consensus as to the meanings of these words, like the red and green of traffic signals, navigation lights and so on - including all the identifying colours of the spectrum known to physicists...

'Ah, yes,' interposed Carter, 'it's what you and Noel were talking about, that they signal the natures, or character-complexes, as you called it, of the various chemical elements and compounds.'

'Right again,' said Turner. 'So, you would say to me, "Where's the problem? What's the point in looking for some cosmical guarantee that these colours are 'really' what they are when, in all forms of practical discourse, they are obviously and sufficiently what everyone agrees they are?"'

"What you're driving at, then, I suppose,' answered Carter, 'is that it's the same with the word "real".'

"Yes, with all words like "real", "reality", "truth", "goodness", and so on. These are words in human language, the language of ordinary practical experience and survival. There's no cosmical copyright on these terms, held by spirits up on high. They are all essentially human devices evolved for practical human purposes. They are misused by old-style, pre-linguistic philosophers who take them out of that human context and present them as if they were bits of angelic sky-writing.'

'Okay, then, Dave, I think I've got the message,' said Carter. 'So "reality", in your terms, is no more nor less than a word which we give to fully tried and tested phenomena. But doesn't that make reality a mere convention of language?"

"Yes, but what's wrong with that?' Turner asked. 'So long as all traffic stops on red, and red and green are understood to mean port and star-

board, what's the point in waxing metaphysical about it? What's more real than that?'

'But,' Carter pointed out, 'some people are colour-blind, aren't they? How can they be part of the consensus decision as to what those words mean?'

"They get it by communicating with others within the conventional language structure, where disabilities like that are taken into account. You see, Tony, it's all too easy to underestimate the power of the common language to discern true from: false. For instance, practically no-one who understands ordinary language, on seeing an oar appearing bent in water thinks that the oar is really bent.'

"Okay, Dave, I take your point,' said Carter.

"That's good,' his friend replied. 'If you think of it, language - conventional language, that is - is an organic system which, when used properly, easily tells us which phenomena are truly interpreted and which aren't because, in a developed, properly organised language, the meaning of any one word or phrase supports the meaning of each and every other, so that anything false sticks out like a sore thumb.'

'I suppose,' Carter pondered, 'this is why forensic justice, for instance, works the way it does, by finding those inconsistencies that tell us whether or not someone is lying.'

'Yes, Tony, and the test of that consistency is, of course, /ogic. Logic tells us not only what is true or false within the language itself but also whether that language is true or false in respect of nature.'

'And that's where science comes in, I suppose, Dave, with its experimental probings into physical phenomena.'

'Right you are, Tony. Some of these probings may reveal new phenomena, so we need to add new words to the language. Other probings may reveal the need for logically revising and rearranging whole sections of the existing language, so that certain words and word-uses which may have been long familiar fall out of use.'

'Like the word "phlogiston" invented by that eighteenth century scientist.'

'You mean Lavoisier,' added Turner helpfully, 'or they have to be replaced by new words, and perhaps whole new concept-systems. Now and again,

in order to fit with new phenomena, the whole category-structure of a language may have to be changed, so as to keep the language squared with reality. Think of the revolutions instituted by the likes of Copernicus and Galileo.'

'Ah, yes, of course!" responded Carter. 'But my mind is going round in circles again. So if I understand you right, you're saying that there's always something beyond conventional language which is even truer, the "language of nature", as we've been calling it.'

'And that,' stated Turner, 'serves as the underlying standard, or common factor, towards which, in good season, all development of the language tends. And also "in good season", Tony, this organic development of language has to go hand-in-hand with science - not science as we find it at present, unfortunately, whose language is so often entirely disconnected and capricious, but a science which gives at least some serious attention to linguistic philosophy.'

By now, they had reached the truck. As Turner climbed into the driving seat, he continued. 'So you see, Tony, while I accepted the nub of what those linguistic philosophers were saying, my only quarrel with them, as I say, was over their conservative anti-science aim of maintaining, at all costs, the integrity, as they saw it, of traditional language, scorning all the growing technical and scientific uses of words. If only linguistic philosophy hadn't got so blinkered by that kind of scholasticism, the students of people like Wittgenstein and Shannon might have collaborated with science, instead of keeping themselves - or, rather, being kept - so academically apart from it. And if they had collaborated, then I believe we would all, by now, be much further along the road to a true philosophical understanding of nature than seems possible at present.'

They pulled out of the lay-by and, having checked that the trailer was now following in good order, they settled back for the drive down the road through the Park, with Turner happily ensconced at the wheel.

After another long silence, Carter said, "Let's see if J can summarise all this. You say, we shouldn't expect to find reality in mysterious depths

beyond language and the appearances of things but in those appearances themselves. And our starting-point, even in science, must be in proper, commonly understood, evolving language.'

"That's right, Tony. Reality lies in the correct and most efficient logical interpretations of physical data, just as illusion and falsity lie in their incorrect interpretation.'

'So again we reach the conclusion,' Carter continued, 'that any search into the reality, or essence, of things, whether scientific or philosophical, we should be looking towards the order there obviously is i nature.'

'Nature as we commonly experience it,' added Turner, 'and order in the sense of free intelligence and communication, not in imagined underlying mechanisms.'

Just so, Dave, and, as I say, I think I've now grasped the relevance of entropy in your communications sense. As I now see it, only where there is, basically, a background of disorder can there be free and intelligent communication.'

"Yes, Tony,' agreed Turner, 'and to avoid creating confusion we must also remember that this order in nature is not some kind of subjective evaluation but a real, objective physical measure - the informational measure of improbability. This is the signature of communication not only in the language of our traditions and conventions but also, and mainly, in the language of nature itself.'

Carter considered Turnet's words.

'And in order to understand this,' he said, as if admonishing himself, 'we must fight the tendency to snap back into thinking of all this as somehow going on against the old idea of a background of continuous, self-sufficient space and time - the "eternal void", as you explained it last night over supper. That's the most difficult part, Dave, for me at any rate, ridding my mind of that materialistic infrastructure to which it keeps shooting back.'

'Yes,' replied his friend, sympathetically, 'that was the most difficult part for me, too, This was before I made the conceptual switch, or flipover, as we might call it, from thinking of "photons" as physical particles with tracks stretched out in pre-existing space and time, to thinking of them as

simply spaceless and timeless information-blips from which, in .observational sequencing, or processing, space and time themselves are spun. But it makes much more sense that way and fits with all the scientific evidence, evidence which is completely mystifying if seen in any other way.'

'It seems to me, then,' said Carter, continuing his musing, 'that what we have to do, in order to understand modern science, is to rub out all the old chalk-divisions in language between "organic" and "inorganic", between "concrete" and "abstract", between "mind" and "matter", between "observer" and "reagent", et cetera, et cetera, and re-draw the whole system of classification and division - in fact, rearrange the whole bag of tricks . . .'

'In fact, the whole conventional category-system to fit all the newly discovered phenomena,' interposed Turner. 'Yes, that's what we were talking about the other evening at the cabin.'

'So what about the old idea, then, Dave, of the "heat death of the universe", that since entropy is irreversible, everything is running down, that all order and - by implication, all communication - will eventually disintegrate into disorder?'

'Well, Tony, that fails on three counts. First, let's take the idea of a "universe", to which the law applies. There's no such thing in quantum physics. Everything is discrete and dissociated, remember? And, secondly, as a matter of logic, can anyone conceive a "universe of disorder" other than from the standpoint of order? That's like trying to imagine a world of shortness without tallness, or a box with an inside and no outside. It's inconceivable that as an entity in any sense whatsoever, chaos can ever be or even become "universal". It can signify only where "order" is already understood as something with a definite meaning, just as "illusion" makes sense only with respect to something that is ascertainably and primarily real.'

'Yes. I think I get a glimmering, now, Dave, of what Walter meant by the distinction between good and evil. There can't be evil, can there, where there is not already good.'

'Like the old saying, "Hypocrisy is sin's tribute to virtue".'

'But you said there are three things Dave. What's the third?"

'The third reason, old sport, is that any idea of something going on simultaneously all over - that is, "universally" - flies in the face of Relativity, according to which there can be no such thing as universal simultaneity. There is no contradiction in thinking of entropy taking place in one place - such as a star dying, say, while in another place the opposite, that is, negentropy, is occurring, with a new star being born.'

'I see, Dave,' Carter replied, 'a more or less steady state in which entropy and negentropy are balanced - like birth and death of life on our planet. This makes more sense than to contemplate an all-over "heat-death of the universe".

'For sure it does,' said Turner. 'But it must be remembered thar all talk of a "universe" in any sense is pure presumption, since there is no way in which everything can be comprehended, simultaneously.'

"You ever thought of becoming a preacher, Dave?' asked Carter, mischievously.

Turner made a deft racing-change of the gears and the truck accelerated.

'God forbid? he replied, irreverently.

Chapter 7: The Road Through the Park

The Fallacy of Universal Entropy

With the boat and trailer bumping behind, Turner settled down to a steady drive back south along the dusty road through the Park. It was a fine late afternoon and the light of the westering sun slanting through the trees made the birch leaves shine like transparent gold. The air was still, as though the forest held its breath. The echoes of the sound of the truck, heard through the open windows increased and decreased in loudness with the different densities of the passing forest on both sides. The breeze through the windows was full of the scent of pine.

A long silence ensued while Carter, steeped though he was in the ambience of it all, nevertheless continued his pondering. After a while he voiced his thoughts.

'Dave,' he said, 'I'd like to go back to what you were saying about those "photons" of Einstein's. You've convinced me that the way I'd thought of them originally, as little material particles travelling in space, is a false interpretation, an over-interpretation, you might say, of the true facts.'

'Yes, as I've said, it's far simpler and more efficient to think of them as discrete, intrinsically spaceless and timeless quantum blips, as the ultimately irreducible bits of physical phenomena.'

'And you've also said, Dave, that it's more appropriate to think of space and time as something generated in the observational processing of those blips.'

Turner, his eyes carefully focussed on a precarious part of the road, replied, "Yes, that's what I'm saying, Tony.'

'Well, okay,' Carter continued, 'as I say, I accept that. It makes sense, especially on the analogy of the way distance is projected from the screen

pixels in a video scenario. Then we argued that the substantiality of things lies, not in those "pixels" as such but in the integrity of the appearances themselves within the context of the scenario as a whole - that is, in the relational character-complexes, or patterns of bits rather than in the bits themselves. Am I right?'

'Absolutely,' Turner agreed.

'And then you said that since there's not just one but many observational frameworks, or perspectives, in which those patterns or complexes configure, the true substance of things is what's agreed in common communication between all the various observers, or observicles - a community, or consensus, of perspectives, I think you said.'

'Near enough, Tony.'

'And because there's no distance nor time, at the quantum level, separating what an object c in itself and how it is observed, there's no radical division between what a thing is, as a character-complex, when it is correctly observed, and what that thing is in itself as identified by that consensus.'

"You've certainly got the hang of it, old pal.'

'Good! Well, next we started talking about language, about what it means to say that a thing "Zs" or "exists", and that's when it got heavy for me. I can see that the existence of a thing - its essential character - can't be something self-sufficient and unobserved, about which nothing can be said. Thar's just silly. So it has to be a bunch of predicates referring to that subject whose name is agreed on by the users of a common language. As we've said, even unknown, or purely guessed-at, things have to be described. And how else can they be described but in that way. I see that, but my difficulty, Dave, which remains, in spite of what we've discussed, is to get all that together with our discussion about entropy. It seems to me a bit of a jump from talking about existence and substance in that language sense, to talking about entropy.'

"Yes, I suppose it is difficult to grasp, Tony. The connection, of course, is Shannon's information-theoretical interpretation of entropy.'

'Te not forgotten that, Dave, nor what you said about randomness lying at the root of things. I can see quite clearly that without randomness there'd be nothing against which order could stand out as significant.'

'So, since you've obviously understood all that, what's the problem?'

'Well, I do see it all, and yer I don't. I'm still trying to get it together in my head, but as I say, it's not easy. Look, let me think about it for a bit. None of your idle chit-chat for a while. Okay?'

Turner was happy to drive on in silence. The hard-packed gravel road was reasonably straight in this area of the Park but was fairly precipitous in places, so he was glad to concentrate on driving as a respite from the notunwelcome but intense discussions he'd been having. Driving fast had always been both a relaxation and a challenge to him - as he might well have explained to a judge fining him for speeding, but with the boat and trailer behind him, he had to be careful in this rugged terrain.

Right now there was a particularly tricky bend with loose gravel and adverse camber to negotiate near Shadow Lake. Then it was straight as far as Bailey's Chute, about eight miles from the mouth of Lake Clearwater. Turner liked Shadow Lake. At the road end, the lake was narrow and overhung by dense forest, hence its name, he supposed, but further along it opened out into an almost circular shape with a little island in the middle. It was too small for serious canoeing, of which, anyway, there was plenty to be had on the Clearwater River. From the river's outlet at the end of Clearwater Lake, unwary boaters had been drawn to disaster at Osprey Falls. From there the river flowed towards its confluence with the North Thompson, All along its length, the river provided challenges enough to those who wished to pit their strength and wits against it.

Turner recalled that on their first trip to Wells Grey Park, Don Frost had warned them about those hazards. One salutary tale Don had told them about was when, early in the previous season, he had taken a group of visitors to canoe the river below the Horseshoe Falls. It was a comparatively calm stretch of water but, unfortunately, one canoe had got into difficulties and capsized. The water, ice-cold even in summer, was heart-stoppingly so in May, when the melt water was still coming down from the peaks. Don had plunged in to rescue the struggling men, both of whom were older than he. Then, having tended them as best he could, he had run more than a mile to fetch the minibus to take them to hospital in Clearwater. From

the time he had dragged them out of the water to the time the doctor saw them, more than an hour had elapsed, and yet the chocolate which he had given the men on the way to the hospital was still solid in their mouths when the doctor examined them.

This accident, fortunately, was not fatal, as both men soon recovered. But a party of young German tourists from a camp near Clearwater had not been so lucky. They had gone out unescorted to canoe the river, north of the town. They had been warned of a section of the river where the water accelerated around a bend called the Tiger Tooth. There, the river flowed so fast that, in spate, the angle of the rushing water up a rocky slope reached almost forty-five degrees from horizontal. Trees, logs, rocks and other bits of wrenched-away detritus had got jammed into crevices to present to a speeding canoe a phalanx of jagged spears. All five lives had been lost. Whatever had prompted them to do that, Turner had wondered, when they might have saved their testosterone for something more sensible, something more beneficial to themselves and society than just ending up prematurely in coffins. Often accused of being a daredevil himself in his younger days, now older and wiser, he could not admire that kind of vain and wasteful foolhardiness.

At this stage in Turner's thoughts, they were approaching the spot called Bailey's Chute. This was a small but quite spectacular waterfall which formed the limit of the Chinook salmon's arduous journey from the Pacific to its spawning ground. Recalling the morning's conversation at the Lodge, he remembered that Noel had said he was going to Bailey's Chute.

'Let's stop and see if Marian was right about there still being salmon jumping at the Chute,' he said to the meditative Carter.

'Oh, . . . right,' agreed Carter, dragged out of his reverie.

They pulled in at an attractive spot alongside the river, where some people were having a barbecue.

'Hey, look! There's Mac,' Carter said. "Those youngsters must be his students.' He called across as he got out of the cab, "Hi there, Mac. We thought you were over at Mahood Lake.'

The Canadian geologist looked round when he heard Carter's voice. 'Hi, guys. Great to see you! Come and have some weiners and coffee.'

Turner and Carter sat with the little group at the stout pine-wood table which the forestry people had provided for such purposes. "Thanks, we've already eaten,' said Turner, 'but a coffee would be fine.'

'Same here,' said Carter.

MacInnes introduced his students, three boys and two girls, all sparkling with the healthy glow which Turner had noticed most Canadian youngsters seemed to possess.

"We finished at Mahood yesterday,' MacInnes explained, "but these guys wanted to retrace the journey the pioneer John Ray used to make. We don't have time to do it the way Ray did, by canoe and mule, so we're cheating by doing it the modern way.' He indicated the big van with "Cariboo College" on its side and its large railed-around roof-rack stacked with equipment.

'John Ray?' asked Carter. "The Ray of Ray's Farm?'

'Thar's right, sir,' replied one of the boys. "He's one of our historical characters.'

"Historical exclaimed Carter, incredulously. 'But he only died in 1947! That's the year I left school!

MacInnes laughed. "That's just yesterday to you Brits, but you must remember that this region has been opened up only in the last thirty to forty years and there are still vast areas to be explored.'

How strange, Turner reflected, was the almost reflex notion that time is the same everywhere, for everyone and everything. How, he wondered, could that notion have ever been conceived when histories overlapped, sometimes by hundreds of years. Here in Canada, history was about a thousand years behind that of Britain, when bears, wolves and other beasts roamed around freely as they do here.

'From what I've heard,' Carter was saying, 'this John Ray must have been a helluva guy. Didn't he clear all that area of forest on his own?"

'He surely did, sir,' one of the girls replied. "He was related to my family through marriage. D'ya know, he lived up here in the bush on his own for more than twenty years, trapping and clearing land. Then he married my great-aunt and they raised three children on that farm.' This girl was obviously proud of her connection with the pioneer.

"Your great aunt must have been pretty tough, too,' said Turner, thinking of his own cosseted Pat, who wouldn't have been seen dead in a log shack in this wilderness.

'Surely,' the girl agreed, 'she was just twenty-one when she married Ray, and he was fifty-four.'

Maclnnes said, "This area was virtually inaccessible in those days. In winter, they were completely cut off.'

"But why did he come in from the Mahood? Wouldn't Clearwater have been more accessible?' asked Carter.

'Before Ray settled in this valley, he would trek across from Hundred Mile House to spend the winter trapping here. The easiest way to do that was via Mahood Lake. The Canim Indians had given him hunting rights to the east of the Clearwater for helping them with his medical skills during a measles epidemic.'

'A remarkable man indeed,' replied Carter, 'I can see why he's such a local hero. I heard he was on the run from the American law or some criminal fraternity who eventually caught up with him and murdered him.'

The girl student was amused.

'No,' she said, 'that's false. We're always having to deny those tales. Ray was a good, honest man who, in the end, simply died of a heart-attack.'

'Sure,' MacInnes said. 'For some reason, folks always have to find a sinister explanation for what motivates heroic men like John Ray, but there was no foul play. He'd already had two heart-attacks and was, in fact, preparing to move to the town when he died.'

'Thanks for putting me straight,' replied Carter. 'I shall look at Ray's farm with new interest from now on. I visited it, once. It still had rusting tins of marmalade among other things, scattered around and on rotting shelves. Spooky! It's a shame that it got so dilapidated.'

'Sure is,' agreed MacInnes. "We're hoping to get some support from the Parks authorities to fix it. But of course, the tourists don't help. They keep taking bits of it away.'

"Well, we won't,' Turner assured him. 'Anyway, we're off to see some salmon jump. Thanks for the coffee, Mac.' With a wave to the group, he and Carter set off down the trail and soon disappeared into the bush.

Several hours later, the fine autumn day had turned into a starry but decidedly chilly evening. At the Lodge, Carter was sitting in the restaurant, waiting for Turner to join him for dinner when Noel entered. the restaurant. The two men greeted each other.

"What'll you have?' Noel called to Carter.

'Chilled beer, please... Cheers,' he said, when Noel brought one to his table in an ice-misted glass.

Pleasantries over, Carter turned to contemplating the events of his day.

'T've been sitting here,' he said, 'thinking about a very interesting discussion Dave and I had today. It was about the law of entropy and its connection with communications theory. You familiar with entropy, Noel?"

"The Second Law of Thermodynamics? Yes, Tony. I suppose your connection with communications theory was Claude Shannon, with his information-statistical version of the law. I can't claim to be an expert, of course,' he added, "but I like to keep up with developments.'

'In that case,' said Carter, 'you might be interested in what Dave was saying, that if it weren't for the rule of entropy there'd be no such thing as communication. Absolute randomness, or "noise", Dave says, is the datumlevel on which order and significance are imposed. He argues - and it makes sense to me - that if all the basic bits of communicational data were "hard-wired" it would be like gluing together all the letters of the alphabet in alphabetic sequence so that they couldn't be used for conveying information." .

Somewhat preoccupied with the events of his own day, Noel had not quite got tuned-in to Carter's thought-processes.

'I think I see what you mean, Tony,' he said. "But what was the point that Dave was trying to make?'

"Well, in that same way, in looking for determinate connections underlying events, such as hidden mechanisms whereby atoms are causally connected, classical science is completely off track. That atomistic "bottomup" approach to physics needs to be replaced by more of a "top-down" approach, starting with ordinary phenomena and seeking out their basic elements, instead of starting with those theoretical elements and trying to build-up from there.'

Carter paused, awaiting some response from his companion, but it was plain that Noel was somewhat preoccupied and not really concentrating. He decided to change the subject.

'Did you get to Bailey's Chute, Noel?' Carter asked. "Dave and I stopped there on our way back from the lake. We saw Mac there with his students.'

"That's who it must have been,' Noel replied, 'in the college van we passed on our way back. But, yes thanks, Tony, we had quite a good day. I think I got some decent shots of the salmon jumping. Ah, here come Marian and Helen.'

The two sisters, spruced up and perfumed, came to join them. Marian flashed them both a charming smile but, Carter noticed with amusement, Helen chose to sit as far away from Noel as possible and neither one acknowledged the other.

'May I get you ladies a drink?' Carter asked. The women gave him their order and while he was at the bar, Turner came in with Rick Jackson.

"Hi, everyone,' Turner said agreeably. 'Mine's the usual, Tony. I'm glad, Noel, to see you've returned in one piece. Rick here's been telling me about your outing.'

'From the male chauvinist point of view, no doubt,' remarked Helen in an icy tone.

"Gee, come on, Helen, give us guys a break,' pleaded Rick.

"Yes, Helen,' Marian intervened. 'You are being a bit unfair.'

Tm intrigued,' said Turner. "What happened at Bailey's Chute? Rick's account gave me no hint of a war of the sexes.'

'No war, Dave,' Helen answered, slightly mollified by Turner's fatherly, bantering tone, 'but I will not be treated like a silly little woman who can't look out for herself. Marian might enjoy it, but I don't.'

With that remark, Marian, Noel and Rick all began talking at once. Turner and Carter tried to intervene. Eventually, Carter made his voice heard.

'Hold it right there! Now let's sort this out. All this shouting gets nowhere. I suggest that one of you tells us what this is all about, and the rest of you keep quiet.'

Marian decided to be the spokesperson.

'Helen,' she explainéd, 'was taking photos of the salmon, but the zoom lens wasn't good enough for her, so she began climbing down from the viewing platform to the rocks below to get a closer view. We all warned her it was dangerous. After all, it's called Bailey's Chute from a man who was drowned there. But my dear sister, of course, persisted. She got down to the rocks at the bottom, then slipped and got wedged between two boulders with the water rushing around her - she was lucky not to have been swept into the falls, like Bailey. The guys, naturally, went to help, putting themselves in danger, too.'

'A tricky situation,' said Turner sympathetically, "but it obviously worked out, otherwise Helen wouldn't be here. But why, Helen, are you so up-tight about it?'

Noel answered. 'It's because I said, without thinking, as I pulled her out, that only a woman could do something so idiotic. Hell, I had every right to be angry. All this "women's lib" type of "Anything you can do..." attitude could cost lives. That's not being a male chauvinist. What the hell, I'm male, so why shouldn't I be what I am? You surely don't say that a male elk or moose is a "male chauvinist" for just being what it is. Besides, any of you guys would have surely have reacted in the same way!'

The other men sipped their drinks and said nothing. Then Turner remarked, diplomatically, 'I admire womenfolk enormously, but one has to accept that there are big differences between the norms of the two sexes.'

'I disagree,' broke in Helen. "When it comes to brute strength, there are obvious discrepancies, but in all other respects, the mental ones, that is, there's absolutely no difference. In fact, from my experience with my students, I'd say that girls are actually superior.'

"You teach languages, you said, Helen?' Turner asked her. She nodded.

"Then that's what one would expect,' Turner continued. "There's much evidence that the female brain is constructed differently from men's in a way which favours the development of language skills, at which women in general are superior. It's also responsible, of course, for other differences which have nothing to do with innate intelligence. As for myself, I'm glad that's so. If my wife was like me in all respects it would be very uninteresting - not just for me but for her, too.'

'That's what I think,' chimed in Marian. 'Tm for equal rights for women as much as Helen. I believe we've every right to the opportunity of using our talents and abilities in every sphere, but as women, Helen, not as pale copies of men. You've forgotten what Mom used to say, "Try to be a first class woman, not a second-class man".'

Helen was at least partly appeased. 'A man, though,' she said, 'could have got into the same difficulties as I did. Bailey did, for example. . .'

'Ah, but. . " Noel began, but Turner judiciously interrupted.

'I think this is the kind of problem on which we must agree to differ,' he said, kindly. "This "war of the sexes" has been around for as long as there have been two sexes and I don't expect to see it solved in my lifetime, no matter how "emancipated" and "modern" we all become. I reckon that although a man and a woman never see things in quite the same way, nevertheless in getting together they can become like two eyes. Each on its own sees only so much, but together, they see more than just the sum of the two. They see the world in binocular perspective, you might say, rather than in some distinctly male or female, one-eyed way.'

With this little homily, the atmosphere lightened. With a smile all round, Turner concluded, 'Right, now that we're all friends again, let's eat.'

Soon the meal was proceeding in an amiable, even merry way as they mulled over the events of the day till eventually, with the meal finished, Marian yawned and got up from the table.

'It's been a long day,' she said, 'with enough excitement to last me a month, so ll say good-night. Coming, Helen?'

"Yes, Marian,' replied her sister. 'It's surely been a hectic day and of course,' she added, with a touch of irony, 'we women must have our beauty sleep! Good-night, everyone . . . and,' she added, somewhat hesitantly, with lowered gaze, 'thanks, Noel.'

Rick also got to his feet 'Tl walk you both back to your cabin,' he said. 'I guess, guys, that ['ll turn in too.'

After they had gone, Noel turned to Carter and Turner.

'Thanks, you guys, for taking the heat out of an unpleasant situation. I was prepared just to forget about it, but I'm glad now that it seems to be resolved.'

"Glad to be of help, Noel,' smiled Turner. 'T think she likes you, really,' Carter added, wickedly.

Some long moments of silence were eventually broken by Noel, obviously glad of the opportunity to change the subject. 'Before you arrived, Dave,' he said, "Tony was telling me about the discussion you and he were having today, something about entropy. Right, Tony?'

"You know about entropy, Noel?' Turner asked.

"Yes, as I was telling Tony, I know about the law of entropy as it applies to heat theory and I know, vaguely, about Shannon's communications version of the law, As I read it, that connection between entropy and information was highly technical and abstruse, far too mathematical and specialised to sustain my interest, All I gained from it was that entropy and information are somehow connected, and I would very much like to understand that from a more philosophical point of view. The standard explanation didn't satisfy me in that way. From my more philosophical point of view something puzzled me about this connection between information and entropy.'

"Tell us about it,' Turner invited.

"Well, if, as we discussed last evening, everything reduces right down to quantum events, and if all quantum-events are basically random, then | don't see how that order, or non-randomness which we see all around us can ever have come about in the first place. How could it ever have started? How could it ever have evolved in the face of the entropy law? How can something arise and evolve out of nothing?'

Carter was remembering that in their discussion on the trip to fix the trailer, Turner had already addressed this question. He was trying to recall this when Noel continued.

'I mean, in a universe governed by entropy, or the rule of randomness, where can that order possibly come from? It can't come from the system itself, because that system is naturally random. But there zs order, as is plain from the fact of the life and communication that's everywhere. So, does that order come from outside? And what can be "outside" a universe - if not some spirit-realm beyond all matter, space and time, the work of some external creator of miracles?'

"You're right, Noel,' Turner answered him. "Nature itself stands as a reductio ad absurdum of the "universal entropy" assumption. Entropy simply cannot be universal. But I would not say that this entails the existence of miracular or outside spiritual intervention.'

Carter had something to say. 'I thoroughly agree, Noel,' he said. 'The very fact that we live and breathe, that we can think, and communicate our ideas to each other is sufficient evidence to convince me that entropy can't be "universal". Nor do I believe in miracles.'

'In that case,' Noel concluded, 'there's just got to be a fallacy in that "universal entropy" assumption. Don't you agree, Dave?'

Turner thought for a moment, then replied, 'I believe, Noel, that actually, there are two fallacies One lies in the false assumption that all events concerning those entities governed by the law take place in the classical "universe", all at the same time everywhere, whereas relativity means that there is no such single instant or universal simultaneity that can be sensibly described.'

Noel considered this. 'I see that,' he said.' But you mentioned two fallacies. Dave. What's the second one?

"The second one,' answered Turner, 'is a more a matter of semantics. This is concerned with the meaning of the word "universal". To say that the law of entropy is "universal" doesn't necessarily mean that it applies to the universe in the way that is so often assumed. . ."

"The assumption that the whole universe is decaying everywhere in parallel,' suggested Noel.

Carter again recalled the discussion he and Turner had on the way back from the lake to the lodge, and he had since been turning this over in his mind.

'Ah, yes,' he interposed, 'the old God's-Eye-View again of an eternal continuity of space and time.' Then he added, 'But of course, all the various durations of all the various things and processes, measured cumulatively over aeons of cosmic history, have to be hugely different. This, incidentally, Dave, supports your argument that the "Big Bang" theory of the universe being thirteen and a half billion years old, is nonsense. As if

that "age of the universe" could be fixed in that way, with that "age" being the same for all things, everywhere, that is, "universally"?

Turner added, 'As if measured by some metaphysical clock ticking some "Cosmic GMT" everywhere behind the scenes, even in empty space!'

'But that,' protested Noel, 'is more or less a repeat of the first reason. The second reason, you said, was concerned with the meaning of the word "universal". Can you explain that, Dave.'

"Well, Noel, suppose I say to you that the ratio of the circumference of a perfectly plane circular disc is pi times its diameter, and that this ratio is "universal". In other words, that this ratio is the same no matter where you might find a perfectly circular plane disc. That may be absolutely true, even if there happen to be no perfectly circular discs anywhere. Similarly, if I say that the law of entropy is "universal", then since there is no universal present moment, as we agree, this does not preclude the possibility of there being different levels of entropy in different places. Right?

'Right,' replied Noel, almost immediately.

'Turner continued. 'And yet it would still be true to say that "entropy is universal", even though it is at different levels in those different places.'

'I see what you're saying, Dave. To say that the law of entropy is universal doesn't mean that the "universe as such", that is, as a simultaneous whole, is subject to that law. So that's your second reason. Thanks, Dave.'

'But how, Dave,' Carter asked, 'would you counter an objection that this second reason of yours is just a matter of words?"

'I would say, Tony, that so are the words "guilty" and "innocent". Uses of words may have powerful practical consequences. All words are directives for action of one kind or another. That being the case, there is no form of speech that can really be "just words".'

'Except in politics, perhaps,' replied Carter, cynically. "But, point taken, Dave. The trouble, though, is that all this talk of entropy and the universe is very abstract. It would help if you could think of a practical example.'

"Well, okay, Tony. Remember what you and I discussed in the truck. Instead of talking about the "universe" in abstract, let's take a galaxy of stars. Within that galaxy, as old stars die, new ones are born, and the energy released in the death of one star fuels the creation of another by the absorption of that energy by clouds of cosmic gas.'

"But, Dave,' interrupted Carter, 'surely that isn't an example of entropy.'

"Hang on, Tony, I'm just coming to that. Now since much of the release of energy in the star's demise is in the form of light, especially in the case of a supernova, and since that light-energy is absorbed by atoms in other galaxies - in the way that our neighbour, Andromeda, and even very distant galaxies are visible to us - that release of energy by those decaying stars is absorbed by anything and everything in range of that luminosity, by those primeval gas-clouds in space out of which, as I say, stars are born. Then entropy shrinks those clouds, causing them to condense into stars and, in some cases, into spiral galaxies.'

"Okay, Dave, I'm with you now, so carry on.'

'Right-o, Tony, I was going to, anyway,' replied Turner wryly. 'And since, as we've seen, there is no overall cosmic entropy governing a "universe" of such processes, the only entropy it makes sense to speak of is what, specifically, applies to actual stars and galaxies, and no doubt, to groups of galaxies which are in luminous range of one another.'

"Yes," said Carter, 'I remember, now, what we discussed about that. As one star or galaxy dies, so another is created out of its ashes. That seems fair enough to me. As I think you said, entropy is always balanced with negentropy. Makes sense.'

Noel thought for a while and then said, "What I don't understand is, since the law of entropy rules that order is always decreased, is there any instance where the reverse is definitely the case, where the order increases?"

'Such effects,' replied Turner, 'certainly do occur in nature, And since they are highly improbable in situations to which the law of entropy is applied, they are, by definition, entropy-reversals or cases of negentropy. For instance, what about the photosynthetic effect of sunlight on plants, in which complex molecules are created out of sets of simple ones, where solar energy generates a whole eco-system ending in highly complex organisms? As you say, Noel, look at this wilderness, teeming with life. And take ourselves with all our intricate internal, social and other systems of communication. Isn't that sufficient evidence of negentropy? I mean, sunlight doesn't do that sort of thing to pebbles on a beach, does it?'

Noel had forgotten something they had already discussed about the non-universality of entropy.

'Ah yes,' he replied, feeling he had found the weak point in Turner's argument, 'but that's on a relatively local scale, the scale of the solar system, which is quite small in the universe as a whole. In that universal system-asa-whole the net result of all those processes taken together, is in accordance with the law of entropy.'

'And who or what,' asked Turner innocently, 'takes all those processes together? Relatively to whom or what do all those processes to which thermodynamics engineers apply their law of entropy ever form a single, all-embracing, universal whole?'

The young man paused for a moment, then shrugged. He gave a rueful smile as he spoke.

'Ah yes, Dave, I can see the trap I've got myself into in snapping back to the notion of a simultaneous, all-over, entropy. But if there really is negentropy, or increase in order, then what is the answer to that other than that there is this ultimate outside influence, or intelligence we call "God", which I cannot accept - at least, not on those grounds.'

Turner had been impressed with Noel's perspicacity. With him you didn't have to "spell things out". Give him the seeds and he would make of them a flower garden.

"Yes, Noel,' he said, 'you obviously see that we can't just blithely prattle on, nowadays, about "the universe" to which physical laws like entropy apply, as people used to in the Newtonian era of physics. We have to specify the system to which that principle applies, There's simply no system that can be identified as consisting of a single universal space enduring in a single cosmical time, to which a "universal" rule of entropy can apply.'

Carter was digesting this. 'So the fallacy, you say, Dave, lies in the subconscious assumption of a universal space and time consisting of just one, unique advancing geometrical and historical sequence of events, all enduring in parallel in some absolute way which only God knows - God, that is, as projected by classical scientists.'

'Yes, Tony, that's it.' He turned to Noel. 'So when you ask, Noel, where does all that order we observe around us come from, the answer is truly from outside. But that "outside" is not from a metaphysical source outside

the system as a whole. It is simply in the form of a message of some kind from some outside observer or observicle to another observer. And in the relativistic pluriverse or polyverse of interactive systems there are always these outsiders, or "others" of that sort with respect to every observational system that may be defined.'

'I see,' said Noel. 'So there's no need, you say, in this context, to postulate the existence of deities acting on the system from some mystical "Outside" or "Beyond",

"That's right, Noel. The whole system of interaction and intercommunication is self-contained, in a steady-state of conservation, in which nothing is fed in and nothing goes missing, with entropy in one place being balanced by negentropy in another.'

'I'm relieved at that, Dave,' Carter remarked, 'because, like Noel, I find miraculous beings hard to accept. Also like you, Noel, I keep making the same mistake, in spite of myself, of snapping back to that old scientistic "God's-Eye", or "universal" view of things which, as Dave has argued, is entirely fallacious. It just shows,' he went on, 'how enslaved we are by that

concept which, even at this late stage in our history, seems almost to be built into our cultural "DNA"?

Having remained quiet for a while, Noel voiced his thoughts. "But that's all part of the cosmological scenario, isn't it, Dave, and I accept your analysis on that score. But I thought you said, Tony, that you and Dave had discussed entropy in regard to communication and information-theory. Right now I'm interested in that informational aspect of entropy as it applies to language. What I don't understand, since the law of entropy rules that order is always decreased, how can it be that in being communicated, order or information is increased? Is there any instance where that reverse is definitely the case, where the order increases as the result of a communication?'

Carter, reminded of something, broke in.

"We can all think of plenty of examples of information decreasing with communication. That old chestnut from the First World War is just one - you know, where the message sent down the lines was "Send reinforce-

ments, we're going to advance" ended up as "Send three and fourpence, we're going to a dance".'

The others chuckled, as Carter continued, 'But, yes, Noel, that's what interests me too. Can you think of any instance, Dave, where a communication like that would increase in information-content rather than decrease as it is passed along?"

"Well, actually, fellas,' said Turner, 'I don't think that's the right question to ask, since in all cases of that sort of communication, clarity is inevitably lost and the message gets confused - as in your wartime example, Tony. The question we should be asking is what és information.'

'And what's the answer to that?' Noel enquired.

'Well,' said Turner, 'we know what complete randomness is. It is the background noise of space, the static in between radio transmissions, the 'snow on a TV screen when the programme is closed down. This represents a state of maximum probability, or entropy; that is, what is to be most expected of those events themselves with nothing organising or directing them.'

'As if they were completely autonomous,' offered Noel.

"Quite so!' answered Turner. Then he continued, "On the other hand, in any sequence of events which are non-random, whether they be sounds, bits of Morse-code, bits of magnetic charge on a video-tape or disc, the appearance of characters in a line of script or a series of quantum events, is in itself, statistically improbable, hence significant of something - in a word, informational.

"Or negentropic,' Carter interposed. 'It's as you said, Dave, an astronomer listening to white noise from space takes any change in that sound as significant.'

'Exactly, Tony. Imagine you are walking along a beach when suddenly, above the hiss of the surf you hear the sound, "Help". You'd scarcely imagine, would you, that the sea itself was making that sound? Nor would the coastguard assume, would he, that your urgent telephone call to him was just line-static.'

'But that's obvious, Dave, so what's your point?"

'My point, here, Tony, was to illustrate the connection between entropy and language - or, rather, negentropy and language in this case. A mean-

ingful sequence of language symbols or a significant pattern of quantum pixels in the eye or a camera, is intrinsically improbable. On the other hand, a sequence of events in which every one in the sequence is, say, mechanically predetermined by its predecessor, or a series of letters of the alphabet repeated over and over...'

'Or I suppose you could take a set of natural numbers, one, two, three et cetera.

"That's right, Noel,' said Turner, 'and with the numbers increasing indefinitely, we approach absolute probability, or certainty. So, on a statistical scale of nought to one, the informational content of a sequence or a pattern of events lies between nought, signifying complete randomness or probability, to one, signifying absolute certainty. The technique of actually measuring the amount of information conveyed in a channel or stored in some form or other is very complicated, as you have discovered, but for our present, more philosophical purposes that may be ignored. Anyway, Noel, that is the connection between language and entropy that Tony and I were discussing on our trip to and from the lake to fix the trailer.'

Throughout Turner's explanation, Noel had been sitting with his chin on his hand like Rodin's "Thinker, the glow of the stove suffusing his handsome face. Then he sat upright and said: 'So that's the connection between entropy and information. Now I get it! Thar fits all that we've been discussing about quantum light-pixels, perception and language. There's nothing spooky about this "information". It comes not from somewhere "on high" but from ourselves, interacting with one another.'

'Right again, Noel,' Turner said, 'and it includes information supplied by our instruments, so we must always remember that everything around us is instrumental in that sense.'

Carter interposed. "Then, Dave, that means that all the various bits of information that are bandied around between bodies, in all categories of being, vegetable, animal or mineral - including ourselves, of course - are different forms of language.'

Noel resumed his musing. 'Now I wouldn't regard myself as either simple-minded or illiberal. Nor can anyone say I'm uneducated. But when

it comes to this kind of thing, Dave, I must admit I find myself struggling. I get a glimmer, a sense of "Eureka!", and then my mind gets swamped with a backwash of intrusive jargon about "electromagnetism", "wavefunctions", "force-fields", and all the rest of the historical paraphernalia of standard orthodox physics, none of which, obviously, is commensurable with your new way of thinking.'

'Tm right with you there, Noel,' answered Carter. 'It's like trying to pick up an orange-pip with soapy hands. Now you've got it and then it's gone.'

'I feel, though,' responded Noel, 'that to grasp these ideas and retain them consistently requires not so much a superior education and intellect, as something approaching the simplicity and innocence of a child.'

'That fits in with what Socrates taught,' said Turner, 'the positive value of "learning to unlearn", an uncluttering of the educationally conditioned mind. This,' he continued, 'marks the essential difference between what natural philosophers do and what scholars and sophisticates in general do. As a philosopher you learn, in a sense, to cultivate ignorance, so that your mind becomes a kind of conceptual re-cycling plant, where conventional "graven images" of ideas are continually thrown into the melting-pot and recast - to the inconvenience, of course, of the Educational Establishment who depend on the textbook knowledge they gained at school and college remaining nicely settled for all time - or, at least, throughout their own lifetimes.'

Noel pondered on this for a moment or two, then he said, "What you're saying, then, Dave, is that in order to advance our understanding we shouldn't set so much store by conventional knowledge, by book learning and educational status, but should learn to think for ourselves. We shouldn't rest on our academic laurels but remain constantly aware of our ignorance of so much that is always greater than our settled human knowledge.'

Carter laughed.

'Well, Noel, Dave's got a head start in that direction since he was an ignoramus to begin with. Give him his due, he worked at it until he became an expert - in ignorance, that is. That's why his wife has to keep reminding him, when he wakes up in the morning where he is and, sometimes, even who he is!

'Actually, Tony,' his friend replied, 'that's about as good a definition of a philosopher as I've ever heard, "an expert ignoramus". I like it?

'It reminds me,' said Noel, 'of what Socrates is reputed to have replied to someone who asked him if it was true what the Oracle had said, that he, Socrates, was the wisest man in Athens. And when Socrates agreed, he was asked to justify that high opinion of himself. "Well," he said, "in Athens we are all ignorant, and since I am the only one who knows it, then it seems I am the wisest man".'

Carter capped this by saying: 'It reminds me, Noel, of a bit in the Bible, that "whereas some shall be wise, others shall be otherwise". I think that was written with Dave in mind!"

He mitigated this remark by giving his friend a good-humoured punch on the shoulder, which Turner returned with gusto. The ensuing short scuffle gave Noel a glimpse of the way these two must have behaved as young men.

'Hey, come on, old man,' said Carter, finally, 'let's finish now. As Rick would say, my brain hurts!'

'Not half as much as mine does after dealing with you all day,' was Turner's genial riposte. He yawned and stretched luxuriously. 'Okay, I'm off,' he said. "Good-night, guys, see you tomorrow.'

After Carter finished his drink, he said, 'Nighty-night, Noel. I'll leave you to dream about Helen! He exited stage left followed by a flying breadroll.

Chapter 8: Green Mountain

Gravitation, the Void and Light in Space

After breakfast the next morning, as Carter and Turner were leaving the lodge they were surprised to see Walter Collins coming up the path.

"Hello, Walter! Carter called to him. "We thought you'd gone home.'

'No, I'm still on vacation. I've been out and about with Charlie on his rides. I'm staying at his cabin down the road . .. He's an old buddy of mine from university,' Walter informed them, as they drew level. "He was a top student in my year.'

Td guessed you two were old friends,' said Turner. "But you're in very different walks of life, aren't you?'

'Not really, if you think of it,' chuckled the young clergyman. "He wrangles horses, I wrangle souls.'

The older men laughed. "But I'm intrigued, though,' said Turner, 'that a man like that, with a university education, should want to bury himself in this wilderness. Doesn't Charlie sometimes wish for a job more in keeping with his qualifications?'

'I guess he does, sometimes. He was a clinical psychologist, you know, but in the end the pressures got too much for him. He went through a bad time for a while. In the meantime, I'd become pastor over Blue River way, so he came to spend some time with me. The life in the bush suited him and as he'd always been a good horseman, he took up wrangling for a living. That's Charlie's life in a nutshell.'

"Thanks for the potted biography, Walter,' Turner said. 'I hope Charlie wont mind that we know.'

'No, he knows you well enough by now and he'd trust you not to spread it around. J think he gets quite a kick out of acting John Wayne for the more gullible guests, although the rest of us know he doesn't take that role

too seriously. I sometimes think he's just laughing at himself. That's part of his charm and it must be a release for him after what he went through.'

'His secret's safe with us,' Carter assured him.

'Surely,' said Walter, "but he needs mental stimulation and he told me he enjoyed our discussions because they provided that stimulation without making social demands on him.'

"Well, that's nice to know,' said Turner. He had begun to feel uneasy in case the others thought he was turning their holiday into a series of seminars. Further reassurance that this was not the case was provided by Walter's next question.

'But tell me, what's been happening since that night at the cabin? I'd like a chance to discuss one or two things I've thought of since then.'

'Certainly,' Turner replied. "Perhaps we can all get together later on.'

'That would be great,' said Walter. "What have you guys planned for today?'

"Nothing in particular,' answered Carter.

'Charlie has a day off, so we're back-packing up to Green Mountain. Why not come along? I know that Charlie would agree to that.'

'A hike up to Green Mountain sounds just fine!' Carter told him. "What do you think, Dave?'

'Sounds great to me,' Turner replied.

"Swell," said Walter. 'Pll tell Charlie and we'll meet with you at the gate in about half an hour.'

When the four men met later, Charlie looked quite different from the cowboy who had taken them to the cabin. With these men, his pose was gone. Wearing hiking boots, jeans and a smart sweater, minus his tengallon hat, it was much easier to accept that he was a well-educated man. It was amazing, Carter mused, how dress can alter one's perceptions of others. No wonder medieval society had strict rules about it. It ensured that everyone kept to his proper place in the feudal system.

They set off along the road in a northerly direction and after about a mile they turned left onto the trail that led to Green Mountain, which was about six miles south of the Lodge. It was well worth the effort of making the steep climb because the panorama from the top was breath-taking.

At one point on the way up, they came across a party of trail-builders, amongst whom, rather to the Englishmen's surprise, were two young, very tough-looking women. These, Charlie quietly explained, were probationers from the prison in Bear Creek, who had chosen this kind of work. They were, however, quite happy and sociable.

'Look out for yourselves up ahead,' the foreman advised. "We had a bit of bother with a she-bear along here yesterday and she might still be around.'

'Just as well Findlay's not with us, then,' Charlie remarked wryly. "He'd have wanted to plug it with his rifle.'

'T've a shrewd idea that Helen Taylor is working on him,' chuckled Carter, 'and don't ever underestimate the power of a woman.'

'Helen Taylor?' Walter was surprised. 'I thought she'd taken a dislike to him.'

"Well, I've seen stranger things happen,' Carter replied. "You mark my words, those two will get together sooner or later.'

"Helen's very good looking,' said Walter, 'in a rather formidable way. But I guess that makes her attractive to some men, who would take her as something of a challenge. Otherwise I'd have thought he'd go for the other one, Marian. But as you say, Dave, stranger things can happen.'

'I've a suspicion,' said Turner, 'that Helen is not as self-sufficient as she makes out.'

"Maybe," said Walter doubtfully, 'I guess only time will tell if you're right.'

They climbed steadily all morning and then came out of the bush into the warmth of the midday sun on the top of the mountain. This was a flat, grassy area, maintained by the Parks authorities, in the middle of which was a wooden viewing tower. From here, looking north, in the crystal-clear air, one could see across the forested valley of the Myrtle River to Pyramid Mountain, with the top of its volcanic cone slipping sideways; and beyond that the view stretched to the high peak of Mt. Garnet. To the south was Trophy Mountain and the valley of the Clearwater. To the east, Battle Mountain, not quite as high, and so-named after a battle over hunting rights between two of the local Indian tribes. To the west were the Cariboo mountains.

By this time, the men were glad to have a rest and something to eat. As usual, Megan Frost had provided delicious packed lunches and Charlie produced a few cans of beer from his rucksack.

"You can hear the wolves up here at night,' he remarked. "This mountain is full of moose, so it's good hunting ground for wolves . . .'

A distant shot echoed through the forest. 'And for men, too,' Charlie added. 'I guess the hunting season is getting underway.'

'T wonder what it will be like this season,' mused Walter. 'Moose have been a mite scarce for the last couple of years. The population varies with the wolves, you know,' he said. 'As the wolf population increases, the moose population decreases and vice versa.'

"Keeping a proper balance, I suppose,' said Carter, 'like the law of supply and demand. I thought I heard a wolf bark the other evening, when we were at the cabin, but it might have been a coyote.'

'Probably was,' Charlie replied, 'although there's the remains of a kill, near Ray's farm. Wolves are unmistakable. They don't always make that long, howling sound you hear in the movies. They make all sorts of "yipping" noises to communicate their presence to each other. The howl of the "lone wolf", as he's called, is really to proclaim his territory.'

'Another example of communication, Dave!' said Carter.

In the ensuing silence, Turner marvelled at the perfect quietness of the world around them. Then Walter spoke. "Speaking of communication,' he said, 'what have I missed on that subject since our last discussion?'

"Tony was referring, I expect, to a long chat we had yesterday on the subject of communication, language and information. It's too long and involved to go into it in detail.'

'That's okay, Dave,' Walter replied, "but I'm interested. Can you just give us the gist of it?"

'Tt started when Tony and I took the Frosts' dogs with us to Clearwater,' began 'Turner. 'They had communicated to us very plainly that they wanted aride...

'They sure do,' put in Charlie, "They do that to me whenever I take my truck to Clearwater. They're just like horses in telling us what they want.'

'Anyway, that's what started the discussion we had yesterday,' explained Carter. "We got on to talking about communication and its connection with entropy. This, as I suppose you know, is the law stating the inevitability of increasing disorder or randomness. We wondered how it was, if this law is true, that the order we see all around us could have ever come about, and the fact that it did seems a complete contradiction of the law.'

"Yes,' Turner added, 'and the discussion continued even after we got back to the Lodge, where Noel joined us. Tony and Noel gave me quite a hard time before we reached a satisfactory conclusion.'

Carter grinned. 'Yes, it was a thundering good argument. You see, Walter, it had seemed to Noel that the existence of so much order in nature, in the face of the law of entropy that forbids it, was by definition a miracle...'

"That makes sense,' Walter interposed, but Carter continued.

'Now I know what you're thinking, there, Walter, that this proves the existence of God as a creator of the universe. But you were there, weren't you, when we came to the conclusion that there's no God's-Eye-View of things, or "universe", that it makes sense to speak about in science, especially in view of relativity and quantum indeterminacy.'

"Yes, I remember that. But as I read it, you aren't dismissing God entirely, are you, Dave? After all, even Einstein believed in God. Didn't he say "God does not play dice"?

'Yes, he did, Walter,' answered Turner, 'but that statement of Einstein's has been very troublesome in view of developments in quantum physics.'

'Why is that?' queried Walter. 'I've read that Einstein didn't agree with Niels Bohr's ideas on quantum theory, but are you saying that this was due to his belief in God?'

'No, I'm only saying that the concept of "God" doesn't make sense in that particular way. Let me explain what I mean. . .' Turner was searching for a way to do this so after some hesitation, he continued, 'If the basis of your belief in God is that he miraculously created the universe and then let it run down by the blind process of entropy, then that's a fallacy. The fallacy, if you remember, lies not in the concept of "God" but in classical science's concept of a "universe".'

"Okay, I'm happy with that,' smiled the clergyman. 'In the trade, we call that deism - identifying God with the "universe" as Science sees it and God's laws with the laws of physics. That doesn't sit well with theists like myself.'

'So, what was this fallacy?' asked Charlie. Carter answered him.

"Well, it's like this, Charlie. We had decided that all rea/ systems are finite systems defined relatively to finite observers. So there's no single "universe" to which entropy can "universally" apply in the way that classical physicists thought. There is simply no identifiable view of the Whole Of Everything that classical physics presumes.'

_ 'With you so far,' replied Charlie.

Turner took up the thread.
