"You see, Charlie, if we assume, in the classical way, that there's just one absolute and all-inclusive system or "universe" to which the law of entropy applies, then the fact that there is order in that universe has to be explained as due to some outside influence. And what else can that "influence" be but the "God" of the scientists - the deists, as you describe them, Walter?'

Charlie agreed. "That makes good logical sense.'

Turner continued, "But in shifting from that classical, "universal" view of things to the modern view of an indeterminate pluriverse of interacting local systems, you still need some agency which is "outside" entropy - a negentropy, as weve been calling it - to explain the presence of order. But that "outside agency" needn't be some single source, like an imagined deity. It can be umpteen ordinary sources of information such as ourselves and other objects, organic or inorganic.'

'In other words, Charlie,' said Carter, 'once we swap that classical "universe" for a modern "pluriverse" we can see that there is no single source of order, or information, like some divine influence outside everything. That information comes from many and various sources, consisting of each observer relatively to every other - using the word "observer", of course, in the way we discussed that evening at the cabin.'

'So, what you're saying, if I'm understanding you correctly,' interposed Walter, 'is that in any system governed by entropy, the existence of order would have to be a miracle, and since, in relativity, there's not just one

system but many, each system is the source of that "miracle" for each and every other. That's so commonsensical that, to me, it's almost a platitude!'

'And that "miracle", of course,' added Turner, 'is simply communication - in its broadest possible sense, that is.'

'I can see,' said Carter, 'that as long as our minds can get shot of the notion of a "universe", of a single "cosmical" space and time - and that's not easy, I admit - the communication of order into things needn't come from "on high" but simply from round about. It's no longer a mystery, as you seem to be agreeing, Walter, but just plain commonsense, as you say, so long as we fight the tendency to see it all as we imagine God might see it. Right, Dave?'

Turner laughed, pleased at his friend's new-found enthusiasm.

'Right, Tony, you're not as daft as I thought!'

'Fancy you saying that,' said Carter, with mock affront, 'when I sprang so loyally to your defence, the other day!'

"Where was that?' asked his friend, smiling but puzzled.

'Well, you weren't there,' Carter answered, genially. 'Some people were saying that you weren't fit to live with pigs, but I told them you were!'

With this jocular remark signalling the end of the discussion, the four men began to pack away the remains of their lunch.

'How'd you like to go on as far as Foot Lake?' asked Charlie. "We've got plenty of time.'

The others agreed and they set off along the trail to the little lake which, as its name suggested, was shaped like a human foot. The top of the Trophy Mountain formed a dramatic backdrop to its still waters. In summer, these mountains would be covered with alpine flowers, but now, as the men emerged from the bush, their eyes were drawn to the snow-covered peaks.

'It surely is beautiful up here,' breathed Charlie. 'I love this whole area. I wouldn't want to live anywhere else in the world.'

'It is very beautiful,' agreed Carter, 'but it must be pretty hostile up here in winter. Perhaps it's my age, but I couldn't spend a// my time in the wilderness. I need to keep at least one foot in civilisation, nowadays. What do you say, Dave?'

'T agree,' replied Turner. 'As a young man, I'd have jumped at the chance to carve myself a piece of this territory and build a log cabin on it, but not now. Besides, my belief is that the life of the pioneer, the kind that John Ray lived, is now out of date. Just think what was happening in Europe all the time he was beavering away on his farm - two world wars, the Depression...

Charlie broke in. 'But he was able to stay out of all that.'

'Okay,' answered Turner, 'so he was, but is that really pioneering in this day and age? Did he really need to live so isolatedly and primitively? Don't you think his talents might have been put to better use than grubbing a living out of the bush while real pioneers were preparing, for instance, to go into space? Already, nature is reclaiming what he spent all those years building up. Something in me admires the man and responds to the romanticism of it all, but in other ways it seems such a vanity, such a waste of a life.'

Charlie was stung by what seemed to him uncomfortably close to an attack on his own life-style.

'Thar's fine,' he said, 'if you're making a success of your life in this modern society. But not everyone can be so lucky. Some people may feel very uncomfortable in that society. For such social misfits, the sort of life that Ray led may be the only option.'

Walter chimed in. 'Lucky for you, then, Charlie! You live the way you do out of choice, not necessity, and anyway, you don't live in much discomfort. You own your cabin and the land around it. You've got electricity, running water, satellite TV . . . Not too bad, eh?'

Carter felt that by introducing this subject he had struck some hidden bone of contention between these two long-time friends, so he tried to pour oil on troubled waters.

'Well, for me, I like being here, but I couldn't make it my whole life to live in the way John Ray did. Everyone to his own poison, eh! Anyway, to change the subject,' he went on, "Dave and I drove up the Trophies the first time we came out here, with our wives. That's when we discovered why Don always insists on knowing where his guests are going.'

'Why? What happened?' Walter asked, glad to be diverted. He had not really wanted to take issue with Charlie, although his friend's hedonistic,

'drop-out' life-style was a nagging offence to him. Knowing the reason, he was content to see his friend continue to shrug off responsibilities better fitted to his intellectual and moral capacity. In his own good time, Walter felt, the wounds left by Charlie's breakdown would heal and he would 'come in out of the cold'. But how long would that be, he wondered. Perhaps these discussions with these older men might help Charlie in some way.

'What happened,' Carter went on, answering Walter's question, 'was when we went to look at Spahats Falls.'

'Fascinating place, isn't it?" Charlie interrupted. 'In fact, I find it more interesting than the Helmcken. All those slabs of volcanic rock which the water has cut away like slices of toast waiting to slide off into the canyon. Fascinating? he repeated.

Carter continued. 'After looking at Spahats Creek, we decided on impulse to go up the track to the Trophies. It was a beautiful day and we'd heard about the alpine meadows up there, so off we went. I didn't have the truck then, so we were driving an ordinary hired saloon. We climbed up and up and then started to descend past some breathtaking waterfalls. It wasn't all that far, but suddenly the weather changed. All of a sudden we were in a blizzard. We could hardly see the track and we hadn't a clue where we were going. We thought there was bound to be a way down on the other side, so we kept on. Then the track began to deteriorate - we were on a logging road, of course, one which hadn't been used for a long time, judging by the state of it.'

Turner put in. 'Do you remember those elk we met on the road?'

'Yes, nothing would induce them to come past us, so they kept running ahead. of us, in a panic, all the way, collecting more and more of them coming out of the bush and joining in. Anyway, to cut a long story short, Walter, it got so bad that we decided to turn around and retrace our route. Bad move, as it turned out!'

Turner took over the story.

'Tt meant reversing on that track, in snow you could hardly see through, with a steep drop on one side and overhanging bluffs on the other. I dont mind challenges like that if I'm on my own or with someone like Tony, here, but not with Pat and Margaret along. Women have a wonderful way

of instilling into a man worries which perhaps he ought to have but normally doesn't. Anyway, we started back the way we'd come, only to find that the track was now partially blocked by a rock fall. By shifting some of the rocks and a careful bit of manoeuvring, we got round it and then, as we moved off we heard a crash and saw a great rock hit the road and bounce across where we'd just been. When we eventually drove down into the sunshine again we could scarcely credit what a hairy little jaunt that had been. But it taught us a lesson, didn't it, Tony"

'T'll say! If we'd been trapped and injured up there, no-one would have known. We had no equipment, nothing left to eat or drink and only our summer clothes on. We really were stupid to try it and, especially, not to tell anyone where we were going.'

"We certainly were!' added Turner. 'It's one reason Pat has never come here again. She was terrified. And boy, didn't those women give it to us, both barrels, when we were safely down!'

'I suppose we deserved it,' answered Carter. "The women hadn't wanted to go up there in the first place. It was a salutary lesson, and at our age we should have known better.'

Yes,' said Charlie, 'you surely were lucky to get away with it. Plenty haven't. As you say, Walter, perhaps I do have a relatively comfortable life out here, but it's still a wild area and you can very quickly find yourself in deep trouble if you're unwary. But I prefer it to the kind of jungle you find in the city. It's more demanding, here, in some ways, but more reassuring in others.'

'Youre right,' Walter affirmed. 'You can never get complacent, anywhere. But out here, Nature is still very much the "red in tooth and claw" variety.'

The men mused over these things for a little while longer, then Charlie suggested it was time to begin the return hike to the Lodge.

As they neared the gates of the Lodge, Rick Jackson's camper overtook them and stopped. Rick leaned out of the window.

'Hi, you guys! Where y'bin?'

They told him and noticed that he had passengers - Noel, Helen and

Marian.

'Hello,' said Carter, to those three, with a grin. 'Are you still on speaking terms?"

'Yes,' Helen replied with a smile. "We've had a terrific day. We spent some time at Helmcken Falls, but this time I was careful and I found so much to interest me without getting into trouble as I did at Bailey's Chute.'

'Tell us about it at dinner, then,' Carter replied as, with his passengers, Rick pulled away. When they had gone, Carter looked at his friends and winked.

'Told you so,' he said.

That evening, it was mild enough to eat on the balcony outside the main restaurant. Carter and Turner had persuaded Walter and Charlie to be their guests and to stand them a meal. Charlie had demurred at the prospect of dining amongst the clients, but Carter had squared it with Don Frost and. now the four of them were enjoying a well-earned dinner.

After finishing their meal, Turner went into the restaurant to fetch some drinks and spotted Rick, Noel and the Taylor sisters eating together.

'We wondered where you and Tony had hidden yourselves,' Marian called to him. 'Avoiding us?' she asked teasingly.

She's certainly a very attractive girl, Turner thought to himself.

'Of course not, my dear,' he smiled. 'You don't think Tony and I would deliberately choose to miss out on such delightful company? It's simply that we arrived early this evening and as we'd invited Walter and Charlie to dine with us, we just got on with our meal. Anyway,' he added smilingly, 'you four seem to be getting on famously without us.'

'An improvement on yesterday, certainly,' remarked Noel and as Helen nodded, Turner thought he saw her blush.

Then she asked, 'Would you like to join us for a few drinks? Tony said he'd like to know what we were doing today at Helmcken Falls.'

'Right-ho, if you're certain we're not intruding,' replied Turner. 'T'll call the others in. It's beginning to get a bit chilly out there.'

A few minutes later, the two groups joined up near the wood-burning stove. Walter and Charlie renewed their acquaintance with the girls and

their companions. The older men were amused to see their momentary surprise at this sophisticated version of the wrangler.

"Well, Helen, what about this interesting day you've had?' asked Carter.

'As I told you, we went to Helmcken Falls. In fact, we hiked right down into the canyon, something I admit Marian and I wouldn't have attempted without Noel and Rick being along.'

Her sister added, 'It was quite difficult in places, but the view of the falls from down there was fantastic. The roar of the water from so close by was most impressive. I made some sketches and took photos and I can hardly wait to get started on my painting of those falls from that perspective.'

'It sure was terrific,' agreed Rick, 'even though the climb back was tough, I guess I'm gonna be a deal fitter at the end of this vacation than when I started. I learned something, too. Noel showed me how to calculate the height of the falls. You count the seconds it takes for the water to fall from top to bottom. You square that number and multiply by thirty-two.'

'That's right,' said Carter, 'because water, like everything else, falls at the rate of thirty-two feet per second per second.'

"Well, that's what we did,' Rick went on, 'and that way we calculated the falls to be about four hundred and sixty feet - that's higher than Niagara, y know - and when we arrived back at the viewing platform at the top, where they give you all the info., guess what! We were almost dead right?

'Funny thing about gravity,' said Carter. 'You can never see it but it pulls on everything with invisible strings. How does that fit into your "observational" theory, Dave, as something which exists but which you can't observe?'

'I think I can answer that,' said Noel. 'P''ve done some work on it. What you observe is not "gravity" but the movements of bodies, like the apple falling to earth. It was from watching these sorts of motions that Newton invented "gravity".

"Invented"? Helen corrected him. 'Surely, you mean "discovered", Noel,'

'No, I mean invented, insisted Noel. 'Gravity was a device of Newton's for explaining the orbital motions of bodies. I've thought about this, some. It was like inventing a rain-god to be responsible for rain when you can deal with rain directly.'

'Except,' Charlie pointed out, 'that Newton's law of gravity is something on which a whole tradition of successful science has been based.'

'Sure,' Noel replied, 'you could build a successful science of meteorology around assumptions about a whole pantheon of weather-gods so long as your story of the invisible antics of those deities fitted the visible facts - like the capriciousness of the weather being due to the moods of the gods, and so on.'

A puzzled Carter asked, 'So are you saying, Noel, that you don't believe that there's such a thing as gravitational force?'

'Sure thing, Tony, I don't. It's always seemed strange to me that Newton has been so revered for doing something which, when you think about it, was real stupid.'

Charlie smiled, reminded of his first impression of arrogance in this young man. Such a lack of guile he now began to find amusing.

'So, why was Newton stupid?' he urged.

'Well, just think what he did! First he invented a "universal law" which said that all bodies left to themselves, without any force acting upon them, travel in straight lines. And then what did he do? He looked around him and saw that no bodies obey that law; that, instead of travelling in straight lines, all freely moving bodies travel in curves, like the moon around the earth, the earth around the sun, and so on.'

'So why does that make him stupid?' asked Charlie.

'Well, Charlie,' replied Noel, 'instead of withdrawing that law, he invented another one to explain why motion didn't obey that first one. The reason why bodies try to orbit one another, he said, is because they exert a "force of gravity" on one another, across empty space.'

'You might be interested to know, Noel,' said Turner, 'that centuries before Newton, Aristotle taught that all free motion is naturally curved, that bodies left to themselves, without any forces acting on them, tend to travel in circles, not in straight lines.'

'So there you are, then,' responded Noel, 'that supports what I'm saying, doesn't it? If you adopt the Aristotelian view of motion you get rid of "gravitational force" at a stroke. And it fits the observed facts much better than Newton's "straight line" assumption. So why, I wonder, did Newton deviate from that Aristotelian observational tradition?'

'Intellectual arrogance?' suggested Charlie, wickedly.

'It just shows,' Helen remarked, rather smugly, 'how gullible you men are to have left those assumptions of Newton's unchallenged for so long.'

'Oh, they've been challenged alright,' said Turner, pre-empting another argument, 'but so far no-one has come up with anything better. Perhaps Noel will be the first to do it, eh?'

'I might well do at that!' replied Noel, modestly. 'As a matter of fact, I've been toying with the idea of making a paraphrase of Newton's laws which replaces linear momentum with angular momentum - that is, substituting for straight-line momentum, angular momentum, which is naturally curved or orbital, dispensing entirely with those "forces" of the kind Newton invented. It amazes me that this has not been recognised for so long, that angular momentum is perfectly sufficient in itself to explain orbital motion without those "gravitational" assumptions.'

'But isn't that what Einstein tried to do in his General Theory of Relativity?' Carter asked him.

"Yes, Tony, but I don't think Einstein really cracked it. Besides, as Dave has pointed out, Einstein's General Theory has difficulties which my theory doesn't.'

'Like what?' Carter asked.

"Well, for one thing the General Theory is so entirely abstruse, so absolutely dense with mathematics. Getting rid of gravity dispenses with all that stuff about a "space-time continuum', "time-warps" and so on which make such a mystique of General Relativity and puts it light-years beyond the reach of common understanding.'

Rick remarked, "Gee, Noel, that reminds me of what Dave told us the first evening I met both him and Tony here. You weren't there, of course, but what you're saying now seems very similar.'

'So,' queried Carter, 'what would you replace that theory with, Noel?'

'Something very, very simple, Tony.'

"What's that?' Carter asked.

'A simple angular momentum equation which gives the orbital radius and velocity of a body as a function purely of its mass and the amount of angular momentum, with no reference whatsoever to "gravity".'

'Show us, then, Noel,' invited Carter, handing him a pen and a menu card.

Marian interjected. 'I hope you're not going to discuss math. hate math.'

Her sister scolded her. 'For Heaven's sake, Marian, it won't bite you.'

Noel continued. 'Anyway, Marian, what I'm talking about here is scarcely higher mathematics. I'll bet it's no more than what you did at high-school.' Then, in answer to Carter, he said. "Let's imagine an object moving freely in space, with no real forces acting on it. No such body is ever seen travelling in a straight line in the way Newton supposed. All bodies travel in curves, or orbits around one another, which means that all bodies are linked in paired and balanced angular momentum relations. So, for simplicity's sake, let one of these be a small body such as, say, an artificial satellite, and the other, a very much larger body such as our earth or the sun around which the satellite is travelling in a simple circular orbit.' With that, he took a menu card and in a blank space on it scribbled the following:

$$L = mvr = \frac{GmM}{v}$$

Then he explained. 'You see, in this equation, L is the amount or magnitude of the angular momentum, m is the mass of the orbiting body such as, say, an artificial satellite. M is the central mass - which we will assume to be the earth; v is the orbital velocity of the satellite, and r is its orbit radius. G has the usual textbook factor, with the same value and dimensions.'

As engineers, Carter and Turner were familiar with this equation.

'Ah, yes,' said Turner, 'the good old Newtonian formula for angular momentum. So, okay, Noel, where do we go from here?'

'Well,' Noel replied, 'just look at that equation. Taken to its opposite extremes makes it perfectly plain that as the angular momentum, L,

approaches infinity, the orbital velocity v in the right-hand expression approaches zero, and as the angular momentum L approaches zero, v in that same expression approaches infinity.'

"Yes, I'm with you,' said Carter as Turner nodded agreement.

Although Rick found it difficult to follow the mathematics, he watched their expressions with great interest, as did Helen and Marian. Noticing this, Noel continued.

'You can see, also, that as the orbital velocity v approaches infinity, the orbit radius approaches zero. And when the opposite is the case, with v approaching zero, the orbit radius approaches infinity.'

After conferring with Turner for a few moments, Carter asked, 'Okay, Noel, so what do we conclude from that?'

'Well, there you are,' Noel answered triumphantly. 'Point proved! The formula, given in terms purely of angular momentum produces the same result as Newton's "gravitational" formula, but without having to assume the existence of any invisible "forces" acting on the bodies across a vacuum. Given nothing more than the amount of angular momentum we have, directly, the orbital radius and velocity, so what need is there of "gravity"?

Carter and Turner looked at one another. "That's truly remarkable, Noel,' Carter observed.

At that point, Charlie spoke up. 'Can I ask you, Noel,' he asked, 'how it is that things fall to the earth and that we weigh what we do if there's no such thing as gravity?'

If Charlie thought he had stumped this arrogant claim of Noel's, he was wrong, because Noel answered him without a moment's hesitation, as if he was prepared for a question he had been asked many times.

'It is because, Charlie, things standing on the earth's surface don't have sufficient angular momentum to orbit freely where they are at that distance from the earth's centre. If the earth's mass was contained in a volume of about the size of a beach ball, they would be able to orbit the earth, freely, in an orbit way down below our feet. But, of course, as the real size of the earth is much greater, its surface prevents them from doing that, so the bodies press on the earth's surface with the force we know as "weight". This is now a real, measurable force, registered on weighing scales and so on, unlike the

»?>

invisible, fictitious force that Newton invented and called "gravity".

Having pondered on this for a few moments, Turner remarked, 'Now that's very interesting, Noel. Why hasn't anyone noticed this before?'

Carter agreed. "What's even more surprising is that nobody seems to have noticed it for about three hundred years!'

'T take it,' Turner continued, 'that you've dealt with the fact that real orbits are not circular but elliptical.'

'I sure have,' Noel replied."°

'Have you published this theory of yours. Noel?' Helen asked him.

'Not so far, Helen,' Noel answered, 'but I'm working on it.!! One professor I showed it to at university advised me not to publish it because, as he warned me, it was "disrespectful" to Newton to challenge such a famous and well-established theory as Newton's Theory of Gravity.'

"Well, Pll be damned!' expostulated Carter. "What a strange attitude for a scientist! It's difficult to believe that such people exist!'

'Oh but they do,' Turner replied, adding sardonically, 'and in my experience, it's no more than par for the course!'

Leaving Noel to answer some further questions about this from the others, Turner made another trip to the bar. As he did so, he noticed Don Frost leaning on the far end of the bar, deep in conversation with a middleaged man, of distinguished appearance. After placing his order, Turner was about to move away when Don called over to him.

'Dave, can you spare a moment? Id like to introduce you to Professor Craig, here. He owns a piece of land further down the valley. He's a Professor of Physics, so I've been telling him about some of your interesting ideas on the subject...'

Before Don could finish, the professor broke in without any preliminaries and said, 'And from what I've heard, you must be right off the Gaussian curve!"

10 The Eye of the Beholder. the Role of the Observer in Modern Physics, Viv Pope, phi Philosophical Enterprises, Swansea, 2004, p. 15 Also 'An Angular Momentum Synthesis of "Gravitational" and "Electrostatic" Forces', A. Although somewhat taken aback by this blatantly aggressive statement, 'Turner asked, as mildly as he could, "Really? What makes you say that?"

Ignoring the disconcerted look on Don's face, the professor replied, "Well, Don tells me you maintain that light doesn't travel.'

"That's not quite how it goes,' said Turner. He could tell, from Don's helpless shrug, that whatever Don had intended to convey had missed its mark. "What Don was probably trying to tell you is that I don't think light travels in the way we ordinarily think of things travelling.' He was about to expand on this when he noticed that his drinks had been delivered to the table.

'Excuse me, professor,' he said, 'I have friends waiting across there. You'd be welcome to join us. Can I get you something"

"Thanks, but this will do for the moment,' said the professor, indicating that he and Don had full glasses. Nevertheless he rose and, together with Don, followed Turner across to where the others were sitting, still discussing Noel's astounding "gravitational" idea.

No sooner had the introductions been made than the professor announced, in tones of amused incredulity, 'I've just been informed that your friend, here, doesn't believe that light travels.' Without waiting for a reaction, he addressed 'Turner in an interrogatory manner, "Tell me, simply, what's your justification for that view?'

'I can explain if you like,' Turner replied, 'but it's not all that simple. It's not a case of, as you might say, "The secret, quick about it, friend" - as Omar Khayyám might say. It requires a radical conceptual revolution, from thinking of light in space to thinking of space -'and everything it contains - as in the light. Even what we call the "velocity of light" is in the light as it is seen.'

'That sounds like pure claptrap to me,' responded the professor. 'I'm sorry, but J can't help being outspoken. I meet too many cranks in my profession, like that "Omar what's-his-name", no doubt. Tell me plainly, in your view, does light travel in space or doesn't it?'

Turner saw that Don looked uncomfortable and inwardly he groaned. If the professor had met too many cranks, he himself had met too many bigots. He had learned to spot them a mile off. Knowing how volatile he himself could be when pushed, he was nervous that the easy amiability of

the evening might be ruined. So, to introduce a note of caution, he countered, 'I think I'd better tell you that I don't particularly favour the adversarial approach to issues of this kind. It can only lead to a polarisation of attitudes, ending in a game of "winners and losers". I present my suggestions in all seriousness, for honest consideration, for what they're worth.'

The professor snorted contemptuously. Turner held himself in check. He felt instinctively that nothing he could say would ever convince this man of anything concerning his unconventional views. But, what the hell, he thought, Pll have some fun trying, and anyway, perhaps it will provide experience for the youngsters here.

Aloud, he said, in answer to the question, "When you say that light travels in space, what do you mean by "space"? If you mean ordinary space, the three dimensions of observational distance which we measure between visible and tangible objects, then my answer is yes; light, in the sense of light-signals, does travel in space. But if, on the other hand, by "space" you mean the classical "void" or "vacuum", then I have to say no; because that idea is philosophically problematical.'

'Philosophically? . .. Pah? Craig spat out. "That's just more claptrap. For an ordinary physicist like myself, wherever there's no air or anything else between physical objects, what is that space if not a "void" or "vacuum"? And light is known to pass right through those voids. You're surely aware of that!'

"Yes,' Turner replied, 'but does that void or vacuum exist in its own right in the absence of objects, as a complete emptiness, with its own volume, age and so on? And does light travel in that emptiness at a constant speed relative to it?' Turner was aware that the others were now leaning forward, eagerly anticipating the prospect of a dialectical battle.

'I don't follow you,' stated the professor. 'Of course the void exists, and light obviously travels in it, otherwise how would we see the sun, stars and planets?"

"Well, light consists of waves, right?"

'Right?

'But for there to be a wave, something must wave. And what is there to wave in a vacuum?"

'If you know anything about physics,' retorted the Professor, irritably, 'you will know that these waves are conveyed by electric and magnetic fields.'

'And are those fields something, or nothing?' As Turner asked this, he saw a glance pass between Carter and Noel and guessed that they could see what was coming.

'Something, obviously,' was the rather impatient reply.

'So in what sense is that something in which your light-waves travel a vacuum?' Turner asked.

The professor looked around at the others for some reaction to this, but their faces were merely attentive.

'Come, now, you're having me on,' he retorted. "The vacuum or void 'm thinking about is empty of bits of matter - except for stray gas particles and so on - but it's a whole set of fields of different kinds, electric, magnetic, gravitational and so on.'

'So your "void" is not really a void - if you take "void" as meaning, literally, nothing,' Turner reiterated. 'So to say that light travels in vacuo is simply a lie.'

The professor snorted his disgust. "This is some kind of undergraduate word-game, right?' he said.

'If you like,' responded Turner. "Let's regard it as a game in which we take words which tell us lies and make them tell us the truth.'

'I don't understand,' Craig stated, dismissively. "What's all this to do with "words"?

"Well, take this word "void",' said Turner, 'which, in physics usage, tries to tell us that light travels in the void - im vacuo - at a constant speed. Literally, that means that light travels in nothing, which is the same as saying that light doesn't travel.'

'Oh, come now,' Craig countered, 'that's not Physics! It's just playing with words, nothing but a language game!"

12 This phrase 'language game', if only the professor had known it, is precisely the phrase that the language philosopher Wittgenstein used in his logical analysis of ordinary and scientific language. Due to the Educational 'apartheid', however, between Arts and Science, few physicists have any idea of who Wittgenstein was or what he did.

'But, surely,' Turner replied, 'to say that a void exists in itself is to say that a nothing is something.'

'So, what's wrong with that? The word "void" is a: perfectly good word with a perfectly good scientific meaning. I've never known anyone confused about it - till now, that is,' was the mocking reply.

Turner spoke very patiently. 'Nevertheless, since "void" Literally means nothing, then saying that bodies are separated by a void or vacuum means that they are separated by nothing - which is to say, in effect, that they are not separated. That's what I mean by words telling us lies, causing confusion. Aristotle noticed this. "If space is nothing," he argued, "then bodies in space are separated by nothing, which means, literally, that they are not separated." This argument of Aristotle's has profound significance at the quantum-physical level, as you must surely appreciate.'

The professor's impatience was now even more ill-concealed. "What can you possibly know about quantum physics. Even among professionals, that's an extremely complex subject.'

'Yes,' said Turner, 'didn't your famous physicist, Richard Feynman, declare "No-one understands quantum physics"?'

The professor ignored this and carried on. "Look," he said, 'I'm a mathematical physicist, and to me these philosophical word-games are sheer nonsense!'

'Really?' Turner retorted. 'So if a judge pronounces you guilty of murder, say, you would just shrug that judgement off as "just words"?

'Of course not!' Craig replied, 'but that's something different entirely.'

'So you are content to go around, as a professor of physics, telling your students that light travels in a void - im vacuo, as the textbooks put it - which is not a void but a set of force-fields. Isn't that bound to cause confusion?"

'No student of mine is ever confused by it,' Craig replied pompously, 'but who ever puts it like that, anyway? Physicists are concerned with the practicalities of things, not with verbal conundrums.'

'Alright,' replied Turner, 'then let's get on to something more practical. Those fields, you say, exist. That is, they are somethings, not nothings.'

"Of course.'

'So when we travel in space, we are travelling relatively to that something. Can that motion, relative to your fields and so on, be measured?"

"You must know the answer to that,' the professor replied, impatiently. "That's what the famous experimenters Albert Michelson and Edward Morley tried to do in their attempts to measure the earth's motion in space...

At this point, Noel couldn' resist interjecting. 'And just about everyone knows that every attempt to measure that motion failed; and that it was to explain that failure that Einstein devised his theory of relativity.'

Noel's reward was a glare from the professor. "Yes, thank you, young man. I'm well aware of that.'

Turner continued his questioning. 'And as a physicist, you accept Einstein's Theory of Relativity, I mean the Special Theory, the one that declares axiomatically that light travels at a constant speed relative to the void, or vacuum?

'In the vacuum, you mean, not "relative to" it. But of course, who doesn't accept that theory nowadays - apart from one or two cranks,' and Craig added scornfully 'who are probably also Jehovah's Witnesses and Flat Earthists into the bargain.'

'And,' Charlie whispered to Walter, 'that would include gypsies, Jews, blacks and hard-left homosexual junkies, if he dared say it!' This remark to Walter was heard by them all, all but the slightly deaf professor. Turner continued his 'Socratic' probing.

"You would agree then, I take it, Professor, that based on the results of the Michelson-Morley experiments, Special Relativity has dispensed entirely with any evidence of that substance, or quasi-substance - call it an zther, field or whatever -- which was supposed to be the conveyor of light-waves and other forms of radiation in a vacuum?'

'Of course I do. But dispensing with zther is not the same as dispensing with electromagnetic fields.'

'Can you tell me, then, what's the difference between a "luminiferous ether" and a light-conducting "electromagnetic field"?

"Well, no-one ever detected the "ether",' huffed Craig, 'but electromagnetic fields are real. Their strengths and directions can be detected and measured.'

'And can our motion relative to those fields be measured in the way Michelson and Morley tried to do with the ether?' Turner persisted, "That is, by the slowing or speeding-up of the speed of light as measured in a system moving in it?'

'No, of course not, because as you move relatively to those electromagnetic fields, the motion electrically affects your measurement of length and time along the path of your motion. This exactly compensates for the motion, so that the speed of light relatively to you, the measurer, is always the constant, c, a universal constant which is completely invariant. Everyone knows that!'

Craig was oblivious to the contradictions in his argument which the others could clearly see, so he was satisfied that he had made an irrefutable point.

'So, what's the evidence, then,' Turner persisted, 'for the existence of that in vacuo field-substance of yours if you can never detect it - if your motion relative to it always causes that measure of motion to be completely erased?'

'Well, there's no direct evidence of that sort, I grant,' Craig replied grudgingly, 'but those fields are definitely there, as I say, because their directions and strengths, in the spaces around magnets and transmitters of electromagnetic radiation, can be measured very precisely, which shows that something exists in those vicinities.'

He gave his audience a look of triumph, but Turner wasn't finished.

'Is the place where you put your field-detecting instrument a vacuum?'

'If there's nothing there, yes.'

'But what about the instrument? That's there, surely, and that's a something not a nothing. So how can the space that the instrument occupies be a vacuum?'

"The space around the instrument is a vacuum, so it's iv a vacuum,' countered the professor.

"Yes, but the space, or void, in which the instrument detects the field is in the place where it is, not where it ism, and, as you admit, that place where it actually does its detecting isn't a vacuum. So how can we ever know that there are fields existing in vacuo?'

At this question, Professor Craig gave way to exasperation. He realised. that in this argument, Turner had put him on the back foot. It was he

himself who had initiated the attack, so he felt that it should be he asking the questions, not this upstart. But like so many of his colleagues, he knew next to nothing of either Linguistic Analysis or the Socratic Method that Turner was employing, far less of its relevance to conceptual issues of science. What mattered was that his professional dignity had been compromised, so he decided to get right back on the offensive.

'I don't believe this! Talk about nit-picking! You're some sort of philosopher, Don tells me. Well, Mr. Philosopher, that kind of argument is what gives philosophy a bad name in my book - and, in my experience, among my physics colleagues in general.'

Turner did not want to let this man off the hook so easily, so he decided, for the moment, to appear conciliatory.

"Well, alright, but why not just play the game? Regard it, if you like as a kind of philosophical Trivial Pursuit. So now it's your move. Let's ask again, is the spot that your field-detecting instrument occupies truly a vacuum?"

'No, not that exact spot,' Craig had to concede. "But, as I say, everywhere else in that region is a vacuum and the field is spread over that region. The bit of the field the instrument detects is therefore a bit of the field that's in that vacuum all over.'

'But you can't put a detector everywhere in that space,' Turner reasoned, 'especially not all at once. So how, in truly observational - or empirical - terms, can you justify this idea of the field as an im vacuo continuum?'

'This' replied Craig irritably, 'is the sort of "clever-clever" stuff that Philosophy departments waste good public money on, when physicists are crying out for funds for things they desperately need, for nuclear research and so on.'

Again, Charlie whispered behind his hand. 'Like hydrogen bombs, intercontinental missiles and things like that.'

Turner continued. 'But we're not "wasting public money" right here and now, are we? Surely, you wouldn't get upset if this was a friendly game of chess or darts. So think of it, as I say, as being just a game, which is for fun and doesn't, in this setting, have to be in any way "cost-effective".'

'Alright.' The professor made a grudging attempt at graciousness. 'So, what else can I say? I agree that the only way you can be a hundred-percent

certain that a field exists all over space all at once would be for your detector to be present everywhere in that space all at once. But that's not even remotely practical, so where does that argument get you?"

"Well, just think, if you did, somehow, have an instrument occupying the whole of space in that way, that space would no longer be a vacuum, would it? It would be a continuous medium. So my question remains. When do you ever determine the existence and measure the strength and direction of a field, truly im vacuo?

"That's very clever,' came the Professor's retort, 'but all it proves is that you can't, by definition, measure an in vacuo field by that particular means - by turning the vacuum into an all-over detecting medium. But that doesn't prove that fields i vacuo don't exist, which is what you seem to be arguing.'

'But by that same token,' Turner persisted, 'what direct or empirical evidence is there that they do exist since, as you agree, there's no way of proving it? That's the point 'm making. Any place you put an instrument to measure a "field im vacuo" only succeeds in measuring a non-vacuum, or medium at that spot. So you must surely see that we still have no evidence whatsoever for the existence of continuous electric or magnetic "fields in vacuo". Indeed, from what you've said, we may conclude that those instruments you employ to "detect" the field are really creating it at that point, that the instrument-system itself is a medium, or mediator of electromagnetic energy, and that the speed of light which it measures is always the speed relative to that instrument, or mediator, never actually in vacuo.'

The professor now found himself, again, too uncomfortably on the defensive and without any dignified excuse to back out. However, seeing what he thought was a fundamental flaw in Turner's argument, he returned to the attack.

"You're saying, then, that in between these instruments - which are, ultimately, atoms, of course - there is nothing, no field, no conduction, no process of light-conduction or anything. Does this mean that in between those energetic encounters between atoms, nothing whatsoever intervenes, that those interacting atoms are entirely unconnected? What sense can there be in that? What you're saying is ridiculous!"

"But isn't that precisely what quantum physics says, that all such interactions are quantised, discrete and indeterminate; that, in your words, those interacting atoms are entirely unconnected? So how is what I'm saying "ridiculous"?'

This response of Turner's rocked the professor right back on his heels. Seeing that he had no way out, he took his escape in bluster.

'So you tell me, then,' he said, 'if there's no light travelling in space - in the void or vacuum - between interacting atoms, then what is it that is measured so accurately as the "velocity of light"? You're surely not going to tell me that all those serious scientists who measured that velocity so meticulously somehow got it wrong!'

'No, not in the sense you're thinking of, that is, in the purely experimental or measurement sense.'

'But what other sense is there that you can you be thinking of? I can see no other alternative.'

"The sense of interpretation, of language, in the way we've been discussing,' Turner replied.

'Eh! How's that?" Craig was somewhat mystified by Turner's answer.

"Well, suppose I were to say to you J have proved beyond doubt that all mammals are vertebrates, would you accept that?"

'As long as you have sufficient experimental proof of it, of course.'

'And would you also accept it if 1 concluded from this that all vertebrates are mammals?"

'Of course not!' said the professor.

"Why not?'

'Because there are all sorts of vertebrates which are not mammals, such as reptiles, fish and so on.'

'So it doesn't logically follow,' continued Turner, 'that because all mammals are vertebrates, all vertebrates are mammals?'

'Obviously not! That doesn't follow logically, I agree.'

'So why would you accept that just because all velocities are measures of distance divided by time, any measure you find which is a distance divided by time has to be a velocity? As you say, that doesn't follow logically.'

"But what else could it be?" demanded the professor.

'A pure constant, Turner replied, 'giving the ratio of different conventional units of length and time, respectively, like, say, 186,000 miles or 300,000 kilometres of distance measure to the second of time measure in the measuring of space.'

"Yes, well, that's not physics as I know it,' Craig responded petulantly. 'I hope you're not going around pushing that kind of stuff. If so, then all you're doing is corrupting public perception.'

'Funny you should say that,' said Turner, 'since it was precisely that charge on which Socrates was arraigned and executed.'

'And not undeservedly, I reckon,' Craig retorted.

There was a pregnant pause as Craig, imperiously, handed his empty glass to Don. 'Same again, Don,' he said. 'Have one yourself and more of whatever these good folks would like.'

Don took the orders and filled up the glasses at the bar. Then the professor returned to the attack.

To Turner he said, 'So, what do you deduce from all this philosophical nonsense that can be of any use to physical science - or to mankind in any sector, for that matter.'

"What I deduce,' Turner replied, 'is that, logically, at the quantum level of atomic interaction, Aristotle's argument comes into play, that what separates those atoms involved in the interaction event is a void in the true literal sense of a zothing, which is tantamount to saying that they are not separated, hence that on that fundamental inter-action level the interacting atoms are virtually touching, in accordance with Newton's law of instantaneous and reciprocal action and reaction,'

'So,' Craig countered, 'on that basis, what is your logical alternative to standard orthodox physics? Does it have anything to offer that can, even remotely, improve our understanding of nature?'

'Turner was ready for this. "The logical alternative, as I've already said, is to think of that constant ratio, c, as just that; a constant, no more and no less, like the constant ratio of 2.25 pounds to the kilogramme, or 39.37 inches to the metre, which is a matter purely of convention in the arbitrary

choice of units. This is what Rémer originally discovered, a constant ratio of current observational distance-units to time-units in the measuring of space, implying only that the time allocated to a distant event is the time of seeing that event minus the distance-time in metres divided by the dimensional constant, c. This observational projection signifies the usual time-delay between the time of that distant object in itself and the observertime without implying the existence of anything 'travelling' in any medium, be it an electromagnetic field, ether or whatever.'

'And what do you imagine is the advantage of interpreting Rémer in that very strange way?'

"The advantage, I claim," said Turner, 'is that it explains why the durations of bodies, as they move away from us, slow down relatively to us.'

'T still don't see what possible advantage there can be in thinking like that,' stated the professor. "What you are describing is much more easily explained by what physicists call the Doppler effect! What's wrong with that?'

"What's wrong - if you put it that way - is that the Doppler effect has to do with the lengthening and shortening of wavelengths with relative motion, whereas what I'm describing has to do with the spectral periods of atoms directly. In this way, all those problems connected with the concept of light travelling in vacuo are completely expunged. There is also the advantage that, like it or not, this is the most conceptually economical and efficient empirical interpretation of Rémer's discovery.'

"What do you mean by "economical"?' asked Craig.

"Well, surely you know of the criterion of conceptual economy called "Ockham's Razor", which rules that in the search for scientific truth one should always choose the interpretation which requires the least number of assumptions or hypotheses.'

"What? Even if it causes confusion?'

13 Olaus Rémer was the Danish Astronomer who is accredited. with being the first to discover the distance-time relation subsequently interpreted as the 'speed of light in space'. This was from his observations, in 1676, of changes in the eclipse periods of Jupiter's moon Io, in direct correlation with changes in the distance from earth of that Jovian system due to the different orbits of those planets around the sun,

'Yes,' replied Turner patiently, 'because it may well be the old and comfortable standard interpretation which causes the confusion. This is what I claim about the standard interpretation of the discovery of what has been called "the speed of light", that it's all too easy to think of but only at the expense of the plainest, least conceptually embroidered interpretation.'

'What do you mean by truth?' retorted Craig. 'As I understand science there's no such thing as truth but only this or that most pragmatically suitable opinion.'

'Is that true or is it only your opinion?'

This flat contradiction was lost on the professor. 'So tell me again, in words I can understand, what is the advantage in thinking that way?'

Throughout this discourse, Noel had been listening intently. Now he decided to join in. 'Excuse me, sir,' he said, 'I think I can help here.'

The professor turned to Noel and eyed him up and down. 'Can you now,' he said, scathingly.

Noel was not to be intimidated. 'Yes,' he replied. 've been thinking hard about this, since our discussions, Dave, on the Lake and at the Lodge afterwards.'

'I remember,' said Turner, 'when we had that talk about "photons".'

'As you explained it to us, Dave,' Noel continued, 'if you think of the "photon" in the conventional way as a travelling particle, then it has two velocities. One is the distance between the source and the receiver divided by the time measured by the observer and the other is that same observational distance divided by the time taken by the photon itself. Okay, Dave?"

'Right, Noel,' replied Turner.

'So, Professor Craig,' Noel went on, 'you say you accept the theory of relativity?'

"Certainly," the professor retorted. 'I've said as much, haven't I?'

"Then you will know that the quantum of light-energy we call the "photon" has those two velocities, the one measured by the observer and the other by the "photon" itself.'

"Of course I do!' Craig replied huffily.

"Well, then, you will also know that relative to the observer, that photon has the finite speed c, the speed of that same photon relative to itself - that is, intrinsically - is instantaneous.'

"Yes, that's elementary,' Craig replied, "but so what?"

"Well, doesn't it occur to you,' queried Noel, 'that in that case, the photon cannot, in itself, be said to "travel" but that it is a quantum event occurring at the source atom and receiver atom simultaneously?'

'If you like, but where are you going with this?'

The rest of the group were wondering the same thing but suspected that Noel had the professor cornered.

'So, regarding what we discussed, earlier, about language, isn't this the same as saying that far from being something having motion, the so-called "photon" is no more than a non-moving element in the phenomenon of motion?'

"What on earth can you mean by that?' asked Craig, genuinely nonplussed by Noel's question.

"Well, as I say, I've been turning this over in my mind since that discussion I mentioned,' answered Noel, 'and now that it has sunk in, I can fully see its relevance to a commonsense understanding of quantum physics. For instance, there is the model for this that you mentioned, Dave, of the cinematograph, where the phenomenon of motion is created out of sequences of still photographs, or "stills". To me that's a perfect model for solving what Feynman described as the problem of understanding quantum physics.'

At this point, Charlie intervened. 'I don't know if I've mentioned this before, but it's what perception psychologists call the "phi phenomenon'. It's used by advertisers to create the impression of motion by means, for example, of light bulbs switching on and off in sequence.'

'Thanks, Charlie,' said Turner, pleased that this point of his had been taken up and thought about.

Addressing Turner, Craig said, loudly, 'So what you are suggesting to these good people is that all motion is really no more than an illusion produced by some great Cinematograph Projectonist in the Sky, that all the laws of mechanics are laws governing a cinematographic illusion!'

Turner replied as evenly as he could. 'At the quantum level, yes, but not, of course, at the everyday observational level, the level of what everyone, including our plants, pets and other animals, take as the primary reality - the only exception being, it seems,' he added, mischievously, 'classical physicists.'

Craig's reddening face betrayed his anger. 'I know of no self-respecting physicist who would have any truck with that absurd suggestion,' he retorted.

'Really?' Turner answered him. "What about your famous physicist, Ernst Mach? His professional approach to physics was precisely as I have described, the philosophical approach known as phenomenalism. You've surely heard of that!'

"Well, yes,' Craig admitted, disconcerted by Turner's point. "Mach was, as you say, a revered physicist, but, as far as I know, only in connection with "Mach's Principle" in cosmology and the "Mach number" in aerodynamics. In my training as a physicist, I can't remember anything being said in connection with Mach about any of this sort of stuff! I know it was said that Mach was a philosopher as well as a physicist but I can't see how that purely philosophical aspect of his work could possibly have had any relevance to practical physics.'

Turner replied. 'It introduces a whole new paradigm of thought in which physics is no longer seeking for preconceived hidden mechanisms of things underlying physical phenomena but looking for the most correct and most logically efficient - that is to say, the truest - linguistic description of the phenomena themselves.'

'So it's all about words. Is that what you're saying?' said the professor in exasperation.

'About /anguage, yes,' responded 'Turner, 'the language in which our perceptions and conceptions are couched, the language which organises the way we think and without which we wouldn't be able to think at all. Language has the unique facility of creating either an understanding or a misunderstanding of physical reality. ft also has the capacity for creating, in science, chimeras - monsters of pure imagination, in other words, which do no more for science than misrepresent reality and cause confusion.'

The professor couldn't follow Turner's train of thought. "To me,' he said, 'that speech of yours is just a lot of words with no practical meaning as far as I can see. Are you or are you not saying that in the end, physics is just like that, about nothing more than just words?'

'T'm saying, again, that there's no use of language that is ever "just words". Words form organised systems of thought called paradigms. These, as you must surely know, are ways of thinking - mindsets, if you like - chat can be so fundamentally different as to be incommensurate with one another, so that statements made in the one may be meaningless in the other, and vice versa.'

'Like what?"

'Like the Ptolemaic, or pre-Copernican, description of the world compared and contrasted to that of Copernicus and Galileo - or, say, the "phlogiston" theory of thermodynamics compared to that of Clausius and Boltzmann and,' Turner added, 'that of Boltzmann compared to the later work of Shannon.'

'So you see yourself, then,' said Craig, almost with amusement, 'as launching a new paradigm, a whole new way of thinking to compete with our present one. I'd sure hate to have your nerve in a tooth!'

Unperturbed, Turner replied. 'All I do is to point out the logical flaw in the traditional interpretation of light as something having a "velocity", the "constant velocity c". On that basis a whole new paradigm logically defines itself. All I do is to follow that logical trail, leaving my marks behind, as it were, for others to follow if they wish or, otherwise, to leave it alone.'

The professor remained implacable. He was far from impressed by what he regarded as this lesson from Turner. He could see it only as a scurrilous attack on himself and his profession, to say nothing of his dignity. His anger could scarcely be contained.

"You're arguing,' he said, 'like a typical philosopher with his feet planted firmly in mid air! So, let's drop all this nonsense and get back to basics. As an experimental physicist I want to know what's your justification for this "new paradigm" of yours? Where's your math, for instance? I see no experimental evidence, no formulas nor anything that I can recognise as being even remotely like physics as I know it. It's just words, words, words, nothing but words.'

Turner was tempted to retort, 'Just like a typical physicist with his head planted firmly in the ground,' but realising that this man was some kind of friend of Don's, he thought better of it and bit his tongue. Before he could frame a civil reply, there was another interruption from Noel.

'But sir,' he began politely, 'this suggestion of Dave's is not to be dismissed out of hand. It's surely something that should be seriously considered. Come to think about it, every significant development in physics was made in this way that Dave is suggesting. It's well said that omelettes can't be made without breaking eggs. As I understand it so far, this new paradigm Dave is talking about is no more than the natural result of following the implications of interpreting light in a more efficient way, a way that is logically there for the taking.'

'Then why is nobody else thinking that way? It would cause havoc!' said Craig defiantly.

'So, okay,' responded Noel, 'it turns physics upside down, making what we are accustomed to thinking of as "appearances" the fundamental startingpoint of physics instead of our tradition of starting with the old ideas of "atoms" and so on. This makes good sense to me, because it puts physics back where it surely should always be, on a footing of actual appearances of things in direct and instrumental observation rather than on ideas in the heads of physicists about things unseen, things which, in the last analysis, are no more than creations of a language which may well have exceeded its sell-by date, by centuries and perhaps even millennia in some cases.'

Walter, who had remained quietly attentive throughout this argument, now took the opportunity to address the professor.

'As I understand it, what Noel is describing here is nothing more strange than what you physicists call "empiricism"; the dependence on direct observation that is sometimes insisted upon by your colleagues in interpreting and reporting the results of experiments. All Dave is doing, it seems to me, is taking that empirical method to its logical extremity, or phenomenalism which, as we've already pointed out, was the view taken by your late colleague, Ernst Mach. As I see it, it is no more than what I would regard as a "radical empiricism".'

Craig was not appreciative of Walter's view on this. "Look here,' he said, '"empiricism" is all very well, but this "new paradigm" we're taking about

turns physical reality on its head, as some kind of shadow show. How can any sane-minded physicist possibly think that the solid, sensible world of mass, length, time and all the other properties and qualities of things are no more than observational projections. That surely takes "empiricism" too far?

Helen took this opportunity to intervene. 'Are you saying, then, Professor, that Mach was insane?'

Craig had been startled by Helen's intervention, as indeed had Noel, but it did nothing for the professor's temper.

'Now see here, young lady,' he huffed, 'you're just twisting my words.'

'So words do have meaning for you, sir,' responded Helen, sweetly.

Noel returned to the Professor's point about things being no more than observational projections.

'Why,' he asked him, 'shouldn' things be observational projections? You must surely know that by such means, very convincing and fully interactive virtual reality scenarios and. holograms can be created by computers, with everything in those scenarios appearing to obey ordinary laws of physics.'

Plainly, the professor did not appreciate this answer. "You're much too smart for me, young man! he retorted, in a tone clearly meant to signify the very opposite of what he was saying. Haughtily, he turned his attention from Noel to Turner, his original adversary.

'Youre mad!' he retorted. 'You must be, to peddle such a suggestion among these impressionable young people. If any students of mine came out with anything like that, their exam papers would be cancelled. And if they persisted, they would be shown the door. How can you, in all conscience, promulgate this kooky idea of it all being reduced to a phantasmagorical shadow-show! And,' he added, 'as I say, none of this is physics as I understand it, What it is, I don't know. It's not even philosophy, if 1 guess it right.'

Helen broke in again. 'Sure it is, Professor,' she reminded him. 'It's called phenomenalism, as Walter mentioned. For those who've studied philosophy as I have, it's a well-known and highly respectable kind of philosophical approach which was taken by Ernst Mach, a physicist like yourself. What Dave's doing, here - and Noel,' she added, glancing in Noel's direction,

'seems to me to be extending Mach's legacy into this present day, bringing that approach right up to date.'

'Phenomenalism! PahY shouted the professor, his face now flushed with anger. "To me that's the same as subjective idealism, or more like the sort of playing with words which is called "Linguistic Philosophy" by some fairies in my university's Arts department. No self-respecting physicist would have any truck with it. If they did, they'd be drummed out of the profession. I really don't know why I wasted my time getting into this idiotic discussion.'

Turner tried to calm the professor. 'But I wasn't aware,' he said, quietly, 'that we were doing professional physics, here. I thought we'd agreed that this was to be just a game, a game with words, or language, if you like, but a friendly game, nevertheless, with nothing hanging on the outcome.'

The professor was not to be mollified.

"Well, I'm sorry.' he said, 'you'll have to leave me out of these pointless games. I'm a busy man, much too busy to get involved in these cloudcuckoo philosophical issues, which can never amount to anything.'

'Too busy? What? While on holiday? Carter burst out on Turner's behalf. He had been amazed at Turner's patient good humour with this man, who typified in redneck intellectual pomposity all that he, Carter, despised.

'My "holiday", as you call it, is a land-development project,' said Craig importantly. 'It's the realities of that kind of enterprise which concern the likes of practical men like me, not rabbitting on about what reality means in some philosophical heaven!'

'Don't you enjoy the natural surroundings, then?" asked Marian.

'OF course I do. I was sitting on my tractor yesterday morning during my tea break, when all the machinery was quiet, admiring the view and listening to the sounds of nature.' He stood up. 'Anyway, I'll leave you to your holiday games, gentlemen. I've got to be up early tomorrow to get the skidders organised.' Best of luck with your ideas, Mr. Turner, but I doubt if they'll ever make any money for you!'

14 See Appendix 1. 15 A skidder is a huge machine chat the timber industry uses for clearing forests.

'I don't doubt that you're right, there!' Turner replied cheerfully.

"Well, I'm glad we agree on something,' was the acid reply. "Goodnight, then, gentlemen - and ladies,' he added, as an afterthought.

With that, Professor Craig briskly got up and left.

Chapter 9: The Lodge Again

Logos

After the professor had left there were some moments of silence. Then Don spoke.

"Hell, I'm sorry I let you in for that, Dave.'

'Don't worry, Don. I thoroughly enjoyed it,' Turner reassured him.

'T think you made the learned professor just a bit cross, there, Dave,' chuckled Carter.

'Just a dittle bit, eh!" exclaimed Noel. The others laughed, easing any tension the confrontation might have created.

'I wonder,' mused Rick, 'where he's operating - with those bulldozers, skidders and all?'

'Back down the valley,' Don informed them. "There are going to be all sorts of people like him coming up here from now on. Some day, this place will probably end up full to creaking like the Bronx. But it brings me business, I suppose, so what can J say, especially when you consider that I was in the forefront of these. When I'm older, the remoteness of this place, especially in winter, probably won't have quite the same appeal. You've got to be a realist, as our professor friend said. So, as long as he doesn't tread on my toes, I'll not tread on his, and we should get along fine. If it gets too crowded here, Megan and J will just sell up and move on.'

The prospect of the wilderness becoming another "Bronx" brought an element of gloom. The group sat for a while in thoughtful silence, which Noel eventually broke.

"That guy sure was arrogant, big time! I guess he regarded his profession as the standard by which all things should be judged.'

This charge of "arrogance" from young Findlay made Carter smile.

'I wonder, though, how representative our friend is of physicists in general,' he remarked.

"Well, they certainly aren't all like that,' said Turner, "but 've met more than a few like him. His idea of "fields" was fairly typical, though. It's interesting, isn't it,' he added, 'how these followers of Einstein are so happy to kick out the old "ether" through the front door and re-admit it through the tradesman's entrance under a different name.'

'Tm sorry he left,' complained Walter. 'I had the feeling we were getting on to something quite important. But now I'd like to take up that argument with you, Dave, if 1 may.'

'You're welcome!' said Turner.

"You said, Dave, that there's no such thing, by literal definition, as a "void" - that there's no such thing as nothing. That, if I recall my Greek history correctly, is an Aristotelian argument, as I think was mentioned earlier.'

"Yes, that's right,' replied Turner. "The Greeks of that era were well aware of the self-contradiction built into that word "void". The peculiar way in which they used it was only because there seemed to be no way of explaining the separations of bodies other than by supposing that this "nothing" was something that stretched between them. This was an idea first put forward by the atomist, Democritus. The alternative to that was that these separations were only apparent, that distance, in itself, was no part of substantial reality but just a subjective dimension, a "convention" supplied by the observer. This, of course, was later taken up in the eighteenth century by the German philosopher, Kant.'

'Germany must be full to the gills with these philosophers and scientists!" exclaimed Rick. 'How come they ever found time to make war?"

'I guess it was their philosophers, like Nietzsche, who thought up the reasons and the scientists who provided the means,' was Charlie's cynical riposte.

Walter could see that the discussion was in danger of being side-tracked. 'Let's leave that to the politicians to sort out, shall we? I'd rather deal with Dave's argument.' Returning once more to his original point, he went on, 'So in that way of thinking - the Aristotelian way, that is - the observer and the things observed were regarded as all part of the same reality, not as things apart.' Seeing the puzzled expression on the faces of some of his listeners, he went on, "What I mean is that reality wasn't split into mental and material in our modern, Cartesian, meaning of these terms. By the

way, Dave, I found that studying Kant was extremely difficult. But as I understood it, Kant showed how Newton's physics could be recast entirely in terms of classifying and categorising phenomena - observation, as we've discussed it. After Berkeley, I think you could say that Kant was the first relativist.'

'So your ideas aren't so radical after all, Dave,' said Don. "They've been around a long time, according to Walter, here.'

Turner was happy to agree. "Yes, Don, they have. As the old saying goes, there's nothing new under the sun. But, as has happened so often in the past, some things of real value become discarded and buried as new ones become more fashionable. I feel, though, that so far as the most recent findings of modern relativistic and quantum science are concerned, some of those old ideas fit better than our present ones.'

'I guess,' commented Marian, 'we tend, automatically, to think that "old" means out-of-date and behind-the-times. It's difficult to accept that earlier generations may have been wiser in many ways than we are.'

'Td certainly go along with that, Marian,' said Walter, 'take the Eleatics, for instance - those Greeks who settled in Elea, in southern Italy. They were monists, who believed in the total one-ness of reality. It was the later ones, the Ionian Greeks, as you mentioned, Dave, such as the atomists Leucippus and Democritus, who introduced the idea of the extended "void" to explain the separations of things. It was they who split reality into "substance" and "appearance". They believed that the substance, or reality, of things lay behind and beyond their appearances, in the form of infinitely hard or material "atoms" separated by "void".'

Carter recalled that he and Turner had discussed this in relation to the function of language in defining "existence" as what is conferred on the subject of a statement by some variant of the verb "to be". He was wrestling with this in his mind when Marian remarked, somewhat inconsequentially, 'Tonia is in Turkey, isn't it? Ir's odd, don't you think, that none of those Greeks was in Greece at all!'

'Perhaps it is,' said Turner. 'Anyway, the monists, or one-ists from Elea, were mainly opposed to the Ionians and could be thought of, if you like, as the fathers of modern relativism, long before Berkeley and Mach. They believed that all dimensions of extension and duration, of materiality in

effect, were just relative or, rather, subjective aspects, or perspectives, of a universal unified whole. This was neither material nor mental in the modern sense of those words, but neutral between the two, a thing of pure reason, you might say, in the manner of Kant's dissertation. It was, as they used to say, "the eternal and indivisible, Eleatic One".'

Rick felt he was going down for the third time in a welter of erudition. But he hung on to what he thought was the essential nub of it. 'It sure must have been difficult to think like that!' he commented.

'For us, at this stage in history, no doubt,' Turner agreed. "But at that formative stage, when what we now call "philosophy" was brand new and exciting, instead of the dry-as-dust, academic subject as we now think of it, it was probably no more difficult for them to think in the way they did than it is for us to think in our way. It's only because Ionian materialism, the science of "atoms and void," gained such a circumstantial advantage and became so central to our modern way of thinking that we fancy it's somehow "easier" to think in our pluralistic, atomised way than in the monistic or holistic way. Who knows how we might be thinking now if history had gone the other way, the way of the Eleatic monists instead of that of the atomists?'

'Is that what you've been arguing, then, Dave,' Carter asked, 'that we should unpick our scientific history and re-weave it along Eleatic lines?"

'Well, knowing how deeply committed our culture has become to the Ionian "atoms-in-a-void" way of thinking,' replied Turner, 'one wouldn't make that suggestion lightly. What I've been suggesting is that something like that alternative is now becoming increasingly necessary in view of the conceptual crisis facing modern atomistic science. But, leaving aside the question of whether such a switch is now socially feasible, I don't think there's any harm in contemplating, from the standpoint of uncommitted philosophy, what that logical alternative might be - so far as we can possibly re-create it in modern terms, which is now, undoubtedly, extremely difficult to achieve conceptually.'

'It seems, then,' suggested Noel, 'that we've re-invoked the problem the Greeks faced originally, of how to separate objects from one another when there's no such thing, by definition, as a space thought of as, literally, a

"nothing" or "void". How do we solve that problem - without creating a paradox, by giving that "nothing" a real extension, in the way those atomists subsequently did?'

'Which,' said Walter, ruefully, 'would simply repeat our scientific and philosophical history right up to the present time.'

'With all the problems and perplexities it has generated,' added Turner.

'Hey, we seem to be going round and round in circles,' observed Rick.

'Still, it's an intriguing problem,' said Noel, 'and a classical one. Vd greatly like to try to solve it.'

'Me, too,' agreed Walter.

'But how?' asked Rick. 'If it took those Greeks so long to try and sort it out and keep getting it wrong, and since it hasn't been solved for thousands of years by all those generations of brainy dudes, what chance have we got?'

'And are we really qualified to attempt it?" wondered Helen.

'Well,' Turner answered her, 'any one of us, here, is as qualified as they were; and the world they studied was the same as ours, with the same ground, the same sky, the same stars and so on. So all we have to do,' he suggested, 'instead of being blinded by that tradition of science which has grown out of those early probings, is to realise the distinct advantage we now have, of an overview of accumulated experience which is the largest in history. If we are not too educationally enslaved by it - as the professor obviously was - it may reveal to us, in hindsight, what went wrong and perhaps suggest how to put it right.'

There were murmurs of assent all round. 'So, okay,' Turner continued, 'et's see what we can do - why not, eh? Our argument seems to have turned history full circle, so that now, just like those Greeks, we are right back at the start of it all. Somehow we have to make sense of the question of how to separate things other than by a reified nothing.'

'A what nothing?' asked Rick.

'Reified,' answered Helen, 'meaning "made into a real thing", from the Latin word res, a thing.'

'Thanks, Helen,' said Rick, 'I'm sure learning a helluva lot from you folks. Okay, we're talking about a nothing made into a thing. Carry on, Dave, I'm with you now.'

But it was Walter who continued. 'It's interesting, isn't it, how this ageold question of physical separation has been resurrected by our discussion about "light travelling". If we agree with Aristotle, that physical separation cant be, literally, a nothing, then it has to be a continuous something - a plenum, as the Greeks called it. There's no contradiction in thinking of that as extended.'

'But,' said Turner, 'that idea of an extended and continuous being, or plenum, was just as difficult because it made motion inconceivable. As the Greeks reasoned, if that substance was everywhere, then it left no space for a body to move into. With everywhere full of that substance, nothing could shift from where it was to anywhere else. Everything would be jammed solid. But it obviously isn't, which, of course, is why the Ionians invented the extended "void" and exploded the Eleatic "substance" into it, in those fragments they called "atoms" in order to give it room to move. But that created all those equally insoluble problems about a somethingnothing that Aristotle talked about. You see the dilemma they were in!"

'I guess so,' added Walter, 'because by that time, they were no longer thinking of "substance" as a thing of reason but more in the modern way, as matter.'

'Right,' agreed Turner, 'so all those confusions to which we are heir were, by then, well on their way. To give that substance - which was now material substance -- room to move, as I say, they had to shatter it into "matter" and "void", yet they couldn't see how that void, a mere nothing, could give that separation.'

Noel had a thought. 'Didn't the concept of "the aether" develop out of that idea of a plenum?'

'Very likely, Noel,' Turner replied. 'The "aether" was probably as good a compromise between "nothing" and "something" - the void and the plenum - as they could manage; a kind of hedging concept of a quasi-substance which was neither a true substance nor a nothing, but which pervades everything, everywhere. It survives almost intact in the modern idea of a "field-continuum". As I said, that concept of an "aether" was thrown out through the front door and readmitted in disguise, at the back.'

'So you are saying, then,' Carter commented, 'that the "aether" was a compromise, a "watering-down" of substance with this "nothing", or void,

to produce a kind of "fluid", thin enough to accommodate movement, so that matter could move about yet not so thick as to stop movement altogether. And that, you say, Dave, is the sort of theoretical jiggery-pokery which has led to modern science.'

'You could indeed say that, Tony.'

'But of course,' remarked Noel, 'that "plenum", in any of those forms, ancient or modern, is of no more use to us nowadays than a "void".'

Don was struggling with all this. "Why exactly is chat a problem?' he asked. 'I know we've discussed it, but will someone please remind me.'

Walter obliged. 'Well, as you may recall, Don, Dave and Noel pointed out in our little chat with the professor, if that "aether" existed, our motion relative to it could be measured. But it can't, so there is no aether.'

'Boy, don't we have the Great Grand-Daddy of all dilemmas here!' observed Charlie.

'Let me get this straight,' put in Rick, trying to summarise, for his own satisfaction, what had been said. "There ain't no such thing as nothing to keep things separate, so everything's together, packed solid, so things can't move. Right?'

'That's neatly put,' said Carter.

'But things do move,' Rick continued, 'so there's gotta be a real nothing, an extended nothing, to allow that motion. But an "extended nothing" makes no sense, which sends us round in circles . . .'

'Absolutely,' agreed Carter.

'And,' Rick went on, 'the trick of mixing nothing and something to make an aether don't work either, because there ain't anything that can be detected.'

*That's about the size of it, Rick! said Turner.

'Holy cow, what a mess!' breathed Rick in something approaching exasperation.

Silence fell on the little group while they sipped their drinks and savoured this remark. Helen looked around at the faces of her companions, some in shadow, some illuminated by the small table lamps which cast pools of soft light against the dark background of seasoned log walls. One could close
