ones eyes a little, she thought, and imagine having travelled back in time and space to the beginnings of philosophy in ancient Greece, or to the medieval monasteries, perhaps, when men like these pondered on such questions by candlelight and rushlight. Strange, she thought, that such an unusual gathering of minds should have taken place on this holiday. Yet is it so strange, she mused. Nowadays, the word "symposium" has an academic ting to it as the sort of thing that is organised at universities. But in the Golden Age of Greece, these symposia were no more than feasting, drinking and discussing groups just like these, in which men got together freely to present and discuss those ideas, so novel at the time, which were to form so much of the basis for future civilization. And what men can do in one era, she told herself, men can surely do in other eras if their minds are not over-encumbered by concerns for brute survival and social conformity. But why only men? Did any women take part in those discussions, she wondered. Unlikely, she thought. Women in those days were supposed to stick to domestic duties - like Socrates' wife, Xanthippe, who history records as being no more than a shrew. She was glad she was a modern woman with the advantages of a modern education, fully capable of holding her own in these sorts of discussions. The feeling of emancipation and involvement excited her. As she brought her attention back to the present, 'Turner was saying,

'I's a sobering thought, isn't it, to realise that this "mess" as you call it, Rick, has remained unresolved right up until the present time. It's reemerged with the failed experiments of Michelson and Morley to measure our motion relative to the aether and with the tortuous reasoning about it that subsequently led to Einsteinian relativity. Unfortunately, Einstein cast that theory in terms of Ionian atomism with its associated view of atoms giving off little bits of themselves to interact with one another in the spacetravelling form of light. And this, of course, raises again the dilemma of how, in the void, these separated bits of matter are supposed to interact. This dilemma is now so entrenched that few are prepared to tackle it. So, despite being unresolved, that dilemma is still there, God help us, right at the root of our modern physics'!

16 See the EPR paradox, FN Ch. 9.

Noel recalled something from the earlier discussion he and Rick had had with Turner and Carter on the boat on the lake. He felt that the answer to Walter's question about of how to separate things other than by a reified void should have been somewhere along the lines of Turner's cinematic model of quantum interaction. Since that discussion, Noel's keen mind had been digesting it, and now he felt he would like to say something about it. However, he realised that apart from himself, Rick and Carter, none of the others had been privy to that discussion. Besides, he was not sure that this "cinematic" idea was sufficiently matured in his mind for him to broach the subject at this stage, So, for the moment, he contained himself and decided that it might be more useful to let the present discussion follow its own natural course. What was being discussed was interesting anyway, he felt, if only from a historical perspective, so he decided just to sit back and listen. Walter was speaking.

'So, Dave, without the advantage, at this late stage in our history, of being able to start with a "clean slate" as those Greeks did, how do you suppose we can now do any better?'

'Well, Walter, let's remember that in those early days they had some peculiar difficulties in getting their act together. Compared with today, the geographical separations between the different schools of thought were, as you know, huge and the communicational links between them comparatively poor. So the various ways of thinking tended to dissociate, both geographically and temporally. Besides, we know far more facts about the workings of nature, nowadays, than they could ever have imagined - even though we don't understand those workings any better than they did. That ought to give us some kind of advantage, don't you think? With our new knowledge and historical hindsight, and those facts at our disposal, we ought now to be able to continue the dialogue they started, but in a much more efficient and coherent way.'

'So, what does that hindsight tell us?' Walter asked.

'T can only tell you what I think,' Turner replied. 'As I say, it seems to me that all those problems we are heir to arose in the switch from the Eleatic to the Ionic, or Democritan, way of thinking. The Eleatic "substance", as then understood, was no more than a part of perception, a thing of reason

applied in the analysis of natural phenomena, but for the Ionians, as you said, Walter, "substance" became a thing of solid matter. But we can cancel that historical move and think instead in the way we agreed at the restaurant, and which I was describing to you, Tony, when we went to repair the trailer. In that non-Ionian way of thinking, the substance of a statement or proposition is simply the subject of correct predication.'

The others, except for Carter, stared at him in surprise at what was, for them, this completely unexpected answer.

'I think you'd better clarify that, Dave,' advised Carter. 'These guys werent in on that discussion.'

'You're right, Tony, I'd forgotten that. Well, okay, as we discussed it, "substance" did not originally mean material substance, as we now think of it. That meaning was a special one coined by the Ionians to solve the problem they'd created...'

"That one of how things can move in a solid world,' interrupted Rick.

"Yes, Rick,' acknowledged Turner. 'And with their materialised substance and no zon-substance, or void, to allow for motion and change, for the Ionians, everything substantial should be in a state of complete gridlock, with nothing moving or changing place. This is what gave rise to what came to be known as Zeno's Paradox, which was simply that all motion was complete illusion.'

'So the paradox, I guess,' put in Noel, 'was that motion and change were facts of experience, while their reasoning ruled that motion and change are impossible.'

'And the only way they could avoid this paradox,' Turner continued, 'was to spatialise the void, which was originally nothing, making it a something into which their materialised substance could be fragmented and given room to move. But what "substance" meant, before that, was simply what "stands under" some set of perceived qualities or properties.'

Turner paused for a moment to allow his listeners time for this to sink in, then he went on, 'In that pristine way of thinking, the substance of a set of qualities or properties - the proper terms are predications or predicates - is the subject of those predicates. For instance, the substance of the statement "Socrates is squat, bald and ugly" is Socrates, and the predicate-system

is the set of those words "squat", "bald" and "ugly". And the verb "is", in this statement about Socrates, is a word signifying the existence of the substance, Socrates, in terms of those predications.'

'I see,' said Helen. 'So instead of there being just oe substance, a shattered Eleatic One, which the Ionians thought of as matter, in the other way of thinking, the linguistic way, there were as many substances as there were things described in language.'

'Precisely, Helen. There was no single, underlying and undifferentiated absolute substance as the Ionians thought of it and as we tend to think of it in classical science today. In other words, substance was regarded not as something underlying and apart from all perception and language but as of perception and language - true language, that is, the acme or end-aim of all linguistic development, in science at any rate. It is what we might call the 'language of nature' and which the Greeks called logos.'

Walter exclaimed enthusiastically, 'I'm with you now, Dave! That concept of gos is certainly different from any of those other things we've discussed, such as "plenum", "aether", "universe" and so on. And it's absolutely consistent with your "linguistic" approach to science!'

Carter caught Walter's enthusiasm. 'Of course, and as a kind of language its contents wouldn't be bits of "material substance", which need a nonsubstance, or "void" to move about in, like the Greek "atoms". There's no need. for a physically extended "void" to separate bits of information - which was your point about the "photon", I think, Dave.'

'Yes, I see that, too,' said Noel, 'Nor do you need "voids" and suchlike to distinguish those character-complexes we talked about, that day at the lake. This makes sense. It all fits!'

'Whar's this "character-complex"?' asked Walter, who had not been party to the discussion at the lake.

'It's something like a tune,' Carter told him, 'which has the same form regardless of what different instruments it's played on or in what key.'

'That,' suggested Charlie, 'sounds to me like what some perceptionpsychologists call a Gestalt, a German word which in English is something like "form" or "figure".'

"Yes, Charlie, the meaning "form?" fits very well,' Turner agreed. 'It's the word which generations of philosophers have used for what Tony calls a character-complex. In medieval times they used the words "species" or "universal" - even "Idea", as Plato did - to signify much the same thing. What's important to note is that to think of an object as an informational character-complex, an integrated form, Gestalt, or whatever you like to call it, is very different from thinking of it as no more than a collection of solid

> atoms.

There was a pause while the others considered this.

Then Walter broke the silence. "To get back to the logos; undoubtedly it's now very difficult, at this distance in time, for us to understand what that meant to the Greeks.'

'I second that!' remarked Rick.

And yet,' Walter continued, 'this logos is not so unfamiliar, really. It's there in the language we use all the time, even now.'

"How's that?' queried Rick.

'I guess you're familiar with words like "psychology", "geology", "physiology", "cosmology", "anthropology", "theology" . . . you name it.'

'Sure,' said Rick, 'like "astrology", "meteorology" . . . there's hundreds.'

'Sure thing,' replied Walter. 'So what I'm saying is that all those "logy" endings stem from the word logos. So, "physiology" studies the logos of the anatomy, "geology" the study of the logos of the earth and its origins, and the same for all the other aspects of the ogos. "Logic", especially, is derived from it.'

'Gee!' marvelled Rick. 'So all those sciences just study different bits of logos. Now I get what you mean! I thought it was an ancient word that noone uses any more. But as you say, we use it all the time!'

"That's right. Rick,' said Walter. 'It surely is surprising to think how much "logos" remains a part of modern language. As I recall,' he said to the group as a whole, 'the word was originally coined by the Hellenic Greeks to mean the power of natural reason, the kind of reason or rationality we recognise in the nature of things, especially in science.'

'I agree, Walter,' put in Turner. 'In its original meaning, "logos" was entirely objective reason. This was not human reason and language but the

language that, as I say, human reason and language aspire to. It meant, in effect, simply relating. It gave order and regulation to natural processes, making the whole system of things rational in the way that makes science possible, hence all those "-ologies" which science has divided itself into.'

'If I recall it correctly,' added Noel, 'it was Aristotle who first tried to sort out the order and regulation in language with what came to be called "logic", which we still have today in commonsense reason and in computers. So, far from being a thing of the past, logos is still very much with

us.'

'Yes,' agreed Turner, 'you might say that Aristotle, with his eye on the way nature was organised, was the first to classify and divide the language we use into its various sectors, called "classes" and "categories".'

Helen, who had been listening for some time in interested silence, suddenly asked, 'Doesn't Christianity identify the Logos with Christ - in the capital el sense, that is?"

'That's so, Helen,' replied Walter, a little taken aback, but nonetheless pleased that this question had arisen. 'You'll find that identification made in the Gospel of St. John. But the Bible renders Logos - in your "capital el" sense a mite inadequately, I've always felt - as "the Word", which is also identified with the divine persona of Christ, which is somewhat puzzling to readers of that passage.'

'But wouldn't you say that was due to the peculiar development of the logos idea in Hebrew philosophy?' asked Turner.

'I guess so,' answered Walter, 'but by then, although the term itself remained very much in use, its meaning had become vague, due mainly to Plato who taught, in opposition to Aristotle, that there is a radical separation between mental "ideas" and material "things". For him, "ideas" or "forms" - what we've been calling "character-complexes" - were things of heaven, and their substances were things of earth. This was the beginning of the idea of the soul being consigned to heaven - or hell, as the case might be - and the body consigned to earth. I'd better say that even as a minister I don't subscribe to that notion. I believe that, as Jesus said, "the kingdom of God is within", not without, and certainly not somewhere "beyond the sky" in any sense. I'm with Voltaire in saying that I don't

believe in a God of empty spaces, whether between atoms, stars, galaxies or whatever.'

'And that kick-started the rise of "dualism", as we discussed at the cabin,' added Helen.

'So it isn't surprising,' commented Carter, 'that in removing dualism, we allow the logos which, as Walter has shown, is still very much with us, to revert to its original secular, lower-case el, meaning.'

Yes,' Turner agreed. 'logos, in its original pre-Christian meaning, comes pretty close to what we've been discussing as "language" - in its broadest sense of "the language of nature", of course,' he reminded them, 'not in the narrow and eternally fixed "Ordinary Language" sense of the Oxbridge Linguistic scholars.'

'It seems to me,' said Charlie, 'that thinking in that original way, the followers of Aristotle should have had none of the problems of substance and language that the others had. For them it should have all been a "piece of cake", a simple matter of logical classification and division. It makes you wonder why on earth they had to complicate things. I've always found Aristotle's rules of logic perfectly adequate for all practical and forensic purposes.'

Noel chimed in. "What strikes me, though, is that despite what you say, Dave, about the Oxbridge scholars, what modern language philosophers and information-theorists understand by "language", or "communication" is more than just a little bit similar to what the Greeks understood by "logos".

"That's surely interesting,' declared Walter. 'I'd say it reflects all we've been saying about the centrality of "language" in these discussions of ours.'

Helen was wondering what the other diners at the lodge were making of these little huddles of intellectual discussion which were sometimes quite vociferous. However, most of these other guests were noisy enough on their own and it was only when these diners began to disperse that she became self-conscious about it. So she now lowered her voice.

'I can see, Dave,' she said, "how logos would answer those problems which bothered the Greeks, about how things can be different from one

another without being separated by watered-down nothings, like "aether" and so on, but can you spell out for me how this connects with what you were saying to the Professor about light in space? I've somehow lost the connection with that.'

From the way Marian nodded her head, Helen had obviously spoken for her too. Don and Rick were also reminded of this. Carter felt he could answer Helen's question on the basis of what he and Turner had discussed on their trip to mend the boat-trailez, but he left it to his old friend to answer.

'Well, Helen,' explained Turner, 'light consists of little irreducible amounts of energy called quanta. If we cease thinking of those quanta as "photons", as the professor believes, suggesting bits of matter travelling in the "void" between atoms, or as "wave-packets" propagated in some modern plenum, or "field", then we may more suitably think of them simply as instantaneous and non-extended quantum events. . .'

Marian interrupted. 'I'm sorry, Dave, but I just don't get it.

'No, it's my fault, my dear,' he replied. 'I tend to forget that not everyone is au fait with physics, so let me put it this way. I guess you understand that from the magnetic blips on a video-tape the viewer projects all the extension and movement of the scenario. This shift away from classical materialism very nicely combines the true essence of both relativity and quantum theory and, in so doing, gets rid of all those problems we've discussed about separation, motion and so on which are created by the classical way of thinking.'

'T think I see what you mean,' said Marian, somewhat hesitantly.

'Or you might think of it,' offered Carter, 'as not having to worry about what happens to light when it is supposedly travelling all on its own in space, unseen by anything and interacting with nothing, which is something like wondering where the things you read about in a book go to when you shut the book, or how they behave in the book when no-one's reading it.'

'Thanks, Tony,' smiled Marian, 'I like that way of putting it. No offence, Dave, but I understood Tony's explanation better.'

'Not to worry, my dear,' replied Turner, 'I'm glad it helped.'

'Can you set me right on something, Dave?' asked Charlie.

'If I can, Charlie. Go ahead.'

'On that subject of light-illumination being instantaneous, what would be your answer to our buddy with the bulldozers if he asked you what, in those purely informational terms, is to be understood by the passing of illumination from one body to another?'

Turner thought for a moment and then replied.

'I would have answered that as a physicist he should know that on quantum levels of analysis, the "waves" in which illumination is said to be conveyed are not waves as we normally understand them but are no more than formal bundles of statistics with wave-like propagational characteristics.'!7

'Of course,' said the wrangler, 'I guess no-one would suppose that statistics are material things that can travel.'

'That's right,' answered Turner, 'that would be what is called a categorymistake. Statistics, as such, cannot exist outside observation, and since those waves of illumination are nothing but statistical probabilities, there can be no question, as I think I've mentioned, of those waves "travelling" in space or anywhere else on their own, with nothing there to wave, which is as nonsensical as the smile on the face of the Cheshire Cat which lingers in the sky when the cat has disappeared, in Lewis Carroll's "Alice in Wonderland"'

'Okay, but tell me, how do you explain the time-lapse in sending and receiving light-signals using purely statistical terms?' asked Charlie.

"Well, Charlie, think of it this way. In what is called - confusingly, I would say - quantum "wave-mechanics", those statistical probabilities do definitely display wave-like characteristics, with dimensions of length, breadth and time, just like waves in air or water. But instead of this propagation taking place in a medium, it's like a wave of panic, or the sort of

17 This refers to the well-known Schrödinger wave-equation. This is not truly a wave according to any commonsense meaning of the word. It is a wave-like mathematical function of the probabilities, in certain situations, of intrinsically motionless quantum events occurring. The physical interpretation of this 'wave-function' is recognised to be one of the most pressing philosophical problems of present-day physics.

"Mexican wave" we see among crowds at football stadiums. That is, it exists only in the sequences of quantum-events, not in any imagined "space" or "void" between.'

'As in the "cinematic" and "video-tape" examples,' commented Walter.

Turner nodded. 'Right, except, of course, that we can no longer think of those informational quantum-blips as connected by anything material, such as the film matrix or the video-tape We must remember that those quantum elements we are talking about are not linked together in any such hard-wired, predetermined way but are purely discrete and sequential; that is to say, all their dimensions are sequential, sme-dimensions, all measured in seconds, or in metres divided by the dimensional constant c, which is the same thing.'

'Can you enlarge on that, Dave?' asked Noel. 'I know I've discussed this with you and Tony, but J guess this stuff might be new to the rest of us.' There was a grateful murmur of agreement.

'Alright, Noel, point taken! Thanks for reminding me. What I'm saying is this. Let's start with talking about birthdays. We are usually happy, aren't we, to think of time as bringing us our birthdays without our having any concern as to whether those birthdays are conveyed to us from the future by anything like "waves" or "photons" travelling in the mechanical sense, in some kind of "void", "ether" or whatever.'

'True,' nodded Noel.

'But we usually think of those sequences of birthdays as happening in just one dimension, the dimension of time, while the other three dimensions are those of space, which we completely dissociate from time. What I'm suggesting is that we get rid of that whole "space-travelling" bag of tricks in respect of light and think instead of quantum-events as happening in the same way as our birthdays, but in all four time-dimensions, not just

one.

Carter was interested in this from his mathematical training as an engineer. "You are saying, then, Dave, that time is polar dimensional, not rectilinear..."

'Not an "ever flowing stream" as the hymn says,' put in Turner.

'Yes,' agreed Carter, 'that is to say, it is observer-centred, with all physical dimensions projected radially in the same units of seconds, or in units of

186,000 miles divided by c. This is what you meant when you told the professor that it's the space that's in the light, not the light that's in space. I can see now, very clearly, that you're saying, in effect, that time does not evolve all over space, in the classical, God's-eye-view way, but only at these polar-dimensional centres where all the surrounding data are decoded by the various observers and spun into what we perceive as reality.

Something else occurred to Carter, so he added, 'I suppose you know, Dave, that whole sets of polar, or point-centred dimensions are mathematically transformable into Cartesian grid-dimensions. So when people talk about "reference frames" in relativistic contexts, they should really be talking about geometrical matrixes of interacting, or inter-communicating, observational spheres.'

"That's right, Tony, in those information-statistical terms weve discussed. In short, the world is projected relativistically in probabilitydistributions and sequences of quantum events in four dimensions. It's not that the probabilities of those events occur in space, they are space - and time, and motion, including what we think of as the 'velocity of light', which is more suitably thought of as the ordinary progression of time but, as I say, in four dimensions, not just the traditional single one.'

'I suppose, then,' responded, Noel, thoughtfully, 'that one could say that you are purifying the "wave-mechanics" of de Broglie and Schrödinger by dispensing with all the "wave-mechanical" terminology, "photons" and so on, and just settling for the pure probability-statistics - the four-dimensional patterns and sequences of quantum events. Gee, Dave, that's neat!'

'So,' said Charlie, having got lost in what was, for him, a welter of technicalities, 'getting back to my question, I guess it's not so much a matter of explaining how illumination gets from one body to another, as that of explaining how the space between those bodies is projected out of the illumination. I see what you're saying, but it's still pretty hard to accept!"

'Far less hard, surely, than trying to accept standard science's all-over "wave-function" which is collapsed into reality by the very act of observation,' Turner replied. "But all you have to bear in mind, Charlie, is that this pure "probability-function', applied to sequences of quantum events,

has the same dimensions of motion in our viewer-projected scenario as "light-waves" are supposed to have in the classical "void".'

Much of this conversation was lost on Marian. But steeped as she was in the comfortable ambience of the situation, with one ear on the conversation she was content with drawing the faces of her companions in her sketchbook. Noel's face was a still-life picture of deep thought. Then he spoke.

'As we've said before, Dave, these ideas are easy enough to understand but difficult to retain, to keep together as a picture. I keep falling back, by reflex, into the classical "God's-eye-view" way of thinking.'

Turner was sympathetic. "That's because our minds habitually persist in putting the physical cart before the informational horse, whereas we're trying to see things the opposite way around. It's not impossible to think consistently of a physics in purely digital or informational terms as the observationally processed result of four-dimensional time-sequences of quantum-blips. But if we try to think of that informational logos as a product of the old universal and determinate physics, there's no way we're going to succeed.'

At this statement, a thoughtful silence descended on the group, then Walter said,

'The opposite of logos, in Greek, was physis. So now it's a return to a case of putting logos before physis, not the other way around.'

Just then, Megan Frost came into the restaurant and looked around.

'Ah, there you are, Don,' she said. 'Sorry to interrupt, folks, but we've got a problem. One of the guests has had a fall and I think he may have broken his ankle.'

Don excused himself and got up to go with his wife. The others expressed their concern.

'Anything we can do? asked Carter.

'Thanks, but we're prepared for emergencies like this,' Don replied. "We'll take him to Clearwater, to the hospital.'

'T've already 'phoned Dr. Macallister,' Megan said, as she hurried her husband down the stairs. 'Mrs. Ainsley will go with you, Don, and Jake will follow in the truck,'

"That's tough luck, breaking an ankle while on holiday,' Carter remarked, "but he'll be in good hands. Canadian medical care is amongst the best in the world.'

'So I've gathered,' said Turner.

"Yes, and for those who can't afford it,' said Helen, 'we have an excellent social security system here. Good health-provision should be a right, not a privilege. You British,' she said to Turner, 'provide that with your National Health Service.'

"We did,' Turner replied, ironically. 'Now the N.H.S. is a matter of concern. It's now got too many bureaucrats and too few resources! But I agree, Helen, the very best medical attention should be available to everyone. Unfortunately, in Britain, people are dying for want of simple operations for which they've been waiting months, even years. It's particularly tragic when this involves children.'

"That's bad,' commented Charlie. 'Here they'd be in hospital within a couple of weeks. When you realise that man is capable of walking on the moon and sending probes to the outer planets, it surely is an indication of a country's moral priorities that it allows such things to happen.'

'Perhaps it's the effect of pressure of population,' suggested Walter.

"We should be thankful, then,' Charlie continued, 'that it's not like that here - not yet, anyway. We haven't got the pressure of population - at least, not until the "practical men" like our professor, have bull-dozed their way in.

This mention of the professor brought some ribald remarks from Carter, which Helen diplomatically terminated by referring to their earlier discussion.

'I would like,' she said, 'to take up the point I mentioned earlier about the Logos being personified as Christ. From the way you were talking about character-complexes, or Gestalten, 1 feel that this would have some bearing on the old theological problem of body and spirit - the question of whether the resurrection of Christ was that of his body or just his spirit.'

Beware! Bears!

A glacier in the Rockies

A wilderness view 'It couldn't have been his body,' stated Noel firmly.

"What makes you so sure?' asked a rather disconcerted Helen.

'Because,' Noel replied, 'if all those body-corpuscles were taken out of the system and sent to heaven there would be an almighty bang, here on earth. It would be on the level of a nuclear explosion.'

This caught Marian's interest. 'How come?' she asked.

She had only half-followed the conversation, science not being her strong point, but Noel's surprising contention had startled her. However, just then Megan Frost came back into the restaurant.

"'Don's on his way to the hospital,' she said. "Poor Mr. Ainsley's in a lot of pain.'

'Is Don likely to be gone long?' asked Rick.

'A couple of hours, at least,' Megan replied. 'so I doubt if he'll be back before midnight.'

Helen was sympathetic. 'And you both work such long hours.'

"Well, that's one of the penalties of working for ourselves and not for a boss,' smiled Megan. 'But it's our choice, so we have to take the rough with the smooth. Anyway, is there anything you want before I close the bar?'

This gentle hint from Megan made them aware that they were the only customers left. Carter was the first to move.

'Not for me, thank you, my dear,' he declined, solicitously. "What about the rest of you?' As no-one replied, he added, 'Then let's go, folks.'

'Oh, what a shame!' protested Marian, 'I was hoping that Noel would explain what he meant about Christ's resurrection having to be like a nuclear explosion.'

"Well, Marian,' Helen gently chided, 'you can leave that for another time. We're on vacation here, but Mrs. Frost isn't.'

'May I make a suggestion?' Turner decided to make a tactful intervention. 'It's still a bit too early for Tony and me to turn in, so why don't we all go back to our chalet for a drink? Any objection, Tony?"

'No, none,' Carter replied. Privately, he felt he would like an early night. 'But how about the rest of you?' he asked. 'After all, Charlie's a working man, too.'

'Don't worry about me,' said Charlie, 'I can sleep on my horse. Oops! I shouldn't have said that in front of the boss's wife!"

He grinned engagingly at Megan, who laughed, knowing how conscientious their wrangler was in fact. So, with agreement all round, and a grateful 'Goodnight' from Megan, they trooped down the track to Turner's very comfortable room in one of the Lodge's large new chalets.

Chapter 10: The Chalet

Truth and Falsity

The chalet where Turner and Carter were staying was built in Swiss style. It had two storeys, with a balcony across the width of the upper floor. Its roof was red-painted and steep-pitched, but its walls were entirely of logs, very neatly fitted together and caulked with a modern plastic foam equivalent of what would once have been sphagnum moss. The decor, inside, was the same as outside; rough-hewn, clear-varnished log surfaces, with the marks of the hoisting-callipers still there in places. Carter's room was on the upper floor, reached by an outside staircase at the rear of the building. He left the party in Turner's ground floor room while he went upstairs to fetch some drinks. When he returned, Turner was in the middle of telling the tale about the 'night of the monster'.

'Everything was still,' Turner was saying, 'when all of a sudden there was an almighty bang, as if a moose had butted the patio doors. When I went outside to look -~- very carefully, I might add - all I could see was Tony, on the balcony above, just as disturbed as I was. But there was nothing in sight except moonlight stretching as far as the snow on Trophy mountain.'

After a pause, Turner continued. 'Three times that happened in all,' he said, 'twice in that same night. After being alerted to it the first time, the second and third times both Tony and I were there in about two seconds. But on no occasion did we see anything. Whatever it was, from the bang it made, it had the bulk and momentum of a charging moose but with none of its visible and tangible qualities.'

'Now shat' spooky!' Marian said with a little shiver. "Weren't you scared? I should have been terrified.'

'Gee, what was it?' asked Rick.

'Eventually, Tony solved it,' said Turner, at which point Carter took up the tale.

'Td been wondering about the "bad fit" at the top of these partitions where they meet the log beams - up there, as you can see, where they are some inches shy of the full height. It seemed a poor show for such expensively built chalets, so I mentioned it to Don. He told me that these log constructions settle about four inches in the first year, due to radial shrinkage of the logs. I'd suspected that something like that was the cause of the bad fit, but I hadn't estimated the extent - I work in concrete and steel, you understand, not logs. In any case, it's not the first thing that occurs to you in the silence of the wilderness at three o'clock in the morning...'

*... when your cortex is still asleep and it's only your thalamus that's woken up! Turner concluded. 'In short, the bang that we'd heard was the sudden release of stress caused by the settling of the logs!'

"Wow, that's something!" Marian exclaimed. 'Td never have thought of that!'

They chatted amiably for a while, until Marian's curiosity could be constrained no longer and she raised the question, once again, of Noel's startling statement that the bodily resurrection of Christ would have been a nuclear explosion.

'How an you say that, Noel?' Marian demanded. "The whole idea is so bizarre!'

"Well, Marian, you've heard of Einstein's famous formula for atomic energy, which says that energy is mass times c-squared,' answered Noel.

"Yes, who hasn't?' she replied.

'Then just think, Marian. With c in that formula being three hundred million metres per second - square it . . . that makes ninety thousand million-million metres squared per second squared. Multiply this by the mass of the human body - what's the average weight of a man's body? Say a hundred and fifty pounds. In kilograms, that's. . ."

'Sixty-five kilograms, as near as dammit,' offered Carter.

'Thanks, Tony. So, sixty-five kilograms times ninety thousand millionmillion, equals . . . about six million-million-million joules of energy.'

Carter had been scribbling on the back of a brochure. 'About a billion - a British billion - kilowatt hours,' he said.

'So?' asked Marian.

Patiently Noel replied, 'Imagine how much water a billion one-kilowatt electric kettles would boil in one hour. That's the amount of energy into which a hundred-and-fifty pounds-worth of body-mass would have to turn to disappear on earth and appear in heaven. The flash and the bang would have left Jerusalem as flat as Hiroshima!'

'That's really gruesome!" shuddered Marian. 'It's a horrid suggestion and I don't wish to think about it. What is it about you men that you can contemplate such things?" |

'But it didn't happen, did it, Marian?' assured her sister. "So what Noel is saying, is simply that what was resurrected, if anything, would have to have been his spirit, or soul - not his body.'

Noel was pleased with Helen's perspicacity and support. Then he recalled his discussion with Carter and Turner about entropy as the inevitable decrease in order.

Addressing the whole group, he said, 'Yes, what we can call Christ's character-complex - his spiritual Gesta/t. But even a character-complex is an amount of something, of order, or information - and for that to disappear it has to decompose into disorder. That is, there has to be a redistribution of energy. When an organism dies, no amount of matter, or mass, is lost to this world. That energy is the energy of chemical decomposition, which deranges the molecular arrangements of the body, downgrading its order you might say. The effect is similar to that of nuclear decomposition - though not on the same scale, of course. The equivalent, in kilowatt hours, in this molecular case, would be a fair-sized log burning to ashes.'

The drink was getting to Noel, Carter judged. This was no longer entirely a philosophical discussion. How serious, he wondered, was Noel in all this, or was there some element of a mating ritual. Nevertheless, Noel's facts seemed to be in order.

'But you're talking about bodily decomposition there, Noel,' argued Helen. 'In that process, what happens to the soul or spirit?"

"Well, logically that ought to be the same,' Noel replied. "Think of it like

this,' he continued, eager to explain his ideas to the two women. 'If by

"soul" or "spirit", we mean the complex of thoughts in the human brain, and if that complex is ordinarily fuelled by the blood conveying nutrients from what we eat, drink and breathe, then when that supply of nutrition ceases, not only does the brain begin chemically to decompose but so does that organisation of the body which makes it an organism and which is what, in religious contexts, is called the "soul" or "spirit". And where else, other than into the physical environment, can that organisation - that "soul" or "spirit" - go to at death? This should still have a physical effect in the form, ultimately, of heat, but nothing like that of nuclear explosion, of course. It would be fairly quick in the case of a cremation, but spread over a much longer time in ordinary decay, or burial. But whichever way it goes, there would have to be an effect. Any idea of that organisation leaving the body without such a measurable effect makes no sense You might say it's impossible.'

'I can't follow this! declared Marian. 'And, anyway, it's all too gruesome for words thinking about Christ going off like a nuclear bomb! I think you are being deliberately obnoxious, Noel.'

"You wanted me to explain it,' countered Noel, rather non-plussed. 'I simply followed the logical consequences. A log or a piece of plutonium can't decompose without there being some effect on the environment, so why should it be any different with a man's body - or his thoughts, or even what we call his "soul"?

"Well," suggested Helen, helpfully, 'perhaps there was such an effect when Christ's soul - his logos, charactercomplex or whatever you like to call it - "ascended" into heaven, as the Bible puts it, and no-one measured it. As I recall my scripture, there was a physical effect, "The sun was darkened and ..."'

""", . the veil of the temple was rent in the midst,"' added Walter, quoting by heart from the gospel of Luke (Chapter 23, verse 45).

'I can't argue with that,' Noel declared with a helpless shrug. He diplomatically refrained from saying what he thought, that in his view the incident never really happened.

'But you'd think,' said Rick, 'that there'd surely be something noticeable after all those generations and generations of peoples' souls disappearing

from earth and going to heaven in such a spooky way without leaving a gap in the physical account.'

The older men listened in amused silence to these exchanges, not certain as to how much of it was serious reason and. how much sheer bravado - or, indeed, the drink. Was Noel displaying his mental prowess like a peacock? Yet Noel's next point seemed serious enough.

'Ic all depends, Rick, where those energies go to,' he said. "They have to go somewhere, so where do they go? If they go into the environment in the ordinary way, as heat, then there's no problem. If not, then where? So you see my problem. I can't see what "resurrection" means.'

'Well, I'm sure you'll solve it, Noel,' said Marian, sweetly - and dismissively.

Bereft of any answer to that, Noel looked helplessly at the two older men. Sympathising with the young man's frustration, Turner was reminded of Helen's earlier concern about sexual equality. It occurred to him that in some ways the "inequalities" needed balancing-up in favour of men, rendered helpless as they sometimes were by female thought-processes.

'Many religious people,' Walter said, 'think that there is a category distinction between scientific reason and faith, that reason has nothing to do with religion.'

Noel, relieved to be extricated from the impasse he had unwittingly got into, was glad to change the subject.

'No doubt religion and science can get along well enough without each other, administratively speaking, but didn't we decide that this is what's wrong with our Western culture, that it is split-minded, or schizophrenic, due to our science having developed along the lines of the Cartesian mindbody dualism? Surely, now that we are aware of that dualistic fallacy, we should try and get those two disciplines back together in the context of a rational theology .. .'

'Sure, we should,' said Walter. "This is what Thomas Aquinas tried to do by marrying together the Christian religion and Aristotelian science. Unfortunately, that "marriage" fell apart with further advances in scientific knowledge when the mediaeval Church rigidified that synthesis in the form of the Canon Law. Like all systems which are "set in concrete", it was bound to shatter sooner or later when it failed to adapt to new knowledge.'

Yes,' Turner interposed, 'there's no law we can devise which is guaranteed survival in the face of continually advancing scientific knowledge. The new "canon law" revolves around Newton's laws of motion and gravitation, and that, too, looks set fair to break up before long - especially if Noel has anything to do with it, it seems!" he added, winking at the others.

"Then why does science bother making laws at all,' Rick asked, 'if they are bound to fall to bits as we get smarter?"

'A very good question, Rick,' replied Turner. "The answer has to be that none of these laws is ever entirely wrong. There's always some element of truth in them, otherwise science would never work in the way it does. The way I see it, the very basis of all knowledge and understanding is the fact that you can be wrong and can be proved wrong. That's what Socrates taught; that the only true and certain knowledge you can have is the knowledge of your own ignorance. Any guarantee that you were absolutely right in everything you thought and did would guarantee only that you were not in any real world but in some dream-world or nightmare-world of your own whims and prejudices.'

"Many people spend their lives in such private worlds,' said Charlie, with feeling. 'In fact, I should say that, in my experience, the majority of people do.'

'Unfortunately, yes,' Walter agreed. 'And not only individuals but whole institutions - even nations, especially those steeped in pure dogma.'

'And not only that kind, I should say,' cautioned Turner, 'but also many of those who would destroy such systems, just as convinced that their own systems are right, by that same nightmarish guarantee.'

"Too true,' Charlie concurred. "There are all sorts of people who are so "right" in their own minds that they would sacrifice the lives of themselves and others to those ideals without hesitation.'

'It's amazing, isn't it,' said Walter, 'how so many people come fresh into this world and who, in their very first flush of conviction are prepared to torture, maim and kill whoever does not agree with their half-baked ideas.'

Carter was reminded of something.

'Wasn't it the Duke of Edinburgh who said that such people should carry a medal inscribed "I am eighteen - or whatever - and have made up

my mind on all important issues, so please don't confuse me with any new ideas?"

Following this remark of Carter's there was a thoughtful silence, which was broken by Walter.

'So the corrective against all that dogmatism, you would say, Dave, is to take the first step on the road to knowledge, which is the realisation of our own ignorance. That's profoundly true. But how does that apply to your search for a new "Canon Law" to reconcile matters of science with those of what you might broadly describe as "theology"? If that were instituted, wouldn't it become just as repressive?'

'Undoubtedly it would,' Turner replied, 'if it were instituted merely arbitrarily with no rational basis.' Carefully measuring his words, he went on, 'But I would say that the only absolute guarantee of truth there is, the only unassailable "Canon Law", if you like, is that the proposition "everything is false" cannot be true without flat contradiction. From that it logically follows that something must be true, which provides the only basis of certainty we have. But there's no similar logical guarantee of what that something is. That's where all certainty ends. So if I were asked to define truth J would say that truth is no more nor less than what remains when you get rid of error. How's that for "Turner's Law" of truth?'

The others smiled at this.

'But then,' argued Noel 'you'll have the same difficulty in defining error. I mean, who knows for sure what error is any more than what truth is?'

'Well,' Turner replied, 'unlike "universal truth" - whatever that might be - error is easy to recognise. Its signature, when it is not simply failure to meet the facts, is internal logical contradiction, like that notion we discussed, of "void" as a "nothing" which separates things and "light" which travels in nothing, where it shines on nothing and which nobody ever sees, which is more "dark" than light. As we seem to have agreed, there's no way in which that contradictory sort of description of things can be true. The error, in that case, is perfectly plain, though what "the truth" might be when all that error is removed might be impossible for us to imagine, let alone anticipate. Take, as an example, the ideas of "light" and "void" we've

talked about. I've told you what J think the truth is in that case, that it's the space that's in the light, not the light that's in space. That, I claim, is a respectable idea - as respectable at least as any of the earlier, more conventional ones. But without implying any lack of confidence whatsoever in my arguments or conclusions, I nevertheless don't claim any cosmical guarantee of rightness. For no matter how convinced in any of these ideas I might be - and indeed I confess to being so convinced - I would not, knowingly, kill a single person to support those convictions.'

"Though you might be prepared to die for them yourself, suggested Helen, 'if those convictions of yours are on a par with what innovative thinkers have had to endure in the past. Imagine being in the hands of institutions like the Inquisition, for example, as people like Galileo were.'

"Well, I trust it won't come to that in my case!' Turner grinned. "But even if it did, I can't see how the threat of punishment or death, regardless of how it might alter my overt social behaviour, could ever alter my real rational convictions. Nor would my death at the hands of those authorities prove, to anyone who was not a fool, that the authorities were right and I was wrong. All it would prove is that they were more powerful than I am. It would be like trying to prove how good a brain-surgeon you were by just stomping around roaring, frightening and killing-off all competition. A fine brain-surgeon that would make you! That's why, in the long term, those repressive institutions you mention are guaranteed to fail. The mediaeval church, despite its power, was not able to establish by decree that the earth is flat or that it didn't revolve around the sun. Torturing and killing those who didn't accept that decree was an irrelevance.'

"Not to those who were tortured and killed, surely!' Marian exclaimed.

'No, of course not,' Turner agreed. 'I meant irrelevant to the historic course of unearthing truth. But do you see what I'm saying?' he continued. 'Depending on how logically discerning and honest we might be, error is much easier to determine than truth. In any case, a continued awareness of how finite we all are, puts the premiums on rational rather than militant solutions, on democratic consensus rather than on "direct action" or the merely reactionary exercise of force.'

'I go along with much of what you say, Dave,' said Noel, moving from his perch on the corner of the dressing-table to stand by the window, 'but how does "Turner's law" apply to the conduct of science? The going rate in science, it seems to me, is to construct more and more competitive, more and more entirely bizarre theories, rather than seeking to "get rid of error'".'

"Well, Turner replied, 'as an expression of anti-dogmatism, or empiricism, "Turner's Law" boils down to this. We find ourselves in the world as finite observers, strung between truth and falsity, between reality and illusion, deluged with information on all sides and having to sort it out by the best means available, with no logical guarantees of anything save that in discovering falsity and illusion we are necessarily uncovering some measure of truth.'

'And,' Walter added, 'in recognition of all that finitude and uncertainty, you would say that the emphasis should fall on rational co-operation and democratic consensus in making decisions as to what is true and what is false, hence which way science - and society - ought to go.'

'So long as that puts no unwarranted curb on individual creativity, yes,' qualified Turner.

'In that case,' Carter commented, 'a great deal would have to depend on gearing Education towards those democratic ends.'

"That's true,' Turner agreed. "Without the right kind of Educational policies, none of that would work. It wouldn't work, for instance, in those sorts of regimes in which the educational emphasis is on commerce and careerism or the mere obedience to authoritarian diktat. No, the kind of society and the kind of Education - and the kind of Science - would have to go inseparably together into something like what was once called Natural Philosophy. But that seems a very far cry from the way things are going at present.'

Throughout this rambling discussion, Walter had been turning over in the back of his mind the earlier issue of Christ's resurrection. At this juncture he thought it convenient to try, once again, to bring this matter to a satisfactory conclusion.

"We still haven't solved the problems Helen and Noel have raised about what, in the terms of our discussion, the resurrection of Christ can mean.'

"With all due respect to your beliefs, Walter,' Noel replied, with deferential courtesy towards the likeable young pastor, 'whether the man called Jesus existed and was, or became, the eternal Christ, "the only mediator and advocate", the only "son of God", and so on, I wouldn't like to say. The same with the question of whether or not he was "raised from the dead". On the other hand, if you accept the social principle that was attributed to him, the principle of forgiveness and brotherly love, and of self-sacrifice in seeking those ends, no-one can fault that. As a formula for society it makes as much sense, at least, as any formula of Newton's or Einstein's does for science.'

"Yes,' Carter added, 'I've often felt that if a teacher of that principle hadn't existed and hadn't shown us how we ought to live according to it, then he would need to have been invented. So it seems to me something of an irrelevance to worry over whether or not the teacher of that principle existed or did not exist, ascended into heaven, and so on. It's like the formula for mass-energy, where the energy is equal to c-squared, It is what it is, no matter who we think was responsible for it.

'But,' Turner pointed out, 'that same principle accredited to the man Jesus, was also demonstrated centuries earlier by Socrates and by many others, B.C. and A.D., forgotten heroes who, in their different ways and to different extents, have also been martyred for the sake of that same humanitarian principle.'

'So perhaps the "Christ" principle really is some kind of inbuilt social ideal,' proposed Helen, 'some moral Gestalt, logos, or whatever, which survives from generation to generation and which the lives of certain individuals, to some extent or another, exemplify in different places and different eras.'

'In that sense,' suggested Marian, 'it could be said that Christ lives in all of us, as the Quakers believe. Might that, perhaps, be the way in which Christ was resurrected, by being transported, not to some imaginary "heaven" but into mankind as a whole?'

Noel agreed. 'I don't see how "resurrection" can be interpreted in any other way,' he said. "What do you think, Dave?'

'T'm not sure,' Turner replied. "The politic thing to say would be that in a democratic society, people are free to believe what they like, that if they

want to think of Christ being resurrected, bodily or spiritually, into the sky or somewhere beyond it, they should be at liberty to do so. But I don't really believe that any more than you do, Noel. All I know is that to believe in something which is nonsensical and stick to it no matter what, is seldom beneficial to society.'

'Thar's all too true,' put in Walter. 'Fanatical attachments to irrational beliefs, which are the roots of dogmatism, can be a curse, a major source of evil, of suffering and suppression.'

'That's very evident,' Turner agreed. 'So having said what I did about dogmatism, I now have to add that I recognise a difference between dogmatism and truly rational conviction.'

'So your antidote for dogmatism is reason,' said Helen. "That's almost a truism.'

It was Charlie who answered her. 'I wish it were, Helen,' he said, sardonically. 'If it is a truism, then it's one that seems to have been lost to just about the whole of modern society.'

'Amen to that!' Turner replied. "Of course, ''m not saying that logical reason can or should dictate belief; but it can, at least, make sure that belief is not wasted on illusory objects.'

Walter responded to this. "What you're saying is "There are truths beyond reason but never contrary to it".'

'Precisely,' Turner agreed. "That's why I feel it's as much our moral duty as any other to be rational. I mean, truly rational and logical in our choice of beliefs and to test those beliefs and, in some cases, the beliefs of others, by those same criteria, which throws emphasis on the need for free unfettered dialogue. As far as "The Resurrection" is concerned, Noel has shown, I think, the absurdity of the idea of bodily resurrection into any form of being which does not, in some sense or another, remain within this system of things, the system in which we are all immersed as observers. But that argument, Noel, didn't prove that Christ never existed, either as a man or as an ideal. He could still have existed as either or both.'

'That's fair comment,' conceded Noel.

Walter wanted to get something clear. "You and Noel, Dave,' he said, 'seem to have agreed that there can be no such thing as a "heaven", which,

of course, goes against the beliefs of Christianity as I understand them, though even I don't visualise heaven as being full of angels with harps and so on!

Turner smiled. "Yes, that's so. I don't believe in a heaven being a world completely remote from this physical one, in the Platonic way as a world of Forms existing "beyond", or "in parallel" with this physical world. Aristotle argued, against Plato, that these Forms - patterns, Gestalten, character-complexes, souls or whatever we like to call them - are in rebus not ante res... Okay, Rick,' Turner explained before Rick could protest, 'that's academic-speak for "in things" not "beyond things". This suggests that if there's any "heaven", a "world of Forms" or whatever we might like to call it, then it must somehow exist in the Aristotelian way rather than in the Platonic way; that is, iz the world we directly see, touch, smell, and so on. And that,' he concluded, 'seems to me perfectly consistent with the conclusions we've reached in our discussions so far.'

'But in the physical world,' Charlie objected, 'everything is temporal, governed by the law of entropy, decaying into less and less ordered forms. How can those forms endure continually and permanently in this system of things?'

Noel, thinking back to his discussion with Carter and Turner in the restaurant, two nights earlier, could see that Charlie was making the same mistake he had made in 'universalising' the rule of entropy when there is no 'universe' which it made sense to speak of. Of course, Charlie and the others had not been present at that discussion, but before Noel was able to set Charlie straight on this matter, Walter made his reply to Charlie's question.

'So you're wondering, Charlie, that if this temporal world is the only one there is, then what place can there be in it for eternal truths? In other words, where can you have a permanence where everything is temporary?'

'Arent we back to this "character-complex" thing again,' suggested Carter, 'where something like a great piece of music can exist from age to age while those who play it and listen to it appear and disappear like dayflies? That gives us a model, don't you think, for understanding how these forms can persist in a changing world.'

'And that,' said Turner, 'takes us back to the third strand of Greek philosophy, which is associated, this time, with the name of Heraclitas. His model of permanence was the candle-flame which burned steadily in a state of continued violent combustion - permanence in the midst of change.'

"Gee, those Greeks were busy,' Rick ventured, admiringly.

"They surely were,' said Walter. "These discussions of ours are giving me some insight into what it must have been like among those ancient thinkers. They were just guys, like us, something I hadn't really appreciated during my academic studies. I feel we've been unconsciously treading in their tracks.'

'Spooky, eh!' remarked Rick.

Concerned that contemplation of this aspect of the discussion should not derail the issue yet again, Walter quickly went on.

"What we're looking for, then, is the kind of permanence which persists in impermanence, and that brings us back to the idea of a Logos of character-complexes, or Charlie's Gestalten or Plato's forms. So "Heaven" - if you allow that it exists - must be that Form which is the total set of forms, an ordered or cosmical whole . . .'

"Though not, however, Turner reminded them, 'a "universe" in the spatial sense of the astronomers, which, as we've seen, is impossible. We must remember that we're now talking, as you said, Walter, about logos not physis.

'But,' objected Charlie, 'not all of those forms are "good" ones in the sense that they could belong to a "heaven".'

"They aren't all necessarily true forms, either,' Carter pointed out. "Even illusions and delusions are structured to some extent. I mean, they're still forms, aren't they?'

'Okay, I accept that,' said Walter, 'so now we have to distinguish between true forms and false ones, good forms and bad forms, or evil ones. What can we say is the common characteristic among the forms we would regard as true, as opposed to those we would regard as false or spurious?"

Turner answered him.

'If we assume that those forms exist in this world and that they are not subject to decay, then the common characteristic of the true forms must

surely be that they consist of whole interconnected hierarchies, in which the relations of the parts, the various forms. is maintained within a Super Form, like the way in which the internal health of the cells of a living organism reflects the health of the whole, and vice-versa.'

"Or like an orchestra,' Helen added, 'in which whatever each musician plays fits in harmoniously with the rest.'

'I see,' said her sister. 'A false form is like a piece of music played on an instrument by a musician who may be very competent but who is blind to the conductor and deaf to what the other orchestra-members are playing. He plays a good tune, perhaps, except that it is the wrong one for the orchestra and clashes with the others.'

"Yes, Marian, "heaven", in those terms,' suggested Walter, 'would be complete harmony, the Harmony of the Whole - the "Lost Chord", so to speak. "

'Played by angels on harps of gold?' asked Rick, not entirely seriously.

'Not quite, Rick,' the clergyman replied. "More like a "jam session", perhaps, where every instrumentalist plays his own part, freely and extempore, but which somehow fits with the theme and the spirit of what the others are also freely playing. Anyone who has ever been a part of such a session knows how uplifting and free-spirited, yet companionable, that can make you feel, and how different it is when some trombonist "blows only bubbles", say, or a pianist "plays all the cracks" - as musicians say, The sense of cooperation and transcendent well-being you get in such a situation, for instance, in some of the singing and clapping congregations I have been to, is the closest mundane analogy I can think of to what people call "heaven".'

'I think I see what you mean, Walter,' responded Noel, 'even for someone like me who is practically tone-deaf. And I fully agree. I can think of all sorts of other situations, outside music, which produce that same profound sense of satisfaction.'

'Perhaps, then,' Turner offered, 'instead of thinking in the classical way of our souls harping on clouds, which has the disadvantage of suggesting other-worldliness, we should adopt Walter's musical analogy, combined with your earlier physiological one, Noel. A true form is then like one in

which each of the body cells, in happily "doing its own thing" nevertheless subscribes to the health of both themselves and the body as a whole, whereas a false, or bad form is one in which those body-cells which become cancers, say, although healthily "doing their own thing", do so at the expense of the body's health and survival - and in the end, of course, of their own.'

'Like viruses and other parasites,' Charlie added, 'which "have a ball" killing their hosts and, eventually, themselves.'

'It's surely interesting,' remarked Walter, 'how "illusion", "delusion", "noise", "disharmony", "illness" and "suffering", "evil" and so on, all group together under falsity. This gives us some idea, I think, of the common characteristic of the spurious forms within the logos which are out of kilter with the rest.'

'T would also stress,' put in Turner, 'that both sets of forms, the true and the false, are to be found in this world, not in some world apart. That would make "heaven" the essence of the life-principle, everything supporting which is "good". "Evil" is then the death or dissolution principle, everything serving which is "bad". What else can good and evil possibly be if not that?'

'And all this,' said Helen, 'lends point to the Christian doctrine of "love". You understand, of course, that I don't mean sexual promiscuity, as some elements in our modern society interpret it - and perhaps that's one of your spurious forms, Walter.'

'I agree,' Walter replied. 'You mean love as sympathetic and empathic communication between observers, enhancing one another's consciousness of others, as opposed to hate, which is destructive of these things.'

'Yes, that's right,' replied Helen. 'What, to me, is "bad" is associated in one way or another with what I suppose is diminution or loss of consciousness - insularity, or selfishness in Christian terms.'

'So now,' Carter summed up, 'we have a world which is not basically of astronomical space and time but, as Dave has suggested, of information or logos.

'Yes, added Turner, 'and what we traditionally call "heaven" and "hell" may be identified as order and disorder, cosmos and chaos, information

and noise, harmony and discord, health and disease, call it what you will; but all in the one world, of course, not in "realms apart".'

'It's interesting, don't you think,' Helen remarked, 'that in that context, the root of the word "holy" is the same as that of "health",'

'Is that so?' asked Rick. 'I wonder how many other words we use have lost their old meanings.'

All this,' said Turner, 'emphasises what we're trying to sort out and make sense of in modern commonsense terminology. As I see it, what we are trying to "resurrect", here, is the sense these concepts must once have contained and which has been buried under so many past centuries of mystical mish-mash. Words like "Logos", "God", "heaven", "Christ", and religious language generally, have become historically stale. They've lost whatever meaning and relevance they might once have had in great measure in those by-gone thought-contexts. Like all clichés, they lose their edge, so that the sense they once conveyed has to be re-articulated in a language more relevant to our times.'

'But the new language doesn't have the poetry, the literary beauty, of the old forms of language,' objected Marian. "This new language, of "information" and all the other things we've been talking about seems so . . . ugly and incongruous.'

'So did windjammers at first,' countered Rick. 'Great hulks with their sails strung out like lines of laundry against the sunset. But ain't they beautiful to us now!'

"The same with the old steam trains,' added Carter. 'I reckon that future generations will see today's factory chimneys and cooling-towers, our spaceships and power-stations in the same romantic way.'

'But I take Marian's point,' put in Turner, 'though I would say that the ugliness and incongruity of modern jargon stems not so much from the sounds of the words as from the fact that they are gobbledegook in ordinary everyday parlance. We'd soon get used to any new language so long as it made logical sense, which so much of the old scientistic language doesn't any more - certainly not for me!'

'Nor for me,' Noel concurred. 'How can you make sense of invisible particles having beauty or charm, or up-ness and down-ness, which has nothing to do with what those words mean in ordinary parlance?

'Tr's for sure that it doesn't provide a very coherent philosophy for any modern society to hang its hat on,' observed Charlie. 'Nothing to instil confidence, you might say.'

'So, what we're trying to do here, then, Dave,' Helen suggested, 'is to revise our language to make the new scientific ideas fit comfortably with others in areas of commonsense morality, religion and so on.'

Turner answered her.

'Something like that, Helen. We're trying to ease the constraints imposed on our thinking by long-stultified conventions and allow commonsense once more to make democratic rational choices. In the same way that presenting space as a void makes ignoramuses out of educated people, to present God as a great patriarch sitting in the sky with a lot of angels buzzing round him playing harps, or as some form of cultural totem or mascot, makes too many "atheists" out of people who might otherwise be firm believers. And even among those believers, all of whom might otherwise concur on the moral principle enunciated by Socrates, Jesus, Mahomet and so on, the persistence of so many outworn conventions as to how that principle came about, who was responsible for it, through what inspired agency, call it "God", "Jehovah", "Allah", or whatever, perpetuates wars which, in the last analysis, are never more than just wars about words. The only way of avoiding that, I would say, is by the process of linguistic analysis leading to a global common language in which words evolve to express more of the true logos than our puny scriptural traditions do.'

'That reminds me of something,' Carter interposed. 'I was once at a meeting where a clergyman friend of mine was challenged about his calling. Someone asked him how he could be so naive, nowadays, as to believe in God. He replied by asking how many atheists were present in the group. He made it plain to the group that by "atheists" he meant people who believed implicitly that there was no such entity as God. In answer, all of them raised their hands. One by one, my friend asked them to describe the God they didn't believe in. So bizarre and diverse were the answers that in every case, he agreed that although he was a confirmed Christian himself, he didn't believe in that "God" each of them described. I now see very clearly what he meant. Even atheists have to agree on what they mean by

"God" before denying his existence. And how much truer must this be for those who say they believe in something they call "God" but which, perhaps, has no common meaning among any two of them.'

This struck a chord with Walter.

'Yes, Tony, the kind of "God" concept which goes with our traditional scientific ideas of space and time - the "God" of the deists, as we've discussed - is different altogether from the humane God of Christianity. The idea of a God who is "everywhere" and "every when", a presence or spirit which is all over space and all over time, all at once, is as offensive to me as the Professor's "fields in space" are to someone like you, Dave.'

"That reminds me,' said Helen, 'of something I read in an essay by Voltaire. "God is everywhere", he wrote, "but everywhere where there is something, not where there is not". To be present in nothing, Voltaire believed, is a contradiction, which is the conclusion we seem to have reached in these discussions.'

'Voltaire also said,' added Walter, "I will not set up the throne of God in imaginary spaces." And for that he was charged with "atheism", although in other respects he was a devout man.'

'So it all depends, I guess,' said Charlie, 'on what you mean by "God". To some - maybe most - people who claim to be believers, "God" is just a creature of their own imaginations, a psychological "graven image", a kind of alter-ego projection which either inhibits them by superstitious dread or else authorises them to do all sorts of mischief which they might otherwise recognise as the workings of a raw id.'

Carter agreed.

"Yes, Charlie,' he said, 'especially when some megalomaniac persuades others that he is plugged-in on a hot-line to God, when he gathers weakminded followers who are in desperate need of something or someone to believe in but who are strangers to critical reason. It's well said that power corrupts and absolute power corrupts absolutely - and a fanatical belief in a God may seem to provide that absolute power. The history of mankind is full of such episodes. It does things to people, like when you see the way a wimp behaves when he gets behind the wheel of a powerful car.'

18 Selected Works of Voltaire, J. McCabe (translator), Publ. by Watts & Co., London, 1935, p. 13.

'I'm glad you said "mankind",' Helen commented, rather smugly.

'Hey, hold on!' responded Charlie. "What about that English queen they called Bloody Mary or-er...'

'... Margaret Thatcher?" suggested Turner, mischievously.

The mention of the "Iron Lady", the first British Woman Prime Minister, caused a laugh which averted another battle of the sexes.

'Okay,' smiled Helen, adding, "but, even so, there's never been a female Hitler or Pol Pot, has there?'

'Not yet, my dear,' said Carter ominously, 'not yet.'

After the discussion, everyone had gone, leaving Carter and Turner to ruminate on what had transpired. Then Turner said, 'How's about a snifter, old son, before turning in?'

'Straight Scotch,' his friend answered, 'no soda, no water. Make it a double - a good one!'

'That's unusual, Tony,' said Turner. Then, sensing something, he added, 'You seemed a little quiet this evening. Anything amiss?'

It took a few moments' silence before Carter answered.

'I suppose you could say that, Dave.'

'Okay, old sport, let's hear it.'

'Well, it's like this, I was thinking about all the tackle that astronomers have at their disposal, nowadays, to study the stars, I was glancing through a magazine in the foyer yesterday and saw something which was quite astounding. It was a full-page glossy picture of the view through a very large astronomical telescope - Mount Wilson, I think it was showing star clusters, spiral galaxies, dust-clouds and so on, picked out in all their awesome clarity and beauty. Those great galaxies, with their millions and billions of stars like our sun - many, no doubt, with planetary systems like our Solar System - appear to go on for ever and ever, at mind-boggling distances.'

Turner nodded his agreement as his friend continued.

'T've seen pictures like that before, of course, but this particular one got me thinking. I suddenly felt that, compared to all that immensity of space and time, with its gargantuan objects and prodigiously violent events

shown in the pictures, these discussions we've been having here about the centrality in all this of ourselves as perceivers, and all that this entails, seem so utterly minuscule and unimportant, so disappearingly small in that vast scheme of things...'

He could see that Turner was about to protest, so he hurriedly said, 'Now I know, Dave, that this is the theme our discussions started off with, your most vivid account of your own personal sea-change towards relativism and all that follows from it, as we've discussed, and I fully respect that. But, gee, Dave, when you think of what we're doing here, on this terrestrial dust-mote, viewed in the context of the true extent of things, it makes these discussions of ours seem less than nothing. On that scale of things, what can it possibly matter what we think, what we may conclude from these discussions? Does it matter whether we believe or don't believe in a God, say, or in the survival of our souls?'

'I'm with you, old chap,' said Turner sympathetically. 'You're wondering how people can possibly think, for instance, that the whole universe is "Christian", say, or, "Muslim" or whatever.'

"That's just it,' agreed Carter, reassured that his friend understood his worries. "How can we believe that our Bible or the Koran - our Shakespeare, our Parliament, our personal or social history, our justice system, the question of resurrection, martyrdom, heaven and hell, of Jesus, Mahomet, Copernicus, Socrates and so on, matter a jot on giant stars, say, in our galaxy or in any one of countless other galaxies, some far beyond the reach of our very best telescopes? It makes our terrestrial concerns with things like politics, religion, commerce and so on, seem entirely silly.'

Again Turner nodded. 'Just so, Tony, and in that state of mind one almost envies those who lack the imagination to see themselves and their mundane concerns against that cosmical background.'

'So, Dave, are we just fooling ourselves by making these noises we call discussions and which may have no meaning whatsoever outside our stupid heads, far less beyond this tiny, insignificant planet?'

Having listened attentively to his old friend's every word, Turner abstractedly swirled his drink around the glass before he responded.

'Well, Tony,' he said, 'obviously it has now finally sunk in what I was trying to say to you at the start of these discussions, I know that frame of mind you describe only too well, having been steeped in it for so long ina state of depression lower than a snake's belly. In that state of mind you feel, as I said, almost envious of those lacking the imagination to see the situation for what it really is without having to clutch at mystical straws.'

'But, Dave, not everyone is blessed with that lack of imagination and preparedness to take refuge in religious superstition. After all, the root of our Western Society is based on expanding scientific knowledge, so freshminded young thinkers scorn what are, for them, the stale traditions and icons of the past.'

'That's right, so they find they have no recourse but to express their irrepressible energies in that directionless form of violence and antisocial behaviour which is endemic in our society. That sudden bout of depression you experienced, Tony, is no more than a taste of what, especially among the youth of our society, is simply the "the going rate". It is, you might say, the default condition of a culture based on a mechanistic science which has no agreed philosophical direction and therefore has to depend for its social cohesion on so many conflicting religious and other irrational ideals. It is well to remind ourselves that those societies of the past, such as the Cynics, the Stoics, Epicureans, Romantics, and so on, were based on policies of despair engendered by the same mechanistic science that persists today. That sense of despair has been, throughout, the same sort of depression you describe.'

'Fair enough, Dave, but that only generalises the depression, without easing it.'

However, Turner hadn't finished. 'And I agree, Tony, that our efforts to deal with that level of depression in any rational way seem utterly pathetic when viewed on that cosmic scale of things. So, this takes us right back to the original "square one" of these discussions. With this new realisation of yours firmly in mind, let's quickly summarise where we went from there. First, let me ask you, is there some overriding Presence that sees everything you saw in those pictures all at once - that is, as if those pictures were taken from everywhere simultaneously?"

"No, Dave, we scotched the idea of there being any overall, simultaneous ubiquitous presence, didn't we?'

'In that case, Tony, the only views there can be of whatever there is, no matter how impressive these views might be in pictures like the one you saw in that magazine, have to be perspectival; that is, related to some specified centre of observation, That includes the astronomical photo you saw. And the same goes for any other observational system, microor macroscopic . . . Right?"

'Sure, Dave, I can't see how else. As I think we agreed, this is the basic principle of nature that holds throughout.'

So ask yourself, then, old chap, what logical bearing can that hugeness, of which you speak, have on those discussions of ours.'

Carter thought for a moment, then replied.

'No /ogical bearing that I can see, Dave, It's just that, regardless of the logic of it, the relativising or observer-centralising of something as immense as that great collection of stars and galaxies of stars, stretching ad infinitum, suddenly seemed so implausible.'

'But so, Tony,' countered Turner, 'did the idea of a round earth which, at the time, seemed utterly preposterous. Implausibility is one thing; logic is another. And logic tells us that classical mechanistic physics and cosmology are in error regarding its view of what it describes as the "Universe".'

"Yes, we did that, didn't we,' replied Carter doubtfully, 'and, as I say, we agreed that there can be no overall simultaneous "God's eye view" we can describe that makes any logical sense. But I can't help wondering, though, whether the "logic" we're talking about here makes any sense in that cosmical context.'

'But, Tony, didn't we agree that what we call "logic", which is the basis of sense in language, is as one with the logic of nature?
