This is our link, in these discussions, with that universal logical principle called logos, the principle on which everything depends throughout the whole scheme of things, regardless of how immensely distant, like those stars and galaxies you mentioned. So it shouldn't matter a damn, should it, how impressively big might be the system to which our logic is applied.'

'No, I guess not, come to think of it.'

'In sum, then, Tony, if these arguments of ours are correct, then there never was any logical basis for that particular sort of depression you experienced in common with the Cynics and so on - and, no doubt with so much of today's unsettled youth. I'm saying that the sort of depression you have now experienced is irrational. Realising this was what we called my "sea-change" from a world-view based on classical absolutism towards one based on relativism. It's that same realisation which now encourages me to pursue this dialogue towards whatever might be its logical conclusion, knowing that, at least, this would have to be radically different from what, as you now appreciate, follows so depressingly from classical mechanistic physics. I have no precept, of course, regarding where these discussions, if continued, might eventually lead, but at the very least, it releases me from the pessimism which I found to be endemic in our classical-science-based society. 1 replaced that pessimism with a new optimism and sense of adventure. I was convinced of the universal power of plain logic to reveal cosmical truth and reality, and this, thank goodness, emancipated me from a need to seek solace in any arbitrary form of religion. So, Tony, although I understand and sympathise with that dreadful feeling you described, I can't relate to it in the way I otherwise might have done.'

Carter sipped his drink, deep in thought. When he spoke, it was in a measured way.

"Yes, Dave, you're right. There's no logical basis for that sort of negativity - unless, as you say, it is what follows inevitably from our mechanistic tradition of physics. And I can see how that undermines the whole of any society which is based upon it. In that mechanistic mindset, there is always the lurking fear of realising our complete personal insignificance among all the atomic and astronomical cogs and gears.'

With that, Carter drained his glass and got to his feet "You can be sure, Dave, I won't "jump ship" on this logical odyssey of ours. Like you. I would like to know, as far as possible, where it leads.'

'Good on yer, Sport,' said Turner, feigning an Australian accent.

As Carter was retiring to his bedroom, he yawned and said, 'If that moose attacks tonight, it won't bother me.'

Turner replied. 'Okay, Tony, though next time it may be a real moose - or perhaps one of Professor Craig's bulldozers!'

'Nice thought, Dave,' Carter responded as he disappeared through the doorway. "Good night, sleep tight!'

The old childhood phrase made Turner smile. Perhaps Tony would get through this depressing phase without harm after all, he thought. He sincerely hoped so.

Chapter 11: The River

The Forms

The next morning was dull and grey. In ones and twos, the little group who had been having such an animated discussion the previous evening, drifted into breakfast.

"Why does the weather have to change now?' asked Helen of no-one in particular, 'Just when we've only a couple of days' vacation left.'

'I guess it's a sign that it's time I moved on,' remarked Rick. Marian was quick to notice the doleful tone in his voice and was surprised to find how disappointed she was by his news.

'Oh, no, you mustn't do that,' she said. "There are plenty of things to interest you here, even if the weather has turned bad on us.'

'You're so right,' he replied, with surprising alacrity.

Carter cleared his throat noisily to make them aware of his presence. 'Cheer up, everyone, it might improve later on. Hello, there's Don. Hi there, Don,' he called, 'everything okay last night?"

'Yes, thank God,' replied their host. 'Fortunately, Mr. Ainsley hadn't broken anything after all, but it was a particularly bad sprain and he'll have to rest up for a while. That means he and his wife will be checking out today, which presents me with a problem.'

'Really?' Carter was concerned. Surely losing a booking wasn't that crucial to the Lodge's finances. "Why's that?' he asked.

"They have to get to Kamloops to catch a flight back east. Mrs. Ainsley doesn't drive and Mr. Ainsley can't, so 'm wondering how the hell I'm going to get them there. Neither Megan nor I can go, and I've no spare staff, so you see my difficulty.'

'Tm sure Marian and I can help you out, Don,' offered Helen. "The weather's not good enough to go into the Park, so why don't we take the

Ainsleys to Kamloops, then we can do some shopping. What do you say, Marian?'

Her younger sister felt a little annoyed at having her day hijacked like this, but she decided not to argue about it. Helen had obviously made up her mind and, anyway, it was quite a good opportunity in the circumstances to look around the shops.

'Well, if you girls would like to do that, it would certainly solve my problem,' said Don. 'It's a straightforward drive, once you're on the Yellowhead. Takes about two hours.'

"Yes, I know,' replied Helen. "We drove up that way, though it was mostly in the dark. This time we may be able to see some scenery.'

Rick Jackson had been listening to this exchange with growing dismay. He saw the chances of furthering his relationship with Marian disappearing fast with such a short time left. He broke in.

"Hey, how about using my camper? It would be more comfortable for the poor guy than an automobile.'

"You mean, you'll lend your camper to us to go to Kamloops?' asked an apparently obtuse Helen.

'Of course he doesn't,' said Marian, smiling at Rick. "He's coming with us, aren't you, Rick?'

"Well, yeah, that was the idea,' he answered, a little perplexed. Helen must have been kidding them, he thought.

'Oh . . . I see,' Helen said, in mock surprise. "But, be warned, Rick. Marian will spend hours in the shopping malls.'

"That's settled, then.' Don was relieved that the problem was no longer his. 'T'll go and tell the Ainsleys what's happening.'

By the time Noel arrived for breakfast, Rick and the Taylor sisters had gone. Turner and Carter noted with some amusement and not a little sympathy the swift look of dismay and disappointment on the young man's face when he learned why Helen wasn't there, but his well-bred composure soon asserted itself.

"That means a day doing my own thing, at last,' he remarked nonchalantly.

'And what might that be?' enquired Turner.

'Stalking moose. Don has just told me, that if I want one, there's now a guide available.'

With that, Walter Collins came in, dressed in worn sweater and jeans.

'Hi, you guys,' he called exuberantly. 'How d'you like to do something different today, something that could be a bit risky?'

Having thus captured the interest of the other three, he went on.

'Let me explain. Charlie needs a couple of guys to pick up all the gear at a cabin out in the bush near the river. He's had to go out there on horseback to bring in the riding party, so he asked me to help out. As there's no way of getting to this place by truck -at least, not all the way - the last bit has to be done by canoe. Knowing you guys can handle a canoe, I suggested you might be prepared to give me a hand. I've done it several times before with Charlie and it's a job that needs at least two, so what do you say? Interested?'

There were three eager replies in the affirmative.

'T thought you were going to stalk moose, Noel,' Carter grinned at him.

'I was,' answered the Bostonian, 'but this sounds more interesting.' He had a sudden thought. "That's if you want me along, Walter.'

'Surely, Noel. We're taking two big Indian style canoes. Two men in each will make it that much easier - safer, too.' The clergyman turned to leave. ll get Don's van ready to load up while you get yourselves organised.'

In a short while, the four men were on their way in the Lodge's wellsprung and comfortable mini-bus, which had been emptied of most of its seats to accommodate luggage. To the roof rack were roped, very securely, the two canoes, constructed of a very tough plastic, with aluminium frames. It was a journey of about thirty miles, Walter told them, as first they had to drive to Clearwater, then along a logging road on the other side of the river. As he drove by, Walter pointed out Charlie's place, a few miles down the track from the Lodge. The cabin could not be seen from the road, but it was obvious from the length of time it took to drive between the boundaries that there was a considerable amount of land attached to it.

'Charlie can't be strapped for cash,' commented Noel. 'I wouldn't have thought a wrangler made that much money.'

Walter's reply was cautious, as he did not wish to let Noel in on the secret of Charlie's past.

'Out here property is cheap, especially with the recession in the logging industry. Besides, Charlie offset much of the cost of the land by selling-off some timber. The logging company, in taking it away, helped to clear some of the land for pasture.'

'A clever move,' said Noel, apparently satisfied with the explanation. If he felt there was any suggestion of a mystery, he did not express it.

The mention of the logging industry led to some discussion between Walter and Carter on the problems of the economy of the province in general; on the conflicting interests between those seeking to exploit its mineral and other resources and those concerned with preserving its great natural beauty.

'For instance,' said Walter, 'considering the size of the population of B.C., the state government spends an awful lot on road-building. This one we're on now, for example, from Clearwater up into the Park, is being extended and improved every year, but what for? For the benefit of the people in the valley or to provide better access for the developers?'

"Well," remarked Carter, 'as one of the people responsible for developing B.C.'s highway system, I suppose I should say it's a good thing for both, but I have to admit that I have mixed feelings about the way the country is being opened up.'

"You can't deny, though,' Turner interposed, 'that the new Coquihalla highway makes the trip from Vancouver a damn sight easier!"

Interested though he was in this discussion, after some moments of silence Noel's thoughts had drifted back towards the ideas which had been buzzing in his mind since the previous evening's discussion. Meeting these very interesting people, he felt, had put his mind in something of a turmoil.

"That was a very thought-provoking discussion we had last night,' he said, finally. "But it was a bit untidy, with so many different threads to follow. But that, I guess, is just how it would have been for the Greeks whose footsteps we were following, as you put it, Walter.'

The clergyman responded. 'Yes, it's humbling to realise how much our modern culture owes to those old grass-roots discussions . . ."

"... and to realise how insecure that basis is,' Carter put in, 'when you realise that those discussions couldn't have been much different from the

ones we've been having here. We accept - or at least, I did - that the way we think, nowadays, was all, at some stage, "written in the sky". But when you examine it, you can clearly see the extent to which our present conventions, like Topsy, "just growed".'!"

'That's right,' agreed Turner, 'and very messily, you might say. It lends point to what we were saying the other day about the purely circumstantial way truth is discovered. Just imagine what a gargoyle of dogmatism our culture would become if it kept on growing like that without being overhauled and revised - sometimes traumatically - from time to time to square with new knowledge.'

'Ir's like trying to build a skyscraper on the foundations for a tin shack,' suggested Carter. 'No wonder there are so many cracks appearing!'

Noel, however, had a more specific point to make.

'One thing which concerned me in particular,' he said, 'but which we didn't get round to resolving at the time was this question of forms. I think we all agreed that these forms, this Logos of character-complexes, Gestalten, or whatever, have to be in things, inextricably bound-up with them, and vice versa, not remote from them in some Platonic world-apart.'

"Yes, we all seem to be agreed on that,' said Carter.

'And wasn't it you, Dave,' Noel continued, 'who said that the candleflame is a good example of such a form steadily persisting while the processes within it are in constant and violent change?'

"Yes, I believe I did,' Turner replied.

'Anyway,' continued Noel, 'although I no longer subscribe to the old metaphysical idea of a "universe" in which entropy is supposed to reign supreme, it nevertheless seems to me that we have to take account of the fact that on a local scale, everything is subject to decay. After all, the candle burns down.'

'Yes, that's true,' Turner admitted.

'And isn't this the fate of all forms in general,' Noel went on, 'whether the form is that of a candle-flame or a man's character-complex - his personality, "soul", "spirit", "mind", or whatever we choose to call it? Like Charlie, I can't see what prevents these forms from just disintegrating

19 Topsy was a character in Harriet Beecher Stow's famous anti-slavery book, Uncle Tom's Cabin'.

- from "burning-down" by the natural process of decay, or localised entropy .

. as they must do as bits of order in #his world, not in some mystical or ideal world apart,' anticipated Turner.

"That's it,' Noel continued. 'I mean, if those forms are in -chings, then what saves them from dissolution as those things decay? And as for the existence of a "heaven", a realm of permanent and perfect forms... well..."

'T've been having a bit of bother thinking about those forms, too, Noel,' said Carter, 'but from a standpoint opposite to yours. You are looking at a candle burning down, but I was thinking of a child growing up . .

"Yes,' Walter interrupted, without taking his attention off the road, 'an unbiased observer, someone untainted by our modern mechanistic science - someone like Aristotle, for instance - would report that growth is at least as much a part of nature as decay.'

"Yes, at the very least,' remarked Turner, 'since form, or order, has to exist before decay can even be contemplated. As we've concluded, I think, logos has to be prior to physis.'

'Precisely!' said Carter. 'So the candle flame exemplifies entropy and the child growing up exemplifies the opposite, negentropy, which we've discussed. As you say, Walter, the world - the real, relative world as opposed to the old metaphysical "universe" - is filled with as many examples of order as of chaos..." He paused.

"Well . . 2? urged Turner, eventually. He was puzzled as to what point his friend. was trying to make.

'I'm getting there, Dave! Just let me pull my thoughts together.' He paused again, then continued, "What I was wondering, concerning examples of this second kind - the growth kind - is that when things are buildingup and developing, like a human being, a plant or some other organism, the form is seldom, if ever, what the thing actually is at any particular time. It's always something towards which that organism is developing. It's what a thing is going to be, or becoming what it ought to be as a perfect specimen in its milieu, which exists, you might say, not here and now but in the future.'

Ah, now I see what you're getting at,' said Turner.

'Good! replied Carter. 'For instance,' he continued, 'take the form of a man. That form is always imperfect to some extent, so the perfect form of a man is something we seem to have at the back of our minds as a standard of perfection for judging human imperfection - some imagined Jesus Christ..."

"Or a "Mister Universe", perhaps?' suggested Noel.

'Right,' acknowledged Carter, 'or the examples of men and women represented by Greek and Renaissance sculpture. And then there's the question of evolution in general. What forms of things does that lead to?'

No-one spoke, so he carried on.

'{ mean, where was the form of modern man and the forms of all his moral ideals and physical laws and so on when there was nothing around but single-celled creatures like the amoeba?'

"What you seem to be wondering, Tony,' put in Turner, 'is where those forms are when they direct the course of development in things but cannot be seen at the present time.'

"That's right,' Carter replied, 'and is "heaven", perhaps, some evolutionary end-state of that kind, which we project while we're at the transitional stages, like apes, or amoebae, on the way towards it?'

There was a thoughtful silence as they all contemplated this question of Carter's. They were now well down the road towards the town of Clearwater where homesteads were becoming relatively more numerous. On the left they could see the remains of a burnt-out cabin, its brick chimney stack sticking up like a broken finger. The mouldering 'For Sale' notice attached to it caught Carter's attention.

'Not much of an advertisement for that property, a ruined chimney stack! he remarked.

Walter glanced across. 'I don't think that will sell easily, not with its recent history,' he said.

"Oh? Why not?' asked Noel.

'It was the scene of a particularly grisly murder, about five or six years ago. That was before Charlie came to live in the valley, but I know that it caused a deal of misery for the folks round here. Businesses suffered from

the bad publicity and it almost wiped out business at the Lodge. Tourists stopped coming to the Park, you see.'

Noel was curious. "But why should it do that? This area isn't in the Park boundary.'

"That's right,' Walter replied, "but it was a family of tourists who were murdered, grandparents, parents and children, six altogether, and it took the police over a year to arrest the culprit, the man who lived at that homestead, there. And the media didn't help. They kept referring to it as "The Wells Grey Murders".' ,

- 'IT remember that,' said Carter, 'the hunt for the murderer spread all over Canada and he was virtually on the doorstep all the time - he even worked at the Lodge for a while, Don told me. But it was the resulting publicity which brought this area to my notice. I don't believe I should have realised the Park was here otherwise.'

'Nor did a lot of other people, perhaps,' added Turner.

"Then that's something good to have come out of a very painful episode for the local people,' offered Carter.

"How did the place burn down?' asked Noel.

'Arson, replied Walter. "There was an enormous amount of ill-feeling towards its owner, not surprisingly, perhaps. But no-one's ever been charged with that arson attack.'

Noel remarked, 'Someone who can execute such a vicious act as murder to eliminate other human beings is a long way from your ideal of a perfect man, Tony, although there are some people, no doubt, like that murderer, who would think such an act was perfectly logical and practical, so long as they didn't get caught. But I can't help thinking that perhaps those of us who condemn such acts do so only with reference to socially inculcated ideals of proper behaviour . . .'

'In other words,' said Turner, 'you're wondering whether these forms, or ideals, can exist and be potentially perfect anywhere outside man's finite imaginings.'

'Precisely,' responded Noel. 'For example, Christ is the Ideal of the Christians. But what about the Muslims, the Communists - or, say, the Mongols or the Vikings? Then there's Rousseau's ideal of the "noble

savage", the monastic ideal of the scholarly ascetic, the saintly martyrs . . .

Think of the difference between the nineteenth-century ideal of the "perfect gentleman", the sort who stayed. behind on the Titanic while the women were sent to the boats, and the modern ideal,' he said, scathingly, 'of the completely unscrupulous "survivor" who many people would now applaud for being saved disguised as a woman.'

The others listened quietly as Noel voiced the thoughts which were troubling him.

'So, which of these ideals,' he continued, 'shall we prefer? Which, shall we say, does evolution favour, as opposed to what might be favoured in some ideal, or heavenly scheme of things, or in some Nirvana - or Valhalla?'

'And on that same score,' put in Carter, 'you could ask whether that "ideal" scheme of things should include the forms of perfect bears, perfect fish and insects, perfect cats and perfect mice, perfect jackals and vultures...

'Perfect leeches and tapeworms, perfect cancers, perfect viruses . . .' interposed Noel. "What sort of "perfection" could ever include forms like that? How can we ever think of "heaven" or Logos, as a harmonious whole like Helen's and Marian's Perfect Symphony if so much that is conflicting and disharmonious is to be included.' He paused, then added, "Unless, of course, all sorts of things that are around now are doomed to be kicked out of the Orchestra - including, perhaps, us, together with all we believe in and hold dear.'

'You're becoming a real cynic, Noel,' commented Walter as Noel stopped to let his words sink in. "That's the kind of thing I'd have expected more of Charlie than of you.'

"Perhaps questions like that have been bothering him, too,' Noel replied.

In the silence which followed, the minibus, with Walter at the wheel, droned on, its deep springing making light of the irregularities of the road surface. The roads were surprisingly flat even though their packed gravel surfaces were rough. In the well-upholstered cladding of the vehicle, the continuous roar of the tyres on the gravel was muted as the forest scenery, wreathed in a soaking mist, swept by with an almost monotonous regularity.

Eventually, the silence was broken by Turner.

"You seem to be raising all sorts of issues there, Noel,' he said, 'and mixing them together with a big spoon. J think I see whar's bothering you, but it needs sorting out.'

'I guess that's so,' said Noel, 'It's no simple matter to get all these thoughts coherently together.'

"That's what philosophy's all about, my friend,' remarked Carter, 'which is why so few people take to it!

"You should know, Tony,' Turner reminded him. "Well, alright, let's try and get it together. The question you and 'Tony have raised between you, Noel, from your opposite points of view, wasn't, originally, whether or not these forms exist or whether they are real or only conventional, or whether they are perfect or imperfect. These are questions entirely separate from the one you started with. That was the question of where these forms, which have objective existence in an information-theoretical sense, can possibly reside when they're not actually in evidence, when they are not right there in front of you.'

'Not only was it a question of where they are when they are in hiding, so to speak,' Carter interposed, 'but also when. I mean, was that Logos, that world of Forms, something which existed in the past, to direct events from there, or is it something which lies in the future, a kind of destiny towards which present events are somehow continually being drawn or steered?'

"Or is it something there, invisibly, all the time,' added Noel, 'exerting its influence continually in some way, as a kind of pure potential? I must admit I have the greatest difficulty in understanding this Logos.'

"Well,'.said Carter, 'I don't find it difficult to think of it as a kind of original design for the world, introduced at the start, because it's no more than standard procedure, in mechanics, to think of causes as operating in that way, working from earlier to later, from past to future. That's more or less the idea of the Creation, the "Big Bang" and all that, wouldn't you say, Walter?'

"You mean the deistic notion of a clockwork universe which was woundup and set to run mechanically, with cause and effect going always, as you say, from earlier to later in a perfectly predictable manner?'

'Thar's it,' agreed Carter, 'As I say, as an engineer I get no difficulty with that. But in contrast, I have the greatest difficulty in thinking of the Logos as existing in the future, because there's no basis in the sort of science. I learned, for thinking of future states, or "destinies", as capable of influencing present processes.'

'Ah, but there is, Tony,' said Turner.

'You surprise me, Dave,' Carter replied. 'Can you give me an instance?'

'Well, the prime example, surely, is our old law of entropy. Regardless of whether we now think of that law as universal or as merely localised it says, in effect, that some future state, namely a statistical state of higher entropy or disorder, which may not yet be manifest in a system, determines the courses of present events.'

There followed a thoughtful pause. Then Carter exclaimed, "Well, of course! That j just didn't occur to me.'

'Nor to me,' Noel confessed. 'Tt's certainly surprising to think that entropy defines a kind of destiny for physical processes! I wonder how many scientists are aware of what a complete revolution in scientific thinking that represented at the time the law was discovered!'

'And I wonder how many scientists have pondered,' Turner added, 'why just that particular future-projected state of affairs, of maximum disorder, should determine the present, rather than any other future-projected state - of maximum order, or perfection, say - in a word, maximum negentropy.'

From the silence which ensued, it was evident that this had given much food for thought. Walter was now driving along the Yellowhead, where the traffic was "heavy" compared with the road along the valley. Soon, he pulled off to the right, passing the small but attractively situated Duck Lake, an ideal spot for picnics and swimming. They crossed the bridge over the Clearwater and took another right turn. About half a mile along, the road opened out into a large space. There were a few shed-like buildings, on one of which was the startling sign, "Clearwater Airport'.

'Airport?' queried Noel, incredulously.

Walter laughed. 'It's hardly on a par with Kennedy or Logan, It's mostly used by helicopters and small private planes owned by the logging companies.

- Beyond the sheds, the paved road became once more a dirt track, running alongside the river.

Then Noel spoke again.

"Let me see if I've digested this point you've made about entropy, Dave. You've already explained that in modern science, due to the principle of indeterminacy, we've unconsciously switched from thinking of sequences of events going mechanically from earlier causes to later effects, to thinking of these sequences as being non-mechanical and so, in a sense, goaldirected.'

'Right,' said 'Turner, 'even if the only such goal conventional science contemplates, so far, is the single one defined in terms of universal entropy, the inevitability of eventual utter chaos.'

'And this,' Noel went on, 'opens the door, you say, to thinking of endstates other than those defined by the law of entropy, as directing the courses of events in the same way. Have I got that right?'

"You have, Noel,' said Turner,

Carter was weighing up this. 'In my accustomed understanding of physics, as I say, I can't picture how that can happen. But at the same time, I can see that the cause of a child growing into a man can't be attributed entirely to the food he eats, though that must be a contributory factor. The main cause has to be his human potentiality, in some sense or other.'

'Right,' added Turner, 'the consumption of the food is a necessary cause but not a sufficient one for the child to become an adult.'

"That brings us to Aristotle again,' said Walter. "He listed all sorts of causes like that, some necessary, some sufficient, some both. Hed have thought it strange to contemplate the monopoly now placed by science on the one-way-mechanical sort of cause. His list was much more in accordance with what commonsense recognises.'

'In that case, "Back to Aristotle", I say,' declared Noel.

"Well, let's not be too hasty about that,' cautioned, Turner. 'For the moment, let's see what we seem to have established so far. First, we recognise that modern science, in embracing entropy, does allow at least that single minimum concession to the commonsense idea of goal-directed action; second, the "goal" defined by entropy, since it's not mechanistic,

must be futuristic; third, it's an objectively measurable factor, not just a human convention.'

'Ah, yes, that's clarified things, some,' Noel remarked.

'To put it another way,' Turner went on, 'we might say that the probabilities or improbabilities we observe in the processes of things, whether they fall under the definition of entropy or its opposite, negentropy, are not so much those of human language and communication as of the "language of nature", or "logos", as we are now calling it. So all that survives of the old idea of mechanical causality, in these new, purely probabilistic, or informational, terms, is the automaticity that results from objects which are essentially simple following the statistically most probable courses for those objects. But as we argued that evening at the lodge, when it was just you, me and Noel, Tony, what is probable for simple objects is not necessarily the same for complex ones - that is, volitional ones which have purposes of their own.'

'That also reminds me,' Walter recalled, 'of our first discussion at the restaurant about physical laws not necessarily being the only ones there

; are.

Walter pipped the horn to some road-workers who had waved him through the track their mechanical shovel had cleared in a rock fall. Keeping the roads passable in these areas was almost a full-time job. The volcanic ash and lava from which the rocks were formed were easily eroded. Winters were bitterly cold and summers intensely hot. In addition to the frost-heaves in the road this climate, together with the abundance of water working on the rocks, had created the spectacular creeks and waterfalls, and the process of erosion was continuous and rapid. As MacInnes had told Carter, it was geologically possible for a cascade the height of Spahats Falls to have been created in just a few hundred years. Civil engineering in this part of the world was certainly a challenge.

After safely negotiating the narrowed track, Walter returned to the subject in hand. 'I see - at least, I think J see,' he said, 'what you're saying, Dave, that there's not necessarily just ove statistical set of probabilities in a

single cosmic time, directing the courses of things. There are all sorts of different probabilities, in all sorts of different times, depending on the kinds of agents involved.'

'In a truly relativistic system, or observational "pluriverse", yes, Walter,' 'Turner replied. 'It wouldn't fit the old absolutist one-space and one-time "universe", of course.'

'I see the sense of that,' agreed Walter.

'Or it might help if we look at it another way,' Turner suggested. 'Let me ask you, how do you think nature works, logically or illogically?'

'Logically - at least, we must assume it does, otherwise there'd be no point in studying it,' answered Walter.

'So it's logical, wouldn't you agree,' Turner went on, 'that simple mechanical objects, like atoms, interacting purely at random, can be expected, by and large, to do nothing significant over a petiod of time but only what is, for them, most probable.'

'Yes, that's certainly logical,' agreed Carter.

"Then," said Turner, 'the "law of entropy" which makes this the rule of behaviour for such simple objects in some given system is a tautology, wouldn't you say?'

'T suppose it is,' replied Carter, after a moment's reflection. 'All the "law" says, in effect, is that simple objects do, by and large, only what it's logical for simple objects to do.'

'Exactly,' said Turner. Then he continued, 'So let me ask you, Tony, is it logical to suppose that what's logical for very simple objects is therefore necessarily so for all objects whatsoever, complex as well as simple?" Carter pondered on this for a moment then replied, 'No, Dave, I don't suppose it is necessarily so.'

'So, to apply the law of entropy to anything other than simple objects, the sorts one would naturally expect to obey the law, is illogical - a fallacy,' Turner concluded.

"Yes, Dave,' responded Carter, 'I think we've got that by now. So you're saying that since not everything is simple - since some things are highly complex.and not capable of acting in simple ways - like, for instance . . . well, just about everything we call organic - we should expect to find that

in the world at large there will be logical consequences of action other than those projected by the simple law of thermodynamics. By and large, things do what it's most logical for them to do - call that Carter's advance on the conventional law of entropy.'

Remembering what Walter had pointed out about the historical connection between 'logic' and 'logos', Noel added, 'And, Tony, in saying that things do what's logical for them to do, you are saying, in effect, with just a little adjustment of language, that each thing tends to behave in accordance with its own logos. And so far as the logos for each object is different, the ways in which those objects behave will be different - in accordance with different sets of probabilities.'

'Yes,' said Turner, "What's probable for one sort of object will be improbable for another, which splits the world into all sorts or categories of different probabilities and improbabilities.'

'I get it,' enthused Carter. "So instead of projecting utter chaos as an inevitable overall end-state, a "universal heat-death" as classical physicists claim, probability theory, applied to complex organisms, projects a world of evolving information and order. Come to think of it, Dave, that also supports our original discussion about the way the environment is forged . .

"That's tight, said Turner, 'We're just looking at it another way, and I'm hoping that the same conclusions we reached in those first discussions will be confirmed from another direction. So now at least we see that if a mostprobable statistical projection for simple objects is one kind of form - in the Logos, which is what we're talking about - then that can't be the only kind of form the Logos contains.'

'So, what about mathematical relations like pi, or Pythagoras' theorem?" suggested Carter. "These forms "exist", potentially, in the nature of things, in just the same way as the purely statistical ones. They "exist" in some sense even when they're not actually in evidence - that is, when there are no perfect factual instances of these circles, right-angled triangles, final statistical end-states or whatever, to be seen.'

'In that sense,' observed Walter, 'you could say that those forms are timeless - permanent, eternal - that their existence lies beyond space and

time, without that implying that they are in any way spooky, or separate from the world they appear in.'

'Yes,' Turner replied. 'Suppose I say something like "An object X cannot be both here and not here in the same instance", or "In a perfect circle on a perfectly plane surface, the ratio of the circumference to the diameter is pi. These statements are true, even when they are not actually stated or even when there are no actual instances of objects X or of perfect circles anywhere. They are formally true, that is to say, not only as forms in human language but also, so long as that language is correct, forms in the very nature - the very Logos, or Logic - of things.'

Recalling his former argument, Noel concluded, 'So one thing's for sure, Dave, these forms, these character-complexes, are not the sorts of things which, in themselves, can become exhausted, or "burn away", like the candle-flame. Nor does any question of where they are - or when - configure, because, as we've seen, being in the nature of logos, they're not describable in ordinary physical dimensions. They are not subject to decay or entropy.'

Carter agreed. 'So all those questions we were asking about where and when the logos is or was, in relation to physical objects and events, were just bum questions. We were getting caught up, yet again, in all the confusions of old-style mechanistic philosophy!'

Rounding a bend, they came upon a young black bear which, facing them, hesitated for no more than a moment, wondering whether to attack or flee, then opted for the latter. Reminded of what MacInnes had said about the speed at which bears can travel, the others urged Walter to keep pace with it. The speedometer, which was graded in miles per hour, read twenty-five, pretty close to what the geologist had told them.

This continued for a short space, the pursuers fascinated by the comical appearance of the bear's pink hind pads looking like the soles of oversized bedroom slippers. The bear was obviously a fine young specimen, and part of Walter's reason for pursuing him was to 'educate' him, as he explained later, to stay away from people who might take pot shots at him. Then, not wanting to distress the bear unduly, Walter slowed the vehicle slightly.

With the river on one side and the cliff on the other, the bear had been forced to keep to the road. Now, encouraged by what must have seemed to him the tiring of his pursuer, the bear suddenly left the trail and clambered expertly up the steep slope, showering down a small landslide of earth and stones on the spot where their vehicle would have been if Walter had not anticipated this. It reminded them again of what MacInnes had said, this time about a bear's ability to run uphill.

'T bet that bear is now telling his mother that a big house chased him,' laughed Walter, when the animal had gone.

'A house with very big ears,' chuckled Noel, thinking of how the vehicle had looked when they had loaded the two large canoes on top.

For a while, the logging trail was so rough that further discussion was impossible. Then Noel spoke again, showing that he was still chewing over their earlier discussion.

'So it's as you say, Dave, we mustn't think of the Lagos and its forms as existing 7m space or outside it or alongside it in any sense. That's the mistake we keep slipping into. As we decided, the Logos, the world of forms, isn't that kind of thing at all. It has dimensions additional to those of ordinary space and time, dimensions which allow alternative courses of action to exist, as it were, side-by-side.'

"Yes. It's best, I would say,' suggested Turner, 'not to think of the Lagos as something weird and wonderful, existing in mystical realms apart, but as no more nor less than what we observe in commonsense experience, in the logical interrelations and progressions of ordinary everyday events, as known to just about everyone. Also, of course, we need to remind ourselves that in modern as opposed to classical physics there is now no justification for the reductionist idea that these processes need to be explained in terms of one-track determinate mechanisms at the atomic and sub-atomic levels. What a wholly integrated science ought to be looking for in the way of causality, are /ogical explanations of phenomena. Making the correct discriminations between mechanical and non-mechanical ones.'

After a reflective pause, Noel said, 'Okay, that's the way we have to think of these forms, as being iz things, not necessarily "here" or "there" or any

"where" like physical objects. They are simply the logical potentialities of things according to their natures, to: be described - like entropy - in terms of future states, end-states, not prior or present ones. So it seems to me that the Logos is like the plot of a play, which directs the positions and movements of characters and objects without any particular location among them at any time. It's, so to speak, "in" the play though not visible on the stage in the same way that actors and the scenery are.'

"Yes, I think that's a good way of describing it,' Turner agreed.

'So,' interposed Carter, 'to return to that question of the perfection of the forms, which we shelved, is that perhaps the way "heaven" might be conceived, as a sort of interactive cosmic "play" with one of the alternatives being a particularly good or perfect ending, and in contrast, hell, the alternative ending projected by entropy. In the Logos, as you say, those opposite endings, or potentials, may co-exist, so that the drama of our existence depends on the sorts of decisions we make at various junctures, like the comedies and tragedies of the Greek playwrights.'

'So,' Noel mused, 'there's no single monolithic, mechanically determinate universe but as many "universes" as there are different observers. That reminds me of something I read somewhere about a "many universes" theory. Is that what you're talking about, Tony?'

"Could be, Noel, but I'm not sure I've got it right,' was Carter's hesitant

reply.

They had now reached the spot Walter had been seeking. This was marked, as he pointed out to them, by a few strands of ribbon tied to a branch. Under Walter's direction, they unloaded the canoes, checking that they contained the necessary paddles and ropes, and then hauled them towards the trees where the ground began to shelve steeply towards the river. Through the trees they could hear the rush of the swiftly flowing river, down which, as Walter told them, they would have to navigate for some distance towards an old trappers' cabin on the far bank.

Although they were old hands at canoeing, Carter and Turner were not indifferent to the prospect of what was to come. Anyone who was too blasé about such things was a danger in this kind of country. But what about

Noel, Carter wondered. Evidently he was enjoying the prospect, but how much did he know about this kind of canoeing?

After letting the first stout-skinned canoe slide down the slope and rattle across the stones on the river's edge, it was brought to a halt by Walter, holding the end of the trailing rope. When it was floating in the river eddies, the second canoe was slid down the slope and launched in the same way.

Walter, like Turner and Carter, was unsure of Noel's experience in this domain. Shouting above the roar of the river, he called, "You come with me, Noel, and sit in the front.' Then, back-paddling in the eddy while the other two settled into the second canoe he called, 'Just follow me,. guys, and copy what I do.'

The trip down-river to the cabin was not long. The current was fast but fairly even on this stretch. Noel found that although he was quite an expert at handling a single-handed kayak with a long, double-bladed paddle, he was not nearly so adept at using the short single-bladed paddle of the much larger, high-prowed, Indian-style canoe, which was entirely new to him.

"You have to use the "J" stroke,' Walter shouted, giving Noel a demonstration of the short outward flick of the paddle required at the end of every stroke to prevent the canoe from veering. Thus instructed, Noel coped quite well and after a while they spotted on the far bank the ancient log cabin that was their destination.

Having disembarked, they formed a chain from the cabin down to the river bank, along which they passed, first, the heavier bits of gear such as the axes and chainsaw, leaving the bedding-rolls, back-packs and other such items till the last. With all this cargo stowed away under tightly ropeddown tarpaulins, the appearance of the canoes now suggested a pair of loaded transport wagons ready to leave their depot. There was barely enough room remaining at the prow and stern for the men to wield their paddles.

'Gee, those canoes look heavy!' Noel remarked. 'I'm not grumbling, far from it,' he said, 'but surely there's a less primitive way of transporting gear than this?"

'Oh yes,' replied Walter, with good-humoured irony. 'It could be done much more easily by helicopter; but I suppose Don figures that hiring one of those each time would eat into his profits.'

When the loading was finished, the four men sat outside the cabin, at the water's edge, to eat their lunch. For a while, the loudest sound was that of the rushing water as the Clearwater sped on its way to its confluence with the North Thompson River, just a few miles beyond the town. There its rushing course would slow to a meander for most of its journey south to Kamloops, the "Place of Two Rivers", where it joined the South Thompson, to become just the plain Thompson, flowing in turn to join the great Fraser River and emptying its waters finally into the Pacific at Vancouver. These rivers had been the original way of reaching the interior of the province and the modern highways still followed the valleys as far as possible, except for the recently constructed Coquihalla Highway which climbed over the mountain ranges between Hope and Kamloops. That new road was a great feat of engineering and it had cut several hours off the journey to Vancouver. But it had led to economic ruin for those on the old section of Highway Five. These were the restaurateurs and owners of motels and gas-stations, who depended on the traffic for a living.

Relaxed after their meal, and in no hurry to return to their task of launching the heavy canoes back into the river, having chatted for a while about ordinary mundanities the four companions mulled over their earlier discussion.

'So we've decided, then,' Walter mused, 'that due to the revolution that has taken place in scientific thinking - which, you say, Dave, has yet to be fully realised - the causes of present events may be sought in the future as much as in the past, as Aristotle thought. This oughtn't present a mystery in modern science any longer, any more than it does in ordinary commonsense. After all, forward planning on the part of organisms is commonplace.'

No-one interrupted, so Walter continued, "Okay, then, so let's bring it all "down to earth". Take this river flowing along, here. What makes it do

that? Whar's the cause of its motion? Each water-molecule, we say, is

"pulled by gravity" down towards its lowest level, which is where the river meets the sea. So what we are seeing here are molecules flowing down the gradient, going from a higher potential of energy at an earlier time, to a lower potential at a later time. Isn't that what we usually suppose?'

'More or less,' said Carter, voicing the general agreement.

'So we might ask,' Walter continued, 'why those molecules don't stop here? How do they &vow that the place where they are right now is nor their final resting-place, that this lies at some further place at some future time?' He paused, but the other three obviously expected him to continue. "They dont know, says classical physics. Those simple entities are entirely mindless. All they do is respond to the forces acting upon them right here and now; and such a force is the gravitational force which they feel at this height above sea-level. Without such forces acting upon them, these molecules wouldn't know what to do.'

"That's a quaint way of putting it,' commented an amused Carter, 'but I think I see what you mean.'

'So do I,.' said Noel, 'but what are you getting at, Walter?'

'T'm not all that sure yet,' said Walter, obviously wrestling with a difficult line of thought, "but let me carry on thinking out loud.'

The others respectfully kept silent while he struggled on.

'According to classical physics, without those forces acting on things, the explanation that the water-molecules are "seeking" their natural level, which is neither up the valley nor here, but down in the Pacific, say, would make no sense.'

"That's true,' murmured Carter.

'And yet, classical physicists often do talk in that way, as if molecules can decide for themselves, in "finding their level" and so on. But, of course, when physicists talk like that they're only talking in a fagon de parler, ox "as it were" sense, while assuming that it's because the molecules are under the influence of those forces we're talking about.'

'Yes, Walter,' Turner agreed. 'In classical physics, since material particles are mindless, that assumption is absolutely implicit.'

Walter nodded in acknowledgement and went on.

'But what science has now discovered, from what you've been saying, Dave, is that this kind of mechanical-causal explanation, of "forces" by

which bodies act on one another, through space and so on, while it works well enough for most ordinary practical purposes, fails completely at the most basic level of physical analysis - the quantum-level.'

'Right, Walter,' said Turner. 'As we've already discussed, physical phenomena at the quantum level are no more than sequences of information-bits, or phenomenological data, which in themselves have fo mechanical forceconnections at all.'

'So,' Turner continued, hoping to clarify what he felt Walter was trying to say, 'to see this river flowing, in quantum terms, you have to abandon all the old explanations that its atoms are pulled by forces like those of "electrostatics", "gravity" and so on. Instead, you have to imagine, as we've already discussed, that what you see as this river flowing is a kind of "video" on a very fine-grained screen . . .'

Carter interrupted him. "Yes, Dave, I've been pondering over that video analogy. I concluded that the word "screen" isn't altogether appropriate in this context, because it suggests a flat representation from which the observer is separated, like a television viewer sitting in a chair in front of the scenario and away from it.'

'T fully agree, Tony,' replied Turner, 'but how else would you describe it? To do it justice you somehow have to imagine a holographic video-type presentation of this scene, in which you are completely immersed - in which you, yourself, are a part of the same scenario. Each one of us is, in effect, an interactive information-processor whose channels of communication, the five senses, are all part of the plot. So the result isn't something you can view remotely - as you say, Tony, like sitting in a chair watching a screen. The informational projection we share, is what we see all around us in all its glory, in all its impressive breadth and depth, its sights, sounds, scents and so on. It's undeniably real because there's nothing to contrast it with and by which it can be judged an illusion.'

'Thanks, Dave,' said Walter. 'I suppose that's basically what I was trying to express, your new "quantum-relativistic" way of thinking about physical reality.'

20 See N. V. Pope, 'A New World Synthesis', Philosophia Mathematica, II, Vol. 4, No. 1, Publ. by Fang, Old Dominion University, Norfolk, Virginia, USA, 1989, pp. 23-28.

'You were reminding us, Dave,' Carter said, 'that in modern science as you see it, ultimate questions about causality need to be answered in these "cinematic", or quantum-sequential terms, rather than in the traditional mechanistic terms.'

'Walter was the one who brought it up,' replied Turner. 'I merely reminded you of the analogy of the video-screen.'

'That surely was helpful,' said Walter. 'It was one of the two things I was trying to get clear in my mind.'

'And what was the other thing, Walter?' Noel asked.

'The other thing,' Walter replied, 'was that adopting this holographic video-analogy view of nature, however we conceive it, doesn't detract in any way from our aesthetic appreciation of nature's breadth, depth and beauty, in the way classical physics does. In other words, this new way of thinking about science doesn't conflict in any way with our normal artistic appreciation of nature. It takes "relativity" and the "quantum" out of the realm of what Marian described as the "ugly and incongruous" and makes it as natural a part of the scenery as those rocks and trees.'

'So, how do you think of the Logos now, Walter?' prompted Noel.

'The way to think of it, I guess,' Walter replied slowly, gathering his ideas together, 'is on the lines of Dave's video-hologram analogy. The Logos, if you like, is the "programme" without which there would be no scenario, such as this around us here, but only, as it were, random quantum "dots and flashes".'

'Scarcely even that,' interposed Turner, 'since there would be nothing around capable of making that absolutely minimal kind of observation.'

'Of course,' Walter agreed. 'Without the Logos, none of this,' he said, with a sweep of his arm at the scenery, 'would be here - we wouldnt be here! It would all be, as the Bible says, "without form, and void"."

Turner took this up.

'T often think that the nearest we get to imagining such a formless null and void world is to sit in front of a TV set when there's no programme running. All you see, as you say, are those random flashing dots in which there's no information whatsoever. That, if you like, is the world as physicists depict it in terms of their imagined "purely mechanical". laws - unless, of

course, you include laws like those of thermodynamics as future-directed in the way we discussed. So, we have to ask ourselves, what's the difference when the programme is running - ignoring the "hidden mechanisms" behind the screen, of course, which over-extends the analogy.'

'In purely screen-terms,' ventured Noel, 'there's no difference at all between the programme and the non-programme except that the sequenceprobabilities have been changed from randomness to significance, from being formless to being informational or logical.'

'That's right,' replied Turner, 'or you could say, from chaos to order or design, or intention. The difference is that in these new terms, the sets of logical forms and sequences which characterise all physical objects, take the place of pure randomness. A whole landscape comes into focus, like this one here. It ceases to be just a random sequence of quantum blips and takes on shape, depth and so on. And hete we are, sitting on these logs, with the river in front of us, the forest on the bank over there and those rocks with the water pouring over them, the mist up the valley, the mountains beyond; and beyond those mountains, the sun, moon and stars, as far as we are capable of observing them on an astronomical scale, right to the quantumlimits of perception. So,' he went on, 'where, in all that, are those forms we were talking about? He answered his own question. 'They're there, right in front of us, among us and within us, where else! And what is the Logos, the programme or plot into which these forms fit and in which they all play their part? Well, again, here it is, just as it will be tomorrow and the next day, whether or not we or others like us are here - since our personal absence from the scene leaves behind all those information-transacting agents, such as animals and plants, right down to those molecules, or "observicles" as you named them, Walter, when we met that first time at the Lodge.'

As Turner paused, Carter said, thoughtfully, 'So logos isn't some dark metaphysical abstraction, of the sort you read about in dusty academic textbooks.'

'Right, Tony! Turner spoke with some passion. 'What else can it be but the sheer ogic of physical events and processes, without which there could be no physics, no science nor anything else to speak of, not even a language to describe it.'

'| wonder, interposed Noel, 'what our Professor Craig would make of all this? It all follows perfectly from what we discussed with him at the Lodge.'

'Very little. I should think,' said Walter. "No more than any other "dyedin-the wool" classical physicist, would, I guess!"

Carter responded: 'Can we blame these physicists, though? It's hardly their fault that they are not taught philosophy, not even the philosophy underlying their own subject. Craig's attitude, it seemed to me, is the result of the artificial division in our Western education between "Arts" and "Science". But nature doesn't work like that, does it?"

Noel added, 'You say, Tony, the "philosophy uriderlying their subject". What "underlying philosophy" would that be? I can't recall any identifiable philosophical underpinning whatsoever underlying modern physics.'

'I fully agree, Noel,' replied Walter. 'As a clergyman it reminds me, naturally, of what it says in the Bible about the house built upon the sand.' He quoted, "The waves rose and the wind blew, and beat upon that house. And it fell, and great was the fall thereof". Do any of you guys think that this might be the fate of our great edifice of Modern Physics?"

Noel replied, 'Well, I can't see how it can survive in its present form, that is to say, without some firm philosophical foundation.'

'Yes, Walter, said Turner, 'the biggest disaster that has ever occurred in the history of science is, surely, its severance from Natural Philosophy, which is like severing its body from its head and brain.'

The others nodded their agreement.

By now, the mist drifting down the valley had turned to a light drizzle. Jewels of moisture were growing on the lush mosses and leaves. Walter rose and pocketed the remains of his lunch. He cast a critical eye beyond the little inlet where the canoes were tethered, towards the swiftly-flowing current they would have to cross, downstream, in the heavily laden craft. Noel had been wondering how, going downstream again, they would get back to their vehicle. But he needn't have worried. The mystery would soon be solved.

'Time to go, I guess,' Walter said.

Chapter 12: The Rapids

Why be Moral?

They took one final look around the old trappers' cabin, leaving it as secure as they could against the unwanted attentions of animals. Then, taking up their original positions in the laden canoes, they paddled out from the small sequestered inlet towards the fast-flowing current. As the prows of the large canoes swung majestically into the flow, the turbulent water began to swirl and hiss all round them.

Sitting in the front of the lead canoe, Noel was enjoying himself. The current, he noticed, was much faster on this stretch of the river. Indeed, it seemed to be accelerating. Then, as they were well under way, he heard Walter call from behind him, 'Make for the veel'

Glancing down the perceptible gradient ahead of him, Noel could see what Walter meant. There, not far off, the rapids narrowed and funnelled through a gap in what looked like a shelf of rock. Not an alarming hazard but enough to arouse, in Noel, a sense of excitement. 'Taking a kayak through there would be one thing, but these heavily laden canoes were something else!

As the ponderous craft picked up speed, Noel let out an involuntary whoop of exhilaration at the prospect of having to steer his end of the canoe between the two in-rushing spouts of water on either side. The gap was not too narrow, but narrow enough, and if the boat were not aimed correctly, it could easily be swamped.

Noel was suddenly aware of his inexperience with this particular craft and, more especially, with this kind of paddle. Use the 'J'-stroke, he repeated to himself, remembering Walter's advice. Was it due to himself, he wondered, or to Walter at the back, that the canoe remained so steady on course in the boiling current. This was good, he thought, with heady

enjoyment. It was no pre-packaged tourist-event, or prepared bit of sport, but a real job of work, done in the way the old trappers had done it, on this same river, in much the same conditions, no doubt, as when the Native Americans dominated this area.

In this happy frame of mind, Noel spotted a bear, making its way along the shingle towards the vacated cabin. He called to the others, but they had already noticed it. Thus preoccupied, and turned half-way round in his seat, Noel was unaware that the canoe was now steering itself towards the vee but somewhat to the left of it. His attention returned quickly to his task just as Walter gave a warning shout. Hurriedly, Noel made to correct his course towards the centre of the water chute.

But the time left to him was too short and his reflexes in using this unfamiliar paddle were all wrong. As the craft pitched forward into the funnelling water, he was hit full force by the inrushing spout on the lefthand side. It was as though someone had emptied a cold bath into his lap. The water, ice-cold from the glaciers not too many miles away, took his breath away. Looking down, he could see his neatly-stowed parka soaking wet between his knees and he realised that he was sitting in what seemed to him like gallons of icy water.

He turned towards Walter to apologise for his mistake, only to find the latter rolling about with laughter. It must have seemed funny from where Walter was sitting, relatively dry behind the barrier of tarpaulin, and with that realisation, he began laughing himself. Carter and Turner, he noticed, were also sharing the joke as, in the rear, they navigated faultlessly through the tumble of water that had swamped him.

Setting aside his discomfort, Noel enjoyed the tide down the accelerating current.

'Make for that beach on the right, just before the bend,' Walter called. 'We must put in there,' he emphasised. 'I'll show you why, later on.'

All Noel had to do was to paddle hard on his left side, leaving Walter to do the steering from the stern. But the canoe, already fully burdened, was now even heavier and down at the bow with its added weight of water. Reaching the bay that Walter had indicated was not easy. They overshot slightly and were nearly swept back into the current. What the hell!

thought Noel. Already soaking wet, it seemed natural to him to jump into the water, hip-deep at that spot, to manhandle his end of the boat towards the bank, leaving Walter to beach the stern with his paddle. Then, jumping expertly ashore, Walter roped the canoe to some tree-roots. As he did so, the other canoe came gliding in alongside.

Now began the unloading. The tarpaulins were removed and spread on the ground. Then the dry and the wet bits of cargo were sorted and laid upon them. Walter, leaving the others to empty and dry the wet canoe, climbed up the bank, carrying the chainsaw, and disappeared through a narrow gap in the trees. Soon the others heard the roar of the saw and some cracking sounds, following which Walter re-emerged, minus the chainsaw, which he had left to be collected further up the trail. 'Windfalls,' he explained, by which he meant fallen birches, those relatively short-lived trees that were forever crashing across the trails and continually having to be cleared.

On Walter's instructions, they part-filled the two canoes with some of the luggage, then hefted them onto their shoulders and set off up the steep and rocky trail to the road. Young and fit as Noel was, he nevertheless found the going tough and quite exhausting. He was glad of the rest every time they stopped to cut through more windfalls. He could not help but admire the stamina of Walter and the two older men.

He hoped he had enough energy left for getting back to the van. They had beached the canoes quite a good distance downstream from where the vehicle was parked. Noel had wondered about this, realising that it would have been impossible to paddle the heavy canoes back upstream against that current.

On reaching the road, they had a short rest and then returned for the remaining gear, having to make three trips in all. During the last of these, some of the water-weakened bags Noel was carrying burst, spreading their contents of sugar and dried milk down the front of his soaking wet trousers. Already uncomfortable, he was now treated to the sensation of a creeping stickiness all round his nether regions. His stoical acceptance of this new discomfort, for a man so used to life's little luxuries, was not lost on the others.

'You smell like a meringue, Noel,' remarked Carter, with a friendly grin. 'Hope the bears don't catch a whiff of you.'

When all the gear was at road level, Walter walked towards a nearby thicket from which he miraculously produced a battered but perfectly serviceable bicycle.

T'll be back with the van in about ten minutes,' he called to them, as he pedalled off.

While he was gone, the other three sat among the baggage and chatted in a desultory way. Then Noel asked the two.older men, "Have you guys done this before?"

'Not on this stretch of the river,' Turner replied, 'but we've done this kind of thing, the canoeing and the portaging, many times on campingexpeditions.' He intuited that the younger man was feeling rather foolish.

'I guessed as much,' Noel said, pensively, then lapsed into silence.

A short time later, Walter returned with the van, from the top of which he then removed the bicycle and replaced it in its hiding-place. Meanwhile, his companions began loading the gear. Noel was glad of this work, which kept at bay the shivers which sitting in his wet clothes had begun to produce.

Loading completed and with the canoes securely roped to the roof, they began their return journey to the Lodge. Not far along the road, Walter stopped and parked the van, inviting them to follow him along a narrow and ill-defined trail which led to a bluff overhanging the river. Below them the river was a roaring torrent, swirling around a bend as if in a centrifuge, then creaming over rocks criss-crossed with the mangled remains of fallen pines, stumps and birches, some of them, stripped of their branches, pointing upstream like lances.

'That's where we would have ended-up, Noel,' said Walter, laconically, 'if we hadn't managed to beach where we did.'

It was the place called the 'Sabre Tooth', he explained, where the party of German canoeists had met their deaths. Noel shuddered, not altogether from the cold. There was no need to expand on it. The implications were plain enough.

On their way back to the van Noel found himself marvelling at the almost cavalier attitude of people in these parts to dangers of this kind, indeed to dangers in general. He thought of some of the junk machinery that Don Frost allowed his employees to drive around and operate, in the environs of the Lodge. Anything like that on the highway or in a factory would be instantly condemned, yet out here there were no recriminations, nor expectations of anything different. You did what you could with whatever you had available. Back in the city, there were people he knew who would regard this attitude as irresponsible. Great emphasis would be placed on Health and Safety and worries about litigation in the event of accidents. How different, Noel thought, were those standardised and conventionalised expectations, full of city-life artificiality, hedged-about with all sorts of official rules and regulations, mandatory precautions and so on. This was not only in the offices and factories but also on conducted "adventuretrips" for education and pleasure. In this wilderness, one faced all the exigencies of nature in the raw, with no rule-book but the one you carried in your head and which you updated continually with reference to your own experience. You looked out for yourself and expected others to do the same, with no fuss. How much, he wondered, had urbanised man lost in terms of that sort of natural self-reliance and sense of adventure, for the gain in official "concern" for standards of "safety". The hypocrisy of it all, he reflected, was that what seemed to be a social concern for other people's safety was no more, basically, than a fear of being faced with legal action.

The drive back to the Lodge was uneventful. The already weary men were made drowsy by the van's heater, which was causing Noel's drying clothes to steam up the windows.

'It's like a Chinese laundry in here,' said Walter and opened a window. Revived by the cool air, they mulled over the morning's events, for the rest of the journey, with the natural satisfaction of men n who have done a good job of work,

When Walter pulled up outside the Lodge's entrance, Don Frost came out to greet them.

"Thanks for doing that, guys,' he said. 'It was a big help . .

He stopped and stared as a creased and bedraggled Noel climbed down from the minibus, looking a far cry from his usual immaculate self.

"Whatever happened to you?' he asked.

The explanation caused their host to react in the same way as the others had done. He laughed out loud at the exaggerated image in his mind of the sophisticated Bostonian gliding along up to his armpits in the river in the nose-down canoe. His wife came out to see what was going on.

Megan Frost was much more concerned than amused at Noel's plight.

'You've been soaked through,' she said, rather stating the obvious. "Don, make sure the stove in the guest sitting room is stoked-up so Mr. Findlay can get out of those wet clothes.'

'''m perfectly okay, Mrs Frost, really,' Noel assured her. "We have to unload all the gear first.'

'Don can help with that,' said Megan decisively, 'while you get yourself changed and comfortable. Get into some clean clothes and bring those wet ones to the laundry room. I'll see that they're washed and dried for you.'

't think you'd better do as you're told, Noel,' smiled Carter. "Never argue with a woman.' ,

'Because women are the only ones with any sense,' answered Megan, pertly. "Off you go, Mr. Findlay! She disappeared inside the Lodge.

Noel gave the others a wry grin. 'Sorry, guys, but I'd better obey orders.'

He went off in the direction of his room in the nearby cabin. On his way, he met Charlie, walking up from the corral. The wrangler eyed him up and down.

"What the hell have you been doing?' he asked.

'Swimming!' Noel replied, cheerily.

'In what?' said Charlie, observing the dried-milk-stains all down Noel's front.

'In the river,' replied Noel, simply.

Charlie looked sagely up at the sky.

"Wouldn't have thought this was the weather for it,' he said.

Some time later, dressed once more in perfectly pressed slacks and cashmere sweater, Noel joined his canoeing-companions and, together with

Charlie, all cat-napped and refreshed, they gathered around the stove for a welcome mug of hot soup. By this time, the wrangler had heard about the mishap. In a strange way, it made him feel friendlier towards Noel.

"Hi? he said, as the Bostonian came in. "Thanks for taking that swim to save the canoe and the gear. It would have been catastrophic if you'd lost it.'

'Never mind their lives, eh? interposed Megan, from behind the bar. "You men are so kind and caring!'

"That's alright, Mrs Frost,' answered Noel. 'In a funny way, I enjoyed it, and I guess it'll make a good after-dinner story back home.'

In this quiet afternoon they had the sitting room to themselves. When the topic of Noel's soaking was exhausted, a long, warm and comfortable silence ensued, during which Noel seemed deep in thought. Eventually he spoke, addressing his companions of the river-trip.

"You know,' he said, 'I've been thinking about what we were discussing just before I got that soaking. I think you said, somewhere, Dave, that logic cant dictate belief, that all logic can do is to test our beliefs for coherence and consistency. In other words, we are free to believe just what we like as long as those beliefs don't turn out on analysis to be logically absurd and therefore unfulfillable in the nature of things. All beliefs, you said, should be tested in that way, in discussions of the sort Socrates demonstrated in his dialogues . .

'And for which he was put down by the Establishment,' Carter added.

"Well, I guess,' said Charlie, 'that's what democratic institutions like Parliament and the American Constitution are for; the encouragement of free thought and speech. But,' he continued, 'I seem to have missed something interesting. I'd hoped we'd be able to continue our last evening's discussion, but I should have known that even a day like you've had, swilling around in water and icing yourselves with sugar wouldn't stop you guys from tossing ideas around.'

'Sorry, Charlie,' said Noel. 'But to give you the gist of it, you'll recall our discussion, last night, about Logos and those forms which you referred to as Gestalten. Well, we tried to fill out the picture of how those forms, or whatever you choose to call them, fit in with the world as traditional

science describes it. We came to the conclusion that they fit modern physics remarkably well - better, it seems, than even trained physicists seem to realise.'

'How so?' asked Charlie. 'I'd have thought that physics would be dead against such non-material things as forms.'

'Classical physics, yes, Charlie,' Walter replied, 'but not modern physics. That's what we discussed during lunch down by the river. Modern physics, as Dave has explained, with its gradual shift in emphasis from mechanical to information-statistical explanations of things, from analog to digital, you might say, is stealthily reversing the old idea of one-way cause-effect sequences.'

'What d'you mean by that, Walt?' Charlie asked.

'It means,' his friend explained, 'that in modern physics, the reasons for things happening may, in some cases, lie in the future rather than the past.'

Turner expanded on Walter's reply. "That makes the form of a thing, its character - or Gestalt, as you called it - a real potential for development of complex beings rather than the totally improbable result of series upon series of randomly-occurring events with no potential for development. So in modern physics we have the paradigm shift in which the relations between things become the /ogical relations between their forms, rather than purely mechanical ones between their atomic parts.'

'This,' Carter added, 'means that things are related as wholes, or characters, rather than as collections of parts.'

"That sounds interesting,' said Charlie. "Whether or not what you're saying might or might not be acceptable in physics, it's sure as hell acceptable in the science of psychology, where that goal-serving sort of activity is simply the norm. It's what psychologists call function, which is as central to clinical psychology as Newton's laws are to mechanical engineering. I've been thinking about it, some, since I saw you last - that science really ought to get its act together. But now I'm interrupting. You carry on. I'll pick up the thread as I go along.'

Noel broke in at this point.

'Tm still not entirely happy, though, about those forms. I can see that they exist, that's obvious; after all, they are what gives shape and significance to what would otherwise be a complete chaos of meaningless impressions

- well, not even that, just randomly occurring quantum blips, which is impossible to imagine - or even talk about anyway.'

His listeners nodded in agreement.

'I can also see,' Noel continued, 'that the opposite of this chaotic state of maximum disorder would be a state of maximum order, or cosmos; in other words, a perfect and harmoniously integrated set of forms which one might call "heaven". So if one adopts your philosophical viewpoint, Dave, then it's no more illogical for religious people to believe in "heaven" as a natural destiny for things than for physicists to believe in utter chaos - the thermodynamical "heat-death of the universe". Ic's simply a matter of choice, pure belief in either case.'

'I would say,' put in Carter, 'that the physicists' belief in the universal rule of entropy now seems the least credible of the two, because without assuming some miracle at the start, there could be no order to begin with. It would be indescribable chaos throughout, with no beginning and no end, with no potential for anything else.'

'I agree, Tony,' replied Noel, "but let's say that I prefer, as I think you do, to remain agnostic on both counts. That is, these discussions have convinced me that neither of these projected destinies is universally assured by any "rules" we might devise, of entropy, negentropy or whatever. For such as myself - and, I] venture to suggest, for commonsense generally - they both remain open possibilities.'

"You would say, then,' suggested Turner, 'that they are ideals or principles which, in this real world, in the nature of things generally, are in continual conflict - between good and evil in the Christian doctrine, as we've discussed."

'Right? Noel continued, 'And I'm now convinced that there's no overriding "determinacy" working towards either the one end or the other. It seems to me, that we have the real choice of deciding for ourselves, freely and individually, at every stage, the way to go in contributing, by our actions, towards either order or chaos - good or evil, or light and darkness, if you like - in this universe of discourse.'

'T surely would go along with that, Noel,' Walter said, 'because without that sort of personal freedom, or "free-will", there can be no rational basis at all for religious conviction of any kind.'

'That's right,' Carter interposed. 'If we are merely bundles of mechanical reflexes and chemical reaction, then we are just like machines. And there obviously can't be any morality, religious or otherwise, in a machine. And the fact that there zs morality on such a global scale, even if only among us humans, implies that nature is not a machine - not entirely, anyway.'

'To take up that point,' said Turner, 'about choosing one's direction, I tend to think of logic as simply the process whereby we try to sort out the natural logical order of the forms of things and events in nature, by classifying and dividing them in what we call chemistry, biology, zoology, geology, mechanics, psychology and so on, whereas Logos, with a capital el, signifies order itself as a projected Ideal, or Potential, a kind of perfect logical end-state of organic evolution with the same nature as human reason but, of course, infinitely greater.'

"To me,' put in Walter, 'that makes sense of the biblical identification of Logos with Christ as "The way, the truth and the life".'

'In other words,' added Turner, 'religion, although it involves belief needn't necessarily be a belief that's in conflict with science, far less with logical reason.'

"That's right,' enthused Walter. 'I've always maintained that the idea of religion and science being antagonistic is a fallacy. And, now, from the way we've argued, I can't see any contradiction whatever that, from the standpoint of modern relativistic science, we can think of "heaven", if only as an Ideal, as the ultimate logical coordination of all the various individual perspectives - or "observer-frames" in the jargon of the physicists. If this is no more than a belief, then so is the scientific belief in universal entropy as the ultimate end of things.'

'Let me see if I've got your drift, Walter,' Carter said. "You envisage all the various organic forms we see around us in all their current evolutionary stages, linking up or developing into a final, single all-embracing organism; potentially something like the community of organisms which make up our bodies and give us the kind of coordinated, overall perception we our-

_selves have, but on a vaster scale, of course. And that "ultimate organism" towards which evolution tends would be your "God", I suppose.'

Walter was about to say something, but Carter carried on.

'So you might also think of your "heaven" and its opposite, "hell", in terms of cosmical "pleasure" and "pain",' he suggested, 'since pleasure is the sensation an organism like us feels when everything is working healthily, in a coordinated and harmonious way, and pain is the sensation we feel when that harmony is disrupted.'
