'That,' said Turner, 'suggests the association, in classical literature, between "hell" as infinite disharmony, pain and suffering - as expressed in Dante's Inferno, for instance - and of "heaven" as complete harmony, of a sort that gives infinite pleasure - to animals and plants as well as us, by all accounts, "

Noel, aware that his objection to the discussion about the forms was becoming side-lined, had waited somewhat impatiently throughout this enthusiastic exchange. Now he broke in.

"That's as may be,' he said, "but for us, as finite creatures living in the here-and-now, all that heavenly projection into the future, however interesting, can never be more than useless speculation.'

'But surely,' objected Carter, 'you wouldn't deny that since there's no logical absurdity in the concept of heaven and hell, as we've just described them - allowing for the archaic terminology, of course - their existence becomes a real possibility.'

'No,' Noel replied. 'I don't contest that those possibilities exist. It's just that I can't quite see what are the real implications of such a belief, for us, in the conduct of our lives, here and now. What can it matter to us, in our short span in this world, what the end-state of mankind might be? What concern of ours can it be that, way-off in the future, there may be these final goals we call "heaven" and "hell", or whatever, which are not likely to be experienced by us or even by the remotest of our successors, on this planet or on any other?"

'But I thought you agreed that there is a Logos,' protested Carter, not

'understanding what Noel was getting at.

"That's right, Tony,' Noel replied. 'I have no difficulty there, any more than I have any difficulty in accepting the real existence of forms. I accept that the Logos exists. It's right there before us, as we discussed this morning

by the river. If it weren't, there would be nothing we could think or speak about - or even be aware of. All that, I've agreed, is what gives things and natural processes their shape, significance and logical progression. The idea appeals to me because it's so down to earth, dispensing with all metaphysical references to abstract and inaccessible realms of being. But the same thing can't be said about this ultimate Logos, this "heaven" as you are now describing it, Walter. Surely you see that!'

There was a pause while the company digested this. Carter got up and stirred the logs in the stove. Not that it needed it, because the logs were burning well and the room was comfortably warm. The others stared through him in thoughtful silence. Watching him with the glow of the fire lighting his face, Turner was reminded of the rock portraits of Abe Lincoln and the other U.S. presidents he had once seen at sunset. He was also reminded of an incident when he and Tony were boys, playing around, together with other lads, on a rubbish tip, parts of which were in a chronic state of spontaneous combustion. The game they were playing was that of finding tin cans with lids, putting some water in them and, with the lids jammed firmly on, poking them into a little furnace which had broken out at the base of the tip and watching them explode. Tony had found a stoutly manufactured can with the word "Zebo" printed on it. This had a metal screw top. which Tony had twisted on as tightly as he could. While the cans of the others were exploding satisfactorily in minutes, Tony's "Zebo" can seemed to take an age. Eventually his patience gave out and he took a stick with the intention of removing the can from the fire to examine it. At the very moment when the stick touched it, the can exploded violently in his face, with the biggest bang of them all. Horrified, his friends raced to where Tony was kneeling, with his face buried in his hands. Fearing the worst, he, Turner, got his friend gently to uncover his face. When Tony turned towards the group their horror turned to hilarity when they were confronted by a Carter whose face was so black that no feature was visible except for his eyes, which blinked like white lights in the dark as he opened

_and shut them. As it turned out, Carter's face was only mildly scalded, but when they read the writing on the remains of the can it had become plain

that it had contained quite a large remnant of what was described there as the blackest grate polish on the market.

Turner's reverie ended as Walter broke the contemplative silence.

'I now see what you meant, Noel,' he said, 'about being unhappy with that part of the discussion which dealt with the forms. There's some confusion, you think, in this association between "heaven" and a final "Logos". You feel that anything which guides the logos towards a perfect end-state, a Logos with a capital el, must be, once again, remote, or "otherworldly"?

'No, not quite,' answered Noel. 'If you'll bear with me, guys, I'll try to explain. For instance, I see that it makes sense to anticipate this logical progression, or logos, of things, towards some end or other, not just from minute to minute, for ordinary, practical, commonsense purposes, but also for long-term, perhaps cosmical reasons . . .'

Noel paused to compose his ideas.

'I mean,' he went on, 'suppose you were out walking and you came across a railroad, or a highway. If you followed it for a while, noticing that it was complete and not broken, and then left it, you would realise that where you met it and where you left it were not necessarily the whole beginning and end of it. Only a fool would make that assumption.'

Again, his companions nodded and murmured their agreement.

'So,'as I say, I see no logical contradiction in projecting our rational beliefs in those channels we are used to as far as we wish, in either direction, past or future. We are just as free to anticipate that the road leads, in the future, "over the horizon" of our life-span on this planet towards a final and. perfect Logos, or "heaven" we will never experience, as that the road leads to a "heat-death" - if that weren't a fallacy, as we've discussed. To me, as you said, Walter. I agree that the one belief is no more "religious" than the other. I don't regard either as being any more or any less speculative 'about the future than any cosmologist's account of the past. I mean, I am inclined at least to be critical of any signposts erected by Science or Religion telling me, as if they knew for sure, that it is necessarily the one destiny or the other.'

'T'm with you there, Noel,' said Charlie. 'Since no-one's ever been to the end to check, in either case, then what faith can you have in what any of those signposts tell, like science's "Law of Entropy" or the "Creeds" of the various Churches? I guess, like me, Noel, you prefer simply to mistrust them all.'

'Yes, Charlie,' Noel replied, 'but no longer in any dogmatically "atheistic" way, I admit. As I keep saying, I now accept that none of these institutionalised guesses, of science or religion, may be any more assured of validity than any other - except that on the basis of the evidence, the scientific belief in the universality of utter Chaos now seems to me so strange that I don't know why I or anyone else could ever have entertained it.'

'Yet you object to what we've concluded,' Carter pointed out. 'You still haven't told us why.'

'No, I haven't,' Noel agreed. 'Pm sorry for getting side-tracked.'

"My fault. Sorry! Charlie apologised.

'That's okay, Charlie,' said Noel. 'I was getting carried away. Let's see. Where was I?'

"You were saying,' Turner reminded him, 'that our account of the Lagos as a final end-state, was lacking something, and you were going to tell us what that "something lacking" is.'

'Okay, got it? Noel said. "Look at it this way. The only rational justification for doing right, as opposed. to what is merely convenient or pleasant, used to be that one's reward was not in this world but in "the next", in "heaven", not on earth. Those who deserved it would reap that reward after death. But we've dispensed with that "next world" idea, haven't we? So, obviously, we can't expect any reward from that quarter. The only "heaven" we can now sensibly anticipate has to be in this world, in that perfect EndState, or Logos, of Walter's. But that's too far off in the future to be attained by any of us in this life - in the only life we can sensibly contemplate - so I guess we're back where we started, in finding ourselves, so to speak, "twixt the devil and the deep blue sea".'

'So your problem,' responded Turner. 'is that you feel it's all purely academic as to what Heaven, or Logos - the perfect end-state - might be when, according to this present line of thinking, we can never have any part in it.'

'Just so, Dave,' Noel replied. 'I mean, what's the point of doing what's right and even perhaps painful, or suffering martyrdom like Jesus and Socrates, as opposed to doing just what the hell we please, whatever turns us on? Why not, as Omar Khayyám put it, "take the wine and leave the rest"?

'But that, again,' Walter broke in, 'opens the door to all sorts of skulduggery. It was that kind of philosophy, remember, that was used to justify the poisonings and other bits of nastiness that became standard politics in Renaissance Florence, for example. You must have read "The Prince", Machiavelli's textbook on pure political expediency.'

'And,' Carter added, 'from the way we see politicians behaving nowadays, that Machiavellian credo seems to have become the instruction-book, not only for princes and politicians but for the masses generally! Just about everyone I meet - present company excepted, of course,' he added, with a disarming smile, 'seems to think that to deal with one's fellows in any way other than in terms of pure expediency, political or criminal, is sheer lunacy?

'And even more lunatic to admit it,' said Charlie, cynically. 'An essential part of the operation of that kind of philosophy, of course,' he pointed out, 'js that you must convince your intended victims that this manipulative sort of behaviour towards you, on their part, is wrong, so that they don't do to you what you would want to do to them! This sure inverts the biblical saying "Do yea unto others as you would they do unto you". It's more like, "Do it unto others before they do it unto you'?

'Youre right,' conimented Turner. "That, unfortunately, is the sort of institutionalised hypocrisy which so much "politics" is and always was, almost by definition. It's as if you were elected President of a jungle. For that you would have to convince the zebras that they would be safe from the lions and convince the lions that they could eat as many zebras as they need. Chicanery would be the name of the game.'

"Hey, you guys,' said Noel, feeling under assault, 'let's get this straight. I wasn't recommending that way of behaving. I'm every bit as much against it as you are, but I dont know why. All I am saying is, that although my

moral standards are more or less - well, standard, among people like ourselves, I guess my only rational justification for it is that it is conditioned by my upbringing.'

'I think, Noel,' smiled Turner, 'that hardly needs saying. I can see what you're driving at. It's that so far as true morality is concerned, as opposed to mere moral conditioning, it is vital to decide, rationally, just what premiums there really are, if any, on maintaining personal standards of good conduct, whatever that may be, as opposed to being merely "political" in choosing and promoting your own selfish interests.'

'Precisely!' said Noel, relieved finally to have made his point. 'I mean, what purpose can there be in behaving "morally" in such a way as to deny yourself the obvious advantages, if you're bold enough and cunning enough, to involve yourself in political or even criminal wheeling and dealing? In that case, what can "conscience" be but an automatic reflex, something which either comes with your genes or into which you are conditioned by social manipulators, in the Machiavellian way, to keep you under their thumb.'

'I see what you're saying,' commented Walter. "The idea of self-denial or martyrdom, for any reason, would make no sense in those circumstances.'

"That's right, it wouldn't,' Noel replied. 'It's just the extreme case of what I'm saying. Rationally speaking, where there is no personal benefit in it, either in this world or the next, martyrdom is pointless.'

'But isn't it of personal benefit to martyr yourself for love of your family, your fellows, your society?' asked Carter. 'I mean, mightn't you calculate, quite rationally, that your own suffering or death might benefit others you care for, increasing the amount of happiness of the whole at the expense of your own?"

'The "felicific calculus",' said Walter, 'the "greatest happiness of the greatest number".'

'Except,' remarked Charlie, ironically, 'that the "greatest number", often as not, is just "Number One'".'

'I guess I see, Noel,' Walter said, 'what a problem this presents to you. If you're right, then what could any kind of sacrifice be, of life, pleasure or personal advantage, but just another kind of reflex, like the mechanical

sacrifices bees make in defence of their hives or ants in defence of their nests, where the question of moral choice doesn't come into it.'

Turner took up the point the young clergyman was making. 'You're wondering how we answer those who might say that such automatic selfsacrifice makes no sense for free-thinking agents like us who, unlike those social insects, have the rational choice to act otherwise. You're saying that from the purely rational point of view of the discussion so far, self-sacrifice is a folly, a kind of romantic madness - like that of the kamikaze pilots in World War Two.'

'Or,' Carter added, 'those suicide squads proliferating everywhere nowadays, where members "psych" themselves up or dope themselves into sacrificing their lives for all sorts of conflicting and half-baked, brainwashed beliefs in ridiculous rewards in the hereafter.'

'Now you're talking, guys! Noel interjected. 'So, yes, that's my point. Where are we to draw the line between selfish suicides and true selfsacrifice? Without any consensus as to what constitutes true self-sacrifice, or true moral behaviour, all these instances of martyrdom - including, I must say, the crucifixion of Christ - have to be judged as no more than forms of social insanity.'

'Right on!' agreed Charlie, with feeling. "You're expressing what I've seen for a long time as the whole nub of the problem facing our Western civilisation. We human beings don't have the assured natural morality of the social insects and animals. We've gotta forge our own behaviourpatterns by reason, according to changing circumstances.'

Charlie's uncharacteristic burst of speech continued. "But in our present ethical vacuum, what social philosophy can we adopt ourselves, let alone teach? All we have at present is a kind of woolly liberalism which advises us to live out our short lives as pleasantly and undemandingly as possible, taking care not to "rock the boat" or draw to ourselves the unwanted attentions of any of the warring factions.'

'As the Epicureans believed,' put in Turner. "Or, if it happens to be unpleasantness - sadism, for example - that turns you on, then your pursuit of pleasure is to inflict yourself on all and sundry to whatever extent you think you might get away with, as do so many terrorists and criminals nowadays.'

'And don't forget,' added Carter, 'the unpleasantness which is dished out by those who are so scared of being terrorised that they become terrorists themselves, by reaction, in revenge killings, for instance.'

'Yes, it's surely clear to see,' said Walter, 'that in a situation like that, as far as motives for behaviour are concerned, you could pick your "morality" with a pin. "Moral" would be no more than a political noise. I think we may now take that argument as read.'

'It just about describes the way things are, anyway,' concluded Carter, despondently. 'But on what rational grounds can we deplore it? I can see Noel's problem clearly now. If there's no assured premium on truly moral personal behaviour, then we might as well all follow our own inclinations, with "the devil take the hindermost" being the order of the day. How can there ever be social harmony on that basis?'

'In that case,' Turner interposed, 'the only limit on freedom to do whatever we want would be expressed as "Your freedom to swing your arm ends where my nose begins." This puts a premium on separation and. remoteness rather than social coherence. In any society seeking that "Lebensraum", as the Nazis called it, war is inevitable.'

'And the fact that in a civilised society we do not just follow our inclinations, ad lib, Noel added, 'means, I repeat, that there's something missing. Tats just my point. I see it more clearly myself, now. There's something vital in the nature of things that we've not yet considered.'

Once again, for a while, the company sat quietly lost in thought. Then, suddenly, Charlie got up and walked to the window. He had heard, outside, the sound of hooves and realised that the horses, now unsaddled and rubbed down by his two assistants, were being herded by them, driving the flivver, to their overnight pasture a few miles down the valley. He opened the door and went out on the deck to watch them, checking, from their gait, that they were fit and healthy.

Herding the horses out on the road with the truck was always a tricky operation. The beasts, free of restraint, simply wanted to run wild, and if they scattered, there was a lot of wilderness in which to do it. Charlie remembered the time he had entrusted to a new assistant the job of bring-

ing the horses in from their field for their work in the morning. She had herded the horses up the road well enough, but had then been slow in getting them penned in. He had come along a few minutes later to find all twelve horses, sated on the barley that had enticed them into the corral, streaming back out of the paddock, intent on exploring Canada. It had taken quite a while and a deal of shouting to round them all up again! He made sure it was never a single-handed job after that.

Steel, the big black gelding, was limping, he noticed. 'Keep an eye on Steel for me, will ya?' he called to the men in the truck following the horses. 'Tl take a look at him, later. Perhaps we'd better rest him up tomor-

>

row.

The wrangler watched until truck and horses had disappeared, then returned indoors. Walter was speaking.

'I take it you accept, Noel, that there's no way of dispensing with morality entirely, Nature tells us that much, at least. Didn't Epictetus say, "He who follows his passions without the constraint of reason is the biggest slave of all"? So, okay, there's got to be some measure of moral calculation and rational control even of bare passion and pursuit of pleasure, otherwise in pursuing some pleasures we'd have to forego, or frustrate others.'

"That's right, Walter,' agreed Turner. "We'd lurch uncomfortably through life like a badly-tuned engine with its pistons firing just any old how and none of its parts working harmoniously. We'd be in a state of continual inefficiency, conflict and misery - which is, of course, what Epictetus meant.' - 'Bur you feel, Noel,' Walter went on, 'that is not a sufficiently objective basis for true morality - the exercise of self-denial and restraint and selfsacrifice for the sake of a balanced overall happiness.'

"You've got it, Walter,' Noel replied. 'In other words, Epictetus's principle doesn't explain true altruism, where people work towards and even sacrifice themselves purely for the benefit of others.'

'Okay,' said Carter, 'I think Noel has now made this point clearly enough.'

'Yes, I agree,' Turner added, "The fact that what we call morality cannot, plainly, be mere "enlightened self-interest" needs to be addressed.'

'So, what sort of answer do you suggest, Dave?' asked his old friend.

'T'm flattered you think I should be able to answer that, Tony,' Turner replied. "That's more Walter's territory than mine, surely.'

'Hey, don't duck out of it, Dave,' Carter responded. "This whole argument is what follows from those ideas of yours we started with. We're putting you on the spot. What you're being asked is, does that radical relativism of yours mean moral relativism. You can't cop out now!'

'Well, then,' replied Turner, 'I can't give a priestly answer - begging your pardon, Walter. So perhaps, together, we may find a logical direction in which an answer may be sought. To start with, let's remember that getting rid of mechanical determinacy puts the onus fairly and squarely on each of us as individual free agents, or "observers" in the relative sense, to deliberate our actions, act on them and accept the consequences. That's what the new relativistic science we've been discussing does. It removes the old barrier between science and what might broadly be classified as the Humanities. That is, it ensures responsibility. So morality, in some form or another, whether we are religious or not, becomes inescapable.'

'I'd say we're now generally agreed on that,' said Carter.

'All the same,' objected Charlie, 'where's the moral virtue in trying logically to prove the justification for morality - if you see what I mean?' Walter responded to that. 'Sure, Charlie, that's always been a paradox, that any proof of final reward for moral behaviour makes that behaviour amoral and removes the element of faith, which is the very essence of moral choice. But we're not trying to prove what's moral, here, are we?' he added. 'What Dave is trying to do, as I see it, is to remove the unnatural barrier that Western science has erected against the natural - not to say, global - commonsense pursuit of religion and morality. He's not saying that this new approach to modern science tells us what to believe, only that we are free to believe. That right, Dave?'

'Right, Walter,' Turner replied. 'So, the problem we're addressing is that if we agree that the distinction between moral and immoral behaviour can't be entirely arbitrary or conventional, then what can "moral responsibility" mean - if anything - in any zon-arbitrary way?'

This met with assent all round.

'Okay,' said Turner. 'So far, there's no problem with "good" and "bad" - or "evil" or whatever - on the purely pragmatical level. For "good", read harmony, and for "bad" or "evil" read disharmony. The rewards for wise, or moral, choices of action, of moderation rather than excess, are perfectly plain for anyone with the intelligence to recognise it, and the punishments for inattention to that are just as plainly automatic, as the existence of laws in all societies shows. Noel's question, though, is about the sort of longterm evaluation of "good" and "evil" which we make in purely altruistic rather than in those purely pragmatic contexts.'

"Yes, that's a fair summary,' Noel agreed.

'So,' Turner continued, 'we need to examine two such long-term or posthumous evaluations.'

"Two?' questioned a somewhat puzzled Carter. 'Why two, Dave?'

"Well, Tony, there's the one we think of, nowadays, as the long-term

"survival" of our progeny and our species - our genes, say - on n this planet after our own days. .

'And the other one?" prompted. Carter.

'Turner smiled at his friend's impatience, so reminiscent of earlier clashes in their youth. 'It's what,' he said, 'for lack of a better phrase, I can only call "personal salvation", or survival of our "soul", as it used to be called, beyond our bodily death.'

'Of course,' agreed Noel. "Those two kinds of concern are as different as chalk and cheese. "Salvation", as P've understood it, in the personal or "soul" serise, is definitely not the same as what is meant by "survival" in the evolutionary sense as of a species, say. I've been conflating those two meanings unthinkingly.'

'Just so, Noel,' said Turner. "Survival", in that temporal sense, is in historical time, the time scale on which historians measure the life-spans of nations and species, or scientists in measuring the ageing of rocks, planets and stars. In that sense of time the end-aims of what we usually think of as morality make no personal sense, as you've been stressing.'

Turner could see that he had the undivided attention of the others, so he continued.

'So in talking about true moral principles we are talking about forms, as distinct from physical processes, and in our discussion at the chalet we decided that forms are not in time; that is, they are permanent in a sense which is different from the way we think of rocks, seas, rivers, the sun, moon and stars, as permanent. In other words, what I'm saying is what we've already decided, that logos is non-temporal; that logos is not physis, far less that it is an accidental spin-off from physis. A model for this difference, I would say, is the way in which sequences of logical propositions or mathematical formulae follow one another in rational discourse, as opposed to the sequences of events in, say, chemical reactions, like decay and combustion. The difference is that whereas the one sort of sequence, the physical one, is temporal, with just one avenue of progression, the other, the logical, or formal one, is atemporal, with many branching avenues of development.'

'It seems to me,' put in Walter, 'that it would be more suitable to think of physis as a spin-off from logos.'

'So,' Carter added, addressing Turner, 'what you're saying, Dave, in effect, is that in our searching for truly moral end-aims, all our talk about "time", in that context, was ambiguous, a "bum steer", you might say.'

'In that case,' said Noel, 'what we have to do is to change tack from talking about end-aims in "this life", so to speak, or even in the full historical span of human existence, toward thinking of what we are, or become, as individuals in the non-temporal sense, as forms, which are not subject to decay in the same way that our bodies and things like rocks are.'

'Ah, yes,' murmured Carter, almost to himself, 'I can see how that puts a whole new complexion on things. I keep thinking of "time" in the old way, as the one-way cause-effect sequence of classical mechanics. You only have to think of it to realise that forms have at least as much physical effect as particulars. The form of a chain-saw, for instance, makes it more causally efficient for purpose than if it were no more than a formless mass of metal.'

'Sure,' Walter remarked, 'it's all too easy to keep slipping back into that old "universal" way of thinking about time as all-over-simultaneous random atomic process, which has created all the problems.'

'It is indeed,' Turner agreed.

"Yeah, I agree,' Noel said. "That's what caught me out when I was arguing about rewards for moral behaviour being available after death. I realise I was thinking of "after" in that "God's-eye-view" sense of historical time, not in this new "radically relativistic" sense. I can see now that it is only in the historical, or temporal, sense that "death" has meaning.'

Carter found this a surprising statement and after some consideration, he said, "What you're saying here, Noel, it seems, is that you can't be born and die relatively to yourself - in relative time, that is. Others can be born and die relatively to you, and in their personal time-spans you can be born and die relatively to them.'

'Turner agreed. "Yes, "birth" and "death" do have those meanings, even in the relative sense.'

Carter ruminated on this. 'So,' he reiterated, 'there can be no death for any observer relative to himself but only relatively to others. Now that's

'odd. I never saw it like that before. It's a highly thought-provoking way of looking at things!'

Charlie had been listening to all this and thinking hard. Now he spoke.

'Sure, that's an interesting angle, I grant, but I don't see how it solves the main problem Noel has raised. Okay, so the "death" of a person relative to himself has no meaning. So what! The length of that lifetime is still finite. It's still no longer nor shorter than it is. And as for "heaven" being a final stage of resolution of the forms, an ultimate and perfect Logos as you called it, Walt, any progress towards that perfection which a guy may make in his lifetime is still so small that it makes a mockery of any attempt of his to reach it. So let's face it,' he went on, 'if a guy dies prematurely, in agony or in poverty, or in a state of senility, insanity, or in some other unsavoury way, then whar's it to him that he may have found and preserved some measure of cosmic integrity? And even if we assume that he had the satisfaction of knowing how "good" his life had been, then what can that mean to him if his last hours are as wretched, or even more wretched than that of someone he judges to be the epitome of evil?"

'T think I see your point, Charlie,' Walter replied sympathetically, "but the quality of a tune or symphony isn't to be judged, surely, by the quality of its final cadence, its very last bar or note! It must be the same with any character-complex, or form, including what we call a man's soul or spirit.

It's the thing as a whole, in the atemporal sense, that's to be evaluated, not as we find it at any particular moment in time.'

'T get you,' said Carter. "What a man is can't be only what he is at any particular instant between birth and death, much less at his final gasp. It's got to be the sum-total of his whole life, as he has chosen to live it, his form, or "soul" in old money...'

This time Noel objected.

'But where, in the actual conduct of our daily lives, does that sum-total of events configure? Where and when is that form defined or completed? Does it come in some sort of revelation at the end? Or is it all totted-up on some cosmic clipboard, by an angel, say, who stands continually by our side, scoring us as we go along? And what about those whose lives are no longer than that of a still-born child or an aborted foetus? How, by any such cosmic standard of moral integrity, are those lives - those "forms", "souls" or whatever - to be judged or evaluated?'

It was Walter, again, who answered him. "Without accepting your idea of an angel with a clip-board, Noel, the inevitable implication is that this life can't be all there is, that there must be some higher awareness than ours in which these holistic evaluations of our lives can be made. And may I remind you guys,' he added, 'that the word "holistic", here, derives from the Greek holos, like "whole" and "holy" - and "healthy", incidentally, as Helen has pointed out, which lends point to the way the word "holy" is commonly used in religious contexts.'

'But,' Noel persisted, 'what about those still-births and aborted foetuses, diseased children, congenital morons, psychopaths and so on? They can scarcely be regarded as healthy in that sense, can they? So are we to treat them as "unholy"? Should they be got rid of, as the eugenicists would advise - or the Nazis, or any other advocates of "ethnic cleansing"?'

Responding to this, Charlie pointed out, 'It bears thinking about that in nature, animals seem to know when something is wrong, or unhealthy with their offspring, For example, the mother of a litter of puppies will often refuse to feed, or may actually push out, the weakest of the puppies. A guy I know who's a vet told me that often as not, even if that runt is removed from the litter and given special care and attention, it sooner or later dies, or else grows up disadvantaged in some way. It makes me wonder

if, in nature, that is the "holy" thing for that mother to do. Also, I reckon we all know of couples whose lives and marriages have been made hell by their refusal to follow the advice of the doctors to have a defective foetus aborted.'

To this, Noel added, 'It also bears thinking about that if our lives are forms - or "souls" as we're now calling them, then so is everything else, from animals, pets, insects, viruses . . . you name them. This world of forms, if it exists, must therefore be more or less identical to this one.'

'Yeah,' drawled Charlie, 'and would that include those other forms that have existed throughout our earth history, such as pterodactyls, diplodocuses and Tyrannosaurus Rex? Do they exist in this other dimension? If so, then what kind of an "afterlife" for us would that be, all co-existing in that mixed-up way, together with those who hate us as well as those we love?"

Again there was a profound silence as this turn in the conversation was digested. Then Turner said, "Who's to say, eh? Surely, that's not for us finite creatures to determine. Logically speaking, the fact that we can't imagine such a set-up as you and Charlie describe, Noel, is by no means a refutation of what we're saying about the forms of things having an existence in themselves apart from that of the particulars. This leaves us free to at least believe in a hereafter, without fear of logical contradiction, which is what is needed for social cohesion and stability. All that can concern us in our finitude, otherwise, is the argument that this dimension we usually think of as our history, in which we live and die, cannot be the one and only dimension there is; that nature is multi-dimensional in a way that our classical science does not take account of. This takes the "mechanistic" straitjacket off speculations of the kind that our scientific tradition puts on beliefs which are vital to society but which it makes us think of as "kooky". The fact that it is not given to us to think in so many dimensions beyond that might well be part of the "plan", so to speak, which presents us with a true moral choice of whether or not to believe, or "have faith" as religious people say.'

He paused. 'Anyway, chaps, talking about nature, let's hold it there for a moment. I must answer one call of nature which in no way can be denied.'

'Me, too,' said Carter and they left the room.

Chapter 13: The Sitting Room at the Lodge

Dimensions of Existence

Dimensions of existence

"They're a couple of swell guys,' remarked Noel after the older men had gone. 'I haven't much liked the English I've come in contact with before. Those I've met still seem to think we're colonials who happen to have done rather well for ourselves, but those two are different.'

'Sure are,' agreed Charlie. "When I first met them, I had them figured for a couple of has-beens trying to catch up on their lost youth. But they're tough cookies who've been around a bit, showing me how wrong I was. And whod have suspected that there was all that thinking going on inside Dave's head!'

"Talking about thinking, Charlie,' said Noel, 'you're no slouch yourself. You are certainly not the kind of guy I first chought you were. I suspect that somewhere along the line you got yourself a very good education. Am I right?'

Walter glanced at Charlie, but he did not appear to resent the Bostonian's probing.

'I guess you've seen through my disguise, Noel,' grinned the wrangler. 'Sure, I went to college. That's where Walter and I became buddies. We graduated the same year. And while he went in for theology, I went in for psychology.'

Charlie was interrupted by the return of Carter and Turner, carrying several bottles of beer from the bar in the next room.

"The Lodge seems to be filling up,' Turner informed them, as he handed each of them a bottle. 'People signing in for the weekend, I expect.'

'I wonder what time Rick and the girls will get back,' remarked Carter. 'It looks as if the restaurant will be pretty busy tonight. We'd better get in early.'

'How about coming to eat at my place?' asked Charlie, taking them all by surprise. 'One of the hunters gave me a chunk of moose-meat. It's big enough to feed an army.'

His companions looked at each other.

'That's very kind of you, Charlie,' replied Carter. 'T'd be delighted to accept.' Walter and Turner agreed. 'How about you, Noel?' Charlie asked him, thinking that the younger man seemed a bit hesitant.

"Well, actually,' Noel replied, rather reluctantly, 'I had hoped to bump into Helen this evening.'

'That's no problem, then,' said Charlie. 'As I told you, there's plenty to go round, so why not bring her - and Rick and Marian, of course. Ask them if they'd like to come along. Tell them it's by my invitation.'

Noel happily accepted the arrangement.

'Right then, that's okay.' Charlie's voice held a note of finality.

Carter said, 'Let's look forward to that, then, Charlie. But it will be a while yet, I guess, before the others get back. So, where were we before Dave and I went out? J felt we were getting somewhere interesting.'

Walter reminded him. "We were discussing the problem of evaluating true as opposed to merely expedient morality and the possibility of an afterlife.'

'Ah, yes. Thanks, Walter,' Carter replied. "Now you said, if I read you right, that there would have to be something of a rational nature like ourselves but of a much higher order to make such a moral evaluation of our lives. Where do we go from here?"

It was Turner who answered. 'Okay, so let's take the character-complex, or form, consisting of a man's whole life, his soul, let's call it - and why not? It's better if we use "off-the-peg" terminology wherever possible. Along with this form would be included, of course, all those other forms, like those you mention, Noel, that are contemporary with that life, such as pets, other people and all the things that go to make up that life, none of which, as we've agreed, has any permanence in ordinary physical time. In that ordinary history, a person's life may be no more than a fleeting "blip" in the temporal continuum. So in what sense of time can this "soul", this historical complex of incidents which make up a life, possibly persist beyond that life? Basically, that's the problem we were addressing, isn't it?'

After giving it some thought, Noel said, 'I think I would say, Dave, that these two times we're talking about, the personal and the historical, are two distinct time-measures, or dimensions, and like all dimensions, each one is zero in the projection of the other.'

"Yes, I agree,' replied Turner. "This implies that the two measures we've distinguished are what mathematicians call orthogonal, or projected at right-angles to each other, like the length and breadth of this carpet, plus the dimension of duration, which, in the same way, extends at right-angles to all three spatial dimensions.'

'Right on,' responded Noel, 'so what I'm saying is that those two times have to be projected dimensionally at right-angles, like the three of ordinary length and breadth, making five dimensions in all.'

'So now,' Turner interposed, 'let's get this right. In effect, you're talking about time as basically two dimensional, the one dimension being what you might call the "world's time", by which physical processes take place, and the other what might be called the "soul's time", the time in which the jorm persists.'

'T get it,' said Walter, 'this other time, the fifth dimension, is the time by which forms endure while the parts change - as in the analogy of the candle flame which remains steady in still air, while its atoms and so on are in a continual state of flux.'

'Or like the sun,' Carter added, 'which maintains its form over billions of years while the process which sustains it is one of continual violent change.'

"The same,' Charlie put in, 'might be said of ourselves. As I understand it, our cells are continually changing, so that every seven years or so our bodies are more or less completely renewed.'

Wanting to return to his argument, Noel said rather dismissively, 'I guess Pm not too sure about that, Charlie, but Pll take your word for it, anyway.' Then he went on, 'But why shouldn't there be that extra time-dimension? In pre-relativity days, who would have imagined what we now know for sure, that there is a fourth dimension, projected at right-angles to the normal three of observational space, and thar all four of these dimensions are measured in the same time-units of seconds??! So why not a fifth

21 Metres divided by the constant c,

dimension vertical to those four, a sixth dimension vertical to those five... and so on? If there is no logical contradiction in that assumption, and if that assumption is useful, then, surely, we are free to assume it.'

But Walter was puzzled.

'I don't see how these times can be at "right-angles" to one another. How can there be a rime at right-angles to time. Time is an imponderable, surely!

'T think I can answer that,' offered Carter, the engineer. 'If you represent one measure by a line going from east to west, say, then the only way you can have another measure of the same kind, which does not extend, or lean in that direction is in another direction directly at right-angles to it, like north and south. The same graphical or geometrical principle that applies to north and south or to the length and breadth of this carpet and the room that it's in, applies to however many measures of that same kind you need to distinguish. The fact that you can't envisage more than three dimensions related in that commonsense way doesn't mean that they can't be dealt with geometrically"

'But,' Walter objected, 'time is not geometrical, is it?'

"Well, Walter,' Turner explained, 'as I''m sure you know, the word "dimension" comes from the Latin dis, meaning apart, and metiri, to measure. So dimensions are simply measures which are apart, and the only way they can be kept apart, or made distinct, as Tony says, is to be extended at right-angles to one another, so that they don't encroach on one another's domains, like the length and breadth of this carpet. Think of what it would be like if a sloppy carpet fitter had measured the length and breadth of this carpet at some angle other than ninety degrees. What a mess he would have made of it, eh! The same goes for dimensions in general. There's nothing at all mysterious about it, because, as we discussed, distances in metres divided by the constant c are time-measures in seconds, which makes spatial separation four-dimensional, in the form which relativists call spacetime. Since all these space-time measures are in the same units, then just like the three dimensions of ordinary space, the whole thing is geometrical, or graphical.'

22 See dimension, Enc. Brit. (1961) 7.387b.

'I can accept that, Dave,' Walter replied, 'provisionally, anyway, although I find it difficult to visualise how such abstractions as time can be literally at right-angles.'

Turner tried to explain.'But its not a matter of being able to visualise it, Walter. There's something altogether futile in trying to visualise what is by its very nature, invisible. It isn't vision, or any other sensation as such, which sets these measures at right-angles but simply the fact that in order to portray measures in the same units in such a way that they don't trespass on each other's territory, they have to be projected at right-angles, which makes them, as I say, automatically geometrical by definition.'

'[ think I see that,' Walter replied. 'So, okay, so where do we go from here?"

'Let's sum it up, then,' said Turner. "We are now assuming that the only answer to the question of how a whole life-time can configure as a form as opposed to a process underlying that form - like the gasses producing the candle-flame - is that there is another time dimension which lies at right-angles to ordinary time in the same way that ordinary time is at right-angles to all three dimensions of space.'

'T agree, just what I was thinking,' said Noel, as the other three nodded.

'So,' Turner continued, 'such an idea seems far from implausible. If, as we say, it contains no contradiction then it is a valid object of belief: That's how good, open-ended, scientific dialectic works, by probing logical possibilities, rejecting only those that can be shown to lead to outright contradiction.'

This intrigued Walter. 'Are you saying, then, Dave, that in this line of thinking we should imagine the hereafter as a kind of Archive, or RecordLibrary, where reels upon multitudinous reels of all our nefarious lives are stored? What could possibly be the cosmic purpose in that?'

A sudden thought struck Carter.

'T see a snag here,' he said. 'As soon as the process ceases by which the form is maintained, the form ceases too, whether it is the flame when the candle is snuffed out or the soul when the body dies - or when the sun, say, runs out of pop and collapses to become a supernova and incinerates the solar system.'

'No, you've got it wrong, old son,' his old friend replied. "The form we're talking about can't die in that sense because it survives in those further dimensions we're discussing. Once it has happened, it can't be made to unhappen. I mean, when the candle dies or even if the sun turns into a supernova, destroying our earth and all its history, consuming ourselves and all that was ever important to us, it can't mean that it never happened. At least, to assume that it can makes complete nonsense. What's been, has been and always will have been, whether we know it or not.'

Walter said: "That reminds me of what I heard, somewhere. "Better the rose to have bloomed and died than not to have bloomed at all"

'Okay,' came Carter's response. 'So it's like you say, Walter, that this stacks up life upon life in a pointless way. If everything that happens cannot be unhappened, then that must mean that everything we do in this life remains permanent throughout all the other dimensions as well.'

'Not necessarily, Tony,' said Turner. 'Don't forget that time is change, by its very definition. So the same goes, by definition, for however many time dimensions there might be. For instance, we already know about how the three dimensions of space change in the fourth: dimension, so why should we suppose that in this new time dimension we are talking about, everything must remain static, like trophies on racks or specimens in a cabinet? Let me ask you, Walter, in our usual way of thinking, is the threedimensional body I have now, exactly the same as I had yesterday or will have tomorrow?'

'No,' Walter replied, 'because, of course, in some way or other it will have changed, because of the normal temporal process we call living.'

'So why,' Turner continued, 'shouldn't my body which is subject to change in this physical dimension be subject to change in however many associated dimensions there may be?'

'Bur,' Carter persisted, 'if there is change in every dimension, then what's left of the individual life in the first dimension, the one we are in right now?'

Turner thought for a moment before he replied. 'As I see it, Tony. that whole set of dimensions is like a building. Once the foundations are laid,

then no matter what is built on top of them, those foundations remain. The same goes with life on that first dimension. No matter what changes there may be in the further dimensions - further lives - the first, or fundamental life stays the same.'

I see,' said Walter. Then, after a pause, 'It now occurs to me that the idea of an afterlife ascending through all these different levels of change has been the basis of some of the most ancient religions.'

Charlie added, "That seems to be the old Pythagorean idea of transmigration of souls. And then there's that business of regression under hypnosis which takes someone back to a previous life in which sometimes they give vivid descriptions of events not known to historians but which turn out, on deeper research, to have been correct.'

Carter responded, 'Yes, that's so, Charlie, and once again, we're not so much generating new ideas as uncovering old ones and expressing them in new language. Fascinating!'

Noel was not so sanguine.

'Hey, hold it there,' he cautioned. 'I know I've said chat these changes that take place in the new dimension imply yet another dimension by which this change can be measured and then another dimension again, by which that progression can be measured, and so on. But now it occurs to me that this commits a well-known logical fallacy, the fallacy of a runaway regress to infinity; change upon change ad infinitum.'

'Not necessarily, Noel,' replied Turner. 'If it were, then science would already be committed to that regress by considering a fourth dimension, since, as you say, any progress in that fourth time-dimension has to be measured against a fifth, and that progress in a sixth . . . and so on, indefinitely. There was a chap, as J remember, name of John William Dunne," who built a whole theory of time on the basis of this very same idea of change requiring infinite dimensions. In that theory, like the four dimensions modern physics ordinarily deals with, those further dimensions are all relative, or observer-centred in the same way. This is taken to mean that all these dimensions, including those of ordinary space and time, are dimensions of individual consciousness. I was much taken up with this theory when

23 An Experiment With Time, by J. W. Dunne. Faber & Faber, Ltd., London, 1927.

I chanced upon it, as a youth. It struck a chord with me because I'd come to something like that same conclusion myself, which is why someone put me onto the book. Apparently, when it first appeared, in 1927, it became something of a "seven day wonder". But although there was never any clear refutation of it, nothing, it seems, ever came of it - at least, nothing like the extent to which Einstein's theory of time was accepted. This was undoubtedly because it didn't have anything practical to offer to science in the way of industrial, commercial or military application.'

'That's interesting,' observed Noel. 'I also recall reading articles by modern theoretical physicists saying much the same as we are saying, the nub of which, it seems to me, is that the dimensions we are ordinarily familiar with can't be the only ones there are.'

Yes,' Turner agreed. The "many worlds" view of some quantum physicists.'24

'It strikes me, though,' Carter remarked, 'that from the standpoint of our relativistic, observer-based science, these old ideas of an afterlife, of transmigration, reincarnation, and so on- even resurrection, Noel - might not be so silly after all. There seems to be some common logical basis to them which was intuited by the ancients and which we are now rediscovering and expressing in more modern language. Perhaps that is the common "bottom line" of human intuition about an afterlife, which keeps society going, and perhaps all the differences there are in expressing it are due to differences in language, with words like "soul", "spirit" - plus "God" and "Allah", of course - which are always sources of intellectual dissention and even war. This would make sense of those quietist religions, like Zen, for instance, which seek to replace language with pure contemplation.'

"Yes, that seems plausible, Tony,' Noel agreed. 'In that case, I can see that, at the very least, there's no logical absurdity in assuming that there is continuity of the soul. But I still maintain that this basic, common assumption, regardless of how it may be socially articulated, has to involve more than just the one ordinary time-dimension. There must be dimensions at right-angles to one another, continually throughout our lives and

thereafter.'

24, eg., Paul Davies: Other Worlds, Penguin, 1988.

'Yes,' added Turner, 'and that remains a valid object of belief, not to be outlawed by science as in our comparatively philistine, Western way of thinking.'

'And I suppose,' Noel continued, 'in the same way that we have branching logical alternatives in this life, and since logos, as we've agreed, is indeterminate, or many-dimensional, so we would have branching logical alternatives in those subsequent dimensions . . .'

'Bearing in mind,' put in Turner, 'that change is the very essence of time in all its possible dimensions.'

'Do you suppose, then, Dave,' asked Carter, 'that in each of those dimensions of existence, as we reach the end of one life we start all over again each time, as in transmigration?'

'No, perhaps not,' Turner replied. 'It's more likely, I should think, that one's "soul", as we're calling it, as it is going through one dimension is also going through all the others at every stage in the process, without the possibility of backtracking, in the same way that a body which moves or changes in space also, necessarily, moves through time. That's more like driving through a labyrinth of one-way streets in a city, where you select, at every stage, just one of those branching alternatives - the streets or roads, which consign you to other sets of alternatives as you progress.'

'That's something like the way a computer works, isn't it," commented Carter, 'except that it does allow back-tracking, in which respect the analogy is, I suppose, somewhat inept,' he added, lamely.

'Yes,' replied Turner. 'But as you well know, Tony, in the case of a computer, the choice is very simple with just two alternatives at a time - with two "streets" as it were - and the simple two-dimensional choice of "left or right" at every operational stage. That's why its language is called "binary", of course. Logos, by comparison, must be more like human discourse which consists of multiple choices in all the different dimensions at every juncture. As far as I know, there's no computer which can quite match that.'

'So if it's like you say, Noel,' Charlie asked, 'then how, in the first place, did we start on this "ground-level" dimension we are now on?'

'But how do we know,' queried Noel in reply, 'that this is, as you say, the "ground-level" of existence? This level we are now on may well be the result of what we did on other sub-levels.'

'But surely,' responded Charlie, 'in such a system there has to be a "ground-level" of existence somewhere?'

'Turner intervened. 'Once again, Charlie, it seems that the ancients have forestalled us in tackling that question. Their answer was to think of the whole many-dimensional system that the soul is presented with as cyclic, as an existential spiral, let's say, being among the lowliest creatures at the bottom and the highest at the top, in a sort of formal paraphrase of Darwinian evolution.'

Charlie fell silent, while his companions continued talking. Then in his eagerness to say something he blurted out, 'Something occurs to me, here, Dave. You said that all contacts between observers are communicational and that communication takes place at the observational rate c. Then you said that in its own proper time, the quantum time, the connection between the sender and receiver of the signal is instantaneous - zero -regardless of distance on the ordinary level.'

'That's right, Charlie,' said Turner. 'So where are you going with this?"

'I guess, Dave, this makes quantum contact literally telepathic - meaning direct "at-a-distance" communication. But this would be in opposition to ordinary physical interaction, which is limited to c.

"That's interesting, Charlie,' put in Carter. 'So what can we deduce from that? Does it mean that on the quantum level everything is in direct contact with everything else?'

'Sure,' Charlie replied, 'that might go some way towards explaining such things as psychic vision and telepathy, clairvoyance and other things that we usually think of as paranormal.'

'But we must remember,' warned Turner, 'that on the quantum level, although, as you say, everything is in contact with everything else, this can't be all at the same time, which would create an Eleatic stasis of the kind we talked about the other day.'

'But,' Carter interposed, 'I thought we'd agreed with Noel's theory of angular momentum as a replacement for "gravity". Didn't we decide that in such a balanced system of angular momentum where everything is in a state of immediate correlation with everything else, any change at one place in the system immediately affects everything else in the system throughout

- that is, instantly, like the quantum contact? It seems to me,' he continued, 'that this might even give some credence to astrology in the casting of horoscopes, insofar as the basis of astrology, that all the planets, stars and so on are in sympathetic motion, is the same in principle as in an angular momentum system.'

'That may well be,' Turner replied, 'but remember, at that level angular momentum is quantised into discrete quantum events which cannot occur all together, otherwise, as I say, there would be a cosmic jam-up. Don't you agree, Noel?

Noel nodded agreement. 'But how,' he enquired, 'does that affect what we've been discussing about dimensions?'

'That's a very good question,' answered Turner and then, looking around the group, he added, 'Any takers on that?'

'Well, for me,' offered Noel, 'it dispels any dogmatic belief that "this life is all", a belief that relieves us from moral responsibility which, as we agreed, is the problem with our Western society. If we could dispose of that spurious idea, which I believe stems from our atomic materialism, then that would surely restore some balance to our social conscience, putting us on a par with other societies which are not as steeped, as we are, in that materialistic outlook.'

'So, Noel,' Walter interposed, 'do you now revise your earlier conviction that there could be no such thing as the resurrection of Christ?'

'Not quite, Walter,' Noel answered. 'I still believe there can be no such thing as bodily resurrection. That still seems absurd for the reasons I gave at the time. But resurrection of the soul in some form or another - and I do mean form - now seems to me more plausible. Without going overboard for the Christian religion, as so many of my countrymen do - in the "Bible-belt" for example - and although I still disdain that sort of fanaticism, I think, on rational grounds, that I mightn't now be quite so sceptical as I once was of true Christianity.'

'And how,' Walter persisted, 'do you now feel about a belief in God?'

'But I thought we'd agreed that there can't be a God,' Charlie protested.

'I must correct you there, Charlie,' said Walter. 'As I recall, what we agreed was not that there is no God. It was that this Being can't exist in the

way that's implicit in classical science, as the Great Overseer of a "Universe" of mechanical or completely determinate happenings - the "God of the Scientists", or Deism as it's been called. What we're talking about now, I guess,' he went on, 'is that in so many dimensions of choice of action we are presented, not with a "universe" but an indeterminate "pluriverse", or "polyverse" - what we might call a logos rather than a physis - which is a Whole of a very different kind.'

'I think I see what you mean,' replied Charlie, after some thought, 'it's a logical rather than a physical Whole, something more like conscious reason and volition than mere mechanics; in other words, a dialectic, as we talked about earlier in connection with Marx and Hegel.'

'So you could say, then,' argued Carter, 'that there has to be a God if we are to talk about all these logical, or rational, alternatives. You can't even think of Reason on that scale without assuming, at the same time, that there is a supreme Reasoner, called "God", or whatever - the name makes no difference.'

'I agree,' said Turner, 'but that, I think, is as far as our finite logical reason takes us. All these speculations, remember, are framed in terms of our own finite experience of time as we ordinarily think of it. The best we can do is to argue in the way we have so far, that from science's discovery that time, the measure of change, is not single-dimensional or determinate, it follows that it is multidimensional. And from that, in turn, it follows that there has to be some kind of supreme Logos in which all these dimensional possibilities, these branching logical alternatives, are somehow comprehended. So, once you assume multidimensionality, you have to assume that there is some sort of supernatural Being cognisant of all those infinite possibilities in some way which we finite creatures cannot possibly comprehend, or even imagine.'

'Doesn't that, though,' objected Carter, 'take us back to the old "God'seye view" we dismissed earlier?'

'Not really,' replied Turner. "What we dismissed, if you remember, was what Walter identified as the deistic view of classical one-dimensional time as being the same all over space, the so-called "God of the Scientists" - traditional mechanistic science, of course. What we're talking about here is not that old "one-space-one-time" scenario of Galileo and Newton but

some sort of cosmic Overseer and Judge of all the alternative routes a consciousness might take through that indeterminate multidimensional maze - assuming that there is such an ultimate Being, of course.'

'Let's see if I've got that,' said Walter. "You're saying that some overarching, all-comprehending, ineffable being has to exist if all these logical branches of Logos exist - which they must if time is indeterminate - and which it definitely is according to science. It surprises me, I must confess, that of all things, it should be science that provides us with such justification for a belief in God.'

'Perhaps so,' replied Turner, 'but we must bear in mind, continually, that this supernatural being we are speculating about is not some entirely subjective construct of our finite human language. The "language" we are concerned with here is superhuman language, or Logos, of which we can never anticipate more than some extremely small part. So there's no way of us ever solving the riddle of what "God" is by pure deduction - by which I mean,' he added 'working inwards from such finite knowledge as we have towards a knowledge we don't have, and may never have, of the infinite - or, at least, non-finite. Deductive reason doesn't work that way.'

'Sure,' agreed Walter, 'it's just the opposite, isn't it? Our only valid approach to problems on this scale is to start with such part knowledge we have and work outwards from there. But we simply can't think in all those many dimensions. To attempt to do so is bound to create nonsense.'

'So why are we bothered with it?' asked Charlie.

'T don't think we should be,' Turner answered, 'at least, not in the normal run of things. All we need to know is that there has to be more than just the one time-dimension. As we've seen, that gives us sufficient basis for supposing that there is something beyond ourselves, beyond our physical or temporal existence, so that it isn't nonsense to believe in it as religious people do. Whatever that "Something" is has to be of a kind we can recognise in ourselves. That is, it has to be both conscious and rational like that part of us which is conscious and rational. Like logic, it has many dimensions of choice but is infinitely greater than we can ever imagine. If this teaches us anything, it is that there's no room for dogmatism, of either any atheistic or religious kind.'

After a pause, Turner continued, "The issue you raised, if you remember, Noel, was that if the goal of moral action lies in a remote future, then how can that concern us when we can never have any part in it, and that if everyone thought that, our society would fall apart. Well, we've decided, haven't we, that classical science's one-line history, in which our lifetimes are so limited and whose ends are so remote, is not the only dimension there is; that there is at least one other dimension, which is entirely personal, and rising through which is something which doesn't happen to society as a whole but only to us, the individuals of which society consists...

'And to go back to our earlier discussions,' broke in Carter, 'by "society", here, I presume you mean not just human society but the "society" of all natural observers, or "observicles", human and non-human, the product of communication in general, logos, in effect. And as we said, whereas in physis time is one-dimensional and determinate, in logos it is multi-dimensional and indeterminate - dialectical, as Charlie pointed out.'

'Precisely, Tony,' said Turner, 'that needed saying. So that dialectical multi-dimensional complex includes not only our social history but historyin-the-round, including all things, animal, vegetable and mineral, which shape our personal history and which we shape in our turn as conscious individuals - "observers" in relativistic terms.'

'So we are saying,' Carter concluded, 'that being aware - as I think commonsense naturally is - that the life we live is not lived entirely on the social or "worldly" plane, that there is a personal dimension, or whole set of dimensions, to it which doesn't necessarily end when the doctor writes our death-certificate. It makes us responsible, and therefore accountable, for our actions at any and every stage in our lives and perhaps even in the hereafter.'

'Yes, that's right,' Turner replied. "This open-ended, multi-choice approach, which scientists call "heuristic", unavoidably entails moral concerns, such as for truth and moral integrity as a person; the sort of concern for which Socrates and others sacrificed their lives.'

'And Jesus of Nazareth,' added Walter.

'Some scientists as well,' Turner reminded him, 'such as the truth-seeker, Galileo, and Giordano Bruno, who was burned at the stake for teaching

that the earth goes round the sun." If mechanistic science had had its way, all concerns of that moral sort would be disallowed.'

'Ah, yes, I see,' said Noel. "That answers my original question, too, of the relevance of self-sacrifice!"

'As you said, Noel,' commented Carter, 'an injection of that kind of moral awareness into our present society wouldn't come amiss, would it? The fact that we may be accountable, in some cosmical kind of way for what we do in our lives, that we don't necessarily escape the consequences of our actions at death, seems to be just the thing to answer your problem.'

Noel agreed. 'It certainly supplies that premium on personal good behaviour which I felt was lacking in our present society,' he said.

'But,' Turner interposed, 'as far as I'm concerned, that's really all we need to know. The rest, trying to project the imagination into al! those different dimensions, is pure speculation, of the kind which forms the worst excesses of classical philosophy and theology. If everyone were to become preoccupied with that, it would stop society dead in its tracks.'

'Yes, I agree, Dave,' said Carter. 'For me, too, commonsense morality - acting in terms of one's free rational conscience in our day-to-day affairs - is good enough.'

"What's important to stress, though,' added Walter, 'is the distinction we're making between two different kinds of morality; the one being expedient morality, or "world-morality" you might say, the kind for living comfortably with one's fellows; the other being personal morality - or "soul morality", to use an old-fashioned phrase. These two different kinds of morality might sometimes conflict, as when we feel we should act in what we call matters of conscience, as exemplified by Jesus of Nazareth, of course, who upset the Roman establishment, not forgetting people like Socrates and Galileo, who, as sticklers for truth - the logos - fell foul of the society they were in.'

This reminded Noel of something. 'I seem to recall that this same distinction between personal and social morality appears, on the one hand,

25 Bruno was a Dominican scientist of the Renaissance who was burned at the stake for teaching Copernicus' account of the solar system as centred. on the sun, not on the earth, as the sacred text of the Old Testament of the Bible taughe.

between the works of people like Bishop Butler, Jeremy Bentham and John Stuart Mill'* and, on the other hand, of writers like John Bunyan in his famous book, The Pilgrim' Progress. The "progress" Bunyan talks about is distinctly different from what the others are projecting. What I mean is, for Bunyan, it is advancement of the individual, not in society as we know it but, implicitly, in all those other dimensions we are discussing.'

'It seems, then,' responded Carter, thoughtfully, 'that the ends of true morality are not so much those dictated by any worldly authority or religious denomination. They are purely intuitive - conscientious, you might say, insofar as each of us, deep down, knows what's right or wrong, good or bad or whatever, for himself, or at least, for him to become if he has the moral strength to pursue it.'

'And in that way,' added Turner, 'if everyone seeks their own moral perfection as they truly intuit it, "to thine own self be true", as Shakespeare put it, then to the extent that everyone achieves this, the morality of their society is assured. This, I understand, was the Greek ideal of arete, of the moral man in the moral society. In that sense, there's no conflict between the two moralities. They're the two sides - the "heads" and "tails" of Logos - the logos and the anti-logos, you might say.'

Charlie objected. "That sounds wonderful,' he said, 'but in my experience there are people who are completely devoid of that intuitive morality, or conscience. In fact, I've seen so many of them that, although I can't say I believe in a God, I can sure as hell believe in a Devil!'

'Yes,' replied Turner, 'that "devil" definitely lurks in our society. If only people could realise that the "God" which each of them casts in his own subjective image - or the image that convention foists upon them for its own purposes - cannot be anything but artificial, then they would be able

26 Joseph Butler (1692-1752), was an English priest and theologian, who, in reaction against the growth of scientific Deism, wrote the famous Analogy of Religion (1736). In this work he argued that a belief in natural theology (the derivation of faith from a rational exploration of nature) is not in conflict with revealed religion, that the two were, in fact, perfectly compatible and complementary.

See also Utilitarianism by John Stuart Mill, ed. by M. Warnock, Publ. by Collins, Fontana, London, 1962. Mill (1806-1873) was a British philosopher-economist, the son of James Mill. He had a great impact on 19th-century British thought, not only in philosophy and economics but also in the areas of political science, logic, and ethics.

to think things out for themselves. And in such a rational society of individuals, the ones you talk about, Charlie, who cannot be relied upon to do that for medically identifiable reasons, perhaps, would be looked after by those qualified to do so. As one of those experts once said, "An insane man in a sane society is better off than a sane man in an insane society." I can't think of anything truer than that. But of course,' he added, 'this does not necessarily entail that anyone who does not comply with the social norm is automatically sick or insane.'

The four men sat quietly for a few minutes, each absorbed in considering the implications of what they had been arguing. Then Walter broke the silence.

"Classical science has much to answer for, then, in my opinion,' he said, with feeling, 'in screwing-up our Western society by removing from it that essential element of moral concern that was always called "conscience" - by putting it about that there is only the one, absolutely determinate, timedimension in which we live and die. This has virtually removed the moral cement that holds society together. If you teach people that they have no souls, that the very idea of a "soul" is "unscientific"; if you convince them that this life is all there is so that when they are gone they cannot be held to account for their actions, then how will they behave?'

"Much as people do now,' replied Charlie, sardonically.

'So there you are, Noel,' said Turner. "That's your answer. If people could at least become unconvinced - or shall we say de-convinced - of the Western scientific dogma that consciousness is entirely fleeting and illusory, that we can have no moral goals in our own right as individuals apart from those dictated by society, that we are no more than social products or statistics - if we somehow got rid of that dogma, then, as we now seem to agree, commonsense morality should naturally reassert itself. Society's self-inflicted wounds might heal and we might enjoy some measure of harmony and peace, true rational democracy as one might say.'

'Just as long as the dogmatists of the opposite kind, the religious zealots and metaphysicians, are kept at bay,' countered Noel. 'I hate to think of what would happen to democracy if those factions attained, nowadays, the

levels of credibility they commanded in the Middle Ages, before budding science clipped their wings.'

'But, surely, Dave,' ventured Carter, 'if you do believe in a "God", then you need to have some identifying idea of "Him - or "It", or whatever. I mean, you can't worship something you can't even define. For instance, what about you, Walter? I suppose you would say that God is some super Consciousness which has oversight of all these lives and souls and things, an infinite Presence which is Everywhere, throughout all those many dimensions.'

'Everywhere except in "empty space", as Voltaire once said,' cautioned Noel.

'A timely reminder, Noel,' said Walter. "We've seen how deadly that Democritan idea of "the extended void" can be. It creates all those absurdities about our daily consciousness and all its concerns as just a "ghost in the machine" as someone has said, and of souls being wraiths leaving the body at death, swanning around in space in the form of "ectoplasm" or some other sort of invisible "soul-substance", all of which gives those of us who believe in souls a bad name.' Then he added, "In talking about "soul substance", here, I'm not pushing spiritualism, you understand, I'm simply talking about differences in uses of language.'

'In that context,' remarked Carter, 'It occurs to me that Modern Science's paraphrase of those alleged soul-substances floating' about invisibly in space is the notion of "photons" and "field-substance". The High Priests of Science, like our Professor Craig, who would probably laugh their socks off at the idea of angel-messengers with wings, haven't yet seen that their modern ideas of "photons", or space-travelling "wave-particles" is every bit as laughable.'

'Turner smilingly agreed. Then turning to Walter, he said. "You, of course, hold to the standard Christian concept of God.'

'As the Creator and Sustainer of the World, yes,' Walter replied firmly, 'allowing for the language, of course - and nothing in our discussions so

'far has given me any reason to doubt it or the validity of the Christian faith. And in accordance with that belief, I don't see Man's aim as being to create social perfection, to build a heaven on earth, a political Utopia. I don't see our moral aspirations as lying wholly in that one dimension,

where they inevitably fail because they are built on the sand of bureaucracy and lust for status and power, not on the rock of love. This is nothing like carnal "love", of course, but what the Bible describes as love of God - the Logos, as we are now calling it. As I see it,' he continued, warming to his subject, 'a society built on the Christian principle of brotherly love - of people behaving rationally and in accordance with free moral conscience - will organise itself naturally, and with the barest minimum of restrictive laws. As it is said, "There is no need for law where love rules." What happens to regimes based on purely theoretical, impersonal ideals, without love? They are usually the dreariest and most corrupt, most fleeting communities of all.'

'Tl say Amen to that, Walter,' said Turner. 'And what about you, Noel?" he asked. 'What's your concept of God?'

'To be truthful, I don't know,' Noel replied. 'I question so many of the presumptions there are in religion about "knowing God", claiming intimate knowledge of what his gender is, what he feels and what his intentions are for us personally, as well as for the human race and the universe generally - especially when those presumptions are institutionalised in the forms of all the various, conflicting denominations. But I can also see that atheism is no less presumptuous, no less dogmatic. It cannot sustain any workable kind of morality and is completely absurd now that the old "scientific" justification for it has gone. How about you, Tony?'

'T don't hold to any denominational belief, Noel,' answered Carter, after some careful thought. 'All I can say is that I simply cannot believe that the consciousness and perception which I enjoy is the highest form of consciousness and perception there is. To me, there has to be some higher perception which includes mine as mine includes the perceptions of all my various cells and senses. I suppose the best you could say is that I am a nonatheist, a position which has been confirmed more and more by these discussions of ours.'

'And you, Dave?' asked Noel. 'T'd say, from the way you argue, you do believe in God.'

'Only as an idea of something which is infinitely beyond any idea I might have of it - or which anyone might have of it, for that matter. For

me, though, its existence is absolutely self-evident in the part-knowledge I have of it. The fact is that wherever I look there is significance, that is to say, a logical order in things and events without which there would be nothing at all. Ir's also self-evident to me that this significance extends far beyond my actual grasp of it at any time, and that as I live my life I see more and more of that significance, especially in retrospect.'

'If I may be allowed,' Turner continued, 'here is a purely personal analogy. It's as though I were given, at birth, a brand new car to drive with an opaque windscreen but very clear rear-view mirrors. To steer it, I have

'to guess where I am going on the basis of where I've been, and just feel my way forward in a probing kind of way, bumping into things, painfully perhaps, as I go. But the further I go, the more I see in my rear-view mirror of where I have been. And that's where I see the significance, in retrospect, that I could never have seen in prospect. This enables me more and more to project my way forward, with reason as a kind of radar. With growing experience, I find that what I may have regarded at the time as disasters often turn out to have been, as it were, "pillars of success". The fact that I

. am still here suggests to me that in my seemingly haphazard ricocheting around I have been protected and guided in some mysterious way and that whatever agency has done this for me so far may be relied upon, cautiously, of course, to guide me from here on. If you say that this is just plain belief, then so be it. Whether it is a religious belief I leave you guys to judge.'

"That makes sense,' said Noel, 'and it confirms my view that all attempts to comprehend this superhuman "being", by the various dogmatic religions, are comic - or tragic, depending on how they inflict their ridiculous dogmatisms on others.'

Carter glanced apprehensively at Walter. It was all too easy to forget that this open-minded young man was a priest. Would he, Carter wondered, take offence at what had been said. Deciding to take the bull by the horns, he asked Walter this question outright.

'By no means,' Walter assured him. "The word "God" is a common Teutonic term indicating just about any personal object of worship you might think of. The basic Hebrew name for what I believe in was Yahweh, which is a corruption of the four letters YHWH. This was not really a
