name - indeed, it was intentionally unpronounceable, although it is usually rendered as "Yahweh" or "Jehovah". It originally signified more or less what you, Dave, seem to be saying, a "something" which undeniably exists but which we should not defile by any naming or imagining.'

Carter was reassured by this response of Walter's.

'So there's really very little basic difference between us, here, in our concepts of what, for the sake of communication, we have to call God.'

'Only in the strength of our commitment to pure faith, perhaps,' replied Walter, 'which is why I wear a dog-collar in my work to signify where I stand,'

'And where is that, Walter?' Carter enquired.

'In the belief that Christ was the embodiment of the Logos and that by his supreme sacrifice he showed us this other dimension we've been discussing, "the Way, the Truth and the Life", the personal dimension which, as we've seen, there has to be if such moral sacrifices are to make sense. I always felt that such a dimension existed, but now I see it as literally at right-angles to the world-dimension which Caesar was concerned with. "Render unto Caesar the things which are Caesar's," as Christ said, "and render unto God the things which are. God's." This distinguishes very clearly, I think, those dimensions we've discussed, the soul-dimension, or dimensions, and the dimension which is the concern of those whose aims are defined in purely social terms.'

'So you would rule out a belief in transmigration,' said Carter.

'As a Christian, yes. I believe in salvation by intercession, by the cleansing - or forgiving - of all sin; and I believe in the efficacy of prayer to that end.'

'It might be worth mentioning, here,' said Turner, 'that the Jews had genuine grounds for concern that Christians were presuming to define the indefinable, which could be said to account for much of the dogmatism and resulting closed-mindedness of our Western culture.'

'The Christianity of the Middle ages, certainly, Dave,' replied Walter, 'the religion of the Catholic Church, with its gaudiness, its incense, its Inquisitions, torture-chambers and all. But that was never true Christianity, as the Carpenter of Nazareth defined it. Nor is it Christianity as | pursue

it, which is no more presumptuous in defining and naming of the ineffable than the Jews would have wanted. Nor am I in any way doctrinaire in my views of these things. I certainly wouldn't persecute or declare war on anyone on account of a mere difference in language.'

Noel digested this. Then he asked the wrangler, 'And what about you, Charlie? Where do you stand?"

'I pass,' said Charlie.

A knocking on the window distracted them. It was Marian. Outside they could see Rick's camper. Noel went across to open the door.

'Hi!' Marian said, happily. "We've had a great day. How about you?' And then, eyeing the beer bottles, she added, 'You haven't been stuck in here all day?"

"We've been drowning our sorrows at your absence!' answered Turner, raising his glass in greeting.

'Especially Noel,' Carter added, with a grin, 'the operative word, in his case, being "drowning".

The reason for the ensuing laughter was lost on Marian.

Chapter 14: The Farm

Finale

A couple of vehicles left the Lodge later that evening and headed in the direction of Clearwater. About fifteen minutes later, they turned off to the right and for half a mile or so followed a track with forest on one side and pasture on the other. The track ended in a clearing. On two sides were various outbuildings and on the third was a long, low cabin. As they drew up, the door of the cabin opened and out came Charlie, wearing a big cook's apron.

'Hi there! You got the right place okay. Come in,' he invited, "Dinner's almost ready.'

The little group entered the cabin and looked around admiringly. It consisted of one enormous room, with log walls and hammer-beam roof, but at the same time, there was an impression of warmth and cosiness. At the far end of the room was a huge window, while the door end housed a fully equipped modern kitchen. Towards each end of the room were the inevitable wood stoves. The one near the window end was a large, futuristic device of shiny red while the kitchen end was heated by an almost comical black pot-bellied one on which was boiling a big black kettle. The floor was covered with brightly-coloured rugs. Four large sofas arranged in a square provided the seating accommodation, except for a rocking chair near the window. All around the walls were shelves of books, and a few paintings.

'Charlie, it's beautiful!' gasped Marian. 'Imagine all this in the middle of the wilderness!'

'Yes,' said Walter, who was standing at the cooker stirring something in a pan, 'it isn't quite what you'd expect from outside.'

They made themselves comfortable while Charlie poured their drinks.

'Gee, Charlie, I sure envy you!" said Rick. 'You've got it made! All this and a job away from all the problems us city-slickers have to face.'

'I tried being a city-slicker years ago,' Charlie replied casually, 'Tm sure some of you've guessed that I haven't always been a wrangler.'

Hearing this, Walter stopped his stirring. Was his old buddy eventually coming out of his shell, he wondered.

"Tell them about it, Charlie,' he encouraged.

"Well, okay,' Charlie continued, 'it was after I left medical school . . ."

'You're a doctor?' asked Helen.

'Sort of . . . a clinical psychologist, in fact. But I realised I wasn't cut out for it, that I was a country boy at heart, so one day I quit and high-tailed it back to the bush.'

Charlie said this with an air of finality which should have precluded further questions, but Marian was curious.

'But isn't it a waste of your education and training to bury yourself away like this? Who knows what good you could be doing in sorting out the mixed-up people there are around in cities these days.'

For a moment, Charlie hesitated. Then, as if he had come to some sort of a decision, he replied to the question.

'I quit because I had a nervous breakdown.' There, I've admitted it, he thought, and realised he felt nothing but relief.

After a short silence, the two women made sympathetic noises.

'Hey, I'm sorry,' said Marian. "You must have had a bad time.'

'I surely did,' said Charlie. He wanted to talk about it to these people who he was now confident would understand. 'It was awful. It was as if my mind and body were coming apart. It was like someone had soldered my nerve-endings to a hundred-watt amplifier. I writhed while asleep and wriggled while awake. Waking and sleeping were only swapping one nightmare for another. Sometimes I was afraid I was going to die and sometimes I wished I would.'

Turner recalled a similar experience of his own, many years earlier.

'I understand,' he said, quietly, almost to himself. "You can't tell whether it's you that's mad or if everyone else is.'

"That's it,' Charlie continued, reassured by Turner's remark. 'It was as though I had this mad urge to make sense of a world of impressions in which there was no sense at all. If I dreamed of leaves blowing, I felt this

fetish, this insane urge, to sort them all out into patterns, classes and categories which I couldn't define. If I dreamed of sheep it was to have to catch them and try to teach them about Jung or Adler, or get them to understand the difference between axons and dendrites - because that somehow seemed cosmically important. It was as though I'd spilled a truckload of soap-tablets in a pond and was trying to pick them up with one hand and tuck them all under my arm.'

"You must have been living a nightmare, Charlie,' remarked Helen, with a sympathetic shiver. 'And what brought all that on? Was it your work?'

How would Charlie respond to that, Walter wondered. Had Helen asked the 'sixty-four thousand dollar question?

'Overwork, I guess,' Charlie said, calmly. "No, that's too simple. It's what, in the medical profession, is called the "Gauguin syndrome", after that French guy who got his fill of being a bank-teller and high-tailed it for the South Seas to paint pictures. It's like this. A guy works hard, towards some ideal, and then he realises that he's on a caning to nothing. His aims get confused and he "loses his north". He carries on, on automatic, and. gets more and more frustrated. But there's no let-out for these frustrations. They build and build and eventually they overflow in bizarre, sometimes very aggressive, behaviour. I guess I'd never been one for deep sorting-out - what you'd call developing my philosophy of life. I'd just applied myself, according to my training, and trusted that those who had taught me knew what they were doing.'

'And did they?' Noel asked.

'At first, I thought so,' came Charlie's quiet reply. "But gradually | came to see the fundamental flaw in what I was doing. My brief was to deal with cases of conflict between the individual and society, to fit the one comfortably to the other.'

'So, what was wrong with that?' Helen asked.

"Well -I guess this doesn't sound very professional - apart from the outright incorrigible ones, congenital psychopaths in some cases - | realised that I was dealing more and more with people who ought never to be forced to fit. They were what they were, free-range individuals, often referred to me by the Authorities, individuals who behaved the way they

did because - as I became convinced - it was the society that was wrong, not them.'

'Then I witnessed the results of annihilation-therapy, or destructive ECT" on some individuals and it began to dawn on me how draconian psychiatry was becoming, cramming these individuals into conformity with a society whose aims no-one bothered to examine. It was a moral and philosophical vacuum. Religion was dead and I realised, from my brush with Academic Philosophy during my student days, that no answers were going to come from that direction. Our society had become totally mindless and aimless.'

There were nods of sympathy from Carter and Turner.

'So,' he continued, 'I found myself less and less convinced of the validity of my job. I began to feel like some Nazi doctor ministering to the Jewburners at Belsen or Dachau, encouraging them to work hard to fill their quotas. What could I do for those I considered to be "right-minded" for which they were sent to me for help? Their anguish was real. They were being disadvantaged, socially dispossessed by following their consciences, whistle-blowers, in some cases, who - in the aircraft industry, for example - were dismissed from their employment for complaining about dangers to their colleagues and the public for some fault their superiors considered too expensive to put right and, in some cases, ended in disaster. Should I have advised people like that to lie, cheat and steal, like all the rest, in order to "get on"? What was the alternative? Sometimes I'd argue about it with Walter and we even fell out over it.'

'I remember that vividly,' murmured Walter, 'and I could see what was happening to you.'

'Plainly you did,' acknowledged Charlie. 'But what was I to do with those patients? For them it was a case of "whether the pitcher strikes the rock or the rock the pitcher, it's bad for the pitcher".'

After a pause, Turner said, "What you say here, Charlie, reminds me of what I believe I said earlier, about what a professor of psychiatry once told me, that in his experience it is much worse for a sane man in an insane society than for an insane man in a sane society.' |

27 ECT: Electro-Convulsive Therapy.

'Yes, that's what you did say, Dave. and it's just about right!' Charlie responded, thoughtfully. 'Anyway,' he continued, 'I felt helpless, completely vulnerable to just about anything. And then - you'll probably think I was real stupid to add to my problems - J got into a sexual fizz, for escape, I suppose - by having an affair with the wife of one of my most respected colleagues. Now I was trapped in a gigantic conflict of loyalties all round, because at the time I was married myself and my colleague and I were still great friends. Looking back, I guess it was this in the end, that triggered my collapse.'

The interested silence and obvious sympathy of his audience had sustained Charlie's confession. Now, as if coming out of a trance, he slapped his thigh. The 'wrangler' in him had returned.

'Hey, you guys,' he said heartily, 'I didn't bring you here to listen to my moaning. I'm over it now, so don't go sneaking for the doors. What's say we eat, okay?

The company gathered round the table and were soon tucking into what, to the women's surprise, was a well-served and beautifully cooked dinner.

"This is a delicious piece of meat, Charlie,' said Helen, raising her fork to her mouth. 'My compliments to your butcher!'

Charlie was pleased.

'Actually, Helen,' he said, all unsuspectingly, 'my "butcher" is a local hunter, a good friend of mine. He gave me that piece of moose this morning,

Helen lowered her fork, paled and then flushed. Watching her, Noel wondered whether this was due to anger or embarrassment. He recalled her disparaging remarks about hunting, only a few days earlier.

Helen, in fact, was in a state of confusion. She had certainly been enjoying the meat, with its leanness and its texture between the coarseness of beef and the smoothness of lamb. But she knew, now, that this animal had not been killed in what she considered a civilised way. Yet, where were the butchers' shops out here? She should have realised . . .

Then Marian, with impeccable female logic, attempted to solve her sister's dilemma.

'Never mind, Helen,' she said soothingly, 'I know you don't hold with shooting animals, but this moose was already shot, so it wasnt your fault, and it would have been a pity to waste it... . Did I say something?' she asked, at the men's laughter.

Helen, however, was not amused. She felt foolish, an emotion she did not often experience. Her unease became obvious and she felt the need to explain.

'T''m sorry, Charlie, but I can't finish it. I keep seeing these animals in my mind's eye, roaming free in the forest, then recalling someone I overheard on a plane journey, boasting about how many "fine, brave specimens" he's killed.'

'I understand, Helen,' said Charlie, gently, 'but not many animals are shot in this area simply for sport, you know. The folks round here use the bush as a "supermarket", to stock up for winter; a hundred fish, say, and a moose or two for the freezer.'

'But why?' Marian enquired. "There's a supermarket in Clearwater, isn't there?'

'Surely, and it charges supermarket prices,' replied her host. 'A lot of folk can't afford it, so they hunt to feed their families.'

'But it is still killing wild animals,' protested Helen, desperately trying to hang on to her idealistic view of the wilderness. "No-one has the right to do that, surely.'

'Listen, Helen,' said Charlie, 'let me tell you something. Most hunters " and trappers have a great respect for wild creatures. My father was a trapper, in Alberta, so I know something about it.'

This further revelation of Charlie's past had them all listening attentively.

"When I was a kid, I sometimes used to help him on his trap lines. We'd go into the bush in November and stay there four or five months. Pa would take the skidoo"® and I'd drive the dog-sled loaded with supplies. It was a real hard life, but psychologically rewarding. We earned good money, enough to pay my way through college.'

28 A motorbike on skis.

'But didn't it bother you that you were killing innocent creatures who werent doing you any harm?' persisted Helen.

'No, because we lived with animals as animals, who live by preying on one another,' came the reply. 'My father never over-trapped and he always gave space for the creatures to regenerate. He inspected his trap lines every two days to prevent unnecessary suffering. Okay, Helen, so the thought of it makes you shudder, but you happily eat meat from a butcher's, and have you ever considered what it's like in a slaughter house or a battery farm?'

Noel rushed to Helen's aid.

"Come on, Charlie, you must know that not all trappers are so careful.'

"That's true,' admitted Charlie, "but they're very few and not much liked by the others. Most of 'em care for the animals, like my pa did. He had a special set of trail clothes which were never washed, so that the animals would recognise his scent, and he'd prepare scents to a secret recipe which he used as lures. Sure, we preyed on the animals, as they do on each other, or on us if they have the chance. But if that's wrong, then blame nature for making it that way.'

'But today we're in danger of losing so much of our wildlife,' Marian pointed out. 'All over the world, man is destroying their habitats . . .'

*,. and our own with it,' added Helen. 'Just look at the destruction of the rain-forests. I'm thankful that at least, here in BC, there is a replanting policy.'

'Perhaps the answer to these problems,' Turner interposed, 'if we are to exercise our natural human drives without endangering other life-forms, is to open up new frontiers in space and leave this planet as a kind of genebank, one great big wilderness park.'

'I think that's inevitable,' said Carter, - for man to spread into space, I mean.' -

"Talking about natural parks,' Turner continued, 'you'll be travelling through the Icefields Parkway on your way to Calgary, Rick. That's really spectacular.'

'But for how much longer?' asked Carter. "Those glaciers are retreating rapidly, some of them more than a mile in the last hundred years. It's all the evidence needed of the greenhouse effect.'

'Is that so?' enquired Rick. 'It'll sure be interesting to see that, though Tm truly sorry to be leaving you folks behind tomorrow.'

'T have to leave tomorrow, too,' said Walter. 'My parishioners are expecting a sermon from me on Sunday. I've got to catch the evening train tomorrow in Clearwater.'

'Tt always surprises me that passenger trains travel only at night through the Rockies,' said Turner, 'and that the freight trains get the best of the scenery.'

"There are special excursion trains,' Helen pointed out. "We did the trip on the Royal Hudson last year. It took four days and was sure expensive, but it was like travelling in a first-class hotel. You ought to try it, Dave. I'm sure your wife would enjoy it.'

'Maybe we will, Helen,' Turner replied, 'I like train trips, but I'm not sure I could stand being cooped-up in one for several days. I'm impressed by Canada's railway system, though. The length of your freight trains, for instance. We couldn't handle such things in the U.K.'

"You mean like the one we saw in Kamloops today?' asked Rick. 'It took half an hour to pass over the level-crossing. A guy told me it was a mile and a quarter long. We stopped for chow while it was passing by.'

Carter chuckled. 'In England, that train could be in two stations at once! But one reason I came out here was because of the development going on in the transport system. Not so much the railroads, of course, as they were already pretty well-developed, but the highway system. I was lucky enough to be working here in time for the completion of the Trans-Canada Highway in 1962. The opening-up of that at Rogers Pass was a great occasion."

'That's the year I was born,' said Marian. "You've been in B.C. a long time, Tony.'

'Not as a resident,' he replied. 'I first came out on a contract, and after a few years of shuttling to and fro across the Atlantic, I decided I'd start my own business and I felt that Vancouver was the place to do it. I've never regretted it. I felt that I was at the cutting edge of history in that department.'

'Canadian history, you mean,' his old friend amiably pointed out. 'T know you've tried often enough to persuade me to move out here. But in

Britain, we've been where Canadians now are. As I see it, the UK is at the real frontier of civilisation, with its overcrowding and all the peculiar problems that entails - in the way you've encountered them in your profession, Charlie. That, for me, is the true "wilderness" in which we have to compete for our future survival. Compared with it, all this, around here, lovely and challenging as it is, is no more than a holiday theme-park. For me, the UK is "where it's at", the forging of the next stage in world civilisation - the first "post-industrial society", as someone has called it - and as a denizen of the communications industry I want to be there to make my contribution.' ;

"That's an interesting way of looking at it,' remarked Walter. "But tell us, Dave, what is this "contribution" you would like to make?"

'T think I would describe it as following the work of Claude Shannon, who was a telephone engineer like myself. As you may know, he was one of the first to treat information which up till then had been regarded as an abstract concept, as a physically measurable quantity, like length, time, mass and so on. But whereas Shannon's work was more or less confined to the area of telephone technology, my concern is to extend these principles, of entropy, negentropy and so on into communication in general, including human language, in the way we've discussed. So, what began as an interest in copper wires and electricity gradually became an interest in "channels" of any sort. And with the coming of light-conduction through fibre-optic cables, my interest grew to include light itself as a quantised communication between everything and everything else. In this way, I began to see how, over the centuries, the accredited "beavers" of theoretical science, have inadvertently clogged the natural course of commonsense thinking about nature. I would say that a scholarly over-concern with keeping our physical concepts in that historical groove - or rut, rather - creates the sorry state of affairs which is the root of ninety percent of the problems society is now facing.'

"You see the next "post-industrial" phase of development, then,' said Noel, 'as basically a scientific one, a change in scientific philosophy. So, in a word, Dave, ideally, how would you want science to develop?"

'In a word? . . . Well, as briefly as I can manage, it is simply that science should come of age and realise that relativity truly és relativity.'

'That's too brief, Dave,' said Carter.' You'll have to be less cryptic.'

"Well, what I mean is that relativity is about relations, so a science based on relativity is as different from classical science as chalk from cheese, and sooner or later science will have to declare it." In classical science, things didn't relate. They simply were what they were, infinitely substantial and self-sufficient, isolated from one another by a "void" across which any contact they might have was purely accidental. You can't just tag-on "relativity" to that idea. I mean, you can't just add-in "the observer" as an afterthought to a culture based on centuries of mechanistic philosophy. In true relativity, or relationism, the observer has to be your starting-point, your very first consideration. Science is produced by people, and people have to matter as people, not as cogs in some imagined social or cosmic machine. This is the very opposite of the Marxist dialectic we discussed at the cabin. Personal experience is primary; "Science" is secondary and inferential, its language, as I say, full-to-creaking with convention. Some of this language as students learn it is useful - even necessary - for the most part, a correct and efficient interpretation of what that observer perceives, but too much of it becomes outworn and spurious and serves only to lead our perceptions astray.'

In response to the ensuing silence, Turner added, 'In short, you might say that by any means whatsoever, I want to help unearth the old commonsense verities that, once, just about everyone naturally recognised and which have now been all but buried by our Western tradition of science.'

'So,' said Helen, 'criticising science, revising its language to fit new facts, is never sacrilege, as that bull-dozing professor seems to think, but is a constant duty of critics, in the same way we have an opposition to government for the maintaining of democracy.'

"That's a good way of putting it, Helen,' said Noel. 'And by that analogy, these discussions we've had have shown me that our present science is like some totalitarian regime in which, despite all the professional in-fighting

29 See N. V. Pope. 'The Overdue Revolution', Mensa, April 1987, pp. 28-29 Publ. British Mensa, Ltd. Also 'Experiments in God's Eye', Times Higher Educational Supplement, Feb. 2nd, 1989, p. 14.

that goes on, there is, in the end, only the one party to vote for - if we were allowed to vote, that is.'

Carter's thoughts were running on different lines.

'So the equations of physics,' he remarked, 'are not, as I used to think, "written in the sky". They are part and parcel of the way we look at the world, with emphasis on the word "look". In other words, to quote somebody whose name I can't remember, we are "writing the observer back into the equations".'

"Yes, that's just it, Tony,' agreed Turner, 'and any true significance those equations might have comes from the logos, the natural language of which our language of science is, at best, only a part - when it's in good season, that is.'

Noel found himself wondering, at this point, what Rick, who had remained quietly attentive, was making of all this. He asked Rick as much.

'Well,' replied the large American thoughtfully, 'I guess what Dave is telling us is that we've been "blinded by science". If we hadn't been, we would see relativity for what it is, as you guys have explained to me, as just seeing things through the eyes we have in our own heads instead of through some big "Eye in the Sky" which sees everything, everywhere, all at once - which ain't anywhere at all, really, except in our brainwashed imaginations.'

"That's just about it, Rick,' Turner said, 'in a nutshell.'

Once again, Noel was quite stunned at the facility of Rick's summingup of what he, Noel, regarded as a highly sophisticated discussion. How many more 'Ricks' were there around, he wondered. To Turner he said,

'T think it will be a long time before science can recast itself in those truly relativistic terms, but the more I think about it, the more I feel it's got to happen eventually.'

'In its own good time, it will,' said Turner, simply.

For a few moments, nobody spoke. They were all aware that this was probably the last time they would be together in such circumstances. Only a few days had passed since they had set out on the trail together, but not one of them could claim to be quite the same person as he or she was then.

Then Marian broke the silence, voicing their thoughts.

"This vacation has surely been different. I came to paint and here I am, learning all sorts of things about relativity and how the ancient Greeks thought, the sort of things I'd have run a mile to avoid before. But you guys have made it all so interesting, and thanks for that. My only regret is that it's coming to an end.'

'Tt needn't, Marian,' said her sister. 'It might be the end of the vacation, but it doesn't have to be the end of what we've been learning from each other here. Besides, we're keeping in touch with Rick and Noel, aren't we?"

"Why just Rick and Noel?' Carter asked mischievously.

Helen blushed, confused again at realising she had revealed what she thought was a secret. Again Noel came to her rescue.

'T've decided to leave with Helen - and Marian, of course - tomorrow to spend some time in Vancouver. My father wants to expand his company into Canada and, like Tony, I believe Vancouver is a good place to start. It also means Helen and I'll have the time to get to know each other better, I hope.' He smiled at her.

'I wish you luck, in all your ambitions,' said Turner warmly, with a pleased glance at Carter, 'but how does Rick fit into this?"

Marian answered him. 'Noel says he'll be able to offer him a good position in the new company, when it's set up, so then Rick will re-locate to Vancouver,' said Marian, delighted to talk about their plans now that they were no longer secret. 'I guess you can tell how I feel about that.' And she squeezed Rick's arm.

'Well, fancy that, Dave,' said Carter. "While we've been wondering in the wilderness, Cupid's been up to his old tricks! Who'd have guessed it, eh?'

Walter, returning from clearing away the dishes, beamed at everyone.

'If you want someone to perform any ceremonies,' he grinned. 'I'm fully qualified.'

"We'll let you know,' laughed Helen.

'Y'know,' said Rick, 'when I met up with Tony and Dave, I was just about to call it a day and head off home, it was so lonesome around here. D'ya think that somehow the logos had it fixed for me to stop at that spot that day?'

"Who knows?' answered Walter. "We've agreed that it provides us with choices, so maybe you were presented with the right ones for you at that time.'

"Whatever it was,' replied Rick, 'Pm sure glad I made that stop at Dawson Falls. I ain't sure how to put this - as you know, speechifying ain't my strong point - but I never knew about guys like you before. I never knew,' he said, guilelessly, 'that you didn't have to be a narrow-minded schmuck to be a pastor, and [ never realised that college graduates are just guys like the rest of us . . . or that a woman can be both cute and smart,' he added, looking pointedly at the women.

"That's right, Rick,' agreed Noel, 'we've all learned something here, it

seems.'

By this time, they had finished their coffee. Carter became aware of the time passing and stood up.

'Come on, Dave,' he said, 'far be it from me to spoil a pleasant party, but our host has a busy working day tomorrow and the rest of you have long journeys to make, so I suggest we make tracks for the lodge.'

'You're right, Tony,' replied Turner, 'but first let's thank Charlie - and Walter, of course, for their hospitality...'

There were murmurs of agreement from the others.

'If ever you come to the U.K.,' Turner added, 'you must let me return the compliment.'

'Hey, Wale!' said Rick suddenly. 'T just thought! Don't I have to go through Blue River on my way to Jasper? Why don't I give you a ride?'

This agreed, good-nights and good-byes were said, then the little group made its way along the bumpy track towards the road. Charlie and Walter returned to the cabin to clear up the remains of the meal.

'How do you reckon what Dave is trying to do?' enquired Charlie, removing the tablecloth in one spectacular swoop, then cursing as the crumbs flew everywhere.

'Well,' his friend replied, starting to stack the dishwasher, 'I guess he's trying to do with modern science what Martin Luther did with the ossified Church of his time, which used Latin in the way that modern science uses

mathematics, to mesmerise ordinary folks, so as to put over on them whatever nonsense they fancy.'

'So Dave is trying to nail his thesis to Science's door, so to speak?'

"More like they'll nail Aim to the door if he's not careful? Walter replied. 'Bur' he continued, after a moment's reflection, 'I guess, you could compare him with Luther, except that Science's "door", unlike the one Luther used, is impossible to locate. Besides, what could Dave's "thesis" convey, that people of today would gather round and read? Most of us are now so totally bemused by Science that we are deaf to any discussion of it. All Dave's "thesis" would signify to people at large, if anything at all, would be just another in-group tussle among scientists. So far as they're concerned, that "thesis" might just as well be nailed in thin air.'

"Yes, from what I've heard of his ideas, I can appreciate that,' said Charlie.

"Tell me, then, what do you make of it, from what you've heard so far?" Walter closed the door of the washer and switched it on.

'I guess the bit that most appealed to me,' replied Charlie, considering his words, 'was his reassurance - because that was how I saw it - that the social dimension, with its standards of conformity, is not the only one that matters; that there is this other, personal, dimension - or set of dimensions - to things. This has convinced me that the reality that relativists talk about and the one psychiatrists and their patients talk about - and even religious people like you, Walter - are not nearly so separate as I'd imagined, that they're all part of the same observational or communicational network. And, even more important for me is that it reassures me that there is a natural integrity, or logic, to that system, which is superior to ourselves and provides a reliable basis for sanity.'

Walter could appreciate just how much that meant to his friend.

"You know, Walt,' Charlie continued, 'I'm beginning to feel that ''m being cured of some deep "schizophrenia" - split-mindedness, to you. But what's most important to me is the feeling of relief that the sickness from which, as you know, I suffered so acutely, was basically not mine, it seems, but society's. I hadn't realised how much at one I had become with those "sociopaths" I described earlier. My God, Walt! he exclaimed. 'I think Iam in a better position to help them now.'

Pity the doctor who's never been sick," eh,' Walter quoted.

'Exactly. But even so, I can't see that Pll ever return to the profession of clinical psychologist.'

"How do you propose to help those. patients, then?' the clergyman wanted to know.

"Hey, steady on, Walt, I haven't had time to work that out, yet. Perhaps Yl become a philosopher like Dave - or perhaps even a pastor like you,' Charlie grinned.

Walter chuckled. 'I guess I don't see you as a pastor, old buddy, nor as an academic philosopher. Anyway, I doubt that Dave would accept that he is a philosopher, certainly not by today's academic standards, any more than an astronomer would relish being called an astrologer - or, for that matter, a chemist being called an alchemist or a doctor a witch-doctor. I guess he prefers to think of himself as an envoy of ordinary intelligent commonsense, as someone who is helping to release commonsense - once again - from the shackles of a stultifying authoritarianism. From what I understand, he would like to get rid of both Physics and academic Philosophy and replace them with a return to what used to be called "natural philosophy", or something like that.'

'And what have these discussions done for you, Walt? You've been more involved in them than I have.'

'T's a funny thing, Charlie, but they've made it much easier to understand my own calling. True science, I now see, is not hostile to religion in the way classical science used to be, any more than it's hostile, as you've pointed out, to psychology and sociology. As you say, these studies are - or at least should be - all parts of a continuous philosophical spectrum, without all the old academic departmentalisation. So I'm sure that future scientists won't consider people like me as fools or charlatans who go around teaching mindless mechanisms that they have souls. ''m also happy that the old "universe" concept, which has depicted man lost in an infinite and uncaring "void", no longer has any case to present. I can now teach moral responsibility without feeling that I'm bucking the trend.'

Having said this, Walter walked across to the big window at the dark end of the room and stood for a moment looking out into the blackness of the

forest and up to the starry sky. There were still some faint traces to be seen of the flickering Aurora Borealis, which on two of the previous nights had been so spectacular.

Then he turned and said, almost wonderingly, 'Ill be good to be able to preach with confidence that God speaks to us, not from up there, from some mysterious "on high", across space and time, begging dumb questions like whether His messages come to us at or faster than the "speed of light". From what we've discussed about quantum immediacy it could now be that we receive those messages directly, through one another and through exemplars like Jesus Christ - or Socrates and Plato, if you like, or Mohammed, or Confucius or Buddha - anyone who is imbued with what, for Christians, is the cosmical "Christ" principle, or Logos. This, I believe, lives in us all, even the very worst of us, if we can only recognise it. This seems to me to bring out the Christian meaning of "redemption".'

Charlie, being well aware of the struggles his friend sometimes had in reconciling his faith with the world of science, was pleased for him.

"That's great, Walt, ' he said.

'And there's something else,' continued Walter. 'I like the idea, which is central to Dave's "thesis", as you call it, that in technical probabilistic terms, ordinary meaningful communication is, by definition, a miracle - a continuously occurring and self-evident miracle of improbability. It beats the hell out of the old deistic view.'

"Which is . . . what?' Charlie interrupted, 'Remind me.'

'It's the view put forward by some cosmologists nowadays - you know, the "Big Bang", "the First Milliseconds of Creation", and all that guff; that there was just one divine miracle, right at the start, which set the world going like clockwork only to run down automatically into a cosmical heatdeath, with no more miracles from then on but everything obeying "laws" like those of mechanics and thermodynamics. As Noel kept emphasising, if the world is a machine, then where can there be any true morality or true religion that is not merely expedient or political? No wonder Christianity and mechanistic science could never get on together.'

Charlie said, 'I remember we dealt with that in these discussions. But what do you see as different with the new science?"

'As I see it,' Walter reminded him, 'the new science is theistic which, to me, means that it is an organism, with all its cells interdependent as transactors of information at various levels of simplicity and complexity. That view makes sense of the idea of a deity as something with which we, as individuals, can communicate - to make supplication to, to pray to, which is capable of interceding on our behalf. I now feel sure that when this machine I inhabit, called my "body", wears out and collapses in a heap, the driver of that vehicle, my consciousness, or "soul", which has driven it to wherever it has ended up will just step out of it and carry on from there in some other vehicle, as it were.'

'Well, maybe you're right, Walt,' responded Charlie, somewhat doubtfully. 'These discussions we've had have convinced me that I'll have to do some serious rethinking. But, quite honestly, I prefer to think that in the end there's just complete oblivion.'

Walter considered this, then he said, "You hope so, Charlie, you hope so!'

His friend thought about this for a moment; then he yawned. 'Well, Walt, old buddy, I'm just going to sleep on it.'

The sun shone again the next morning. Breakfast over, Rick and Noel left the restaurant to unhook the camper while the two sisters took their leave of the Frosts and their staff. A short time later, all four gathered at the front of the Lodge, where Carter and Turner were waiting to make their final farewells.

'I'm stopping off at Charlie's place to pick up Walter,' said Rick, as they all shook hands.

'And I have to take my hire-car back to Kamloops and cancel my flight,' Noel informed them, 'then [ll travel on to Vancouver with Helen and Marian." ©

"Take care of yourselves,' said Carter, kissing each sister in turn. Marian suddenly gave him a big hug, then she did the same with a somewhat disconcerted Turner.

'Youre great guys, you two,' she said. 'I hope your wives appreciate you!' She smiled mischievously, 'I should, if I were twenty years older!'

'Really, Marian!' said Helen.

Turner and Carter watched in silence till the last of the vehicles had disappeared, then Carter spoke:

'Dave, how do you rate the chances of meeting such a collection of folks again?"

'Pretty slim, I should imagine. This was a rare "happening" to be sure, although I like to think it would have been no more than standard in the "Golden Age of Greece" when science and philosophy were at their beginnings.'

'Yes, we certainly got something going there, Dave. What amazes me, though, is what you wete carrying in that head of yours which you managed to keep under your hat all those years we were trundling around, just grunting and hollering at each other. Now I think I understand you more than at any time before, but it took all that discussion to do it. Some of those answers you came out with were surprising.'

'D'you know, Tony,' replied his old friend, 'I was just as surprised as you. It must be what in modern jargon is called "group dynamic" or something like that - or what Walter might call "the spirit that moves men". But whichever way you look at it, it's a case of the whole being, as we said, more than just the sum of the parts.'

Then Turner, with a thoughtful look on his face said, 'Last night, I had a dream which seemed to me quite profound. The dream started with a car up on jacks with a flattened tyre. I took the wheel off and rolled it downhill to a garage at the bottom of the slope. When I got there, I became involved in talking with someone and forgot about the wheel. Then when I looked for it, it had gone. I searched for it everywhere in something of a panic, -knowing how vital that wheel was for me to drive home from what was a remote and dreary place. But for the life of me, I couldn't remember where Id left it. It was very worrying. Then, some bloke in overalls came towards me rolling the wheel which was now repaired and inflated. "Strewth!" I said to him, "I've been looking for that all over." "You left it by the airhose", said this bloke. At this my sense of relief was palpable. This was now, if you understand me, a dream within a dream. I found myself wondering how it could be, that in a dream which ostensibly was all mine, this bloke I didn't know was able to inform me of something I'd forgotten, which was that the tyre had been taken to the repair-shop.'

'Now how was it that I was so surprised by this? Why didn't I know where that wheel was until that guy told me? Who was he? He wasn't me, that's for sure, because, since it was all my own dream, I would surely have known it. The guy seemed to have an autonomy, a life of his own which wasn't mine - at least, wasn't under my control or command.'

Carter nodded sympathetically. "Yes, old chap, that is a bit of a puzzle.'

'This got me thinking - in the dream, that is,' Turner continued. 'How was this possible for a figment of my own imagination to act in this way? Then - you know how it is in dreams - some young woman came along and said to me, "You know the reason. It's because we are all no more than characters in a computer game." This made me think - in the dream, that is - that it's as if some constructor of video-games had succeeded in making his characters self-willed and autonomous and that one of those creatures was myself, while the other bloke was some character distinct from myself, under some other operator's control.' He paused, a distant expression on his face.

'Okay, carry on. This is intriguing,' urged his old friend.

"Then I woke up and thought about it. You may recall, Tony, in our preoccupation, years ago, with the works of Omar Khayyám, there was a verse which I remember particularly well. It goes:

The ball no question makes of Ayes and Noes, But Right or Left as strikes the Player goes;

And He that toss't Thee down into the Field, He knows about it all - HE knows - HE knows!

"Yes, I do recall that, now that you mention it,' said Carter. 'Are you saying that this dream of yours has given you a new slant on the idea of a deity?"

'Not quite, Tony. It just made me feel, at a depth I've never felt before, that something I've so far only thought of as a theory is not a theory but a fact. This feeling, if I can put it into words, was to realise the extent to which our modern advances in science and technology have created what seems to be an inexorable drift away from analog towards digital in our

dealings with nature. It makes the world look less and less like a machine, with light zipping around all over the place, and more and more like a super-interactive purely informational Hologram. The more I think about it, the more such a model fits what we now know as the relativisation and quantisation of physical phenomena. As I say, I've thought of this before, but somehow, that dream made it appear in a much clearer, more definite light, with a new depth of feeling to match the idea. It's as though I had been treating that idea as more or less just a model or analogy, and then come to realise, in a "Eureka-like" flash, that it's not just a theory but something I'm really caught up in, that Nature is trying to tell us that it's really the way it is, that we should dump "mechanisms" and think information.'

"Yes, Dave, but that's what we discussed that day with Walter and Noel by the river. So, what's different now?'

''m not quite sure, Tony. I guess it's more a matter of feeling than of reason. That dream has given me a new depth of insight into that flip-side view of reality. With me it's gone from an "as it were" level to a "what else can it be" level, if you get my drift. In this new frame of mind our classical mechanistic tradition of science now seems as primitive as the myths and superstitions of our remote ancestors. We've seen cinematic technology develop from the level of elementary "flickers" to jerky films like the old silent movies - "King Kong", for instance - and from there to colour television and more. Perhaps, in that discussion, I should have mentioned to Walter and Noel that in my work in communications I get to know of the latest developments in that domain. For instance, as you probably know, Tony, there is the Link Trainer .. .'

"Yes, that's been used in the aircraft industry since nineteen-twentynine.'

"You're right, Tony,' replied Turner, 'and since it was coupled to computers in the nineteen-sixties, it now gives trainee pilots both a visual and tactile experience of handling an aircraft in all sorts of conditions, of storms, engine failure and so on, making the pilot's experience virtually real. This is to the extent that when they "crash" it's an experience which is quite traumatic for them as if it was for real. One fella reported to me that as an experimenter in this area of technology he had to take the bus home rather

than his car so as to unstick his mind from the idea that, as he put it, he would gain points for knocking down as many old ladies as he could on zebra crossings!"

'I can well understand that, Dave. It reminds me of the audio-visual movie-drome I went to in the Science museum in Vancouver showing what it was like on a lifeboat rescue. My body wasn't moving, yet I came out as seasick as if I'd actually been on that lifeboat in a heavy sea. And the most impressive holograms I ever saw were in the Trocadero in London . . .'

'I remember that, too, Tony, though I must have seen it at a different time. At the entrance there was a huge trumpet sticking out over the doorway, suspended in the air.'

'And even though we knew it was a hologram, I remember that I and other people inadvertently ducked under it as we entered! These technological advances in just the space of my own lifetime have been awesome. Lord knows, Dave, what it may get like from here on!

'Well, then, Tony, in your imagination, project those developments towards the distant future to where these virtual realities get closer and closer to reality as we know it, to the extent that, eventually, they become indistinguishable from the real thing. This, of course, would mean embellishing current videos with inputs of various kinds until they become interactive quantum holograms, presenting us with the-same sorts of phenomena that we are normally used to, not only visual and auditory but also tactile, with scents, tastes and whatever, in all five categories of sensation.'

'Sure, that's already happening. Dave. As I say, I've witnessed those things myself, as you have, in all sorts of separate incidents. But so far as I know, those facilities have not yet been combined into one single scenario in the way you project. But certainly we now have all the necessary elements for it, such as computers, holograms, interactive computer games and, as you say, flight-simulators. It seems inevitable that, sooner or later, all these separate technological advancements will be put together into a single package.'

"Well, there you are, then, old son. Now imagine these developments continuing till they appear on the grandest and fullest possible scale, where

3.43 they are absolutely indistinguishable from what we know of as ordinary reality. This, you understand, is only a hypothetical projection. In no way am I suggesting that it can ever actually be achieved.'

'So, what are you suggesting, then, Dave?'

"Well, doesn't that suggest to you, Tony, that in that purely theoretical or hypothetical limit, virtual reality and true reality merge. The word 'virtual' becomes redundant and drops out, so that "virtual reality" becomes just reality, with nothing else to which it can be compared and contrasted that is more real and in comparison with which it can sensibly be called an illusion.'

"You mean where it all becomes equally quantum-digital and relative, the same with the real situation as with the virtual one?' asked Carter.

'Precisely, Tony. So it seems to me that we may gather from this purely hypothetical projection that reality itself; as we know it, is not only quantumdigital and relative in that same way, but is also holographic, interactive, and informational, as opposed to mechanical, in the way we've discussed; that it has different parts which are independent and autonomous - that is to say, individually motivated to the same extent that we and other organisms are; that these entities are capable of making plans and have the power of will to put their intentions into effect, in all those ways we normally know about - you see what I'm saying, Tony. As you said, all the elements are there in modern information-technology and in the way we now know that natural phenomena are formed - that is, quantised and observer-based in the same way; that all space, time, motion and so on are observer-projected in the manner that Mach described and which Einstein and Planck - plus physics, generally, have confirmed.'

'So you're saying, in effect, then, Dave, that the intuitions of those empiricists in the tradition of Berkeley, Kant - and of course, Mach - "weren't wrong, but that they were too right, and were simply ahead of their time.'

'Yes, Tony, and that they were unfortunate in not having modern science to support their world-view in the way that Galileo and Newton were supported by the mechanistic science of their day. There is no doubt in my mind that if all these guys had been contemporaries, faced with the same

empirical situation that is presented to physics nowadays, Berkeley's worldview would have won, hands down - not in its original form, of course, but in a far more informed and sophisticated way, as something seriously to be examined as more of Physics than of what now languishes in the name of Philosophy. All the fiddling little scholastic issues about 'realism versus idealism' and suchlike, in which university students of Philosophy have been examined for decades, would have been swept aside and Physics would be merged with Philosophy as a new, more integrated and progressive Natural Science.'

'Okay, Dave, so you are saying, I take it, that what has been a neglected philosophy should be looked at again as a serious contender for the position of true Science, and in that new Science, the model of the Informational Hologram would be supreme in the way that the model of the Machine has been in our physics so far. Anyway, it seems to me that the model of the Computer, or Hologram, as the "God of the Scientists" isn't any more absurd than the Machine has been for classical science - that is, of course, if we think of there being a "God" at all.'

'T agree, Tony. It would put Theology and Cosmology on a par with Physics and Philosophy, all seamlessly joined together on an entirely divergent, common track away from what has gone before, establishing a whole new thought-paradigm, in effect. Just think! All those questions that have been asked so far about nature and the cosmos, our relation to it as conscious beings and so on, would have to change radically.'

With some feeling, he carried on. 'Scholastic Philosophy, now regarded by educationalists as more or less defunct and socially irrelevant, would have to be put back right at the forefront of the university curriculum. It would regain the status it once had at the high point of our Western culture, as "the clearing-house of the sciences". It would be a whole new philosophical ball-game, as revolutionary as anything in our history so far. Pragmatically speaking, though, Tony, I can't see that happening, can you?'

'Not on any global scale, Dave, I agree. But what does that really matter? If it's true, then it's true and the question of whether or not it is socially acceptable must go hang. After all, it's philosophy we're talking about here, not politics or diplomacy. So, what matters, surely, is that it's all there for

the taking, by anyone who has enough confidence in himself and his own reason to put his formal conditioning aside and think for himself. . .'

Turner added, 'As it was in the educationally unfettered way of the original Greek philosophers during that 'Golden age' where just about everything was intellectually "up for grabs".'

'Right, Dave, and goodness knows, we might have started something here with these discussions of ours, which seem to have developed along those same philosophical lines. It's done something for me, at any rate.'

"Well, that's something at least, Tony. Let's see how it goes, eh!'

Carter, leaning on the rail, stared out over the valley seeing it all, with its beckoning distance, the trees, the sky, the sounds and scents. He thought of the Aurora they had seen the previous evening, the wolves howling far off on Green Mountain - the stars, galaxies and so on, the towns and cities back home, the engineering structures he had helped to design and build. Was it really possible to see it all as some kind of super-active, multidimensional Hologram? It was difficult, to say the least. On the other hand, it was now even more difficult to visualise it all as some great Machine, which he now found impossible to square with the findings of modern physics. He tried to balance the two mutually exclusive idea-systems together in his head. It was a challenge, certainly, every bit as big as anything the Wilderness had to offer. The pioneer in him stirred. It was, he decided, something he would have to lock into.

With the sun warming their faces, they leaned for a while longer on the rail of the deck where Smokey and Blue, lying on their backs, were also soaking up the sun. In the distance was the snow-capped peak of Trophy mountain, searingly picked out against the faultless blue of the sky, and every now and again could be heard the peculiar clicking sound of the wings of the remaining butterflies. Every small shift in the near-still air brought a shower of shining yellow-gold leaves from the birches across the way.

Then Carter said, 'I only wish, Dave, that we could have started on this when we were younger.'

"Well, Turner replied, "Better late than never, eh!'

'Sure enough!' his old friend agreed and lapsed again into a contemplative silence. Then he exclaimed suddenly, 'It's a great life, though, Dave, and whichever way we cut it, whether it's all a Machine or a Hologram, whether or not there's a deity, deities or whatever having oversight of it all, it's Life, and life is there for the living.'

'Well, then,' Turner replied, a barely perceptible move of his body making the dogs immediately attentive, 'Let's away and make the most of it!'

From The Mists of Avalon by Marian Bradley

'For this is the great secret, which was known to all educated men in our day: that by what men think, we create the world around us, daily new.

And now the priests, thinking that this infringes upon the power of their God, who created the world once and for all to be unchanging, have closed those doors (which were never doors, except in the minds of men), and the pathway leads only to the priests' Isle, which they have safeguarded with the sound of their church bells .. .

'Truth has many faces and the truth is like the old road to Avalon; it depends on your own will and your own thoughts, whither the road will take you, and whether, at the end, you will arrive in the Holy Isle of Eternity or among the priests with their bells and their death..."

Religion passes out of the Ken of Reason only where the eye of Reason has reached its own Horizon; and. . . Faith is then but its continuation: even as the Day softens away into the sweet Twilight. And Twilight, hushed and breathless, steals into the Darkness.

Samuel Taylor Coleridge

Authors' Notes

AUTHORS' NOTES

Note 1: A Short History of Modern Phenomenalism

The first accredited modern phenomenalist was George Berkeley (16851753). In his rejection of classical materialism, he made the first move towards modern relativism as this was to appear in the form given to it by Einstein.

In the atomic materialism of Democritus (circa. 460-370 BC) the reality of physical objects lay, not in the appearances they present in observation (phenomena) but in their unseen and only guessed-at atomic constituents. What Berkeley did, presaging the relativism of Einstein, was completely to invert this Democritan way of thinking, giving precedence, in our scientific knowledge of the world, to the observational information-data, the phenomena, over Democritus' theoretical 'atoms'. In other words, for Berkeley, the reality of anything consisted, first and foremost, in being observed - or 'perceived? in the language he used at the time. Anything else we may think or talk about, space, time and even the existence of atoms and the passage of light between them, is projected from information supplied a priori by the senses.

This choice of the word 'perceived', however, was unfortunate because it made it seem that, in order to exist, a material object has to be actually observed by a human observer, which made the object's unobserved. existence as a thing in itself indescribable. The problem Berkeley thus bequeathed to his followers was that of how we can ever be assured of the reality of anything other than our own subjective impressions - or 'ideas, as Berkeley called them.

This problem was handed on, via Berkeley's successor, David Hume (1711-1776), to Immanuel Kant (1724-1804). By taking Berkeley's system of ideas (idealism) to its logical conclusion, Hume had shown that if these "ideas are all there is, then there can be nothing to hold them together other than one's own pure 'habits' of perceiving. This reduction to absurdity,

by Hume, of Berkeley's 'way by ideas' galvanised Kant. He could not see how it was possible, if what Hume had deduced was true, that the burgeoning science of Isaac Newton (1642-1727) was so spectacularly successful.

To reconcile Berkeley's phenomenalism with Newtonian science, Kant dispensed with Berkeley's 'ideas' and replaced them with 'concepts'. These he sorted into a system of logical classes and categories, designed to paraphrase, in his phenomenological terms, the practical science of Galileo and Newton.

In this way, Berkeley's initial move towards modern relativism was brought, by Kant, to its second stage of development. Unfortunately, Kant's 'concepts' were little improvement on either Berkeley's 'ideas' or Hume's 'habits', because they were still confined within the human psyche. As viewed from the standpoint of objective science, Kant's version of phenomenalism was therefore no more than another form of 'subjective idealism'.

The third stage in the move towards modern relativism was made by Ernst Mach (1838-1916), who was the philosophical mentor of Albert Einstein (1879-1955). Mach replaced Kant's psychological system of 'concepts' with a system of objective relations between objects as viewed in different observational frameworks, or perspectives. This, in effect, was the system that became the famous Special Theory of Relativity presented by Einstein in 1905.

'Io Mach's consternation, however, Einstein took the backwards step of attempting to reconcile the logically irreconcilable, by basing his relativism on the atomic materialism of Democritus. As Mach wrote:

I gather from publications which have reached me, and especially from my correspondence that I am gradually becoming regarded as the forerunner of relativity . . . 1 must, however, as assuredly disclaim to be a forerunner of the relativists as I withhold from the atomistic belief of the present day school.2°

30 Mach, E. (posthumous publ. 1921): Prinzipien der physikalischen Optik. Quoted in Wolters, G.: 'Emst Mach and the Theory of Relativity', Philosophia Naturalis (Transl., S. Gillies), 21, 630641, 1984,

In this way, light, which for Mach was simply the sensory/instrumental data from which the phenomena of space, time and motion are observationally projected, was presented by Einstein, not as an integral part of that phenomenological scenario but as a conveyor of those bits of data through space, in the form of esoterically conceived wave-particles, called 'photons', from the underworld of atoms that Democritus had supposed was their root source. This was what Mach spurned as the 'atomistic belief of the present-day school'.

For Mach, the transition that Berkeley had initiated from Democritan materialism to modern relativism had stalled. Einstein's version of 'relativity had ended-up in a 'hung state' in which it was neither the one thing nor the other, which is why 'Relativity', for all its practical usefulness to science, became notorious as a by-word for intellectual obscurity. In Mach's view, therefore, Einstein's theory was no more than a 'transitory inspiration in the history of science'.3! At the present time of writing, this opinion of Mach's is supported by the growing number of conferences and discussion groups, nowadays convened to accommodate the increasing dissatisfactions of scientists with Einstein's idiosyncratic version of Machian relativism, not least on account of paradoxes the theory generates when employed in contexts of quantum physics. This offers testimony to the fact that, despite the purely practical-physics advances made by Einstein, the philosophical transition from metaphysical materialism to phenomenalism - that is, from a classical matter-based to a truly empirical information-based science - remains to be completed.

It is this philosophical irresolution, right at the heart of contemporary physics, and its implications for society generally that forms the basis of discourse for the characters in this book.

Note 2: Light-Speed versus Instantaneous Action-at-a-Distance

How can it be that light-signals are transacted between observers at the finite distance-time rate c whereas in their own 'proper' time the quantum elements of the signal are instantaneous? This is generally regarded as a

31 Mach, E., op. cit.

paradox, a contradiction in terms.*? Nothing so self-contradictory, it is assumed, can occur in nature. Ah, but it can. A row of electric light-bulbs flashed in succession can create the impression of motion, which perception theorists call the 'p47 phenomenon'. This, of course, is the basis of cinematography in which a series of photographs called 'stills' are flashed through a projector to create the typical movie scenario.

So it just isn't true that in distant interaction, relativistic time-delay and quantum instantaneity are contradictory. There is no logical contradiction in cancelling the customary idea of the 'photon' as an inscrutably travelling particle and thinking of it instead as a quantum element of the light signal, what Mach called a 'sense-datumy' (sense/instrument datum), or quantum 'still'. It is out of patterns and sequences of these that the ultimately irreducible elements of all physical phenomena are 'cinematically' projected.

Note 3: The Phenomenon of Reflection in Direct Quantum Interaction

In a two-way communication between relatively stationary observers, a central time-keeper, A, signals 'time-zero' to B. (Recall that there is no question of the proper-time taken by the 'signal itself" from A to B which is instantaneous. This was explained by Turner in Chapter 5.) However, because the distance s of A from B is, for B, a time s/c in seconds, when B sees A's signal he sets his own clock to 0 + s/c. Recall that c, here, is a pure constant, or observational conversion factor, which is the same for all observers in all systems of reference, not a 'velocity' which can in no way be the same for relatively moving observers.

This immediate response of B to A's time-signal is then observed back at A, in the same direct-action way; that is, with no question of how or at what 'speed' the signal 'travels'. But again, the distance s of B from A is, for A, another time s/c. So when A observes B's immediate response to his signal there is added to that signal, which reads '0 + s/c another time s/c,

32 This is the well-known EPR (Einstein, Podolski, Rosen) paradox, which is the seeming contradiction between Einstein's 'finite speed-limit' interpretation of c and the instantaneous actionat-a-distance of the Copenhagen School of quantum physics after Niels Bohr and John Bell, of

CERN.

so that the total time for the sending and return of the signal at A, is 2s/c. The same applies, of course, for a central time-system or "GMT" involving any number of observers, so long as there is no significantly large motion of any one or more observers with regard to the system, in which case, time-dilation becomes a factor in the manner described in 4, below.

This answers to what is classically conceived as the interval for the transmission and reflection of a 'light-signal in vacuo', except that it is now couched in purely phenomenological, direct-action terms (see 2 above), without having to assume any underlying 'God's time' in which the signal takes place, or any ubiquitous perception of the two distant observations simultaneously, which is impossible. This removes any necessity to think of some travelling agency, in the form of waves, particles, wave-particles, in 'fields', 'ethers' or whatever, being responsible for conveyance of the signal. It thus dispenses with all metaphysical speculations as to what the signal may be 'doing, in itself', behind the scenes, between the two assignations.

Note 4: Time Dilation and the Notorious "Twins Paradox"

Let the intrinsic spectral period of a particular atom be denoted by t, and let that period relative to an observer be denoted by tR. The distance in metres between that atom and the observer is then denoted by s.

Now in Normal Realism, this distance s in metres, divided by the constant c is a time in seconds, where c is a dimensional constant, not a 'velocity'. (This was discussed in the argument with Professor Craig in Chapter 8.) Since these measures, t, tR and s/c are all in the same units, they can be represented dimensionally in the following orthogonal, or rectangular way (see

[ Figure 1: Time-dilation triangle - $t$, $s/c$, and $t_R$ related by Pythagoras ]

In this depiction, the length of the body's motion vector, unlike the traditional scalar graph of a uniform motion, is represented by the sloping line in the diagram which, by Pythagoras, is the geometrical resultant of the two components, t, tR and s/c, viz.:

$$t_R = \sqrt{(s/c)^2 + t^2} \tag{1}$$

This simple, sixth-form school-level space-time formula gives precisely the same value of time-stretching ('dilation') as is given by Einstein's far more complicated formula for relativistic time-dilation. This can be seen if we substitute for the distance s in (1) the relative, or observed, velocity vtR and then simplify by ordinary rules of algebra. This produces the standard orthodox, Einsteinian equation:

$$t_R = \frac{t}{\sqrt{1 - v^2/c^2}} \tag{2}$$

Note that the direction of v is inconsequential, since in squaring of the term, whether v is negative or positive, the result is uniformly positive. Note also that this result is obtained without involving any of the historical rigmarole of 'electrodynamics', 'light-signals in space', the 'ether' and so on, hence is in perfect accordance with the need for economy of conception (Ockham's Razor). This is simplifying but not simplistic. Everything of positive value for physics that follows from Einstein's theory follows from this Pythagorean formulation.

The idea that there is a paradox connected with relativistic time-dilation stems from the mistaken assumption that when a pair of twins travel away from each other and then return together, the effect of the time-dilation on both of the twins is symmetrical so that each one's time is dilated equally relative to the other. This creates the conundrum that the twins end-up both younger and older than each other which is, of course, nonsense. However, there is no such conundrum, as may be seen as follows.

Let tA be an atomic period or set of atomic periods in the life of an observer A, and let tB be that same period in the life of another observer B. Now let these observers be a pair of twins starting close together in empty space, remote from any very large bodies, and then moving apart at a uniform speed v relative to each other. Let us also assume that throughout the motion they keep observing each other through telescopes fitted with spectroscopes which record the number of atomic periods counted. With v in the standard time-dilation formula (2), being the same for both twins, the time-dilation of each twin relatively to the other is, so far, perfectly symmetrical.

However, let us now suppose that as viewed in B's spectroscope, twin A, having travelled an observational distance s in the time tA seen to be registered by A's atomic periods, stops and immediately reverses his motion to return to B over that same observational distance s at the same speed v in another of his own atomic time-periods tA. This is where the symmetry of the two observers ends because, quite obviously, the inertial (e.g. visceral) effect of that reversing action undertaken by A is immediate for him while, as viewed by B, that same reversing action takes place at the distance s, hence a time s/c away. Clearly, then, during the out-and-back relative motion of the two observers, the number of A's atomic periods counted by B cannot be the same as B's periods counted by A. As if it needed saying, this is because the switch from longer to shorter periods (red to blue) takes place earlier for B, as seen by A, than those of A as seen by B. So when the two return together 4's atomic clocks will have registered fewer ticks than B's, hence he will have aged less than B who, conversely, will have aged more than A.

This means that there is no symmetry, hence no paradox. It is the reversing action which makes all the difference. If it is A who takes that action, then it is A who ends up the younger. On the other hand, if it is B who takes that action, then the situation is the opposite way around.

Note 5: Refraction

A conducting medium is therefore, in effect, a system travelling - or, in this case, 'vibrating' - at a speed v relative to the observer, in which all

processes are time-dilated, or slowed, in comparison with the observer's own time (see note 4 above). In an experiment by Armand Fizeau, in 1851, a transparent medium (i.e., water) was made to flow at great speed around a u-tube. It was discovered, in effect, that the refractive index of the liquid increased with its speed v. In other words, the 'speed' c, of the transport of light-energy in the liquid increased in the direction of the flow but in diminishing amounts with the increase in v precisely in accordance with the relativistic time-dilation formula. That is to say, the process of light conduction, like all other processes in the medium, is time-dilated relatively to the observer. So refraction is just another aspect of time-dilation as explained in note 2. This 'slowing of c in a medium, therefore, does not conflict with the instantaneity of quantum interaction. It simply consists of a slowed-down series of direct interactions instead of a single direct action.

The relative speed v (or in this case, frequency of vibration) is, of course, different in different media, with the refraction being more or less proportional to the density of the medium.

Summary

What is discussed in the foregoing chapters is the possible completion of the revolution which Einstein began towards Machian phenomenalism. This is by taking light out of space and putting the space - and all it contains - in the light, This should allow the 'Relativist Revolution', which began with Einstein in 1905, to turn full circle instead of being, as it now is, stuck half way between the twentieth and twenty-first centuries.***4
